<image-desc>The multi-colored Google logo in its characteristic serif typeface.</image-desc>

This is a digital copy of a book that was preserved for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project to make the world’s books discoverable online.

It has survived long enough for the copyright to expire and the book to enter the public domain. A public domain book is one that was never subject to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary from country to country. Public domain books are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture, and knowledge that is often difficult to discover.

Marks, notations, and other marginalia <note>original: "marginalia"; refers to notes or scribbles written in the margins of a book</note> present in the original volume will appear in this file—a reminder of this book’s long journey from the publisher to a library and finally to you.

## Usage guidelines

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the public, and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive; therefore, in order to keep providing this resource, we have taken steps to prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on automated querying.

We also ask that you:

+ *Make non-commercial use of the files:* We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for personal, non-commercial purposes.

+ *Refrain from automated querying:* Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google’s system. If you are conducting research on machine translation, optical character recognition, or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help.

+ *Maintain attribution:* The Google “watermark” you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it.

+ *Keep it legal:* Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, that the work is also in the public domain for users in other countries. Whether a book is still under copyright varies from country to country, and we cannot offer guidance on whether any specific use of any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book’s appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner anywhere in the world. Copyright infringement liability can be quite severe.

## About Google Book Search

Google’s mission is to organize the world’s information and to make it universally accessible and useful. Google Book Search helps readers discover the world’s books while helping authors and publishers reach new audiences. You can search through the full text of this book on the web at <insert>http://books.google.com/</insert>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page marks the beginning of Aristotle's Nicomachean Ethics, introducing the fundamental concept that all human activity aims toward a perceived good.</meta>

### THE
# NICOMACHEAN ETHICS.

---

## BOOK I.

---

### CHAPTER I.

Every art and every inquiry <note>original: "method"</note>, and similarly every action and deliberate choice, seems to aim at some good. For this reason, it has been rightly said that *the good* is that which all things desire. 

However, there appears to be a certain difference among ends. Some ends are activities <term>energies/energeia: the state of being in active operation or exercise</term>, while others are specific products <term>works/erga: the finished results produced by an activity</term> that exist apart from the activities themselves. In those cases where there are ends beyond the actions, the products are naturally superior to the activities that created them. 

Since there are many types of actions, arts, and sciences, there are also many different ends. For example, the end of medicine is health;

<summary>Aristotle introduces the idea that every human pursuit aims at a specific "good" and distinguishes between activities that are ends in themselves and those that produce a separate result.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, ethics, the good, ends, activities, products, medicine, health</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle's discussion of the hierarchy of arts and sciences, explaining how subordinate crafts serve higher "master" arts, and introduces the search for the ultimate "highest good" that motivates all human action.</meta>

->4<-
->THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK I.<-

the end of the shipbuilding art is a ship; of the military art, victory; and of the art of household management <note>original: "economic art"</note>, wealth. But arts of this kind are organized <note>original: "arranged"</note> under a single primary authority <term>power/dynamis: a faculty, capacity, or field of expertise</term>. For example, the craft of making bridles is subordinate to the art of horsemanship <note>original: "equestrian art"</note>, as are all other crafts that produce equipment for horses. 

Both horsemanship and every action in war are, in turn, organized under the military art. In the same way, other arts are subordinate to other higher authorities. In all these cases, the ends of the master arts <term>architectonic: the governing or foundational sciences that organize subordinate ones</term> are more desirable <note>original: "eligible"</note> than the ends of the subordinate arts. This is because the subordinate ends are pursued only for the sake of the master ends. It makes no difference whether the activities <term>energies/energeia: the state of being in active operation or exercise</term> themselves are the ends of the actions, or if the end is something else produced by them, as seen in the sciences mentioned above.

***

### CHAPTER II.

If, therefore, there is a certain end for our actions which we wish to obtain for its own sake, and if we desire everything else only for the sake of this end—and if we do not choose every single thing for the sake of something else (for that would lead to an infinite regress <note>original: "progression to infinity"</note>, making our desire <note>original: "appetite"</note> empty and useless)—if this is the case, it is clear that this must be the good, and indeed the highest good.

<summary>Aristotle explains that subordinate arts (like bridle-making) serve higher master arts (like horsemanship) and argues that there must be an ultimate "highest good" that we pursue for its own sake to avoid an endless chain of motives.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, ethics, master arts, architectonic, hierarchy of goods, infinite regress, highest good, military art, economics</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s argument that there must be a "highest good" for human life. Having established that such a goal exists, he now seeks to identify the specific field of study responsible for understanding it, identifying "political science" as the master discipline.</meta>

->5<-
# CHAP. II. ETHICS.

It is clear that this goal will be the good, and specifically the highest good. Wouldn't knowing this goal be of great importance for how we live our lives? Like archers with a target to aim at, we will be more likely to achieve what is right if we have a clear mark. 

If this is the case, however, we must try to outline <note>original: "adumbrate"</note> what it is and show which science or field of study it belongs to. It would seem to belong to the most authoritative science—one that is truly <term>architectonic: the governing or master science that organizes all subordinate fields</term>. Political science <note>original: "political power [or science]"</note> appears to be this kind of field. It is this science that ordains which other sciences should be established in cities, which ones each person should learn, and to what level of mastery. 

We also see that the most respected fields of study are organized under political science; for example, military strategy, household management <note>original: "economical powers"</note>, and public speaking <note>original: "rhetorical powers"</note>. Since all other practical sciences make use of political science, and since it also establishes laws regarding what we should do and what we should avoid, the goal of this science must encompass the goals of all the others. Therefore, the goal of political science is the human good itself. 

Even though the good of an individual and the good of a city-state are the same in nature, securing and maintaining the good of the city appears to be something greater and more complete. While we should be satisfied to achieve the good for even one individual, it is far more noble and divine to achieve it for a whole nation or for cities. This, therefore, is the focus of our current method of study, which is a form of political science.

<vocab>Ethics, architectonic (master-science), political science, human good, practical sciences, military strategy, household management, public speaking</vocab>

<summary>Aristotle identifies political science as the "master science" because it organizes all other disciplines and aims at the highest human good—the well-being of the entire community.</summary>
<keywords>highest good, political science, architectonic, ethics, social well-being, hierarchy of sciences</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introduction to the study of ethics and political science. Aristotle has just established that political science is the master science because it aims at the highest human good. He now shifts to a discussion of methodology, warning the reader about the level of precision they should expect from this field of study.</meta>

->6<-
# THE NICOMACHEAN ETHICS, BOOK I.

### CHAPTER 3.

Our discussion will be adequate if it is made as clear <note>original: "perspicuous"</note> as the subject matter allows. We must not look for the same degree of accuracy in all discussions, just as we do not expect the same level of precision in every type of craftsmanship. 

The concepts of what is noble <note>original: "beautiful"</note> and what is just—the primary concerns of political science—contain so much variety and uncertainty <note>original: "ambiguity"</note> that some people believe they exist only by custom <note>original: "law"</note> and not by nature. The idea of "the good" carries a similar uncertainty, because it often happens that people are actually harmed by things that are generally considered good. For example, some have been ruined by their wealth, while others have lost their lives because of their courage <note>original: "fortitude"</note>. 

Therefore, when we speak about such variable subjects, we must be satisfied to demonstrate the truth with a rough outline <note>original: "a rude adumbration"</note>. Since we are discussing things that are only true for the most part <note>original: "which have a frequency of subsistence"</note>, our conclusions will naturally share that same level of general accuracy. 

In this same spirit, the student should accept the arguments presented here. It is the mark of an educated <note>original: "erudite"</note> person to look for only as much precision in each subject as the nature of that subject allows. Demanding strict logical demonstrations from a public speaker <note>original: "rhetorician"</note> is as absurd as accepting a "perhaps" or a "probably" from a mathematician. 

Every person is a good judge of the things they know...

<summary>Aristotle explains that ethics and political science deal with variable human behaviors, meaning they cannot provide the same mathematical certainty as the hard sciences. He argues that an educated person should only expect a "rough outline" of the truth in these fields.</summary>
<keywords>methodology, precision, ethics, political science, nature vs. law, courage, wealth, logic, rhetoric</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle's discussion in Book I, Chapter 3 of the Nicomachean Ethics. He is concluding his point that it is as absurd to accept persuasive rhetoric from a mathematician as it is to demand strict logical demonstrations from a public speaker.</meta>

->7<-

# CHAPTER 3. ETHICS.

...which he knows, and of these he is a good judge. Therefore, a person who is educated in a particular subject judges that subject well, but a person who is broadly educated is a proper judge of everything in general. 

Consequently, a young person is not a suitable student <note>original: "auditor"</note> of political science <note>In this context, "political science" refers to the broader study of human affairs, including ethics and social behavior.</note>, for they are inexperienced in the practical actions of life. Since our reasoning is derived from and concerns these very actions, a youth lacks the necessary background. Furthermore, if a person yields to their passions, they will study ethical doctrines in vain and without any benefit, because the goal of this study is not theoretical knowledge, but practical action. 

It makes no difference whether a person is young in years or simply immature in character. The deficiency is not caused by the passage of time, but by living and pursuing every goal based on impulse <note>original: "passion"</note>. For such people—much like those who lack self-control <note>original: "the intemperate"</note>—knowledge is useless. However, for those who regulate their desires and actions according to reason, a knowledge of these subjects will be immensely beneficial. 

This concludes our introduction regarding the proper student of ethics, how they should approach these discussions, and the topics we propose to consider in this treatise.

<summary>Aristotle argues that a student of ethics and politics must be mature and experienced, as the goal of the study is to improve one's actions rather than to simply gain theoretical knowledge.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Ethics, political science, student, experience, passion, reason, action, maturity</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from the introduction of ethics to the definition of its primary goal: happiness. Having established who is fit to study political science, Aristotle now begins to investigate the specific nature of the highest human good.</meta>

->8<-

THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK I.

## CHAPTER IV.

**REPEATING**, therefore, what we have said: since all knowledge and deliberate choice aim for a certain good, let us explain what we say political science <note>As noted previously, "political science" refers to the study of human flourishing within a community.</note> aspires to, and what the supreme good of all our actions is. Regarding its name, nearly everyone is in agreement; both the common people <note>original: "the vulgar"</note> and the educated call it happiness <note>original: "felicity"</note>. They believe that to live well and to act well are the same thing as being happy. 

Regarding the nature of happiness, however, people disagree; the general public does not hold the same opinion as the wise. For some believe it belongs to the category of things that are clear and obvious, such as pleasure, wealth, or honor; but others claim it is something else entirely. Frequently, the same person will change their opinion: when they are sick, they believe happiness is health, but when they are poor, they believe it is wealth. Those who are aware of their own ignorance often admire those who make grand claims that are beyond their understanding. 

There are also some who believe that, besides these many specific goods, there is another Good that exists by itself <note>Aristotle is referring here to Plato’s theory of "Forms," which suggests that a perfect, abstract version of "Good" exists independently of physical things.</note>, which is the cause of all these other things being good. To examine every single opinion would perhaps be a pointless undertaking; it will be enough to consider...

<summary>Aristotle observes that while everyone agrees the ultimate goal of life is happiness, people define it in wildly different ways—ranging from physical pleasure and wealth to abstract philosophical concepts.</summary>

<keywords>happiness, felicity, political science, supreme good, pleasure, wealth, honor, Plato's Forms, Aristotle</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the nature of the "highest good," shifting focus to the proper methodology for studying ethics. Aristotle begins to argue that a student of ethics must already possess a foundation of good character to understand the "why" behind moral principles.</meta>

<page-num>9</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER IV. ETHICS.</header>

...those that are most prominent, or which appear to be reasonable in some respect. We must not overlook the fact, however, that arguments starting *from* principles and those leading *towards* principles differ from each other. For Plato rightly questioned and investigated this: whether the path should lead from principles or toward them, much like a race that goes from the presiding official to the finish line, or the reverse. 

For we must begin with things that are known. But "known" things exist in two different ways: some things are known to us, while others are known in an absolute sense <note>original: "simply known." In Aristotelian philosophy, "known to us" refers to our sensory experiences and observations, while "simply known" refers to universal, scientific truths that exist regardless of our perception.</note>. Perhaps, therefore, we should begin with the things that are familiar to us. 

Consequently, it is necessary that anyone listening to discussions about things that are beautiful and just—and in short, about political concerns—must have been raised with good habits if he is to benefit from the teaching. For the starting point is the fact *that a thing is so* <insert>namely, that certain actions are honorable and others are not</insert>. If this is sufficiently clear, it is not at all necessary to know *why* it is so. A person with this background either already possesses ethical principles or will easily acquire them. However, let the person who has neither of these qualities listen to what the poet Hesiod says:

> That man is best who figures everything out for himself,
> Foreseeing what will be best in the end;
> He also is good who follows wise advice.
> But he who is neither wise on his own,
> Nor willing to take another’s words to heart,
> Is a useless and worthless man.

<vocab>Plato, Hesiod, principles, ethics, political concerns, excellence</vocab>

<summary>Aristotle discusses the methodology of ethics, arguing that one must start from familiar moral observations and that a student must have a good upbringing to truly grasp ethical principles.</summary>
<keywords>methodology, Plato, Hesiod, moral education, principles, character development</keywords>

## CHAPTER V.

Let us return, however, to the point where we drifted from our subject. For it seems that people quite reasonably form their opinions about the nature of the "good" and happiness <note>original: "felicity"</note> based on the different types of lives they lead. 

The unrefined <note>original: "vulgar"</note>, and the most worthless part of mankind, place happiness in pleasure; for this reason, they embrace a life focused on sensory enjoyment. There are three primary types of lives that are most prominent: the one we have just mentioned (the life of pleasure), the political life, and the third is the contemplative life <note>the life of philosophical study and reflection</note>. 

The majority of people, therefore, appear to be completely slavish, deliberately choosing a life fit for cattle. They justify their opinion by pointing to the example of many people in power who have preferred a life of indulgence and lived like Sardanapalus <note>Sardanapalus was a legendary king of Assyria, famous in the ancient world as a symbol of extreme luxury and decadence.</note>. 

However, people of refined minds and those who are active in public life place happiness in honor; for honor is almost the ultimate goal <note>original: "end"</note> of the political life. Yet even this appears to be more superficial than the "good" which is the object of our current investigation. Honor seems to depend more on those who bestow the honor than on the person who is honored. But we intuit <note>original: "prophesy"</note> that the true "good" is something belonging properly to the individual, which is difficult to take away from its possessor. Furthermore, it appears that people pursue honor so that they...

<summary>Aristotle identifies three main types of lives—pleasure, political, and contemplative—and argues that while the masses mistake pleasure for happiness, refined individuals mistake it for honor, which is too dependent on others to be the "highest good."</summary>
<keywords>three lives, happiness, pleasure, political life, honor, Sardanapalus, contemplative life, the Good</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of common definitions of happiness. Having argued that honor is too superficial because it depends on others, the author now examines whether virtue itself—the quality for which honor is given—is the ultimate "good."</meta>

<page-num>11</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER V. ETHICS.</header>

...they may believe themselves to be worthy people. Therefore, they seek to be honored by wise men, and by those who know them, with the goal of being recognized for their virtue. It is evident, then, that according to these people, virtue is more excellent than honor. Perhaps, however, someone might argue <note>original: "apprehend"</note> that virtue itself is the ultimate goal <note>original: "end"</note> of the political life. 

But even this appears to be more incomplete <note>original: "imperfect"</note> than the "highest good" ought to be. For it seems possible that a person who possesses virtue might sleep, or remain inactive <note>original: "unemployed"</note> throughout their entire life; furthermore, they might be afflicted with evils and experience the greatest misfortunes. No one would declare a man living such a life to be happy, unless they were simply trying to defend a stubborn philosophical position. We have said enough about these matters; I have discussed them sufficiently in my other writings <note>original: "miscellaneous writings"; likely referring to Aristotle's more popular or lost works</note>. 

The third type of life is the contemplative life <note>the life of philosophical study and reflection</note>, which we shall make the object of our consideration later. 

The life dedicated to the acquisition of riches, however, is a sort of forced <note>original: "certain violent life"; meaning a life constrained by the necessity of labor or unnatural pursuit</note> existence. It is evident that wealth is not the "good" we are investigating, for wealth is merely useful for the sake of obtaining something else. Therefore, the goals mentioned previously <note>pleasure, honor, and virtue</note> might be considered "ends" <note>ultimate goals</note> rather than wealth, for they are loved for their own sake. Yet it appears that happiness <note>original: "felicity"</note> does not consist in those either, even though many arguments are brought forward to prove that it does. We shall, therefore, dismiss these topics.

<vocab>virtue, honour, political life, contemplative life, riches, wealth, felicity, ethics</vocab>

<summary>Aristotle concludes that neither honor, virtue, nor wealth constitutes the "highest good," as virtue can coexist with suffering and wealth is merely a means to an end.</summary>
<keywords>virtue, honor, wealth, happiness, contemplative life, political life, ends and means</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the nature of the "highest good." Having dismissed wealth, honor, and physical pleasure as the ultimate goal of life, Aristotle now turns to a more abstract philosophical target: the Platonic Theory of Ideas, specifically the "Idea of the Good."</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>
<header>THE NICOMACHEAN ETHICS: BOOK I.</header>

## CHAPTER VI.

Perhaps, however, it is better to examine the concept of universal good and investigate how it is said to exist <note>original: "subsist"</note>. Such an investigation will be difficult <note>original: "arduous"</note> because the men who introduced the doctrine of "Ideas" <note>This refers to Plato and his followers, who argued that "the Good" was an abstract, perfect Form that exists independently of the physical world.</note> are our friends.<sup>1</sup> But it may...

<sup>1</sup> Nothing demonstrates more clearly that Aristotle was not actually hostile toward the Platonic Theory of Ideas than the objections he raises against the existence of "good" when it is considered as something existing independently—as the primary cause of all other goodness. The ease with which these objections can be answered proves what we have noted elsewhere: Aristotle’s opposition to Plato’s doctrine was intended only to protect it from being misunderstood by people with shallow minds. 

Before addressing Aristotle's objections, it is necessary to briefly explain Plato's view on "the Good." Plato described the primary principle of the universe as ***the One*** and ***the Good***. The first name refers to its ultimate simplicity and its role as the primary cause <note>original: "causality"</note>. The second name refers to its existence as the ultimate object of desire for all things. 

Because this principle is ***the One***, Plato describes it as "beyond being" <note>original: "superessential"</note>; this is because "being" itself implies a variety or plurality that "the One" must transcend. Therefore, all things derive their existence <note>original: "subsistence"</note> from this principle through its goodness. They expand toward it and strive for it, as it is the source of the perfection and goodness that they are naturally designed to receive.

When Aristotle, therefore, says, "Perhaps it is better to consider universal good," the word ***universal*** here must not be understood in the... <note>The text cuts off here, but likely continues to distinguish between a logical "universal" (a category) and a metaphysical "universal" (a source).</note>

<summary>Aristotle begins a critique of Plato’s "Theory of Ideas" regarding the nature of "the Good," while the commentator argues that Aristotle’s critique is meant to clarify rather than debunk Plato’s philosophy.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Plato, Theory of Ideas, universal good, the One, causality, superessential, Nicomachean Ethics</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues Aristotle’s critique of the Platonic "Idea of the Good." Having established that it is a sacred duty to prefer truth over the opinions of friends (referring to Plato), Aristotle begins to argue that "good" is not a single, uniform concept but something that manifests differently across various categories of existence.</meta>

<page-num>13</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER VI. ETHICS.</header>

...it might perhaps seem to be better, and indeed necessary for the <term>preservation of truth</term> <note>original: "salvation of truth"</note>, to overturn the <term>opinions</term> even of our friends. For since both are our friends [that is, <term>Plato</term> and the <term>truth</term>], it is a sacred duty to give the preference to <term>truth</term>. 

Those, however, who have introduced this <term>opinion</term> do not create <term>Ideas of things</term> in which they admit there is an order of <term>first</term> <note>original: "prior"</note> and <term>second</term> <note>original: "posterior"</note>. Because of this, they do not establish a single <term>Idea of numbers</term> <note>Platonists believed numbers followed a sequence (1, 2, 3), and therefore could not be grouped under one single abstract "Idea."</note>. 

But the word <term>good</term> is used in relation to <term>substance</term> <note>original: "essence"</note>, to <term>quality</term>, and to <term>relation</term>. However, that which exists in itself <note>original: "subsistence per se"</note> and has its own <term>substance</term> is naturally prior to that which exists only as a <term>relative</term> thing. For a <term>relative</term> thing resembles a <term>branch of a tree</term> or an <term>accidental property of being</term>; therefore, there cannot be one <term>common Idea</term> shared between these different categories. 

Again, <term>good</term> is described in as many ways as <term>being</term> itself is. For it is described in <term>substance</term>, such as <term>God</term> and <term>intellect</term> [which are <term>substances</term> said to be <term>good</term>]; in <term>quality</term>, such as the <term>virtues</term>; in <term>quantity</term>, as the <term>moderate</term> amount; in <term>relation</term>, as <term>utility</term>; in <term>time</term>, as the right <term>moment</term> <note>original: "occasion"</note>; and in <term>place</term>, as a suitable <term>dwelling</term> <note>original: "habitation"</note>; and in the same manner across the other <term>categories</term> <note>original: "predicaments"</note>. 

It is evident, therefore, that there cannot be one single, <term>common universal good</term>; for if there were, it would not be described in all these different ways. 

<note>The following section appears to be a philosophical commentary defending the Platonic view against Aristotle's critique.</note>

The case is not the same as in <term>logical studies</term> <note>original: "speculations"</note>. In logic, the <term>universal</term> (the general concept) that is applied to many things is <term>derived later</term> <note>original: "of posterior origin"</note>—meaning we see many good things and then create the concept of "good." But here, the "Universal Good" exists <term>before the many</term>, and the many things derive their <term>existence</term> <note>original: "subsistence"</note> from it. 

In short, just as all <term>Ideas</term>—when considered in their primary <term>existence</term> within a <term>divine mind</term> <note>original: "intellect"</note>—are *wholes* and *universals* that have a <term>substance</term> prior to and separate from the physical <term>forms</term> found in bodies, even more so must *the* <term>Good</term> (the *indescribable principle* of all things) be called *universal*. It is the *whole of all wholes*, comprehending *all* things within itself in a way that is beyond substance and beyond description. 

Hence, the objection of <term>Aristotle</term> (as we shall see) applies only to that *universal* <term>good</term> used in <term>logical definitions</term> <note>original: "predication"</note>, and not to the Good which is the <term>principle of the universe</term>. The former comes <term>after</term> the many things, but the latter exists <term>before the many</term>.

<summary>Aristotle argues that "good" cannot be a single Platonic Idea because it is used across different categories of existence (substance, quality, etc.). The commentary counters that while logical universals come after physical things, the Divine Good exists before and causes all things.</summary>

...in all the categories, but in only one. ¹ Furthermore, since there is one <term>science</term> <note>original: "science," referring to a structured branch of knowledge or *episteme*</note> for things that exist according to a single idea, there should be one specific science for all good things. However, there are actually many sciences of things...

¹ Plato, as we have previously noted, named *the One, the Ineffable, and the Good* as the common cause of all beings and placed it above all things. He says that it is the cause of all things, but is not itself any one of those things. For this reason, it is "above being" and is not itself a "being"—not because it lacks existence, but because it is situated above all existence. All secondary goods are therefore referred back to it as the <term>Common Good</term>, which is shared by all other goods. 

Every good that comes after <note>original: "posterior to"</note> this inexpressible <note>original: "ineffable"</note> principle—being something distinct—is considered "good" because it participates in that principle, to the extent that each thing is capable of doing so. But *the Good itself* has a transcendent <term>existence</term> <note>original: "super-expanded subsistence"</note> and is nothing other than *the Good*. For this reason, it is also truly and primarily One, as it cannot be viewed in connection with anything else. 

What contradiction follows, then, if—as the cause of all things that gives existence to everything described by the <term>ten categories</term> <note>Aristotle’s classification of all things that can be said about an object, such as its substance, quality, or quantity</note>—it is said to be common to all things? It is their cause and the <term>Universal Good</term>, existing before the entire multitude of goods to which it gives existence and goodness. These other things are called "good" only through their participation in and relationship to it. 

Even Aristotle himself, at the start of this treatise, praises those who claim that *the Good* is that which all things desire. By using the words *the Good* and *that which all things desire*, it is clear that he actually agrees with Plato in recognizing a first and most universal Good. To Plato and his followers, the term *the Good* is given to the primary and universal Good. By adding "which all things desire," Aristotle demonstrates <note>original: "evinces"</note> that this is indeed the most universal and the first Good. For if all things desire it, it must necessarily be above all things, since the word "all" leaves no room for anything to exist outside of it. *The Good* is above all things—the first, the most causal, and the most universal of everything. It is not contained within the many, nor is it secondary to them; after all, how could that which is first desire that which is second? It is clear, therefore, that Aristotle does not in reality oppose Plato's doctrine regarding the Universal Good.

---

¹ Again, it is evident that what Aristotle says here does not in any way undermine the existence <note>original: "subsistence"</note> of the First Good, which is nothing other than *the Good* itself. For it is this Good that benefits all things; and no one endowed with intelligence <note>original: "intellect"</note> would doubt that every thing is said to be good through a proportional <note>original: "analogous"</note> participation in it. For what if one thing is more good, while another is less? Or if one thing is closer to it, while another is more remote? Or what if one thing is good by itself <note>original: "per se"</note>—that is, essentially, like health of the body and virtue of the soul—while another thing is something that contributes to these, such as diet and exercise, or a certain medicine and remedy? Even a certain malady or severe physical discipline might be considered good if it helps the soul become robust and calm <note>original: "impassive"</note>. 

For there is an order in all things, so that among them one thing is more honorable, another is second, and another is third in honor, and so on. The participation in the Good is also present in every thing according to its order. And if order itself is good—just as disorder is an evil to all beings—how is it reasonable to suppose that goodness should not be granted to things in an orderly manner? Or will anyone require that all things should be equal in rank <note>original: "co-ordinate"</note>, of a similar form, and a similar nature? 

If this were the case, the differences between all beings, their essence, and their hierarchy <note>original: "order"</note> would be destroyed; nor would there be any order or harmony in them, but all things would be randomly <note>original: "casually"</note> confused. But if there is order and beauty <note>original: "ornament"</note> in them, and all things come from one source—except that they are not the same according to their species or genus, but have great differences between them, so that some are subordinate while others are superior—if this is the case, then some things will be superior and others inferior. Some will be closer to, while others are more remote from *the Good*, and the participation in the First Good will be proportional in each. If, therefore, natures are different, and the honor and order adapted to each is different, is it not necessary that the soul, in contemplating each, should apply itself appropriately to each, and appropriately survey each, and that it...

<meta>This page continues the critique of the Platonic Form of the Good, specifically questioning the distinction between the "Idea itself" and its particular instances. The commentary explains why human knowledge is divided into various arts and sciences, arguing that while the divine intellect sees unity, the human soul must divide knowledge into different modes to understand the variety of goods.</meta>

<page-num>16</page-num>
### THE NICOMACHEAN ETHICS, BOOK I.

[It might be ask]ed what their intention is in naming <note>original: "denominating"</note> every Idea *itself*, since in "Man *itself*" and in "man," there is one and the same definition of man. For so far as they are both "man," there is no difference between them. But if this is the case, neither [should the Good itself differ from a specific good].

...should develop arts and sciences in accordance with the <term>genera and species</term> <note>These are categories of classification: "genera" are broad groups, and "species" are specific types within those groups.</note> of things, and examine their natures according to what is analogous in each? For the intellect <note>original: "intellect" (Greek: *nous*), referring to the highest form of divine or intuitive reason</note>, being freed from matter and body, observes all things as a whole and all at once. It understands things that are many as a unity; it understands things that are divisible as indivisible. 

But the soul indeed desires to understand the complete activity of the intellect, striving for the perfection it contains and the one simple form of its thought <note>original: "intellection"</note>. However, not being able to obtain the simultaneous intelligence of the intellect, the soul moves—and, as it were, circles around the intellect—and through the movements of its thoughts <note>original: "transitions of its projections"</note> it divides the indivisibility of the Forms. 

Hence, the soul conceives different modes of knowledge so that, by applying itself appropriately to each object, it may acquire knowledge of all things. For just as a different nature and order are suited to a different thing, so too is a different mode of knowledge suited to a different thing—namely, when the knowledge is balanced <note>original: "co-ordinate"</note> with the thing known. 

Therefore, just as there is not one single balanced knowledge of all beings simply as beings, there is also no single balanced knowledge of all partial goods simply because they are good. Neither is there one for specific opportunities <note>original: "occasions," referring to the Greek *kairos* or the "right time" for action</note>, nor for moderate things, nor for other things similar to these. It is not at all surprising if each being is known through different modes of knowledge based on what it is—for instance, whether it is physical, mathematical, or divine; or further, whether it is celestial or terrestrial, aquatic, aerial, or fiery. 

Thus, the mode of balanced knowledge by which an opportunity, or a moderate thing, or anything else is known, is also different. Therefore, all goods, because they participate in one "First Good," are referred back to that one. It is the task of the same science to know all of them as they relate to and participate in it. However, so far as each thing exists by itself <note>original: "has a subsistence by itself"</note>, and so far as it is a particular thing, it belongs to a different art and science suited to its own nature and perfection.

<summary>This page discusses the relationship between the universal "Idea" and its specific instances, explaining that while the divine intellect sees all things as a unified whole, the human soul must divide knowledge into various specialized arts and sciences to understand different types of "goods."</summary>

## CHAPTER 6. ETHICS.

...so far as they are good, [the "Good itself" and specific "goods" will not differ]; nor will the Good be better simply because it is eternal. After all, something that is white for a long time is no whiter than something that is white for only a single day. <note>This is a famous Aristotelian critique of Plato's Theory of Forms: Aristotle argues that adding "eternity" to a concept does not change its essential definition.</note> The

***

¹ To resolve Aristotle's doubt, one might ask: how can "the good" be the same and exist in the same way in both an immaterial and a material nature? How can it be the same in an image and its perfect pattern <note>original: "paradigm"</note>, in something simple and something complex, or in something that exists by participating in a higher form and that which is <term>imparticipable</term> <note>In Neoplatonic philosophy, "imparticipable" refers to a primary source or principle that remains distinct and does not share its own substance directly with lower things.</note>? Or how can there be the same definition for the very nature of a thing in entities that are as far apart as those we have mentioned, unless both the image and its original <note>original: "archetype"</note> received the definition of "essence" <note>original: "essence" (referring to the Greek *ousia* or fundamental nature)</note> in the same way? Furthermore, according to Aristotle himself, the definition is only perfect in material and physical things when matter and form come together. But if we used these same definitions for immaterial things, then immaterial things would be treated as if they were material. This is impossible. Therefore, there cannot be the same definitions for material entities and those things that exist prior to matter.

² However, one might say in response to this that an eternal good is indeed better than one that is short-lived <note>original: "ephemeral"</note>. For if existence <note>original: "being"</note> is better for everything than non-existence, then to exist perpetually is better than to exist only for a time. When existence is taken away from something that exists only for a time, it becomes non-existence; meanwhile, the eternal continues to exist and is thus better than non-existence. A more excellent good is said to be "more excellent" by its <term>intension</term> <note>In this context, "intension" refers to the internal strength, depth, or purity of a quality's definition.</note>; and that in which the essence of "good" has this strength is good to a greater degree. Furthermore, one cannot truly compare something that lasts a long time with something that lasts a short time in the same way one compares the eternal with something that lasts only a day. Things that last for short or long durations might share the same nature. But the eternal and the short-lived cannot. While the short-lived consists of a material and changing <note>original: "flowing"</note> essence—constantly coming into being but never truly *existing*—the eternal consists of an immaterial and unchanging <note>original: "unflowing"</note> essence; it possesses real being and exists above the realm of change <note>original: "generation"</note>.

The Pythagoreans, however, seem to speak more plausibly <note>original: "probably"</note> regarding the nature of the good; for they place *the one* <note>Unity or singleness</note> in their list <note>original: "co-ordination"</note> of good things. Speusippus <note>Plato’s nephew and successor as head of the Academy</note> also seems to have followed them in this. But the discussion of these matters belongs to another study.

A certain difficulty, however, arises concerning the details we have just mentioned. This is because arguments are not applied to every kind of good. Instead, those things which are sought and loved for their own sake are described <note>original: "predicated"</note> as falling under one category <note>original: "species"</note>; while those things which produce these goods, or preserve them in some way, or prevent their opposites, are described as "good" only because of their relationship to the first group, and in a different sense. It is clear, then, that things may be called "good" in two ways: first, some things are

---

¹ Pythagoras organized the world into two lists <note>original: "co-ordinations"</note> of opposing principles:

| | |
|---|---|
| Finite | Straight |
| Odd | Light |
| One | Square |
| Right | Rest |
| Male | Good |

The other list is:

| | |
|---|---|
| Infinite | Crooked |
| Even | Darkness |
| Multitude | Oblong |
| Left | Motion |
| Female | Evil |

By placing *the one* in the list of good things, the Pythagoreans highlighted its power to perfect and preserve. For everything reaches its full potential <note>original: "is perfected"</note> in this unity; it is benefited and preserved by remaining as one, since it is then not scattered or divided. But when a thing loses its oneness, it also loses its very existence <note>original: "being"</note>.

Indeed, some things are good in themselves (*per se*), while others are good because of their relationship to these. Therefore, separating things that are good in themselves from things that are merely useful, let us consider whether they are described according to a single Idea. But what kind of goods can be said to be good in themselves? Are they those that are pursued for their own sake, apart from anything else—such as wisdom, sight, and certain pleasures and honors? For even though we might pursue these for the sake of something else, they may still be ranked by someone among goods that are good in themselves. Or is the "good in itself" nothing other than the Idea? If so, the Form—or the Idea—would be pointless.¹ But if these other things also rank among goods that are good in themselves, it would be necessary for the same definition of "the good" to be clearly evident in all of them, just as the same definition of "whiteness" applies to both snow and ceruse <note>white lead</note>; however, the definitions of honor, prudence, and pleasure are different and distinct from one another specifically in how they are goods. <meta>This page continues the critique of the Platonic Idea of the Good, specifically questioning its practical application to human ethics and the specific sciences, following the previous discussion on whether "good" can be defined by a single idea.</meta>

better adapted to another philosophy. And it is the same regarding the idea. For even if there is some single good which is predicated in common, or which is something separate in itself, it is evident that it can neither be achieved in practice nor acquired by man. But now, it is that which is achievable by man, and which he may obtain, that is the object of our investigation. Perhaps, however, the knowledge of this separate good may seem to some to be better with respect to those goods which can be acquired and which are achievable. For by having this as a model, we may better know those things which are good for us, and by knowing them, we may more easily obtain them. This claim, therefore, certainly has some probability, but it seems to conflict with the sciences. For all the sciences strive for a certain good and investigate what is missing, while omitting the knowledge of it; yet it is not reasonable to suppose that all practitioners are ignorant of and do not search for an aid of such great importance. It is likewise doubtful what benefit a weaver or a carpenter would gain for their arts from the knowledge of the good itself; or how he who contemplates the idea itself of the good will become more skilled in medicine or in commanding an army.¹ For it appears

<note>¹ Though a study of the idea itself of the good may be of no service to the arts, and for the common purposes of the merely animal life, yet we may say with Plato in the 7th book of his *Republic*, that “He who is not able by the exercise of his reasoning power to define the idea of *the good*, separating it from all other objects, and piercing, as in a battle, through every kind of argument; endeavoring to refute, not according to opinion, but according to essence, and proceeding through all the dialectical activities with an unshaken reason, is in the present life sunk in sleep, and occupied with the delusions of dreams; and that before he is roused to a vigilant state, he will descend to Hades, and be overwhelmed with a sleep perfectly profound.”</note>

that the physician does not consider health in this abstract way, but rather considers the health of a human being, and perhaps even more specifically, the health of this particular person. For he restores an individual to health. And so much for these matters.

---

### CHAPTER VII.

Now, however, let us return to the good we are investigating and show what it is. For it appears that there is a different good in different actions and arts; since there is one good in the medical art, another in the art of military command, and similarly in the remaining arts. What, therefore, is the good in each? Is it not that for the sake of which everything else is done by that art? In the medical art, this is health; in the art of military command, it is victory; in the art of building a house, it is a house; and in every other art, it is something else. And in every action and deliberate choice, the good is the end; since all of them¹ perform other things for the sake of this. Hence, if there is one certain end for all actions, this will be the practical good; but if there are many ends, these will be the practical goods. The reasoning, how—

<note>¹ That is, every art, action, and deliberate choice.</note>

ever, in its progression, arrives at the same conclusion [as we asserted at the beginning].<note>¹ Namely, that the end of every action will be good, and specifically that which is most excellent.</note> But we must try to make this even clearer. Because it appears that there are many ends, and we choose some of these for the sake of others—such as wealth, flutes, and, in short, instruments—it is evident that not all ends are perfect. That which is most excellent, however, appears to be something perfect; so that if there is only one specific perfect end, this will be what we are investigating; but if there are many, it will be the most perfect of these. 

We call that which is pursued for its own sake more perfect than that which is pursued for the sake of something else; and we call that which is never chosen for the sake of another thing more perfect than those things which are chosen both for their own sake and for the sake of something else.<note>² Some things are chosen solely for their own sake, such as joy and happiness; others are chosen both for their own sake and for the sake of something else, such as health, sight, and wisdom; for they are not only desirable in themselves, but also for the sake of felicity. Still other things are chosen only for the sake of something else, such as physical labor, taking medicine when ill, the practice of medicine, and other arts.</note> In short, the completely perfect is that which is always chosen for its own sake, and never for the sake of something else.

Felicity, however, especially appears to be such a thing; for we always choose it for its own sake, and never for the sake of anything else. But we choose honor, and pleasure, and intellect, and every virtue, indeed, for their own sake (for even if we derived no further advantage than what the possession of them provides, we would still choose each of them); yet we also choose

<summary>Aristotle distinguishes between "perfect" ends, which are pursued for their own sake, and "instrumental" ends, which are pursued for something else. He identifies felicity (happiness) as the only end that is always chosen for itself and never as a means to another end.</summary>

...them for the sake of happiness. No one, however, chooses happiness for the sake of these, nor, in short, for the sake of anything else. The same conclusion also appears to follow from the concept of self-sufficiency; for the perfect good appears to be self-sufficient. By "self-sufficient," we mean that which is not only enough for a person living a solitary life, but which is also sufficient for parents, children, a wife, and, in short, for friends and fellow-citizens; since man is by nature a political animal. 

A certain limit, however, must be set for these relationships; for if the good is to be extended to ancestors and their descendants, and to the friends of friends, the list would continue to infinity. But we shall consider this matter later. We define the self-sufficient as that which, existing by itself alone, makes life desirable and lacking in nothing. We believe that happiness is a thing of this kind. 

Furthermore, we think that it is the most desirable of all things, and it is not simply counted as one good among others; for if it were counted alongside even the smallest additional good, it is evident that the combination would be more desirable, since that which is added would create an excess of good. And a greater good is always more desirable. Happiness, therefore, appears to be something perfect and self-sufficient, being the ultimate end of all actions.

Perhaps, however, saying that happiness is the greatest of all things is merely stating something acknowledged by everyone; it is necessary, therefore, that we should describe more clearly what it is. This might be achieved if the specific work <note>Or "function" (Greek: ergon).</note> of man is identified. For just as with a flute player, a sculptor, or any artist—and in short, for all those who have a certain work and function—the good...

<summary>Aristotle defines happiness as the ultimate, self-sufficient end of human life, encompassing social responsibilities because humans are naturally political. He proposes that to define happiness more clearly, one must identify the unique function or "work" of a human being.</summary>

Are there, therefore, certain works and actions indeed of a carpenter and a shoe-maker, but of man is there no work, and is he naturally indolent? Or shall we say, that as for the eye, the hand, and the foot, and in short for each of the parts of the body, there appears to be a certain work, so likewise for man, shall we admit that besides all these there is a certain work? 

What then will this work be? For to live appears to be common also to plants <note>as well as to men</note>. But the peculiar work of man is now investigated. The nutritive and growth-oriented life, therefore, must be rejected. And a certain sensitive life will follow this. It appears, however, that this also is common to a horse and an ox, and to every animal. A certain practical life, therefore, accompanied with reason remains. 

But of this life, one kind is obedient to reason, while the other possesses reason and is active through discursive thought. Since this life, however, is spoken of in a twofold respect <note>that is, according to activity and according to habit</note>, it must be admitted to exist according to activity; for this appears to be the more primary mode of existence. 

But if the work of man is the activity of the soul according to reason, or at least not without reason; and we say that the same thing is the work of the human species and of a worthy man, just as the same thing is the work of a harper and of a good harper, and in short, this is the case in all things—excellence according to virtue being added to the work (for the work of a harper is to play on the harp, and of a good harper to play well on it); if this be the case, and we admit the work of man to be a certain life, and this to be the activity of the soul,

<meta>This page concludes the definition of human happiness as the activity of the soul in accordance with virtue and discusses the methodology of the inquiry, emphasizing that precision must match the subject matter.</meta>

<page-num>26</page-num>

and actions in conjunction with reason, and if these things are performed well and beautifully by an excellent man, and everything is well accomplished according to its proper virtue—if this is the case, human good will be the activity of the soul according to virtue. But if there are many virtues, it will be the activity of the soul according to the best and most perfect virtue; and furthermore, in a complete life. For just as one swallow does not make a spring, nor does one day, so neither does one day or a short time make a man blessed and happy. 

Let this, therefore, be a description of the good; for it is perhaps necessary, <note>as in a painting</note>, first to outline it and afterwards add the colors. It seems that anyone might be able to draw out and clearly arrange things that are well outlined, and that time is the inventor of, or a good collaborator with, things of this kind. This is also how advancements are made in the arts, for anyone can add what is missing. 

It is also necessary to recall what was said before and not to look for the same degree of precision in all things, but to investigate it in each case according to the subject matter and only as far as is appropriate to the method <note>pertaining to the inquiry</note>. For a carpenter and a geometrician investigate a right angle differently: the former does so only as far as it is useful for his work, but the latter explores what it is, or what specific quality it possesses, for he is a contemplator of truth. 

We must proceed in the same way in other matters, so that secondary details do not become more numerous than the main works themselves. Nor should the cause be demanded in the same way in all things; in some cases, such as with first principles, it is enough to have properly shown *that they are*. But the existence of a thing <term>(το οτι)</term> is the first thing and the principle.

<summary>Aristotle defines the human good as the soul's activity in accordance with the highest virtue over a complete lifetime. He also explains that the level of precision in any study must be appropriate to its specific subject matter.</summary>

<header>CHAP. VIII. ETHICS.</header>

Of principles, however, some are observed by induction, others by perception, others by a certain habit, and others in different ways. But we should try to discuss everything as far as its nature permits and should earnestly apply ourselves to defining them well; for this is of great importance regarding what follows. The principle, therefore, seems to be more than half of the whole, and many of the things we investigate become clear through it.

---

### CHAPTER VIII.

**HAPPINESS**, therefore, must be considered by us not only based on our conclusion and the details that make up its definition, but also based on the assertions of others concerning it. For everything that is inherent in a subject agrees with the truth, but the truth is quickly at odds with what is false. Since goods, therefore, have a twofold division, and some are said to be external while others pertain to the soul and the body, we call those pertaining to the soul the most proper and principal goods; and we categorize these as psychical.<note>That is, pertaining to the soul.</note>

<meta>This page continues Chapter VIII of Book I, comparing the definition of happiness (as the activity of the soul according to virtue) with various historical and philosophical opinions to show how they align with this conclusion.</meta>

actions and activities involving the soul. Therefore, it is well said, according to this ancient opinion which is supported by philosophers, <note>that happiness is the activity of the soul according to virtue</note>. It is also rightly said that certain actions and activities constitute the end <note>i.e., happiness;</note> for thus happiness will consist of goods pertaining to the soul, and not of external goods. With this reasoning, likewise, the assertions agree that the happy man lives well and acts well; for happiness will essentially be a certain way of living well and acting well.

Furthermore, it appears that everything which is sought for in happiness is inherent in the definition we have given of it. For to some, indeed, happiness appears to be virtue, to others prudence, and to others a certain wisdom; while to others it appears to be these things, or one of these, accompanied by pleasure or not without pleasure. Others, also, include <note>in the definition of happiness</note> external prosperity. But of these opinions, some are supported by the authority of many ancient men, and others by a few renowned men. It is not, however, reasonable to suppose that either of these groups has wholly erred, but rather that they have erred in some specific detail and are right in most things. 

Therefore, our assertion agrees with those who say that happiness is all virtue or a certain virtue; for it is the activity of the soul according to virtue. Perhaps, however, it makes no small difference whether we conceive of the most excellent thing as consisting in possession or in use—that is, in a habit or in an activity. For it is possible that a habit, when present, may produce no good, as in someone who is asleep or who is in some other way inactive; but this cannot be the case with activity. For he <note>who possesses virtue in activity</note> necessarily acts, and

He distinguishes between the mere possession of virtue (habit) and its active exercise (activity), arguing that the latter is essential for achieving the highest good.</summary>

acts well. But as in the Olympic games, it is not the most beautiful and the strongest who are crowned, but those who compete; for it is from among these that the victors emerge. In the same way, those who act rightly obtain the beautiful and good things in life. Their life is also delightful in itself [independent of external pleasure]. For to feel delight is among the things pertaining to the soul. To everyone, that which they love is delightful; for example, a horse is delightful to a lover of horses, and a spectacle to a lover of spectacles. In the same manner, just acts are delightful to a lover of justice, and in short, everything pertaining to virtue is delightful to a lover of virtue. 

Therefore, things that are delightful to the majority are often in conflict with each other because they are not delightful by nature. But to those who love what is noble in conduct, those things which are naturally delightful are pleasurable; such are actions according to virtue. Thus, these actions are delightful to such people and are so *per se*. Their life does not need pleasure as an extra addition but contains pleasure within itself. For in addition to what has been said, a man is not good if he does not rejoice in noble actions; for no one would call a man just who does not rejoice in acting justly, nor call him generous if he does not rejoice in generous actions—and similarly for the other virtues. 

If this is the case, actions according to virtue will be delightful in themselves; they are also good and noble, and possess each of these qualities in the highest degree, provided that the virtuous man judges them correctly—and he judges them in the way we have stated. Happiness, therefore, is the most excellent, most beautiful, and most delightful thing. Nor are these qualities to be separated from each other as they are in the Delian inscription: "That which is most just is

At the same time, however, it appears, as we have said, that external goods are necessary for happiness; for it is impossible, or at least not easy, to perform noble actions without the help of external resources; since many things are performed as if by using instruments, through the help of friends, wealth, and political power. The lack of certain things, such as noble birth, good children, and beauty, also stains a blessed state of existence; for a man cannot be entirely happy if he is very deformed in body, or of low birth, or if he leads a solitary life and is childless. And perhaps he can be happy to an even lesser degree if his children are very wicked, or if they were good but died. As we have said, therefore, <note>a completely happy life</note> requires such prosperity as this; which is why some categorize prosperity, and others virtue, in the same rank as happiness.

---

<note>To a happiness that is in every respect complete and perfect, as far as is possible for man in the present life, external goods are necessary; though a truly worthy man will still be worthy, and *essentially* happy, if deprived of them. I say he will still be essentially happy because his happiness consists in intellectual activity, and he can never be deprived of this by any adverse circumstances, because the activity of the intellect is the same as its essence. I refer the reader who wishes to see an example of this perfect happiness to my translation of the *Life of Proclus* by Marinus, which is prefixed to my translation of *Proclus on Euclid*. Such an instance of complete happiness in one man as is shown there is perhaps hardly to be paralleled in all antiquity; and it would be folly to attempt to find a parallel to it in modern times.</note>

<summary>Aristotle explains that while happiness is rooted in virtuous activity, it also requires external prosperity—such as wealth, friends, and good health—to be truly complete. The text notes that the absence of these external goods can tarnish one's blessed state, leading some to equate happiness with prosperity itself.</summary>

# CHAPTER IX.

HENCE, also, it is questioned whether felicity is a thing which may be acquired by learning, or habit, or in some other way by training; or whether it comes by a certain divine allotment, or by fortune. If, therefore, any other thing is the gift of the gods to men, it is reasonable to suppose that felicity also is the gift of divinity, especially because it is the best of human concerns. This, however, will perhaps be better suited to another discussion. But it appears that even if it is not sent by divinity <note>to men</note>, but is instead procured through virtue and a certain learning or training, it belongs among the most divine of things; for the reward and end of virtue appears to be most excellent, and something divine and blessed. Felicity will also be a very common thing; for it can be available, through a certain learning and attention, to all men who are not incapacitated with respect to virtue. But if it is better that felicity should be acquired in this manner <note>That is, either from divinity or through the exercise of virtue.</note> rather than from fortune, it is reasonable that it should be so acquired; since natural productions exist in such a way as is most beautiful for them to exist; and in a similar manner

things which are produced by art, or by any other cause, and especially those produced by the most excellent cause. To commit, however, the greatest and most beautiful of things to fortune would be highly unreasonable and reprehensible. The object of our investigation is likewise apparent from the definition of felicity; for we have said that it is a certain energy of the soul according to virtue. But of the remaining goods, some indeed are present from necessity, while others cooperate and are naturally adapted to be useful organically <note>That is, as instruments or tools.</note>. These things will also accord with what we said in the beginning. For we established the end of political science as the best end; and this science pays the greatest attention to the citizens in order to make them characters of a certain description, so that they may be good men and practitioners of beautiful actions. 

Reasonably, therefore, we call neither an ox, nor a horse, nor any other <note>irrational</note> animal happy; for it is not possible that any one of them can partake of such an energy as this. For this same cause, a child is not happy either; for he is not yet, on account of his age, a practitioner of things of this kind. But those children who are said to be happy are proclaimed to be blessed through hope <note>the hope that when they become men they will obtain the rational energy in perfection</note>. For felicity, as we have said, requires perfect virtue and a perfect life. For many mutations <note>Changes of fortune.</note> and all kinds of fortunes happen in life; and it is possible that someone whose affairs are in the most prosperous condition may in old age fall into the greatest calamities, as is told in heroic poems concerning Priam. But no one would call a person happy who experiences such misfortunes and who dies miserably.

## CHAPTER X.

**SHALL** we say, therefore, that no other man is to be proclaimed happy as long as he lives, but that according to Solon it is necessary to look to the end of life? If, however, we admit this, is a man then happy when he dies? Or is this perfectly absurd, especially to those who say, as we do, that happiness is a certain energy? But if we neither call him who is dead happy, nor was this the intention of Solon, but his meaning is that a man may then be securely proclaimed blessed, as being now out of the reach of evils and misfortunes—even this is also attended with a certain doubt. For there appears to be a certain good and evil to him who is dead, just as there is to him who is alive but without sensation, such as honor and dishonor, and in short, the prosperity and adversity of his descendants. This, however, also presents us with a difficulty. For it is possible that for the offspring of him who has lived to old age blessedly and has died rationally, many changes may happen; some of them may be good and may obtain a life according to their merit, while the contrary may take place for others. It is likewise evident that there may be a wide variety of departures in them from the manners of their parents. It would, therefore, be absurd if he who is dead should also be changed togeth-

Let us, however, return to the previous subject of doubt; for perhaps what is now being investigated may be understood through it. If, therefore, it is necessary to look toward the end of life and only then proclaim each man blessed—not as being blessed now, but because he was so before—is it not absurd that when he is happy <note>that is, while he is living</note>, the happiness that is present with him <note>that is, happiness</note> should not be truthfully asserted of him? This occurs because we are unwilling to proclaim the living happy due to the fluctuations of life, and because we perceive happiness to be something stable and by no means easily changed. But fortunes frequently revolve around the same people. For it is evident that if we were to follow fortune, we would frequently call the same man happy and then miserable, thus demonstrating the happy man to be like a chameleon, possessing a weak stability. Or shall we say that it is not at all right to follow fortune? For living well or poorly is not among the gifts of fortune; rather, human life, as we have said, requires the goods of fortune. The energies, however, according to virtue, are the governors of happiness, while the contrary energies are the governors of its opposite. This point, which is now the subject of doubt, bears witness to our assertion. For in no human affairs is there so much stability as in the energies according to virtue; since they appear to be more stable than even the sciences themselves; and of these very energies, those that are

The object of investigation, therefore, belongs to the happy man, and he will remain so throughout his life. For he will always, or more than any other man, perform and contemplate things pertaining to virtue; he will bear the changes of fortune most beautifully and in a perfectly elegant manner, as one who is truly good and a "square"<sup>1</sup> without blame.

Since, however, many things happen by chance, differing in greatness and smallness, it is evident that both prosperous and adverse circumstances, when they are small, are of no consequence to a person's life. But those that are great and numerous, if they are indeed prosperous, make life more blessed—for they are naturally suited to adorn life, and the use of them is beautiful and good. On the contrary, if they are adverse, they oppress and damage happiness. For they bring distress with them and are an obstacle to many energies. At the same time, however, the beauty of good conduct shines through even in these situations, when a man bears many great misfortunes easily—not because he is insensitive to pain, but because he is generous and magnanimous. But if energies are what govern life, as we have said, no one who is blessed will ever become miserable;<sup>2</sup> since he will never

<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter X regarding how a virtuous man maintains his character and happiness despite external misfortunes, specifically referencing the Platonist perspective and a passage from Plotinus.</meta>

<page-num>86</page-num>
<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK I.</header>

do anything that is odious and base. For we are of the opinion that the man who is truly good and wise will [maintain the distinc]tion between happiness and misery; this is an assertion which, when rightly understood, is not discordant with the doctrine of the best of the Platonists. For it is necessary for the perfection of happiness that it should exist in the most perfect energy; but some calamities may be so great as to make this impossible. Even in this case, however, the happiness of the truly worthy man will continue essentially the same, though it will not be the same in *perfect energy*; instead, it may then be said to be, as it were, in a *dormant state*. The truth of this is most beautifully illustrated by Plotinus in his book *On Felicity*, from which the following is an extract.

> “The wise man is never oppressed with evil through ignorance of his own concerns, nor changed by the fortunes of others, whether prosperous or adverse. But when his pains are intense, he bears them as far as it is possible to bear; and when they are excessive, they may cause him to become delirious; yet he will not be miserable in the midst of the greatest pains, but his intellectual light will constantly shine in the inner sanctuary of his soul, like a lamp secured in a watchtower, which shines with unceasing splendor, even though surrounded by stormy winds and raging seas. But what shall we say if, through the violence of pain, he is ready to destroy himself? Indeed, if the pain is so intensely prolonged, he will, if conscious, consider what is necessary to be done, for in these circumstances the freedom of the will is not taken away. At the same time, it must be observed that things of this kind do not appear as dreadful to men of excellent virtue as they do to others, nor do they reach the inward and true man. If anyone objects, however, that we are so formed by nature that we ought to grieve for family misfortunes, he should understand that, first, not all men are so affected, and second, that it is the business of virtue to improve the common condition of nature to something better and more noble than the judgments of the common people. But it is more noble to consider everything that appears grievous to our common nature as being of no importance. For the wise man is not unrefined or unskillful, but like a strenuous wrestler, he vigorously repels the strokes of fortune, endeavoring to throw his fortune to the ground. He knows that such things are displeasing to a common nature, but

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Plotinus's views on the happiness of the wise man, emphasizing that true virtue remains constant even during external suffering or periods of unconsciousness.</meta>

<page-num>37</page-num>
<header>CHAP. X. ETHICS.</header>

...bear all fortunes in a becoming manner, and from existing circumstances will always perform the most beautiful [actions], <note>The text likely implies "beautiful actions" or "noble deeds" based on the preceding context of the previous page.</note> showing that to such a nature as his own they are not really grievous, but are terrible only as it were to boys. Hence, he contemplates even the slaughter and destruction of cities, the plunder and prey, like scenes in a theater, as nothing more than certain transformations and alternate changes of figures; and weeping and distress everywhere as deceptive and fictitious. For in the particular acts of human life, he knows it is not the inner soul and the true man, but the outer shadow of man alone, which laments and weeps, performing his part on the earth as in a grander and more extended scene, in which many shadows of souls and phantom scenes appear.

> "But what shall we say when the wise man is no longer himself, as a result of being overwhelmed with disease? We reply that if in such a state it is granted he may retain his proper virtue, like one in a deep sleep, what is there to prevent him from being happy? Since no one would deprive him of his happiness in sleep, nor consider that interval of rest as any hindrance to the happiness of his whole life. Again, if it is said, how can he be happy, though endowed with virtue, while he does not perceive himself to be virtuous, nor acts according to virtue?—we reply, though a man does not perceive himself to be healthy, he may nevertheless be healthy; and again, he will not be less beautiful in his body, though not aware of his beauty; and will a man be less wise if he does not perceive himself to be wise? But perhaps someone may say that wisdom should be accompanied by perception and conscious awareness, for happiness is present with wisdom in action. We reply, if this action of wisdom was anything accidental, there might be some weight in the assertion; but if the existence of wisdom is situated in a certain essence, or rather in essence itself, this essence will neither perish in him who is asleep or delirious, nor is it denied to be any longer conscious of its happiness. And, indeed, the energy of this essence resides in the soul of such a person, and is an energy perpetually vigilant; for then the wise man, considered as wise, acts, whether he is in a dormant state or overwhelmed with infirmity. But an action of this kind is not concealed from the whole itself, but rather from some particular part; just as with respect to vegetable energy in its most flourishing state, a conscious awareness of such energy..."
