deeds; just as a good general will use the army under his command in the most warlike manner, and a shoe...

---

...does not pass into the external man by means of a sentient nature; and if we were entirely the same as our vegetative power, there is no doubt that we would energize whenever such a virtue was active. Since, however, the case is otherwise, and we are the energy of that which is intelligent, we energize as a consequence of its energy.

> “Perhaps, indeed, such an energy is concealed from us because it does not reach any sentient power; for to this purpose it would have to energize through sense as a medium. But why should intellect not energize, and the soul around the intellect, preceding all sense and animadversion <note>conscious awareness or perception</note>? For it is necessary there should be some energy prior to animadversion, since the energy of intellect is the same as its essence. But animadversion appears to take place when the energy of intellect is reflected; and when that which energizes according to the life of the soul rebounds, as it were, back again—like images in a mirror quietly situated in a smooth and polished place, so as to reflect every form which its container holds. For as in things of this kind, when the mirror is not present, or is not properly positioned, the energy from which the image was formed is indeed present, but the resemblance is absent; so with respect to the soul, when it energizes in quiet, certain resemblances of thought and intellect shine upon our imagination, like the images in the smooth and polished mirror. And in a perceptible manner, as it were, we recognize that our intellect and reason energize together with the former knowledge. But when this medium is disturbed because the harmony of the body is disrupted, then intellect and reason understand without an image, and intellection is carried on without imagination. Hence, intelligence may be considered as existing together with the phantasy <note>the faculty of imagination</note>, while, in the meantime, intelligence is something very different from the phantasy. Besides, it is easy to discover many thoughts of men when awake, and worthy actions, in the performance of which it is evident that we do not perceive ourselves thinking and acting. For it is not necessary that he who reads should be conscious he is reading, especially when he reads with the greatest attention; nor that he who acts vigorously should acknowledge his vigorous energy; and the same consequence follows in a variety...”

It argues that we can perform complex actions, like reading or virtuous deeds, without being constantly conscious of the act itself.</summary>

maker from the leather with which he is supplied will make the most beautiful shoe; and the same thing will be true of other operations: so that sensible perceptions appear to render the actions which they attend more weak <note>The original text uses "debile."</note>: but when they are done alone, they are then pure, and seem to possess more energy and life. And, hence, when worthy men live in such a state, it follows that they live in a more perfect manner; since their life is not at that time spread out into sense, and by this means diminished in its energy, but is collected into itself, in one uniform, intellectual tenor.

“Nor are the wise man’s energies entirely prevented by the changes of fortune, but different energies will take place in different fortunes, yet all of them equally worthy, and those perhaps more worthy which rightly compose jarring externals. For the greatest discipline always resides with him, and this is even more so, though he should be placed in the bull of Phalaris <note>A notorious ancient Greek torture device.</note>. For what is there pronounced in agony, is pronounced by that which is placed in torment—the external and shadowy man, which is far different from the true man, who, dwelling by himself, so far as he necessarily resides with himself, never ceases from the contemplation of the supreme good.

“But he who does not place the wise man in such an exalted intellect, but subjects him to the power of fortune and to the fear of evil, certainly presents us with a mixed character and life, composed from good and evil, and which possesses nothing great, either pertaining to the excellence of wisdom or the purity of goodness. Happiness, therefore, cannot consist in a common life; and Plato rightly judges that the chief good is to be sought from above; that it must be beheld by him who is wise and wishes to become happy in the future; and that he must study to approach its likeness and to live its exalted life. It is necessary, therefore, to possess this alone in order to obtain the end of life; and the wise man will consider all else as certain changes of situation, which in reality contribute nothing to happiness. In every circumstance of being he will judge what is right and act as necessity requires, as far as his abilities extend. To which we may add, that though he lives a life superior to sense, he will not be hindered from taking proper care of the body with which he is connected, always acting similarly to the musician, who cares for his lyre as long as he is able to use it; but when it becomes useless and ceases any longer to perform the office of a lyre, he either changes it for another or abstains entirely from its use.”

<summary>The author argues that the wise man's true happiness and intellectual energy remain constant regardless of external fortune or physical suffering. He compares the wise man's relationship with his body to a musician's care for a lyre, which is maintained only as long as it remains a useful instrument.</summary>

Nor, again, is he fickle and easily changed; for he is not easily moved from happiness, nor by any minor misfortunes, but only by those that are great and numerous. After such calamities as these, he will not become happy again in a short time; but if he does recover his happiness, it will be after a long and complete period, during which he will come to share in things of a great and beautiful nature. ## CHAPTER XI.

THAT the good or bad fortune, however, of descendants and of all friends should contribute nothing <note>to the happy man</note> appears to be very unkind and contrary to the general opinions of mankind. But since many things happen, and they possess every kind of variety—some affecting us to a great degree and others to a lesser degree—discussing them individually appears to be a long and endless undertaking. It will, therefore, perhaps be enough to speak of them in general terms and to outline what they are. Just as some of the calamitous circumstances that happen to the happy man have a certain weight and importance in life, this is likewise the case regarding all his friends. It makes a difference, however, whether each of the calamities happens to the living or the dead; and the difference is much greater than whether the illegal and dreadful deeds which are the subject of a tragedy were committed in the past or are being committed now. In this way, therefore, the distinction may also be inferred. Perhaps, however, it should rather be questioned whether the dead participate in any good or evil. For it appears from these considerations that though something should reach them—whatever it may be, whether good or the contrary—it is something weak and small, either in its own nature,

The prosperity, therefore, and in a similar manner the adversity of friends, appears to contribute something to the dead; yet with respect to them, these things are of so little consequence that they neither make those who are happy unhappy, nor effect anything else of that kind.

---

### CHAPTER XII.

THESE things being discussed, let us consider, with respect to happiness <note>felicity</note>, whether it is among the number of things laudable, or rather of things honorable; for it is evident that it does not consist in power. It seems, therefore, that everything which is laudable is praised because it possesses a certain quality and is, in a certain respect, referred to something else. For we praise the just and the brave man, and in short the good man, and also virtue, on account of their works and actions. We likewise praise the strong man and the racer, etc., because they are naturally adapted to possess certain qualities and have reference in a certain respect to something good and worthy. But this is also evident from the praises which pertain to the Gods; for they appear to be ridiculous,

<summary>Aristotle concludes that the fortunes of the living have a negligible effect on the dead and shifts to analyzing whether happiness should be praised as a relative good or revered as an honorable, absolute end. He notes that praise is generally given to things that are useful for some higher purpose, whereas happiness may be the ultimate purpose itself.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of why certain divine or perfect things are not "praised" in a relative sense, but are instead "blessed" as absolute ends, concluding Chapter 12.</meta>

<page-num>43</page-num>
<header>CHAP. XII.          ETHICS,          43</header>

ridiculous when referred to us. This, however, happens, as we have said, because praise is based on relation. But if praise is given to things of this kind, it is evident that no praise can be given to the most excellent things; rather, something greater and better belongs to them, as also appears to be the case. For we proclaim the Gods to be blessed and happy, and we also proclaim the most divine of men to be blessed; and in a similar manner we celebrate what is good. For no one praises happiness <note>felicity</note> in the same way as he does justice; instead, he proclaims it to be blessed, as something more divine and excellent than justice. Eudoxus, likewise, in his defense of pleasure, appears to have rightly awarded it the palm of victory; for because it is not praised, even though it is among the number of good things, he considered this an indication that it was more excellent than things that are laudable. But God and *the good* are things of this kind; for all other things are referred to these. For praise is indeed given to virtue, since it is through virtue that we are enabled to perform noble <note>beautiful</note> deeds. Speeches of praise <note>encomiums</note>, however, belong to deeds, and in a similar manner to bodies and souls. The precise discussion of these things is perhaps better suited to a treatise on Encomiums; but for our purposes it is clear, from what has been said, that happiness <note>felicity</note> is among those things that are honorable and perfect. It also seems to be so because it is a principle; for all of us do everything else for the sake of this; and we admit that the principle and the cause of what is good is something honorable and divine.

<summary>Aristotle explains that happiness is not "praised" like virtue because it is an absolute and divine end rather than a relative means to something else. He concludes that happiness is an honorable, perfect principle for the sake of which all other good deeds are performed.</summary>

### CHAPTER XIII.

Since, however, happiness <note>felicity</note> is a certain energy <note>activity</note> of the soul according to perfect virtue, we must direct our attention to virtue; for perhaps we shall thus also investigate <note>speculate</note> better concerning happiness <note>felicity</note>. But it seems that he who is skilled in the administration of public affairs works especially on this; for he wishes to make the citizens worthy persons and obedient to the laws. As an example of these, we have the legislators of the Cretans and Lacedæmonians, and any others there may have been of this kind. If, however, the investigation <note>speculation</note> itself belongs to political science, it is evident that the inquiry will be consistent <note>conformable</note> with our intention from the beginning. But our discussion must be concerning virtue—namely, human virtue; for we investigate the human good and human happiness <note>felicity</note>. We call human virtue not the virtue of the body, but of the soul; and we say that happiness <note>felicity</note> is the energy <note>activity</note> of the soul. If, however, this is the case, it is evident that he who is skilled in the administration of public affairs ought to know whatever pertains to the soul, just as he who intends to cure the eyes ought to have a knowledge of the whole body;¹ and this in a greater

degree, by as much as political science is more honorable and excellent than medicinal science. Likewise, the more refined physicians are busily occupied with the knowledge of the body. Therefore, he who is skilled in the administration of public affairs must direct his attention to the soul; but he must direct his attention to it for the sake of these goals, and only so far as is sufficient for the objects of inquiry. For considering the soul with even greater accuracy is perhaps more laborious and difficult than the present discussion requires.

We have also said enough concerning it in our popular writings, and those should be consulted; such as that one part of the soul is irrational, and the other rational. But whether these parts are separated, in the same manner as the parts of the body and everything that is divisible, or whether they are two parts in definition <note>alone</note> and are naturally inseparable—as the convex and the concave are in the circumference <note>periphery</note> of a circle—is of no consequence in the present discussion.

Of the irrational part, however, one part resembles the common and vegetative power; I mean the power which is the cause of nutrition and growth <note>increase</note>. For such a power as this may be admitted to exist in everything that is nourished, in embryos, and also in mature animals; since it is more reasonable that this power should exist in them than any other. The virtue, therefore, of this power appears to be common and not human. For this part seems to be especially active <note>energize</note> during sleep, and a good man and a bad man can be distinguished least of all during sleep; hence it is said that the happy differ in no respect from the miserable during half of their lives. But this happens for good reason; for sleep is an inactivity of both the worthy and the depraved soul, except so far as certain motions gradually reach the

There appears, however, to be another certain irrational nature of the soul, which nevertheless participates in a certain respect in reason; for we praise the reason of the <term>continent</term>, and also of the <term>incontinent</term> man, and that part of the soul which possesses reason, for it rightly excites them to the most excellent deeds. There appears, however, to be in them <note>that is, in both the continent and incontinent</note> something else naturally contrary to reason, which wars against and resists reason. For indeed, just as the paralyzed parts of the body move to the left when we wish to move them to the right, so it is also in the soul. For the impulses of the incontinent are in a direction contrary <note>to the dictates of reason</note>. In bodies, however, we see that which is moved contrary <note>to the intention of the will</note>, but in the soul we do not see <note>that which is moved contrary to reason</note>; though perhaps we ought nevertheless to think that in the soul, also, there is something opposite to reason, which is adverse and proceeds in a direction contrary to it; but it is of no consequence in what manner it is different from reason. This part, however, appears, as we have said, to participate in reason. It is obedient, therefore, to the reason of the continent man; and perhaps it is still more obedient to the reason of the temperate and brave man; for all things are in concord with his reason. It appears, therefore, that the irrational part is twofold; for the vegetative part in no respect participates in reason; but the part which desires, and, in

--- Page 56 ---
<meta>This page concludes the analysis of the soul's relationship to reason and introduces the classification of virtues into intellectual and moral categories.</meta>

<page-num>47</page-num>
<header>CHAP. VIII. ETHICS. 47</header>

In short, the <term>orectic</term> part participates in a certain respect in reason, so far as it is attentive and obedient to it. In this way, therefore, we say that a man has a regard for, or pays attention to (<term>εχειν λογον</term>), his father and his friends, and not in the same manner as he has a regard for the mathematical sciences. But that the irrational part is in a certain respect obedient to reason, all admonition, reproof, and exhortation indicate. If, however, it is necessary to say that this part also possesses reason, then that which possesses reason will be twofold: one part, indeed, possesses it properly and in itself, but the other resembles a child who is attentive to his father.

Virtue, likewise, is distributed according to this difference. For we say that some of the virtues are <term>dianoetic</term> <note>or belong to the power which reasons scientifically</note>, but others are ethical. And we indeed name wisdom, intelligence, and prudence as <term>dianoetic</term> virtues; but liberality and temperance as ethical virtues. For when we speak concerning the character of a man, we do not say that he is wise or intelligent, but that he is mild or temperate. We also praise a wise man according to his habit; and we call these praiseworthy habits virtues.

<summary>This page defines how the desiring part of the soul participates in reason through obedience and establishes the distinction between intellectual (dianoetic) and moral (ethical) virtues.</summary>

## NICOMACHEAN ETHICS.

### BOOK II.

### CHAPTER I.

Since, however, virtue is twofold—one kind being <term>dianoetic</term> and the other ethical—the <term>dianoetic</term> indeed mostly receives both its origin and increase from teaching, for which reason it requires experience and time. But ethical virtue is produced from custom, from which it also derives its name, which differs only slightly from εθος, *ethos*, custom. From this, likewise, it is evident that none of the ethical virtues are implanted in us by nature; for nothing that has a natural existence can be trained by custom to act differently from its natural tendency. Thus a stone, which naturally tends downward, cannot be accustomed to tend upward,

<summary>Aristotle introduces the distinction between intellectual (dianoetic) and moral (ethical) virtues, explaining that while the former is learned through teaching, the latter is developed through habit and is not naturally innate.</summary>
<keywords>virtue, dianoetic, ethical, custom, nature, habit, Aristotle, Nicomachean Ethics, ethos</keywords>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that ethical virtues are not innate but are developed through habit, comparing the acquisition of virtue to the learning of crafts and the role of law in society.</meta>

### THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK II.

even if someone should hurl it upward ten thousand times; nor can fire be accustomed to move downward, nor can anything else among those things that have natural tendencies different from these be accustomed to any other tendency than that which it has by nature. The virtues, therefore, are neither from nature, nor are they produced in us contrary to nature; but they are produced in us because we are naturally adapted to receive them, and we become perfect through habit. 

Again, regarding those things that are produced in us by nature, we first receive the powers, but afterward we employ the activities of those powers; this is evident in the senses. For it is not from frequently seeing or frequently hearing that we receive these senses, but, on the contrary, because we have these senses we use them, and we do not possess them by using them. With respect to the virtues, however, we receive them by first acting according to them, just as in the other arts; for those things which we must learn how to do, we learn by doing them. Thus, by building we become builders, and by playing the harp we become harpists. Similarly, by acting justly we become just, by acting prudently we become prudent, and by acting bravely we become brave. 

What happens in cities testifies to the truth of this. For legislators, by accustoming the citizens <note>to virtue</note>, make them worthy characters; and this is indeed the intention of every legislator; but those who do not accomplish this well, fail. And in this, one <term>polity</term> differs from another—the good from the bad. Furthermore, from the same causes and through the same means, every virtue is both produced and destroyed;

<summary>Aristotle argues that virtues are acquired through practice and habituation rather than being innate, much like learning a craft or a musical instrument. He notes that the primary goal of legislators is to make citizens good by habituating them to virtuous actions.</summary>
<keywords>virtue, habit, nature, practice, habituation, senses, legislation, polity, Aristotle, Nicomachean Ethics</keywords>

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that ethical virtues are developed through practice and habit, drawing a parallel between learning a craft and cultivating a moral character.</meta>

# CHAP. I. ETHICS.

and every art is similar in this respect. For by playing the harp, both good and bad harpists are produced; and similarly, builders of houses and all other craftsmen. For by building well, they will be good builders, but they will be bad by building poorly; since if it were not so, there would be no need for a teacher, but all men would be <note>naturally</note> good or bad craftsmen. The same also takes place in the virtues. For by acting in our dealings with men, some of us become just, while others become unjust; and by acting in terrifying situations, and by being accustomed either to be afraid or to be confident in danger, some of us become brave, while others become timid. The reasoning is likewise similar regarding desire and anger; for some men become <term>temperate</term> and mild, while others become intemperate and <term>irascible</term>; the former from behaving in one way in these matters, and the latter from behaving in another. And in a word, <term>habits</term> are produced from similar <term>energies</term>. Therefore, it is necessary to perform <term>energies</term> of a certain quality, for <term>habits</term> follow the differences in these activities. It is of no small consequence, then, to be accustomed in one way or another right from our youth; rather, it is of very great consequence—or rather, it is everything.

<summary>Aristotle explains that moral virtues and artistic skills are both acquired through repeated practice, meaning that the specific habits we form from a young age determine our character.</summary>
<keywords>virtues, habits, energies, character, Aristotle, Ethics, moral development, temperament</keywords>

## CHAPTER II.

SINCE, therefore, this current treatise is not for the sake of theory, like other discussions; for our attention is not directed to this work so that we may know what virtue is, but so that we may become good people—since otherwise no benefit would be derived from it—this being the case, it is necessary to consider how actions are to be performed. For as we have said, actions are the determining factors of the qualities that <term>habits</term> possess. To act, therefore, according to <term>right reason</term> is a general principle, <note>That is, for actions to be good, it is universally required that they be performed according to right reason.</note> and is now assumed to be so. We shall, however, later speak concerning this and show what <term>right reason</term> is, <note>In the 6th Book.</note> and how it exists in relation to the other virtues. But this must be previously granted: that every study of practical affairs ought only to be a general outline, and not an exact discussion, just as we also observed at the beginning; this is because reasoning is required to be consistent with the subject matter. In practical affairs and the things that contribute to them, nothing is fixed, just as nothing is fixed in matters of

<summary>Aristotle asserts that the goal of ethics is practical improvement rather than mere theoretical knowledge, emphasizing that we must study how to act because our actions determine the character of our habits.</summary>
<keywords>ethics, right reason, habits, practical affairs, virtue, moral action, Aristotle, Nicomachean Ethics</keywords>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the lack of absolute precision in practical ethics, moving into the central concept that moral virtues are destroyed by excess and deficiency and preserved by a proportionate balance.</meta>

<page-num>53</page-num>
-># CHAP. II. ETHICS.<-

salubrious.<note>For healthy things change along with the condition of bodies and the changes of time.</note> Since this is the universal reason, the discussion of particulars will be even less accurate; for it falls under neither a specific art nor any fixed precept. However, those engaged in practical affairs must always pay attention to the opportune moment, just as in medicine and in the art of navigation. But although the current discussion is of this nature, we must try to support it.

In the first place, therefore, it must be observed that things of this kind <note>namely, actions that produce the habits of virtue in us</note> are naturally prone to be corrupted by excess and deficiency, as we see in strength and health <note>which are the virtues of the body</note>; (for it is necessary to use visible things as evidence for things that are invisible), since exercises that are excessive, as well as those that are deficient, ruin the body's strength. Similarly, food and drink, when taken in too great or too small a quantity, ruin the health; but when they are proportionate, they produce, increase, and preserve it. This is also the case with temperance, fortitude, and the other virtues. For he who runs away from and fears everything, and endures nothing, becomes a coward; and he who fears nothing at all, but faces everything, becomes reckless. Similarly, he who indulges in every pleasure and abstains from none is intemperate; but he who avoids all pleasures, like boorish men, is an insensitive person. For temperance

<summary>Aristotle explains that because ethics deals with specific, changing circumstances, it cannot be an exact science and requires balancing excess and deficiency. He illustrates this by showing how virtues like temperance and fortitude are ruined by extremes but preserved by moderation.</summary>

and courage are ruined by excess and deficiency, but are preserved by the mean. However, not only are the origin, growth, and destruction of virtues produced from and by the same actions, but their activities also will exist in the same way; since this is also the case in other things which are more obvious; as, for instance, in strength. For strength is produced by eating much food and enduring much labor, and the strong man is particularly able to do both of these. It is the same with the virtues; for by abstaining from pleasures we become temperate, and having become temperate, we are especially able to abstain from them. The same thing happens with courage; for by becoming accustomed to despise and endure frightening things, we become brave, and having become brave, we are especially able to endure frightening things.

### CHAPTER III.

However, it is necessary to consider the pleasure or pain that accompanies actions as an indicator of one's habits. For he who abstains from bodily pleasures and finds delight in doing so is a temperate man; but he who is distressed when he abstains from them is intemperate. And he, indeed, who endures terrifying things, and

<summary>Aristotle explains that virtues like temperance and courage are both built and exercised through the same actions, and that the pleasure or pain felt during an action indicates whether a virtue has become a settled habit.</summary>

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on how the pleasure or pain experienced during an action serves as an indicator of whether a person has truly acquired a virtuous habit.</meta>

<page-num>55</page-num>
### CHAP. III. ETHICS.

is delighted with his endurance, or feels no pain from it, is a brave man; but he who feels pain from enduring them is a timid man. For ethical virtue is concerned with pleasures and pains. For we act basely through the influence of pleasure, but we abstain from noble conduct through the influence of pain. Hence it is necessary, as Plato says, to be educated in a certain way from our youth, so that we may be delighted and pained by the things that we ought to feel pleasure or pain from; for this is right education.

Furthermore, if the virtues are concerned with actions and passions, and pleasure and pain follow every passion and action, for this reason also virtue will be concerned with pleasures and pains. The punishments, likewise, which are inflicted because of these, indicate the truth of this; for they are certain remedies, and remedies are naturally adapted to work through contraries.

Again, as we have also observed before, the nature of every habit of the soul is related to and concerned with those things by which it is made better or worse. But habits become corrupted through pleasures and pains, by pursuing or avoiding them—either those that ought not to be pursued or avoided, or when it is not proper, or in such a way as is not proper, or in as many other ways as such things are distinguished by reason. Hence, some persons define the virtues as certain apathies and tranquillities; but they do not define them well, because they speak in absolute terms and do not add "in such a way as is proper," and "when it is proper," and such other qualifications as are usually made. It is admitted, therefore, that virtue is a thing of this kind, which is concerned with pleasures and pains and practices things of the most excellent nature, while vice is the contrary. From what has been said, likewise, we may obtain still greater

<summary>Aristotle argues that moral virtue is fundamentally concerned with pleasure and pain, as these motivate our actions and are the tools used in education and punishment. He refutes the idea that virtue is merely a state of emotionless apathy, insisting instead that it is the proper management of feelings.</summary>
<keywords>ethical virtue, Plato, pleasure and pain, education, habit of the soul, apathy, vice, moral discipline</keywords>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on how virtue is fundamentally concerned with the proper management of pleasure and pain, and how these factors influence moral development and character.</meta>

<page-num>56</page-num>
<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK II.</header>

evidence concerning these matters. For since there are three things that pertain to choice, and also three that pertain to aversion—namely, the noble, the advantageous, and the delightful—and three that are their opposites—the base, the disadvantageous, and the painful; the good person acts rightly in all of these, but the bad person acts wrongly, especially regarding pleasure. For pleasure is common to all animals and follows everything that is an object of choice; for both the noble and the advantageous appear delightful. Again, pleasure has been nurtured within all of us from our infancy; for this reason, it is difficult to wipe away this feeling, which is ingrained in our lives. We also guide our actions by pleasure and pain as if by a rule, some of us to a greater degree and others to a lesser. Therefore, it is necessary for this entire discussion to be concerned with these things; for feeling joy or pain rightly or wrongly is of great importance in our actions. Furthermore, it is more difficult to fight against pleasure than against anger, as Heraclitus says. But both art and virtue are always concerned with what is more difficult; for a task well done is even better when it is achieved with greater difficulty. For this reason, the entire study of both ethics and politics is concerned with pleasures and pains. For he who handles these well will be a good person, but he who handles them poorly will be a bad one. We have shown, therefore, that virtue is concerned with pleasures and pains, and that it is increased or destroyed by the same actions that produced it, depending on how they are performed; and it is also active in the very same things from which it first arose.

<header>CHAP. IV. ETHICS.</header>

## CHAPTER IV.

It may, however, be doubted what we mean by asserting that people become just by acting justly, and temperate by acting temperately; for if they act justly and temperately, they are already just and temperate, just as those who do things pertaining to grammar and music are grammarians and musicians. Or shall we say that this is not the case in the arts? For it is possible that a person may do something grammatical both by chance and at the suggestion of another person. He will, therefore, be a grammarian only if he does something grammatical in a grammatical way—that is, according to the grammatical art which he possesses. Again, the situation is not the same for the arts and the virtues; for things produced by the arts contain their own excellence within themselves. It is enough, therefore, for these to be produced in a certain manner; but actions performed according to the virtues are not done justly or temperately simply because they result in a certain state, but only if the person who does them acts from a certain disposition. First, indeed, he must do them knowingly; second, he must do them with deliberate choice, and specifically choose to do them for their own sake; and third, he must do them with a firm and unchangeable

<page-num>58</page-num>
<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK, II.</header>

disposition of mind. These things, however, are not counted as requirements for possessing the other arts, except for the knowledge of them alone. But for the acquisition of the virtues, the knowledge of them has little or no effect, while the other factors pertaining to them are not merely influential, but all-powerful; and these are obtained by frequently acting justly and temperately. Therefore, actions are said to be just and temperate when they are the kind of things that a just or temperate person would perform. But a person is just and temperate not simply by doing these things, but by doing them in the same way that just and temperate people do them. It is well said, therefore, that a person becomes just by acting justly and temperate by acting temperately, but without doing these things, no one will ever become a good person. 

Most people, however, do not act this way; instead, they take refuge in words and imagine that they are philosophizing, and that they will thus become virtuous characters. They act like sick people who listen attentively to what their doctors say, but do nothing that they are ordered to do. Therefore, just as these patients never achieve a healthy body through such a method of treatment, neither is the soul ever well in those who "philosophize" in this manner.

## CHAPTER V.

**In** the next place, we must consider what virtue is. Since three things are produced in the soul—namely, passions, powers, and habits—virtue must be one of these. By passions, I mean desire,* anger, audacity, envy, joy, love, hatred, greed, emulation, pity, and, in short, those things followed by pleasure or pain. I call "powers" those capacities through which we are said to be susceptible to these passions; namely, those which enable us to feel anger, or pain, or to be inclined toward pity. But I call "habits" the ways in which we are well or ill disposed toward the passions. For instance, regarding anger: if we are too violent or too indifferent toward it, we are badly disposed; but if we feel it moderately, we are well disposed. The same applies to the other passions. Therefore, neither the virtues nor the vices are passions, because we are not called good or bad based on our passions, but we are called so based on our virtues or vices; and because through our passions we

---

<note>By *desire*, as I have previously observed in a note on the *Rhetoric*, one should understand that irrational appetite which is solely directed toward external objects and the gratification arising from possessing them.</note>

<summary>This page defines virtue by categorizing the contents of the soul into passions, powers, and habits, arguing that virtue must be a habit rather than a feeling or a simple capacity. It explains that humans are judged morally based on how they manage their passions (habits) rather than the passions themselves.</summary>

virtues and vices. Furthermore, we may be angry and

by nature we do not become either good or bad. We
have, however, spoken about this before. If, therefore,
the virtues are neither passions nor powers, it remains
that they are habits. And thus we have shown what virtue
is in its general category.

## CHAPTER VI.

It is necessary, however, not only to show that virtue is a habit, but also to show what kind of habit it is. We must say, therefore, that every virtue renders that of which it is the virtue well-disposed, and causes its work to be well-accomplished. Thus, for instance, the virtue of the eye causes both the eye and its work to be good; for by the virtue of the eye, we see well. In a similar manner, the virtue of a horse causes the horse to be good for the race, for carrying its rider, and for withstanding the enemy in battle. But if this is the case in all things, the virtue of man will also be a habit from which a person becomes good, and by which he accomplishes his own work well. How this will indeed be achieved we have already shown; but it will now become manifest again if we consider the specific quality of the nature of virtue. In everything, therefore, which is continuous and divisible, it is possible to take more, less, or an equal amount; and this is done either with respect to the thing itself, or with reference to us. But the equal is a certain middle point between excess and deficiency. I call the middle of a thing, however, that which is equidistant from each of the extremes, and which is one and the same in all things. But with reference to us, the middle is that which neither exceeds nor falls short of what is appropriate. This, however, is neither one

<summary>This chapter defines virtue as the habit that allows a thing to perform its function excellently, using the examples of an eye and a horse. It introduces the concept of the "mean," distinguishing between a mathematical middle and a relative middle that is appropriate for the individual.</summary>
<keywords>virtue, habit, excellence, function, the mean, excess, deficiency, Aristotle, Ethics, moderation</keywords>

--- Page 71 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the nature of the "middle" or mean, distinguishing between a mathematical center and a practical middle relative to the individual's needs and circumstances.</meta>

<page-num>62</page-num>
<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK II.</header>

nor the same for everyone. Thus, for instance, if ten things are many, but two are few, six is assumed as the middle point with reference to the object itself, for six exceeds the one and is exceeded by the other by an equal amount. But this is a middle according to arithmetical proportion. The middle or mean, however, with reference to us, is not to be assumed in this way. For if eating ten pounds of food is a lot, but two pounds is a little, it does not follow that the trainer will order six pounds to be eaten; for this will perhaps be too much or too little for the person who is to take the food. For Milo <note>Milo of Croton was a famous Greek wrestler known for his great strength and immense appetite.</note>, indeed, it would be too little; but for someone beginning their exercises, it would be too much. And the same must be understood regarding running and wrestling. In this way, therefore, every expert will avoid excess and deficiency, but will search for the mean and make this the object of his choice. He will, however, explore that mean which is not the middle of the thing itself, but is a middle with reference to us. 

If, therefore, every science accomplishes its work well in this way—when it looks to the middle and refers its products to it (which is why it is common to say of well-finished works that nothing can be added to or taken away from them, acknowledging by this that excess and deficiency corrupt what is excellent in them, but that moderation preserves it)—and if good artists, as we say, work while looking to this, and virtue, in the same manner as nature, is more accurate and better than any art; if this is the case, then virtue will aim for the mean as its goal. I speak, however, of moral virtue; for this is concerned with passions and actions, and in these there is excess, deficiency, and the middle. Thus, for instance, it is possible to be afraid, to be confident, to desire and abhor, to be angry and to pity, and, in short, to be

<summary>This page explains that the virtuous "mean" is not a fixed mathematical average but is relative to the individual, using the example of a trainer's dietary prescriptions. It concludes that moral virtue aims for this relative middle ground in passions and actions to achieve excellence.</summary>

### CHAP. VI. ETHICS.

being pleased and pained to a greater or lesser degree, and experiencing both of these improperly. But to experience these passions when it is appropriate, regarding such things, toward such persons, for the sake of what is proper, and in the manner that is proper—this is the mean and the best, and it pertains to virtue. In a similar manner, there is excess, deficiency, and the mean in actions; but virtue is concerned with passions and actions, in which excess is indeed erroneous and deficiency is blamed, but the mean is praised and possesses correctness: and both of these belong to virtue. Therefore, virtue is a certain mean, and it aims for the middle as its goal. Furthermore, one can err in many ways; for evil, as the Pythagoreans suggest, belongs to the infinite, and good to the finite; but it is only possible to act rightly in one way. For this reason, the one is easy, but the other is difficult; it is easy, indeed, to miss the mark, but difficult to hit it; and on this account, excess and deficiency belong to vice, but the mean belongs to virtue. For,

->Simple is the good, all-various are the bad.<-

Virtue, therefore, is a pre-elective habit <note>or a habit accompanied by deliberate choice</note> existing in a mean with reference to us, which is defined by reason, and in such a way as a prudent man would define it. It is also the mean between two vices, one being characterized by excess and the other by deficiency. Furthermore, it is defined by the fact that some vices fall short of what is fitting, while others surpass it, in both passions and actions; but virtue both discovers and chooses the mean. Therefore, according to its essence and the definition that explains the very nature of a thing, virtue is a mean; but according to what is best, and sub-

<summary>This page defines virtue as a deliberate habit that seeks the "mean" or middle ground between the vices of excess and deficiency. <meta>This page continues the discussion of virtue as a mean, specifically clarifying that certain actions and passions are inherently base and do not admit of a middle ground.</meta>

<page-num>64</page-num>
<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK II.</header>

...sists well, it is a summit. However, not every action or every passion admits of a mean; for some passions, as soon as they are named, are associated with depravity, such as malevolence, rejoicing in the misfortunes of others, impudence, and envy; and among actions, adultery, theft, and murder. For all these, and others of a similar kind, are named as such because they are inherently bad in themselves, and not merely because they are excesses or deficiencies of something else. Therefore, it is never possible to act rightly regarding these things, but they are always accompanied by error. Acting rightly or wrongly in matters of this kind does not depend on committing adultery with the right person, at the right time, or in the right way; rather, simply to do any of these things is to act wrongly. To demand a mean in these things, therefore, is just as if someone were to think it appropriate that there should be a mean, an excess, and a deficiency in committing an injustice, or in acting cowardly and intemperately; for in that case, there would be a mean of excess and deficiency, an excess of excess, and a deficiency of deficiency. However, just as there is no excess or deficiency in temperance and fortitude—because the mean is, in a certain respect, the summit—so too there is no mean, excess, or deficiency in those aforementioned passions and actions; rather, in whatever manner they are performed, they are accompanied by error. For, in short, there is no mean for excess or deficiency, nor is there an excess or deficiency of the mean.

<summary>Aristotle explains that certain actions and passions, such as murder or envy, are inherently evil and do not have a virtuous middle ground or "mean." He argues that just as virtues like temperance are already a "summit" and cannot have an excess, these inherently base acts are always wrong regardless of the circumstances.</summary>

<header>CHAP. VII. ETHICS.</header>

# CHAPTER VII.

It is necessary, however, not only to state this universally, but also to apply it to particulars. For in what is said concerning actions, universal statements are indeed more common; but those that are specific are more true; since actions deal with particulars, with which statements ought to agree. These, therefore, are to be taken from the description.

Regarding fear and confidence, therefore, fortitude is the mean. Among the characters that exceed, however, the one that exceeds through a lack of fear is nameless; but that which exceeds in confidence is audacious. And he who exceeds in being afraid, but is deficient in confidence, is timid. In pleasures and pains, however—though not in all pleasures, <note>but in those that are bodily, and especially those that pertain to touch</note> and to a lesser degree in pains—the mean indeed is temperance, but the excess is intemperance. But those who are deficient in the pursuit of pleasures do not occur very frequently; for this reason, they have not been given a name. They may, however, be called insensitive. In giving and receiving money, the mean indeed is liberality, but the excess and deficiency are prodigality and illiberality; in which men exceed and are deficient in opposite ways. For the prodigal indeed exceeds in spending money, but is deficient in receiving it; and the illiberal man exceeds in receiving, but is deficient in spending money. At present, therefore, we think it sufficient to summarize these points in outline.

<summary>Aristotle explains that universal ethical principles must be applied to specific actions, defining the "mean" for virtues such as fortitude, temperance, and liberality. He identifies the corresponding vices of excess and deficiency for each, noting that some lack common names due to their rarity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Aristotle's detailed examination of specific virtues and vices, following the discussion of liberality, as he explores the means, excesses, and deficiencies related to wealth and honor.</meta>

<page-num>66</page-num>
<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK II.</header>

these things; but we will discuss them more accurately later. Regarding wealth, however, there are also other dispositions of the mind; and the mean, indeed, is magnificence. For the magnificent man differs from the liberal man in this, that the former deals with great things, while the latter deals with small things. The excess, however, is a lack of elegance and decorum, and illiberal profusion; while the deficiency is an indecorous parsimony in spending money. And these vices differ from those that surround liberality; but how they differ, we will show later. With respect to honor and dishonor, the mean indeed is magnanimity, but the excess is called a certain inflation of the mind, and the deficiency is pusillanimity. As we have said, however, that liberality corresponds to magnificence but differs from it in this—that it deals with small things—so to magnanimity, which deals with great honor, another certain virtue corresponds, which is also itself concerned with what is small. For it is possible to aspire to honor in a proper manner, as well as more or less than is proper. But he who exceeds in his desire for honor is said to be ambitious; he who is deficient is unambitious; and the middle character between both is unnamed. The dispositions are also unnamed, except for the disposition of the ambitious man, which is called ambition. Hence, the extremes compete for the middle place. And we indeed sometimes call the middle character ambitious, and sometimes unambitious; and sometimes we praise the ambitious man, and sometimes the unambitious man. But the reason why we do this will be shown hereafter. Now, however, following the way we began, let us speak about the rest.

<page-num>67</page-num>
<header>CHAP. VII. ETHICS:</header>

**With respect to anger, therefore, there is likewise excess, deficiency, and a mean;** but since these are nearly anonymous, we call the person at the mean a mild man, and the mean itself mildness. But of the extremes, let the one who exceeds be called wrathful, and the vice be called wrathfulness. And let the one who is deficient be a man void of anger, and the defect a lack of anger. There are likewise three other means, which have a certain similarity to each other, but also differ from each other. For all of them are concerned with the interaction of words and actions; but they differ because one of them is concerned with the truth within them, while the others are concerned with what is pleasant. And of this (namely, what is pleasant), one kind consists in humor; while another consists in all the general concerns of life. We must, therefore, also speak about these, so that we may more clearly perceive that in everything the mean is praiseworthy, but the extremes are neither right nor praiseworthy, but rather reprehensible. Most of these are also unnamed; but we must endeavor, in the same manner as with the rest, to give names to them for the sake of clarity and to make what follows easier to understand.

**With respect to truth, therefore, the middle character may be called truthful, and the mean, truth;** but regarding dissimulation, the kind that exaggerates may be called arrogance, and he who possesses it an arrogant man; and that which minimizes may be called irony, and he who employs it may be called ironical, or a dissembler. With respect, however, to what is pleasant, and specifically the kind that consists in humor, the middle character may be called facetious, and the disposition itself face-

And he who is deficient, and in all things unpleasant, is contentious and difficult to please. There are, likewise, means in the passions, and in things pertaining to the passions; for bashfulness is not a virtue, and yet the modest man is praised. For in these things, one indeed is called the middle character, another is said to exceed, and another to be deficient. And he indeed who exceeds, and is bashful in all things, is as it were astounded; but he who is deficient, and is not ashamed of anything, is impudent; and the middle character is the modest man. Indignation is a mean between envy and joy for the calamities of others; but these habits are concerned with the pain and pleasure arising from what happens to others. For he who is prone to indignation is indeed pained by those who do well undeservedly; but he who is envious, surpassing the indignant man, is pained by all who do well; and he who rejoices in the calamities of others is so deficient in feeling pain <note>from the prosperity of bad men</note> that he is delighted with it. These things, however, are discussed by us elsewhere <note>that is, in the second book of the Rhetoric</note>. ### CHAPTER VIII.

Since, however, there are three dispositions of the soul—two of which are vices (one existing according to excess and the other according to deficiency) and one being virtue, which is a mean—all three of these dispositions are, in a certain respect, opposed to one another. For the extremes are contrary to the middle and to each other, while the middle is contrary to the extremes. For just as the equal is greater in reference to the less, but less in reference to the greater; so the middle habits exceed in reference to the deficiencies, but are deficient in reference to the excesses, in both passions and actions. For the brave man, when compared to the timid man, appears to be audacious, but when compared to the audacious man, he appears timid. In a similar manner, the temperate man, when compared to the insensitive [insensate] man, appears to be intemperate, but when compared to the intemperate man, he appears insensitive. The liberal man, when contrasted with the illiberal man, appears to be a prodigal, but when compared with the prodigal, he appears illiberal. Hence,

<summary>This chapter explains that virtue is a middle state (mean) situated between two opposing vices of excess and deficiency. It illustrates how the virtuous person can appear to possess a vice when compared to someone at the opposite extreme.</summary>
<keywords>dispositions of the soul, virtue as a mean, excess and deficiency, moral opposition, bravery, temperance, liberality</keywords>

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of how the middle state (virtue) relates to the two extremes (vices), following the statement that a virtuous person may appear to possess a vice when compared to someone at the opposite extreme.</meta>

<page-num>70</page-num>
<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK II.</header>

the extremes push the medium toward one another; the timid man calls the brave man audacious, while the audacious man calls him timid; and similarly in the other extremes. These, however, being thus opposed to each other, there is a greater opposition between the extremes than between an extreme and the medium; for the extremes are more distant from each other than they are from the medium—just as the great is more distant from the small, and the small from the great, than both of them are from the equal. Furthermore, in some extremes there appears to be a certain similarity to the medium, as in audacity to fortitude, and in prodigality to liberality; but between the extremes there is the greatest dissimilarity. Things that are very distant from each other are defined as contraries; thus, those things that are more distant are more contrary to each other. 

But to the medium, in some things, the deficiency is more opposed, and in others the excess. Thus, to fortitude, audacity (which is an excess) is not opposed, but timidity (which is a defect); and to temperance, the lack of sensitivity (which is a deficiency) is not opposed, but intemperance (which is an excess). This, however, happens for two reasons: one is from the thing itself; for since one of the extremes is nearer and more similar to the medium than the other, we do not oppose that one, but rather its contrary, to the medium. Thus, for instance, since audacity appears to be more similar and nearer to fortitude, but timidity appears more dissimilar, on this account we oppose timidity to fortitude rather than audacity. For things that are more distant from the medium appear to be more contrary to it. This, therefore, is one cause from the thing itself; but another cause comes from ourselves. For those vices to

<summary>This page explains that extremes are more opposed to each other than to the virtuous mean, though one extreme often appears more similar to the virtue than the other. Consequently, we define the more distant or dissimilar extreme as the primary vice opposed to the virtue.</summary>

<header>CHAP. IX. ETHICS, 7</header>

to which we are naturally more inclined, appear to be more contrary to the mean. Thus, because we are naturally more inclined toward pleasures, we are more easily driven toward intemperance than toward moderation in the pursuit of pleasure. Therefore, those things toward which we have a greater natural tendency are said to be opposites to a greater degree; and for this reason, intemperance, which is an excess, is more contrary to temperance <note>than the other extreme</note>.

## CHAPTER IX.

It has been sufficiently demonstrated, therefore, that ethical virtue is a mean, and in what way it is so; that it is a mean between two vices—one consisting of excess and the other of deficiency—and that it is such because it aims at the mean in passions and actions as if at a target. For this reason, it is also difficult to be virtuous; for in everything, it is difficult to find the middle. For instance, the center of a circle cannot be found by everyone, but only by someone skilled <note>in geometry</note>. In the same way, getting angry, or giving and spending money, is within everyone's power and is easy; but to be angry, and to give and spend money to the right person, in the right amount, at the right time, and for the right reason,

<vocab>ethical virtue, mean, vice, excess, deficiency, temperance, intemperance, geometry</vocab>

<summary>Aristotle explains that virtue is a difficult-to-attain middle ground between two vices, and that we often perceive the extreme toward which we are naturally inclined as being the most opposite to virtue. He uses the analogy of finding the center of a circle to show that while anyone can act on impulse, acting virtuously requires specific knowledge and effort.</summary>
<keywords>ethical virtue, the mean, excess, deficiency, temperance, intemperance, moral difficulty, geometry analogy, Aristotle</keywords>

# CHAP. IX. ETHICS,

Perhaps, however, this is difficult, and especially in specific cases; for it is not easy to determine how, and with whom, and for what reason, and for how long it is necessary to be angry. For we, indeed, sometimes praise those who are deficient in anger and call them mild; but at other times we praise those who are easily provoked and call them manly. He, however, who deviates only a little from the right path, whether he inclines toward the more or the less, is not blamed; but he who deviates much from it is blamed, for the error of such a person is not hidden. It cannot, however, be easily determined to what extent and how much he is blameworthy, just as this is not easy in any other matter of perception. But things of this kind are among specific cases, and the judgment of them belongs to the senses. This much, therefore, is indeed clear: that the middle state is in all things praiseworthy, and that it is necessary at one time to incline toward excess and at another toward deficiency; for in this way we shall easily obtain the mean and correctness of conduct.

## BOOK III.

### CHAPTER I.

Since, therefore, virtue is concerned with passions and actions, and praise and blame accompany things of a voluntary nature, while pardon and sometimes pity are given to those that are involuntary, it is perhaps necessary for those who study virtue to define the voluntary and the involuntary. This will also be useful to legislators in awarding rewards and imposing punishments. Now, those actions appear to be involuntary which are performed by force or through ignorance. An act is violent when its starting principle is external, being of such a nature that it contributes nothing to the advantage of the person who

With respect to such things, however, as are performed through the fear of greater evils, or for the sake of something beautiful in conduct; as if a <term>tyrant</term> who is the lord of parents and children, should command someone to do a certain <term>base action</term>, and on these conditions: that if he did it, his parents and children should be saved, but if he did not, they should die;—with respect to such things as these, it is dubious whether they are <term>involuntary</term>, or <term>voluntary</term>. Something of the same kind also happens in losses at sea, when in a tempest the goods of the ship are thrown overboard; for considered simply, no one throws them into the sea willingly, but everyone endowed with <term>intellect</term> does so for his own safety and that of the rest of the crew. Such actions, therefore, are mixed; but they are more similar to <term>voluntary</term> actions; for they are chosen at the time when they are performed; and the end of the action is according to the opportunity. A thing, therefore, must be said to be done voluntarily or involuntarily at the time when it is done. But he threw his goods into the sea voluntarily; for the <term>principle</term> of moving the <term>organic parts</term> in such actions is in the man himself. But those things of which the <term>principle</term> is in himself, he has the power to perform or not. Such things, therefore, are <term>voluntary</term>. Considered simply, however, they are perhaps <term>involuntary</term>; for no one would choose any one of these for its own sake. It is, however, sometimes difficult to judge what should be chosen in preference to something else, and whether *this* should be endured instead of *that*; and it is even more difficult to persevere in our decisions, since, for the most part, the things we expect involve distress, and the things we are compelled to do are base. And those, indeed, who act from violence and unwillingly, act with pain; but those who are influenced by what is pleasant act with pleasure. It is, therefore, ridiculous for a person to blame external things rather than themselves when they are easily captivated by such things, and to consider themselves the cause of their noble actions while blaming pleasant things as the causes of their shameful actions. Hence, the "violent" appears to be that of which the principle is external, and to which the person compelled contributes nothing.

Everything, however, which is done from ignorance is not voluntary. But that is <term>involuntary</term> which is attended with pain and repentance. For he who does anything from ignorance, and is not at all upset by the action, does not indeed perform it willingly, because he acts from ignorance; yet, on the other hand, he does not perform it unwillingly, because he feels no pain from the action. Therefore, among those who act from ignorance, the one who repents of what he has done appears to have acted involuntarily; but the one who does not repent, since he is a different character from the former, may be said to have acted "not willingly." For since he is a different character, it is better that he should have a specific name. To act *from ignorance*, likewise, appears to be a different thing *from acting ignorantly*; for he who is intoxicated or enraged does not appear to act *from ignorance*, but from one of the conditions mentioned above; yet he acts not *knowingly*, but *ignorantly*. But an action should be called involuntary not because the person who does it is ignorant of what is advantageous; for ignorance in the deliberate choice of a thing is not the cause of involuntary conduct, but of depravity. Nor is the ignorance of the universal the cause of it, for men are blamed on this account; rather, it arises from the ignorance of particulars, in which and about which every action is concerned. For in these cases there is pity and pardon, since he who is ignorant of any one of these acts involuntarily. Perhaps, however, it will not be amiss to define what and how many <note>the particular circumstances are which render an action involuntary</note>.

They are, therefore: the circumstance of the principal agent, the circumstance of the instrument, the circumstance of the end, and the circumstances of the action itself. No one, therefore, will be ignorant of all these unless he is insane. But it is evident that the person who acts will not be ignorant of them; for how can he be ignorant of himself? A man, however, may be ignorant of what he does, as is the case with those who say that they have spoken unawares, or that they did not know that what they said was arcane—as Aeschylus said regarding the mysteries<note>Aeschylus had revealed the mysteries in one of his tragedies, for which he was tried in the Areopagus, but was acquitted by showing that he was not initiated.</note>—or as when someone fires a catapult, not knowing what he is throwing. A person may also imagine, like Merope, that a son is an enemy, and that a spear with a sharp point is blunt like a ball, or that a stone is a pumice stone. Those things which appear to be of principal importance are the action itself and the purpose for which the action is undertaken. Since the involuntary is defined by an ignorance of this kind, it is also necessary that the action should be painful and accompanied by repentance. 

But as the involuntary is that which is done through violence or through ignorance, the voluntary would seem to be that in which the principle of action is within the agent, who knows the specific circumstances in which the action consists. For perhaps it is incorrect to say that actions produced through anger or desire are involuntary. For in the first place, if this were admitted, no other animal would act voluntarily, nor would children. And in the next place, are none of the actions which we perform through the influence of desire or anger done by us voluntarily? Or, shall we say that virtuous actions are performed by us voluntarily, but base actions involuntarily? Would this not be ridiculous, since there is one cause for both of these? Perhaps, too, it is absurd to call those things involuntary which it is necessary to strive for. Indeed, it is necessary to be angry with certain things and to desire others, such as health and discipline. It appears, however, that involuntary things are painful, but those which are the objects of desire are pleasant. Again, what difference is there between the errors which are caused by reason or by anger with respect to their being involuntary? For both should be...

## CHAPTER II.

**HAVING**, therefore, defined the voluntary and involuntary, it follows that we should discuss pre-election, or deliberate choice. For deliberate choice appears to be most closely allied to virtue, and by this, as a rule, a judgment may be formed of character more than by actions. Deliberate choice, therefore, appears indeed to be a voluntary thing, yet it is not the same as what is voluntary, but the voluntary is more extensive. For children and other animals partake in the voluntary, but they do not partake in deliberate choice. And we say, indeed, that things which we do suddenly are done voluntarily, but not according to deliberate choice. But those who call it desire, or anger, or will, or a certain opinion, do not appear to speak correctly. For deliberate choice is not shared by us and irrational animals; but desire and anger are. And the incontinent man, indeed, acts from the influence of desire, but not from deliberate choice. On the contrary, the continent man acts from

<summary>This page concludes that actions driven by anger and desire are voluntary and human, then introduces "deliberate choice" as a distinct concept from the voluntary. It argues that while all deliberate choices are voluntary, not all voluntary acts—such as those by children or animals—qualify as deliberate choice.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Ethics, deliberate choice, voluntary vs involuntary, pre-election, virtue, character, incontinent, irrational passions</keywords>

--- Page 91 ---
<meta>This page continues the detailed analysis of "deliberate choice" (proairesis), distinguishing it from desire, anger, will, and opinion. It specifically explores why choice is restricted to things within our own power, unlike will or opinion.</meta>

<page-num>82</page-num>
<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK III,</header>

deliberate choice, and not from the impulse of desire. And desire indeed is contrary to deliberate choice; but one desire is not contrary to another desire. Desire, likewise, is concerned both with that which is pleasurable and that which is painful; but deliberate choice is concerned with neither the painful nor the pleasurable. Much less is deliberate choice the same as anger; for actions caused by anger seem to be driven by deliberate choice the least of all. Nor yet is it will, though will appears to be closely related to it. For deliberate choice, indeed, is not among the number of things impossible; and if anyone should say that he deliberately chooses impossibilities, he would appear to be foolish. Will, however, can be directed toward things which are impossible, as, for instance, toward immortality. And will, indeed, is also concerned with things which can by no means be accomplished by the person who wills them; such as wishing that a certain actor, or a person engaged in athletic contests, may be victorious. No one, however, deliberately chooses things of this kind, but only those which he thinks can be achieved through his own efforts. Furthermore, will is more directed toward the end <note>The goal or purpose</note>, but deliberate choice is directed toward the things pertaining to the end. Thus, we wish to be healthy, but we deliberately choose those things through which we become healthy; and we wish indeed to be happy, and we say that this is our wish; but it is not appropriate to say that we deliberately choose to be happy. For, in short, deliberate choice appears to be concerned with the things that are within our power. Therefore, deliberate choice cannot be opinion either; for opinion, indeed, appears to be concerned with all things, and no less with things eternal and impossible than with things in our power. Opinion, likewise, is divided into the false and the true, and not into the good and

<vocab>deliberate choice, desire, pleasurable, will, immortality, end, happy, opinion</vocab>

<summary>Aristotle distinguishes deliberate choice from desire, anger, and will by noting that choice only concerns attainable means within our power, whereas will can desire impossible ends. He also differentiates choice from opinion, noting that while opinions are judged as true or false, choices are judged as good or bad.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the nature of "deliberate choice" (proairesis), moving from its distinction from will and desire to its distinction from opinion, eventually defining it as a voluntary act involving reason.</meta>

<page-num>83</page-num>
<header>CHAP. II. ETHICS.</header>

evil; but deliberate choice is categorized by the latter rather than the former. In short, therefore, perhaps no one will say that deliberate choice is the same as opinion <note>in general</note>, or any particular opinion. For by deliberately choosing good or evil, we acquire a certain quality of character; but this does not happen to us simply through forming an opinion. And we choose, indeed, to obtain, avoid, or do something of that kind; but we form an opinion about what a thing is, or for what it is advantageous, or in what manner; and we do not usually form an opinion to obtain or avoid it. And deliberate choice is praised because it pertains to that which it is necessary to embrace more abundantly or with rectitude <note>moral uprightness</note>; but opinion is praised for its truth. We likewise deliberately choose those things which we know most certainly to be good; but we form an opinion about things which are not very well known to us.

And the same people do not appear to deliberately choose and form opinions about the most excellent things; instead, some form an opinion of what is better, but because of vice, they choose those things which ought not to be the objects of choice. It is, however, of no consequence whether opinion precedes or follows deliberate choice; for our attention is not directed to this, but to the consideration of whether deliberate choice is the same as a certain opinion. What then, or what kind of thing is deliberate choice, since it is none of the above-mentioned particulars? It appears, therefore, to be something voluntary. Not everything, however, which is voluntary is the object of deliberate choice, but only that which has been the subject of previous deliberation; for deliberate choice is accompanied by reason and the discursive energy <note>The step-by-step active process of reasoning</note> of reason. And the name itself appears to signify this, the object of deli-

### CHAPTER III.

BUT do men deliberate about all things, and is everything a subject of deliberation, or are there certain things about which there is no deliberation? Perhaps, however, we must define a subject of deliberation not as something a foolish or insane person might deliberate about, but as that which is an object of deliberation for a person endowed with intellect. Regarding eternal things, however, no one deliberates—for example, about the universe, or the diagonal and the side of a square, because they are incommensurable. Nor does anyone deliberate about things in motion that always come into being (γινομενων) in the same way, <note>Meaning the heavenly bodies; see the treatise *On the Heavens*.</note> whether by necessity, by nature, or by some other cause, such as solstices and the rising of stars. Nor does anyone deliberate about things that occur differently at different times, such as drought and rain; nor about chance events, such as the discovery of a treasure; nor yet about all human

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Chapter III regarding the scope of deliberation, transitioning from things outside of human control to the specific areas where human action and intellect can effect change.</meta>

<language>English</language>

concerns; for no Lacedaemonian deliberates on how the government of the Scythians may be best administered; since none of these things can be achieved by us. But we deliberate about things that can be performed by us; and these are the remainder of the things we have not yet mentioned. For nature, necessity, and fortune appear to be causes; and besides these, intellect and everything that operates through man. However, individuals of the human species deliberate about things that can be performed by them. And indeed, in those sciences which are exact and self-sufficient, there is no deliberation; for instance, there is no deliberation about letters; for there is no dispute about how we should write. But we do deliberate about such things as are performed by us, yet not always in the same way; such as matters pertaining to medicine, the art of making money, and the art of the pilot more than the gymnastic art, because the former is much less exact than the latter. In a similar way, we also deliberate about other things; but we deliberate more in the arts than in the sciences, for we disagree more about them. Deliberation, however, takes place in things that occur frequently, but whose outcome is not apparent, and in matters where there is uncertainty. Also, in matters of great importance, we employ advisors, distrusting our own judgment as being insufficient. We deliberate, however, not about ends, but about the means to those ends. For neither does a physician deliberate whether he shall heal the sick, nor a rhetorician whether he shall persuade, nor the politician whether he shall establish <term>equitable legislation</term>, nor does any of the remaining characters deliberate about the end; but having proposed a certain end, they consider how,

It notes that we deliberate most in areas of uncertainty, such as the arts or complex practical matters, rather than in exact sciences.</summary>

--- Page 95 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the process of deliberation, focusing on how one works backward from a desired end to a practical first step.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>86</page-num>
<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK III.</header>

and by what means it may be obtained. If it also appears that this end is to be obtained through many means, they consider through which of them it may be obtained in the easiest and best manner. But if it is obtained through one means, they consider how it may be accomplished through this, and through what means this itself may be obtained, until they arrive at the first cause, which is discovered last. For he who deliberates appears to investigate and analyze in the manner mentioned above, as if he were investigating and analyzing a mathematical diagram.<note>The person who deliberates—the end having been proposed which is not immediately in his power—investigates the means by which it may be obtained; and if this means also is not immediately in his power, he explores another, and afterward another, until he discovers the first means, which is immediately in his power. With this discovery, the deliberation is terminated, and the accomplishment begins, through which the end is generated and obtained. The first means, therefore, which is the last in the analysis or investigation, is the first in the generation or accomplishment. For that which is immediately in our power, as it is discovered last, is arranged first.</note> It appears, however, that not every investigation is a deliberation; for mathematical inquiries are not deliberations, but every deliberation is an investigation; and that which is last in analysis is first in generation. And if indeed in deliberating we meet with an impossibility, we desist from deliberation; as if there should be a need for money, and this cannot be procured. But if that about which we deliberate appears to be possible, then we endeavor to obtain it. Those things, however, are possible which may be accomplished through ourselves; for things which are accomplished through our friends are, in a certain respect, effected through ourselves; for the principle of action is in us. Sometimes the instruments are sought, and sometimes the use of them; and similarly in other things, sometimes the means are sought, sometimes the manner, or the agent.

---

<summary>This page explains that deliberation is an analytical process where one works backward from a goal to find the first actionable step. It distinguishes deliberation from other types of investigation and defines "possible" actions as those we can achieve ourselves or through friends.</summary>
