through ourselves; since the principle of action is within us. But sometimes instruments are explored, and at other times the use of them, and similarly in other matters; sometimes, indeed, the means through which [the end may be obtained] are investigated, and at other times the manner of achieving it. Man, therefore, as we have said, appears to be the source of his actions; but deliberation concerns things which may be performed by man, and actions are for the sake of other ends. Hence the end itself will not be the object of deliberation, but rather the things which pertain to ends. Nor, therefore, will particular facts be the objects of deliberation—such as whether this thing is bread, or is well baked, or is made as it ought to be—for these things belong to the senses; and if a man were always to deliberate, there would be an infinite regression. The object of deliberation, however, and the pre-eligible (or object of deliberate choice) are the same, except that the object of pre-election or deliberate choice is something which is now definite; for the *pre-eligible is that which is preferred as a result of deliberation.* For everyone ceases to investigate how he shall act once he has traced the origin of action back to himself, and specifically to that part of himself which acts as the leader; since this part is what makes the deliberate choice. This is also evident from the ancient political systems which Homer depicted; for the kings of these states announced to the people what they had deliberately chosen to do. Since, however, that which is pre-eligible is an object of deliberation, being a desirable thing among those within our power, *pre-election also, or deliberate choice, will be a desire for or tendency toward things in our power, accompanied by deliberation;*¹ for by forming a

## CHAPTER IV.

THAT will, however, pertains to the end, we have shown; but this end appears to some people to be the good, and to others the apparent good. But it happens to those who say that the object of the will is the good, that what a person wills who does not choose rightly is not truly an object of the will; for if it were an object of the will, it would also be

<note>
in their philosophy. Mrs. Carter, however, in her translation of Epictetus—which is as good as a person ignorant of philosophy can be expected to make—uniformly translates this word, wherever it occurs, as *choice*, as if it were *hairesis* [αἱρεσις], and not *proairesis* [προαιρεσις]. But *choice* is a very different thing from *pre-election*, or *deliberate choice*, since the former may exist without deliberation, whereas the latter is necessarily accompanied by it. A certain person translates this word as *preference*; but this is just as erroneous a translation as *choice*. For it is possible to prefer one thing to another without deliberation—as, for instance, an Englishman over a Scotsman—but such preference is not *pre-election*.

# CHAP. IV. ETHICS.

good. It may, however, happen to be bad. And it follows for those who say that the object of the will is the apparent good, that the object of the will does not have a natural existence, but is merely what appears to anyone to be desirable. However, different things appear desirable to different people; and if this were the case, then contrary things would appear desirable. If, therefore, these consequences are not acceptable, we must say that, in a simple and real sense, the good is indeed the object of the will, but to each individual, the object of the will is that which appears to be good. To the virtuous man, therefore, the real good is the object of the will, but to the bad man, it is whatever happens to seem good; just as with bodies, those things are healthy to people in good condition which are truly healthy, but other things seem healthy to those who are diseased. And the same happens with things that are bitter, sweet, hot, heavy, and all the rest. For the virtuous man judges everything correctly, and in every case, the truth is evident to him. For according to every disposition, there are beautiful and delightful things that are unique to that disposition. And perhaps the virtuous man excels others most in this: that he sees the truth in everything, being, as it were, the standard and measure of things. But among the masses, deception occurs because of pleasure; for pleasure, though it is not the good, appears to be so. The masses, therefore, choose what is delightful as if it were the good, but flee from pain as if it were an evil.

### CHAPTER V.

Since the end, therefore, is the object of the will, but things pertaining to the end are the objects of deliberation and deliberate choice, the actions which are concerned with these will be actions of deliberate choice and voluntary. The activities of the virtues are concerned with these. Virtue, therefore, is also in our power; and in a similar manner, vice. For in those things in which to act is in our power, not to act is also in our power; and in those things in which we have the power not to act, we also have the power to act. Hence, if to act worthily is in our power, not to act basely will likewise be in our power; and if we have the power of not acting worthily, we have also the power of acting basely. But if to act—and in a similar manner not to act—worthily and basely is in our power, and this defines being good or bad, then it will be in our power to be worthy or depraved characters. And to say [with a certain tragic poet] that “No one is willingly depraved nor unwillingly blessed,” seems to be partly false and partly true. For no one is unwillingly blessed, but depravity is voluntary; or unless this is admitted, what we have just now asserted must be disputed, and it must not be said that man is the principle and generator of his actions in the same manner as he is of his children. But if these things are admitted, and we cannot refer them to any other princi-

<summary>This page argues that because we have the power to both act and refrain from acting, virtue and vice are voluntary and within our own control. It disputes the idea that wickedness is involuntary, asserting that man is the primary source of his own actions.</summary>
<keywords>virtue, vice, free will, voluntary action, deliberate choice, moral responsibility, principle, Aristotle</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous page, completing the sentence regarding the internal "principles" or origins of human action and how they relate to moral responsibility.</meta>

<header>CHAP. V. ETHICS.</header>

The truth of this appears to be supported both privately by individuals and publicly by legislators themselves; for they castigate and punish those who act wickedly, unless they act from compulsion or from an ignorance for which they were not the cause. But they honor those who act well, in order to encourage the former and deter the latter. No one, however, encourages others to perform such actions as are neither in our power nor voluntary; because no advantage can be gained from persuading us not to be hot, or in pain, or hungry, or anything else of that kind; for despite the persuasion, we will suffer these things no less. For legislators also punish a man for his ignorance if he appears to be the cause of that ignorance. Thus, double punishments are ordained for those who are intoxicated; for the principle is within themselves, because they have the power not to become intoxicated, and this <note>that is, intoxication</note> is the cause of their ignorance. They likewise punish those who are ignorant of anything established by law which ought to be known and which is not difficult to know. It is similar for other things which people appear to be ignorant of through negligence, and which it is in their power not to be ignorant of; for it is in our power to pay attention to what ought to be known.

Perhaps, however, <note>it may be said that</note> a man is a person of such a character that he cannot pay attention; but such persons are themselves the causes of their characteristic qualities as a result of living negligently. Likewise, the causes of men being unjust,

# THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK III.

or intemperate, are so by their own doing, as a consequence of the former acting wickedly and the latter spending their time in drinking and similar pursuits. For activities of every kind make those who engage in them similar to the activities themselves. This, however, is evident from those who train themselves for any contest or action; for they persevere in those activities. To be ignorant, therefore, that habits are produced in every situation by the activities performed in them, is the mark of a very senseless man. Again, it is absurd to suppose that he who acts unjustly does not wish to be unjust, or that he who acts intemperately does not wish to be intemperate. 

But if anyone knowingly performs the actions that make him unjust, he will be unjust voluntarily. Nevertheless, even if he should wish it, he will not cease to be unjust and become just; for a diseased man does not become well <note>simply by wishing to be so</note>, even if it happens that he is voluntarily diseased by living intemperately and disobeying his physicians. Before he lived intemperately, it was in his power not to be diseased; but after having abandoned himself to intemperance, it was no longer possible, just as it is not possible for someone who has thrown a stone to take it back. At the same time, it was in his power to release it from his hand and hurl the stone, for he held the principle of action within himself. Thus, also, for the unjust and the intemperate man, it was possible from the beginning not to become such; for this reason they are voluntarily so. But once they have become such characters, it is no longer possible for them not to be so.

Not only, however, are the vices of the soul voluntary, but in some persons, the vices of the body are also voluntary, which

The same also takes place in weakness and mutilation. For no one would reproach a man who is blind from nature, or disease, or a blow, but would rather pity him; but everyone would reprove him who is blind from drinking wine to excess, or from any other species of intemperance. Therefore, of the vices pertaining to the body, those indeed that are in our power are blamed, but those which are not, are not criticized. But if this is the case, in other things also, the vices which are blamed will be in our power. 

If, however, someone should say that all men aspire after what appears to be good, but that we have no authority over our <term>phantasy</term>, and that as every person is, so does the goal appear to him to be—if, indeed, everyone is to himself in a certain respect the cause of his own habit, he will also be in a certain respect the cause to himself of his <term>phantasy</term> <note>that is, of the conception which he forms of a thing in his imagination</note>. ## THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK III.

—if these things are true, why will <term>virtue</term> be more <term>voluntary</term> than <term>vice</term>? For the end appears and is similarly established both for the good and the bad man, either by nature or in some other way; but by referring other things to this, they act in whatever manner they do. Whether, therefore, the end (whatever it may be) is not apparent to everyone by nature, but there is also something depending on the person [who acts], or whether the end is natural, yet because a virtuous man performs other actions voluntarily—and therefore <term>virtue</term> is <term>voluntary</term>—<term>vice</term> also will be no less <term>voluntary</term>. For in a bad man as well as in a good man, there is similarly a power of acting from himself in what he does, even though the vision of the end is not in our power.

If, therefore, as we have said, the <term>virtues</term> are <term>voluntary</term> (for we ourselves, in a certain respect, are the <term>concauses</term> of <term>habits</term>, and because we are disposed in a certain way, we propose a certain end to ourselves), if this is the case, the <term>vices</term> also will be <term>voluntary</term> for a similar reason. We have, therefore, spoken generally concerning the <term>virtues</term>, have outlined their <term>genus</term>, and have shown that they are <term>media</term> and <term>habits</term>; we have likewise explained what the things are from which they are produced, and have shown that they are caused by <term>energies</term>, and are the principles of <term>energies</term> similar to those by which they are generated; that they are likewise in our power and are <term>voluntary</term> things, and this in such a way as <term>right reason</term> shall ordain.

Actions, however, and <term>habits</term> are not <term>voluntary</term> in the same way; for we are masters of our actions from the beginning to the end, since we have knowledge of <term>particulars</term>; but of <term>habits</term>, we are only masters of the beginning. The gradual accumulation of <term>particulars</term>, however, is not perceived, just as it is in diseases; but because it is in our power to act in this way,

<summary>Aristotle argues that if virtue is voluntary, vice must be as well, since individuals contribute to their own habits and the ends they pursue. He distinguishes actions, which are voluntary at every stage, from habits, which are only voluntary at their inception.</summary>

<header>CHAP. VI. ETHICS.</header>

—or whether or not to use **particulars**; on this account, our **habits** are **voluntary**. Resuming, therefore, the discussion of each of the virtues, let us show what they are, what the **quality** of the things is with which they are concerned, and how they exist; at the same time, it will be **manifest** how many there are. And in the first place, let us consider **fortitude**.

## CHAPTER VI.

That fortitude, therefore, is indeed a medium which is concerned with fear and audacity has already been observed by us. But we evidently fear things of a terrible nature; and these are, in short, evils. Hence, also, fear is defined as the expectation of evil. We fear, therefore, all things that are evil, such as infamy, poverty, disease, the lack of friends, and death. The brave man, however, does not appear to be concerned with all evils; for it is necessary and noble to be afraid of some things, and not to be afraid of them is base; as, for instance, not to be afraid of infamy. For he who is afraid of this is a worthy and modest man; but he who is not afraid of it is impudent. He is, however, metaphorically called a brave man by some, for he has something similar to the brave man, since the brave man is also fearless. But perhaps it is not proper to fear poverty, or disease, or—

<summary>The author transitions from the general nature of virtues to a specific analysis of fortitude, defining it as a mean between fear and audacity. It explains that while fear is the expectation of evil, a brave man is not fearless of all things, as some fears—like the fear of infamy—are actually signs of a modest and worthy character.</summary>

in short, such things as neither proceed from vice nor from ourselves; yet, even a person who is fearless regarding these is not necessarily a brave man. We call him brave, however, by similarity; for some men who are timid in the dangers of war are generous and possess a proper confidence when losing money. Therefore, neither is he timid who fears insolent conduct toward his children and wife, or envy, or anything of that kind; nor is he a brave man if he is confident when he is about to be whipped. With what kind of terrifying things, then, is the brave man concerned? Shall we say with those that are the greatest? For no one endures terrifying things better. But death is the most terrifying of things; for it is the end of life; and nothing further appears to remain for one who is dead, whether good or bad.¹ But the brave man does not appear to be concerned with every kind of death; for instance, death at sea or from disease. With what kinds of death, therefore, is he concerned? Shall we not say, with those that are most noble? But these are the deaths which happen in war; for such a death is accompanied by the greatest and most noble danger. And the truth of this is confirmed by the honors which cities and monarchs bestow on those who conduct themselves bravely in war. Therefore, he may properly be called a brave man who is fearless regarding a noble death, and such things as are the causes of death when they are near. But things of this kind are especially those that happen in war. Nevertheless, at sea and in diseases, the brave man is fearless; yet not in the same manner

<note>¹ Aristotle says this not as his own opinion, but as the opinion of the common people.</note>

<summary>Aristotle defines the brave man as one who is fearless in the face of a noble death, specifically those deaths occurring in the dangers of war. He distinguishes true courage from being fearless regarding common misfortunes like poverty, disease, or non-combative deaths.</summary>

## CHAPTER VII.

THE same thing, however, is not terrifying to all men; but we say that there is also something which is beyond human endurance; <note>Such as violent thunder, earthquakes, and floods.</note> this, therefore, is indeed terrifying to everyone endowed with intellect. But the terrifying things which do not exceed the endurance of human nature differ in magnitude and in degree. And the same takes place in matters involving confidence. The brave man, however, is unterrified, as far as a man can be. He will therefore, indeed, fear things of this kind, yet in such a manner as is proper and as reason prescribes, for the sake of nobility in conduct; for this is the goal of virtue. But it is possible to be terrified by these to a greater or lesser degree, and it is also possible to fear things which are not terrifying as if they were. And in a similar manner, this applies to what pertains to confidence. He, therefore, who endures and fears things which it is necessary to endure and fear, and for the sake of that for which it is necessary, and in such a way and at such a time as is necessary—and in a similar manner, he who feels confidence in this way—is a brave man; for the brave man suffers and acts according to the importance of the situation, and in conformity with reason. But the goal of every activity is the goal according to habit, <insert>that is, the beautiful in conduct;</insert> and to the brave man, fortitude is beautiful. The goal, also, is a thing of this kind; for everything is defined by its goal. For the sake of the beautiful in conduct, therefore, the brave man endures and performs all that pertains to fortitude. 

Of the characters, however, who exceed this, he indeed who exceeds in fearlessness is nameless; but it has been observed by us before that many things are nameless. He, however, who fears nothing—neither earthquakes nor floods, as it is said of the Celts—would be an insane person, or one who has no sense of pain. But he who exceeds in confidence respecting things of a terrifying nature is audacious. The audacious man also appears to be arrogant and a pretender to fortitude. Therefore, such as the brave man is with respect to terrifying things, such the audacious man wishes to appear; and hence, in those things where he is able, he imitates him. On this account, many audacious persons also have timidity united with audacity; for as a result of their audacity when danger is not imminent, they do not endure dreadful things <insert>when they occur</insert>. But he

<summary>Aristotle defines a brave man as one who fears and faces the right things for the right reasons, guided by reason and the pursuit of noble conduct. He contrasts this virtue with the extremes of insane fearlessness and arrogant audacity.</summary>

<keywords>fortitude, courage, bravery, audacity, fearlessness, Aristotle, Nicomachean Ethics, ethics, virtue, Celts, the beautiful in conduct</keywords>

### CHAP. VII. ETHICS.

He who exceeds in fearing is timid; for he fears what he ought not to, and in such a manner as he ought not to fear, and all such qualities follow from his character; but he is deficient in confidence. Since he exceeds in his reactions to distress, however, he is more obvious. The timid man, therefore, lacks hope; for he fears all things. But the brave man is the opposite; for confidence belongs to the man who hopes for the best. The timid, the audacious, and the brave man, therefore, deal with the same things; but they are affected by them differently. For the timid and the audacious man exceed or are deficient; but the brave man is disposed toward terrifying things in the middle way, and in the proper manner. And audacious men are indeed impulsive, wishing to encounter dangers before they arrive; but when they do arrive, they lack fortitude. Brave men, however, are eager when encountering danger, but before it arrives, they are calm. As we have said, therefore, fortitude is a mean that deals with terrifying things and with matters of confidence, as we have mentioned; and it chooses and endures them because it is noble to do so, and because not doing so is shameful. But to die in order to avoid poverty, or on account of love, or because of something painful, is not the action of a brave man, but rather of a timid one. For it is weak to flee from laborious tasks; such people do not endure death because it is noble to do so, but in order to escape from evil. Fortitude, therefore, is a virtue of this kind.

<summary>This page defines the brave man as the virtuous mean between the timid man, who fears too much, and the audacious man, who is impulsively overconfident. It concludes that true fortitude involves enduring danger for the sake of what is noble, rather than seeking death to escape life's hardships.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Ethics, fortitude, courage, cowardice, audacity, the mean, nobility, virtue</keywords>

## CHAPTER VIII.

Other kinds of fortitude are also named according to four modes; and in the first place, indeed, is <term>political fortitude</term>, since this most resembles fortitude truly so called. For citizens appear to endure dangers because of the punishments and disgrace inflicted by the laws, and also because of the honors they confer. Hence, the bravest men appear to be found among those where the timid are disgraced and the brave are honored. Homer, likewise, introduces such persons, as, for instance, Diomed and Hector:

> Shall proud Polydamas before the gate...
> Proclaim his counsels are obeyed too late,
> Which, timely followed but the previous night,
> 'What numbers had been saved by Hector's flight?' <note>Iliad, Book 22.</note>

And Diomed:

> But ah! what grief should haughty Hector boast;
> I fled inglorious to the guarded coast! <note>Iliad, Book 8.</note>

This species of fortitude, however, is especially similar to the one previously mentioned, because it is produced from

<summary>Aristotle introduces political fortitude, a form of courage driven by the desire for social honor and the fear of legal punishment or disgrace. He uses examples from Homer's Iliad to illustrate how citizens are motivated to face danger to avoid shame and gain praise.</summary>

Those who are compelled to be brave by their rulers may also be ranked among brave men; but they are inferior to the former <note>that is, the politically brave</note>, because their conduct is produced not by shame, but by fear, and is not the result of fleeing from what is base, but from what is painful; for they are compelled by their masters. (102)

[Socrates] thought that fortitude was a science. <note>See the *Laches* and *Protagoras* of Plato.</note> And indeed, there are other such people in other fields; but soldiers are such in military affairs. For it seems that there are many false alarms in war, <note>Such as in ancient battles: the clashing of weapons, the rushing together of horses, etc.</note> of which soldiers are especially aware. Soldiers, therefore, appear to be brave because other people do not understand the nature of these alarms. In the next place, they are especially capable, due to their experience, of attacking their enemies without being injured themselves. They also know how to defend against and strike their enemies because they are able to use their weapons and have the kind of armor that is best for attacking without being harmed by their adversaries. They fight, therefore, like armed men against unarmed men, <note>This must be understood as applicable only to skillful soldiers.</note> and like athletes against those who are unskilled in athletic exercises. For in such contests, it is not the bravest who are best suited to fight, but those who are strongest and whose bodies are in the best condition.

But soldiers become timid when the danger is excessive and they lack sufficient numbers and military equipment. For [those who are merely skillful] are the first to flee; but those who act bravely according to political principles die while remaining at their post, as happened at the Hermæus; since for citizens, flight is shameful and death is more desirable than such a preservation. But the soldiers [in this battle at Hermæus] encountered the danger at first because they thought they were superior to their enemies; but when they saw the full extent of the

Anger, also, is related to fortitude; for men likewise appear to be brave on account of anger, just as wild beasts rush on those that wound them; because brave men also are irascible. > — Strength be added to anger.
> And, — he roused his ardor and his wrath.

For everything of this kind appears to signify the energy and impulse of anger. Brave men, therefore, act on account of the beautiful in conduct; but anger cooperates with them. And savage animals act through the influence of pain; for they act because they are wounded or terrified; since if they are in a wood, or in a marsh, they do not attack anyone. Hence those persons are not brave who are impelled to danger by pain and anger, foreseeing nothing that is dreadful; since in that case, asses also would be brave when they are hungry; for they cannot, even by blows, be driven from their pasture. Adulterers, likewise, perform many audacious deeds through their lustful desire. Those, therefore, are not brave, who are impelled to danger through pain or anger. Fortitude, however, appears to be most natural when it arises on account of anger, and when it assumes deliberate choice and that for the sake of which a thing is done <note>or the final cause</note>. But they are similar to brave men, because both of these characters are confident. Brave men, however, are indeed confident for the reasons we have already assigned; but these people are confident because they imagine they are superior to others and that they will suffer no harm from their opponents. Those who are intoxicated also act in this manner, for they become full of hope; but when they are thwarted in their expectations, they flee from danger. It is, however, the characteristic of a brave man to endure things that are, and appear to be, terrifying to man, because it is noble to do so and shameful not to endure them. Hence, it also appears to be the mark of a braver man to be fearless and without agitation in sudden terrors, rather than in those that were foreseen. For this rather proceeds from habit, and to a lesser degree from preparation. For things, indeed, which were foreseen, may be chosen through deliberation and reason; but in things that happen suddenly, a man can only conduct himself fearlessly from the habit of fortitude. Those persons, likewise, appear to be brave who are ignorant of danger; and they are not very different from those who are full of hope. They are, however, inferior to them, because they have no preconceived opinion of overcoming the evil, whereas the former do. Hence, the fortitude of those who are full of hope continues for a certain time; but the fortitude of those who are ignorant of...

## CHAPTER IX.

Since, however, fortitude is concerned with confidence and fear, it is not concerned with both in the same way, but rather to a greater degree with things of a terrifying nature. For he who remains without agitation in these, and who conducts himself in them as he ought, is braver than he who does so in matters pertaining to confidence. Brave men, therefore, as we have observed before, are called brave because they endure things of a painful nature. Hence also, fortitude is accompanied by pain <note>The OCR text reads "unaccompanied with pain," but the logic of the passage and standard translations of Aristotle's *Ethics* (1117a) indicate that fortitude is praised specifically because it involves enduring what is painful.</note>, and is justly praised; for it is more difficult to endure pain than to abstain from pleasure. Nevertheless, the end sought by fortitude may appear to be pleasant, but it is obscured and obliterated by surrounding circumstances; just as happens in athletic contests. For to pugilists, indeed, the end for which they compete is pleasing—namely, a crown and honors—but to be beaten, since this affects the body, is painful, as is

<summary>This page concludes the discussion on pseudo-courage and begins an analysis of fortitude as the virtue of enduring pain. It explains that while the ultimate goal of a brave act is honorable and pleasant, the process of facing danger and bodily harm is inherently painful.</summary>

likewise every hardship. However, because the circumstances that produce pain are many, and the goal for which they strive is small, it seems to offer nothing pleasant. If, therefore, this also applies to fortitude, then death and wounds will indeed be painful to a brave man, and to one who is naturally unwilling to suffer them. The brave man, however, endures them because it is noble to do so, or because it is dishonorable not to endure them. And the more he possesses every virtue and is happy, the more he will be pained by the prospect of death. ¹ For such a man most especially deserves to live, and he is knowingly deprived by death of the greatest goods; and this is painful. He is, however, no less brave; and perhaps he is even more brave, because he chooses conduct in battle that is noble in preference to these goods. To act, therefore, with pleasure does not apply to all the virtues, except insofar as they achieve their end. But perhaps nothing prevents not only the bravest from being the most excellent soldiers, but also those who are less brave and possess no other advantage; for these are prepared for danger and are ready to lose their lives for a small gain. And that concludes our discussion of fortitude. It is not difficult, from what has been said, to outline what it is.

## CHAPTER X.

Next, let us speak concerning temperance; for these <note>meaning fortitude and temperance</note> appear to be the virtues of the irrational parts. We have already observed that temperance is a mean concerned with pleasures; for it is concerned to a lesser degree, and not in the same way, with pains; however, intemperance is also involved with both pleasures and pains. We must now explain, therefore, what those pleasures are with which temperance is concerned. Let pleasures be divided into those belonging to the soul and those belonging to the body. For instance, the pleasures belonging to the soul are ambition and the love of learning; for each of these delights in the object of its desire without the body being affected at all, but rather the rational part. Those who are occupied with such pleasures are called neither temperate nor intemperate. The same applies to other pleasures that are not physical; for we call those who love stories and tales, and who spend the day on whatever random events arise, idle talkers, but not intemperate. Nor do we call those people intemperate who are pained by the loss of wealth or friends. Temperance, however, will be concerned with physical pleasures, yet it will not be concerned with all such pleasures. For those persons

<summary>Aristotle introduces the virtue of temperance, defining it as a mean concerned with physical pleasures rather than pleasures of the soul or mind. He distinguishes temperance from interests like ambition or storytelling, which do not involve the body in the same way.</summary>
<keywords>temperance, fortitude, irrational parts, pleasures, pains, intemperance, soul, body, ambition, rational part, physical pleasures</keywords>

--- Page 117 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of temperance, specifically distinguishing which types of physical pleasures are subject to this virtue and which are not.</meta>

<page-num>108</page-num>
<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK III.</header>

...are not called either temperate or intemperate who delight in objects of sight, such as colors, shapes, and pictures; though it seems there is also a proper way to enjoy these things, and it is possible to be pleased with them in excess or deficiency. The same is true for things related to hearing; for no one calls those people intemperate who take excessive delight in melodies or actors, nor are those called temperate who enjoy them in a proper way. Nor are those called temperate or intemperate who delight in smells, except by accident. For we do not call those people intemperate who delight in the smell of apples, roses, or sweet-smelling incense; rather, we call those people so who delight in the smell of perfumes and food. For intemperate people are pleased with these because they remind them of the objects of their desires. Others may also be seen who, when they are hungry, delight in the smell of food; but to delight in such things is the characteristic of an intemperate person, for to such a person, these things are objects of desire. Nor do other animals receive pleasure from these senses, except by accident. For dogs do not delight in the smell of hares, but in eating them—the smell merely triggers the perception <note>that is, causing them to perceive that food is present</note>. Nor does the lion delight in the voice of the ox, but in eating him; however, he perceives through the voice of the ox that he is near, and he is seen to take delight in this perception. Similarly, the lion does not delight in seeing or finding a deer or a wild goat; rather, he is pleased at seeing that from which he will obtain food. Temperance and intemperance, therefore,

<summary>Aristotle clarifies that temperance and intemperance do not apply to the pleasures of sight, hearing, or most smells, as these are not the primary objects of physical desire. He notes that even animals only value these sensations insofar as they signal the presence of food.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of temperance, specifically identifying which physical senses are associated with the virtues and vices of the appetite.</meta>

<page-num>109</page-num>
<header>CHAP. X. ETHICS.</header>

are concerned with pleasures of this kind, in which irrational animals also share. Therefore, these pleasures appear to be servile and beastly; they are the pleasures pertaining to touch and taste. Temperance and intemperance, however, appear to involve taste only to a small degree, or not at all; for the judgment of flavors <note>sapors</note> is the domain of taste, which is used by those who test wines and season food. The intemperate, however, are not very much delighted with these flavors, but with the enjoyment of the food; all of which is experienced through touch, in meat and drink, and in what are called sexual matters <note>venereal concerns</note>. Thus, a certain person named Philoxenus, the son of Eryx, who was most gluttonous in eating, wished that he had a neck longer than that of a crane, because he delighted in the sensation of touch. Therefore, the sense of touch, with which intemperance is concerned, is the most common of all the senses; it appears to be rightly considered disgraceful, because it exists in us not as human beings, but as animals. To be delighted by and especially enamored of such pleasures, therefore, is beastly; for the most refined <note>liberal</note> of the pleasures perceived through touch are not to be included with these—such as the pleasures in gymnastic exercises produced through friction and heat—since the touch of the intemperate man does not pertain to the whole body, but only to certain parts of it.

<summary>Aristotle argues that intemperance is primarily associated with the sense of touch rather than taste, as it focuses on the physical sensation of consumption shared with animals. He characterizes such excessive desires as base and beastly because they neglect the refined or uniquely human aspects of pleasure.</summary>

## CHAPTER XI.

Of desires, however, some appear to be common, while others are peculiar and acquired. Thus, for instance, the desire for food is indeed natural; for everyone, when in need, desires either dry or moist nourishment, and sometimes both. And, as Homer¹ says, both the young man and he who is in the prime of his life desire the joys of love; but not everyone desires this or that specific food, nor the same food. Hence, this desire appears to be uniquely our own, though it also has a natural element; for different things please different people, and the same thing is more agreeable to some than to others. Few, therefore, err regarding natural desires, and they err in these in only one way: namely, in excess. For to eat or drink whatever happens to be available until one is excessively full is to exceed the quantity that conforms to nature, since natural desire is simply the filling of a need. Hence, such people are called

<note>
¹ Aristotle refers to the *Iliad*, Book 24, line 129, in which Thetis complains to Achilles that he is—

->Mindless of food and love, whose pleasing reign<-
->Soothes weary life, and softens human pain: ->POPE.<-<-

But the words, “*the young man and he who is in the prime of his life*,” are added by Aristotle, as Victorinus observes, to clarify the poet’s meaning.
</note>

<summary>Aristotle distinguishes between natural desires, like the need for food, and acquired desires that vary by individual preference. He notes that errors in natural desires typically occur only through excess, where one consumes more than is necessary to satisfy a physical need.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of temperance and intemperance, focusing on how individuals err in their personal desires and the relationship between intemperance, pleasure, and pain.</meta>

<page-num>111</page-num>
<header>CHAP. XI. ETHICS.</header>
<warning>The page contains several horizontal black ink marks or scratches across the text, likely from the printing process or scanning, though the text remains legible.</warning>

gluttons, as replenishing the deficiency <note>of nature</note> beyond what is fitting; and those who are very servile become men of this description. But in those pleasures which are individual or personal, many people err, and in many ways; for they are called lovers of such things either by being delighted with things which are not appropriate, or by being pleased with them more than is appropriate, as is the case with the masses, or not in such a way as is appropriate, or not in that respect in which it is appropriate. The intemperate, however, exceed in all things; for they are delighted with some things with which it is not appropriate to be delighted, since they are odious; and if it is necessary to be delighted with some such things, they are delighted with them more than is appropriate, and in the manner of the masses. It is evident, therefore, that excess in pleasures is intemperance, and that it is blameworthy. Regarding pain, however, a person is not called temperate for enduring it, as in the case of fortitude; nor intemperate for failing to endure it; but he is indeed intemperate who feels more pain than is necessary because he does not partake of pleasures; so that the pleasure itself causes him pain <note>as a result of being desired by him beyond measure</note>. And he is called a temperate man who is not pained by the absence of pleasure and by abstaining from it. The intemperate man, therefore, desires all pleasant things, or those which are most especially pleasant; and he is led by desire to choose what is most pleasant in preference to other things. Hence, he is also pained both when he is deprived of pleasure and when he desires it; for desire is accompanied by pain, though it seems absurd that a person should feel pain on account of pleasure. Those, however, who are deficient in pleasures and are delighted with them

<summary>Aristotle defines intemperance as seeking pleasure in excess, in the wrong objects, or in the wrong manner, and notes that the intemperate man suffers pain from his own desires. In contrast, the temperate man is characterized by his lack of distress when such pleasures are absent.</summary>

--- Page 121 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of temperance, moving from the definition of the intemperate man to the rare state of insensibility and the ideal character of the temperate man.</meta>

<page-num>112</page-num>
<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK III.</header>

less than is proper, are not very frequent. For an insensibility of this kind is not human; since other animals also distinguish food, and are delighted with some kinds of it, and not with others. But he to whom nothing is delightful, and for whom one thing does not differ from another, is very far from human nature; such a person is also unnamed, because he does not very frequently exist. The temperate man, however, with respect to these things, exists in a middle state; for he is neither delighted with those things that especially delight the intemperate man—in fact, he is rather disgusted by them—nor, in short, does he rejoice in things he should not, nor is he very much delighted with anything of this kind. He is not pained if it is absent, nor does he desire it, except moderately, and no more than is proper, nor at an improper time, nor, in short, anything of this kind. But those things that are delightful and contribute to health or to a good physical condition—these he desires moderately and in a proper way. He also desires other delightful things that are not an obstacle to these, or which are not contrary to what is noble in conduct, or beyond his means; for he who is affected in that way loves such pleasures beyond their worth. The temperate man, however, is not a person of this description, but is one who acts in accordance with <term>right reason</term>.

<summary>Aristotle defines the temperate person as one who maintains a middle state, desiring pleasures only in moderation and in accordance with health, noble conduct, and right reason. He contrasts this with the rare and "non-human" state of insensibility, where a person finds nothing delightful.</summary>

--- Page 122 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of moral virtues and vices, specifically examining the voluntary nature of intemperance in contrast to timidity or cowardice.</meta>

<page-num>113</page-num>
<header>CHAP. XII. ETHICS.</header>

# CHAPTER XII.

**INTEMPERANCE**, however, appears to be more voluntary than timidity; for the former exists because of pleasure, but the latter because of pain; of these, the one indeed is desirable, but the other is to be avoided. And pain indeed shocks and disturbs the nature of the person who experiences it; but pleasure produces nothing of this kind. It is, therefore, more voluntary; and for this reason, it is also more disgraceful. For it is easier to become accustomed to these things, since there are many such opportunities in life; and becoming accustomed to them carries no danger. But the opposite is true in things of a terrifying nature. Timidity, likewise, may appear not to be voluntary in the same way as specific acts. For timidity itself is without pain; but specific situations so shock men through pain that they throw away their weapons and act improperly in other ways; and for this reason, those acts appear to be forced. The opposite, however, is true of the intemperate man; for specific acts are voluntary for him, since he desires them and his appetite is directed toward them. But the whole <note>of an intemperate life</note> is less voluntary; for no one desires to be intemperate. We also apply the name of intemperance to childish errors; for they possess a certain similarity; but which of these is named after the other is of no importance to the current discussion. It is, however,

<sig>*Arist.* VOL. II. H</sig>

<vocab>intemperance, timidity, voluntary, pleasure, pain, puerile errors</vocab>

<summary>Aristotle argues that intemperance is more voluntary than timidity because it is motivated by pleasure rather than the overwhelming pain of fear. He distinguishes between specific intemperate acts, which are chosen, and the general state of being intemperate, which no one truly desires.</summary>
<keywords>intemperance, timidity, cowardice, voluntary action, pleasure, pain, moral responsibility, childish errors</keywords>

--- Page 123 ---
<meta>This page continues the conclusion of Chapter 12, Book III, of the Nicomachean Ethics, completing the analogy between childish errors and intemperance and finishing the discussion on the virtue of temperance.</meta>

<page-num>114</page-num>
<header>THE NICOMACHEAN ETHICS. BOOK III.</header>

It is evident that the latter is named after the former; nor does the transition appear to be poorly made. For that which desires what is base—and which grows abundantly—ought to be punished. Desires in a child are especially of this kind; for children live according to desire, and in them the appetite for what is pleasant especially flourishes. If, therefore, this appetite is not obedient and subject to the governing power <note>reason</note>, it increases abundantly. For the appetite for pleasure is insatiable and spreads in every direction in a foolish man; and the exercise of desire increases whatever is allied to it, so that if the desires are great and vehement, they drive out the power of reasoning.

Hence, it is necessary that they should be moderate and few, and in no way contrary to reason. We call such a state obedient and reformed by correction; for just as it is necessary for a child to live according to the instructions of his teacher, it is also required that the part of the soul which acts according to desire should live according to reason. Thus, it is necessary that this part of the soul in the temperate man should agree with reason; for the goal proposed by both <note>that is, by reason and desire in the temperate man</note> is the beautiful in conduct. And the temperate man desires those things which it is proper to desire, and in the manner and at the time that is proper. Reason likewise ordains this. This concludes the discussion concerning temperance.

<summary>Aristotle compares the discipline of a child to the regulation of human desire, arguing that the appetitive part of the soul must be subordinate to reason to achieve temperance. A temperate person aligns their desires with rational principles, seeking only what is appropriate and noble.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Nicomachean Ethics, temperance, reason, desire, appetite, moral discipline, self-control, ethics</keywords>

## THE
# NICOMACHEAN ETHICS.

### BOOK IV.
#### CHAPTER I.

In the next place, let us speak concerning liberality. It appears to be a mean <note>medium</note> concerning wealth <note>riches</note>. For the liberal man is praised, not in warlike matters, nor in those things for which the temperate man is praised, nor, again, in judicial affairs, but in the giving and receiving of wealth; and more in giving than in receiving. We call wealth <note>riches</note>, however, everything whose value is measured by money. But prodigality and illiberality are the excesses and deficiencies regarding wealth. Indeed, we always ascribe illiberality to those who pay more attention to wealth than is proper; but in a combined sense, we sometimes attribute prodigality to the intemperate. For we call both the incontinent and those who consume

<summary>Aristotle introduces liberality as the virtuous mean concerning the management of wealth, specifically focusing on the balance between giving and receiving. He defines wealth as anything measured by money and identifies prodigality and illiberality as its corresponding vices.</summary>

<keywords>liberality, wealth, mean, prodigality, illiberality, Aristotle, ethics, virtue</keywords>

--- Page 125 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter 1 of Book IV, further defining the terms "prodigal" and "liberal" in relation to the use and management of wealth.</meta>

->THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK IV.<-

their property in intemperance, prodigals. Hence, men of this description appear to be most depraved; for at one and the same time they have many vices. They are not, however, appropriately named. For he is a prodigal who has one certain vice, namely, the consumption of his property. For he is a prodigal who is destroyed through his own actions; since the consumption of his property appears to be a certain destruction of himself, as it is through wealth that the means of living are obtained. In this way, therefore, we consider prodigality.

With respect to those things, however, of which there is a certain use, it is possible to use them well or ill. But wealth is among the number of things useful. And he uses everything in the best manner who possesses the virtue pertaining to each thing. He, therefore, will use wealth in the best manner who has the virtue pertaining to riches; and he is the liberal man. The use, however, of riches appears to be expense and donation; but the accepting and preservation of riches is rather possession. Hence, it is more the role of a liberal man to give to those to whom it is proper, than to receive from where it is proper, and not to receive from where it is not proper. For it is more the role of virtue to benefit than to be benefited, and to perform things which are noble <note>beautiful</note>, than not to perform things which are base. It is not, however, unclear that benefiting others and acting nobly follow from giving; but being benefited or not acting basely follow from receiving. Thanks, also, are presented to the giver, but not to the receiver; and praise is rather bestowed on the former than the latter. It is, likewise, easier not to receive than to give; for men are less willing to bestow what is their own, than not to

<summary>Aristotle defines the true prodigal as someone who destroys themselves by wasting their property, as wealth is necessary for life. He explains that liberality, the virtue of wealth, is primarily characterized by the noble act of giving to appropriate people rather than the act of receiving or merely possessing riches.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Nicomachean Ethics, prodigality, wealth, liberality, virtue, giving, receiving, noble actions</keywords>

--- Page 126 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of liberality from the previous page, specifically focusing on the motives and methods of the liberal person's giving and receiving.</meta>

# CHAP. I. ETHICS.

receive what belongs to another. Those, also, who give are called liberal; but those who do not take are not praised for liberality, though they are praised no less for justice. Those who do take, however, are not praised very much. But of all those who are loved for their virtue, the liberal are nearly the most beloved; for they benefit others, and this consists in giving.

Virtuous actions are noble <note>beautiful</note> and are performed for the sake of the noble. The liberal man, therefore, gives for the sake of the noble and gives rightly; for he gives to those to whom it is proper, gives what is proper, at the proper time, and follows all other requirements of right giving. This he does either with pleasure or without pain. For that which accords with virtue is pleasant or painless, and is painful only in the smallest degree. 

But he who gives to those to whom it is not proper, or not for the sake of the noble, but for some other reason, is not liberal but must be called by some other name. Nor is he liberal who gives with pain; for such a person would prefer riches to a noble action, which is not the mark of a liberal man. Nor does the liberal man take from improper sources; for such taking is not characteristic of one who does not value riches too highly. Neither will the liberal man be quick to ask for a favor; for it is not the role of a benefactor to be easily benefited by others. 

But he will take from proper sources—for instance, from his own possessions—not as something noble in itself, but as a necessity so that he may have the means to give. Nor will he neglect his own affairs, because he wishes to use them to supply the needs of others. Nor will he give to just anyone, so that he may have enough to give to those

<summary>Aristotle defines the liberal person as one who gives for the sake of the noble, doing so with pleasure and according to proper circumstances. He also explains that the liberal person values wealth only as a means to benefit others and avoids taking from improper sources.</summary>

<keywords>liberality, justice, virtue, the noble, the beautiful, wealth, giving, benefactor, Aristotle, ethics</keywords>

--- Page 127 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of liberality from the previous page, specifically focusing on the motives and methods of the liberal person's giving and receiving.</meta>

<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK IV.</header>

to whom it is proper, when it is proper, and where it is noble to give. It is, likewise, very much the nature of a liberal man to give so much that he leaves but little for himself; for it is the characteristic of a liberal man not to focus on himself. But liberality is defined according to the property one possesses; for liberality does not consist in the quantity of gifts, but in the character of the giver; and this character prompts one to give according to their means. Nothing, however, prevents a person from being more liberal even if they give fewer things, provided they give from smaller means. 

But those people who have not acquired wealth themselves but have inherited it from others appear to be more liberal; for they have never experienced want, and everyone is more attached to what they have created themselves, as is evident with parents and poets. It is not, however, easy for the liberal man to be rich, since he is neither anxious to obtain nor preserve wealth, but is more inclined to give; he does not value riches for their own sake, but for the purpose of giving. Hence, fortune is also blamed because those who most deserve wealth possess it in the smallest degree. This, however, does not happen without reason; for it is not possible for someone to be rich who pays no attention to the means of obtaining wealth, just as is the case in other matters. 

Nevertheless, the liberal man will not give to those to whom he should not, nor when he should not, and so forth; for if he did, he would no longer be acting in accordance with liberality. By wasting his wealth improperly, he would lack the means to give to those to whom he truly should give. For as we have said, he is a liberal man who spends according to his possessions and on things he should spend on; but he who exceeds <insert>[his means]</insert> in

<summary>Aristotle explains that liberality is measured by a person's character and means rather than the total amount given, noting that those who inherit wealth are often more liberal than those who earn it. He also observes that the liberal man rarely becomes rich because he prioritizes noble giving over the accumulation or preservation of wealth.</summary>

<keywords>liberality, wealth, character, Aristotelian ethics, fortune, inheritance, generosity</keywords>

--- Page 128 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of liberality as a virtue of the mean, specifically focusing on the liberal person’s balanced approach to spending and receiving wealth compared to the excessive behavior of the prodigal.</meta>

<header>CHAP. I. ETHICS.</header>

spending, is a prodigal. Hence, we do not call tyrants prodigals; for it does not seem to be easy to exceed the abundance of their possessions through gifts and expenses. Since, therefore, liberality is a middle ground which is concerned with giving and receiving riches, the liberal man will give and spend on things he should, and as much as he should, in both small and great matters; and he will act in this way willingly and with pleasure. He will likewise receive from proper sources and take such things as he should receive. For since this virtue is a middle ground regarding giving and receiving, he will do both of these in a proper way; since receiving in this manner follows from giving fairly; but receiving in any other way is the opposite. Therefore, things that follow from one another may exist together in the same person; but it is clear that opposites cannot. But if it should happen that the liberal man spends beyond what he should and beyond what is appropriate, he will feel pain, yet only moderately and in a proper manner. For it is the nature of virtue to be pleased and pained by those things that warrant it, and in a proper way. The liberal man is also very easy to deal with in financial contracts. For he may be disadvantaged, since he does not value riches; and he is more indignant if he has not spent what he should than pained if he has spent what he should not; for he does not agree with Simonides.¹ But the prodigal also errs in these matters. For he is neither pleased nor pained with

<summary>This page defines the liberal person as one who gives and receives wealth appropriately and with pleasure, maintaining a middle ground between greed and wastefulness. It also notes that the liberal person prioritizes proper conduct over the hoarding of riches, unlike the poet Simonides who praised the pursuit of profit in old age.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of prodigality and illiberality, specifically comparing the character of the prodigal to the illiberal man and explaining how the prodigal might eventually achieve the middle ground of liberality.</meta>

<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK IV.</header>

things with which he ought, nor as he ought; but this will be more evident as we proceed.

It has, however, been observed by us that prodigality and illiberality are excesses and deficiencies, and in two things: namely, in giving and receiving. For we place expense in the same category as giving. Prodigality, therefore, exceeds in giving and not receiving, but it is deficient in receiving. And illiberality is deficient, indeed, in giving, but exceeds in receiving, except in small matters. The characteristics, therefore, of prodigality cannot be very much combined. For it is not easy for him who receives nothing to give to everyone; since the wealth of those private individuals—who also appear to be prodigals—is rapidly exhausted. For a man of this description does seem to be better, though not much, than the illiberal man; for he is easily cured by age and by poverty, and may arrive at the middle ground. For he has the qualities of the liberal man, since he gives and does not receive; yet he does so neither as he ought, nor in a proper manner. If, therefore, he should happen to become accustomed to this, or in some other way should be changed, he would become liberal; for he would give to those to whom it is appropriate, and would not receive from sources where it is not appropriate. Hence, the prodigal does not appear to be depraved in his character; for it is not the quality of a bad or base man, but of a foolish man, to exceed in giving and not receiving. But he who is prodigal in this manner appears to be much better than the illiberal man, for the reasons mentioned above, and also because the one benefits many, but the other benefits no one, and not even himself. The majority of prodigals, however, as we have said, receive from sources where they ought not, and in this respect are illiberal. But

<summary>This page distinguishes between the excesses of prodigality and illiberality, arguing that the prodigal is more virtuous and more easily reformed than the illiberal man because the prodigal's habits of giving align more closely with the liberal mean.</summary>
<keywords>prodigality, illiberality, liberality, virtue, Aristotle, giving and receiving, moral character</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the prodigal man, specifically how his lack of financial discipline leads to other vices, and begins the discussion of illiberality as a more deep-seated and incurable flaw.</meta>

they become eager to receive because, through their willingness to spend, they are unable to do so with ease; for their resources for spending quickly fail them. Consequently, they are compelled to obtain money elsewhere; yet at the same time, because they pay no attention to what is noble in conduct, they receive money carelessly and from everyone indiscriminately. For they desire to give, but it is of no importance to them how or from where they obtain what they give. On this account, their gifts are not truly liberal; for they are not noble, nor are they given for the sake of noble conduct itself, nor are they bestowed as they ought to be. Instead, they sometimes make those wealthy who ought to be poor, and give nothing to men of moderate character, but bestow much on flatterers or those who provide them with various other pleasures. For this reason, most of them are also intemperate; for as they spend their money easily, they also spend lavishly on things of an indulgent nature; and because they do not live with a view toward what is noble in conduct, they incline toward pleasures. The prodigal, therefore, falls into these vices unless he is corrected; but through care and diligence, he may arrive at the mean and at what is appropriate in conduct.

Illiberality, however, is incurable; for old age and every infirmity seem to render men illiberal, and it is more natural to them than prodigality. For the majority of people are more desirous of gain than disposed to give. Illiberality also extends widely and takes many forms, since there appear to be many modes of it. For, consisting of two things—a deficiency in giving and an excess in receiving—it is not wholly and entirely present in all illiberal men, but is sometimes divided; and some, in-

He contrasts this with illiberality, which is described as a more common and incurable vice that manifests as both a refusal to give and an eagerness to take.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on illiberality, specifically distinguishing between those who are merely stingy with their own property and those who shamelessly take from others.</meta>

# THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK IV.

Indeed, some exceed in receiving, while others are deficient in giving. For all those to whom such names apply—such as niggardly, tenacious, and sordid—are deficient in giving; yet they do not desire the property of others, nor do they wish to receive it. Some do this, in fact, out of a certain integrity and an avoidance of shameful conduct. For some of them seem to look after their own property, or at least say that they do so, so that they may never be compelled to do anything shameful. Of these characters, however, the skinflint, and everyone of that kind, is so named because they give nothing to anyone. But others of these abstain from property which is not their own through fear, because they believe it is not easy for a person who takes what belongs to others to keep his own property safe from violation. Hence, they are inclined neither to receive nor to give.

Others, again, exceed in receiving by taking from all sides and everything; such as those who perform illiberal work, along with pimps, moneylenders, gamblers, swindlers, and other plunderers, as well as those who, for the sake of a small amount, subject themselves to great disgrace. For all these take from where they should not, and what they should not. The pursuit of shameful profit, however, appears to be common to all of these; for they all endure disgrace for the sake of profit, and a small one at that. For we do not call those people "illiberal" who take great things from where they should not, such as tyrants, those who overthrow cities, and the plunderers of temples; rather, we call them wicked, impious, and unjust. The gambler, the robber, and the swindler, however, are among the number of illiberal characters; for they are addicted to shameful profit. For the sake of gain, they devote themselves to these occupations and endure dis-

<summary>Aristotle distinguishes between types of illiberal men: those who are stingy but otherwise honest, and those who pursue shameful profit through dishonorable occupations like gambling or usury. He notes that grand-scale criminals like tyrants are considered wicked and unjust rather than merely illiberal.</summary>

grace. And some, indeed, expose themselves to the greatest dangers for the sake of what they may get; but others take from their friends, to whom they ought to give. Both of these, therefore, since they wish to enrich themselves from sources they should not, are addicted to base gain; and all such acquisitions are illiberal. It is also reasonable that illiberality is said to be the opposite of liberality; for it is a greater evil than prodigality, and people err more in this than in the prodigality which we discussed above. And that is enough concerning liberality and its opposite vices.

## CHAPTER II.

It would seem to follow that we should next discuss magnificence; for it also appears to be a certain virtue concerned with wealth. It does not, however, extend to all financial actions in the same way as liberality, but only to those that are lavish. But in these, it surpasses liberality in scale; for, as its name signifies, it is a fitting costliness in great matters. Magnitude, however, is relative; for the same expense is not appropriate for the commander of a three-ranked galley and the organizer of a public spectacle. What is appropriate, therefore, exists in relation to

to him who spends, and to the thing on which he spends his money, and the money which is spent. He, however, who spends with decorum in small or in moderate things, is not called magnificent; such as,

->To wandering beggars I have often given;¹<-

but he who spends appropriately in great things. For the magnificent man is a liberal man; but the liberal man is not necessarily magnificent simply because he is liberal. Of this kind of habit, however, the deficiency is called parsimony; but the excess is called vulgar ostentation and an ignorance of what is elegant, and such other names as belong to habits which do not exceed in magnitude regarding things in which great expense is fitting, but instead exhibit a splendid profusion in things in which such profusion is not proper. Concerning these, however, we shall speak later. But the magnificent man resembles the scientific man; for he is able to perceive what is decorous and can spend large sums with elegance. For, as we said in the beginning, a habit is defined by its activities and by those things which the habit concerns. But the expenses of the magnificent man are great and fitting; and such also are his deeds; for in this way the expense will be great and adapted to the deed. Hence, it is necessary that the deed should be worthy of the expense, and the expense worthy of the deed, or even surpassing it. The magnificent man, therefore, spends in this manner for the sake of what is beautiful in conduct; for this is common to all the virtues; and he also spends with pleasure and lavishly, because an accurate attention to expense is the province of a parsimonious

<summary>Aristotle defines magnificence as the virtue of spending large sums appropriately and with elegance, distinguishing it from both parsimony and vulgar ostentation. He emphasizes that magnificence requires a sense of what is decorous and that the expense must be worthy of the deed.</summary>

## CHAP. II. ETHICS.

man. The magnificent man, likewise, will rather consider how he may accomplish the most beautiful and becoming work than the money it will cost, and how it may be accomplished with the least expense. It is necessary, therefore, that the magnificent man should also be a liberal man; for the liberal man spends what he ought and as he ought. But in these things, whatever is great pertains to the magnificent man, magnificence being, as it were, a certain greatness of liberality. Since, however, liberality is concerned with the same things as magnificence, the magnificent man will produce a more magnificent work from an equal expense. For the virtue of a possession is not the same as the virtue of a work; since the virtue of a possession is to be of great worth and most precious, like gold, but the virtue of a work is to be great and beautiful. For the observation of a thing of this kind is admirable. But the magnificent is admirable; and the virtue of a work is magnificence in magnitude. Among expenses, however, which we call honorable, are those that pertain to the worship of the gods, gifts dedicated to the divine, the building of temples, and sacrifices; and in a similar manner, those things that pertain to every spiritual nature <note>The original text uses "demoniacal nature," referring to *daimones* or lesser deities/spirits in Greek philosophy rather than the modern sense of "demons."</note>, and those bestowed on the community at large from a laudable ambition. Thus, the expenses of the magnificent man will be of this kind, if he should think it necessary to furnish public spectacles splendidly, or triremes <note>The original text refers to "three-ranked galleys," the standard Greek warship.</note>, or to host a feast for the city. But in all things, as we have said, it must be considered who the agent is and what means he possesses. For the expense ought to be such as is worthy of the means, and not only adapted to the work but also to him by whom it is performed. Hence, a poor man cannot be a magnificent man; for he does not have the means of spending much

<summary>Aristotle explains that the magnificent man prioritizes the beauty of a work over its cost and that magnificence is a superior form of liberality applied to large-scale, honorable public and religious expenses.</summary>

# THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK IV.

in a fitting manner. Therefore, the poor man who attempts to do so is foolish; for such an effort is contrary to his means and to what is fitting. But what is done correctly is done according to virtue. Such expenditure, however, is appropriate for those who possess inherited wealth, or have earned it themselves, or have received it from their ancestors or through a legacy. It is also fitting for those who are noble and famous, and other persons of a similar kind; for all these possess greatness and dignity. The magnificent man, therefore, is primarily a person of this sort; and magnificence, as we have stated, consists in expenditures of this kind, for they are the greatest and the most honorable.

Regarding private expenses, however, those that pertain to the magnificent man are those incurred only once, such as a wedding or anything else of that nature, as well as matters that earnestly occupy the whole city or those who hold a dignified position. This also includes expenses related to receiving and seeing off guests, along with gifts and rewards. For the magnificent man does not spend lavishly on himself, but on the public. Gifts, moreover, share a similarity with objects dedicated to the gods. It is also the duty of a magnificent man to build a house in a way suited to his wealth <note>for this also is a certain ornament</note>, and to spend more on works that are more enduring, for these are the most beautiful. It is likewise his responsibility in each of these cases to observe what is fitting; for the same things are not suitable for gods and men, whether in building a temple or a tomb. And indeed, every essence is great in its own kind; and that which is great in a great thing is most magnificent; but that

<summary>Aristotle explains that magnificence is a virtue reserved for the wealthy and noble, involving large-scale expenditures that benefit the public or create lasting, beautiful monuments rather than personal luxury.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of magnificence, specifically contrasting the character of the magnificent man with the vices of vulgarity (excess) and parsimony (deficiency).</meta>

<header>CHAP. II. ETHICS.</header>

For there is a difference between magnitude in a work and magnitude in expense; since a ball, indeed, or a very beautiful jug, possesses the magnificence of a gift for a child, but the price of these is small and illiberal <note>meaning stingy or ungenerous</note>. For this reason, it is the role of a magnificent man to do magnificently whatever he may do, in every kind of thing. For a thing of this kind cannot easily be surpassed, and the scale of the expense is appropriate. Such, therefore, is the magnificent man.

But he who exceeds and is vulgarly ostentatious exceeds by spending, as we have before observed, beyond what is appropriate. For in small things, and those which require only small expense, he spends much money and is inappropriately showy. Thus, for instance, he will prepare a wedding dinner for the sake of ostentation and give money to the performers who are present at the entertainment as if it were for the public benefit. And in plays, he will introduce a purple curtain before the stage, as is done by the Megarensians. He will likewise do everything of this kind, not for the sake of what is noble in conduct, but so that he may display his wealth, imagining that he will be admired for these things. Similarly, in matters where much expense is required, he spends but little; but where little expense is required, he spends largely.

The parsimonious man, however, is deficient in everything; and even when he has incurred a great expense, looking toward the completion of the work with excessive scrutiny, he leaves it unfinished by holding back a small amount of money. Everything he does is also accompanied by delay and over-thinking; for this reason, he complains and imagines that he does everything on a larger scale than he should. These habits, therefore, are

### CHAPTER III.

But magnanimity is concerned with great things, as
is evident from its very name. However, we must first 
consider the nature of the things with which it is concerned. 
But it makes no difference whether we examine the habit <note>a settled disposition or character trait</note>,

appears to be magnanimous, however, who, deserving 
great things, believes that he deserves them; for anyone 
who thinks this of himself without deserving it is 
stupid. But no one who is endowed with virtue is 
either stupid or a fool. The character mentioned above, 
therefore, is magnanimous. For he who deserves small 
things and thinks that he deserves them is a modest man, 
but not a magnanimous one; since magnanimity con-

form, but they are not beautiful. However, he who thinks

is proud; though not everyone who, deserving

<summary>Aristotle introduces magnanimity as the virtue of being worthy of great things and rightly recognizing that worth. He distinguishes it from modesty, pride (vanity), and pusillanimity (smallness of soul) based on the relationship between one's actual merit and one's self-estimation.</summary>
<keywords>magnanimity, Aristotle, ethics, virtue, pride, modesty, greatness of soul, habit, pusillanimity</keywords>

--- Page 138 ---
<meta>This page continues the definition of the pusillanimous man and explores the relationship between magnanimity, merit, and the greatest of external goods: honor.</meta>

<page-num>129</page-num>
# CHAP. III. ETHICS.

-mous, if, deserving things of a moderate or small nature, he thinks himself to deserve still less than these. And he will especially appear to be a character of this kind who, deserving great things, <note>has this humiliating opinion of himself.</note> For what would he do if he were not deserving of such things? The magnanimous man, therefore, is at the summit in terms of magnitude, but in that which is appropriate, he holds the middle position; for he thinks himself deserving of exactly what he does deserve, while the other characters exceed or are deficient. Hence, if deserving great things he thinks that he deserves them, and especially if he deserves the greatest things, he will principally be concerned with one thing. What this is, therefore, must be assumed from merit, and merit is defined with reference to external goods. We must, however, consider that as the greatest of external goods which we attribute to the gods, which those who are in a dignified situation especially aspire to, and which is the reward of the most noble deeds. Honor is a thing of this kind; for this is the greatest of external goods. The magnanimous man, therefore, is concerned with honor and dishonor in such a manner as is proper. And indeed, without any reasoning process, the magnanimous appear to be concerned with honor; for great men especially think themselves deserving of honor; and they think so deservedly. The pusillanimous man, however, is deficient both with respect to himself and the merit of the magnanimous man. But the proud man exceeds, indeed, with respect to himself, yet not with respect to the magnanimous man. The magnanimous man, however, if he is deserving of the greatest things, will be the best of men; for a better character always deserves something greater, and the best of characters deserves the greatest things.
