greatest of things. Hence, it is necessary that the truly magnanimous man should be a good man; and that which is great in every virtue will appear to belong to the magnanimous man. Nor does it by any means accord with the character of the magnanimous man to flee while agitated <note>with fear</note>, or to injure anyone. For on what account would he act basely, to whom nothing is great? But from a survey of particulars, the magnanimous man will appear to be ridiculous if he is not a good man. Nor, indeed, will he be worthy of honor if he is a bad man; for honor is the reward of virtue and is conferred on good men. Magnanimity, therefore, appears to be, as it were, a certain ornament of the virtues; for it causes them to be greater and does not exist without them. On this account, it is truly difficult to be magnanimous; for it is not possible to be so without integrity and worth.

The magnanimous man, therefore, is especially concerned with honor and dishonor. And with great honors, indeed, and those which are conferred by worthy men, he is moderately pleased, as being things familiar and adapted to him, or rather less than he deserves; for there can be no honor equal to the merit of all-perfect virtue. Nevertheless, he will admit these honors because they have nothing greater to confer upon him. But he will entirely despise the honor which is paid to him by casual persons, and for things of a trifling nature; for these do not accord with his merit. And in a similar manner, he will despise dishonor; for it will not justly befall him. The magnanimous man, therefore, as we have said, is especially concerned with honor. Nevertheless, with respect to

wealth also, and power, and all prosperous and adverse fortune; he will conduct himself in these moderately, in whatever manner they may take place. And he will not be very much elated in prosperity, nor very much dejected in adversity. For he is not even affected by honor as if it were the greatest of things, since dominion and wealth are desirable for the sake of honor. Those, therefore, who possess these, wish to be honored through them. To him, however, to whom honor is a small thing, other things will also be small. Hence, likewise, magnanimous men appear to be supercilious.

Prosperity, however, seems to contribute to magnanimity. For those who are nobly born are thought worthy of honor; and also men in authority, and those who are rich; for they surpass others. But everything which excels in good is more honorable. Hence, things of this kind also cause men to be more magnanimous; for they are honored by certain people because of them. In reality, however, only the good man is to be honored; but he who possesses both of these <insert>namely, good fortune and virtue</insert> is reckoned more deserving of honor. Those, however, who possess such goods without virtue, neither justly think themselves worthy of great things, nor are they rightly called magnanimous men; for magnanimity cannot exist without all-perfect virtue. 

But those who possess things of this kind become supercilious, insolent, and bad men; for without virtue, it is not easy to bear prosperity gracefully. But not being able to bear prosperity, and imagining that they surpass other men, they despise them and act in a careless manner. For they imitate the magnanimous man without resembling him; and they do this in those things of which they are capable. They do not, therefore, act

The magnanimous man also neither exposes himself to small dangers, nor is he a lover of danger, because there are few things which he considers to be of great importance. But he exposes himself to great dangers, and when he is in danger, he is not sparing of his life, because he does not consider life as a thing of great importance. He is likewise disposed to benefit others, but he is ashamed to be benefited; for the former is the trait of one who surpasses, but the latter of one who is surpassed. And the benefit which he returns exceeds what he received. For in this way it will happen that he who first bestowed the benefit will become his debtor and will be benefited by him. Magnanimous men also seem to remember those whom they have benefited, but not those from whom they have received any advantage; for he who receives is inferior to him who confers the benefit. But the magnanimous man wishes to excel. Hence, Thetis does not mention the benefits she had conferred on Jupiter, nor do the Lacedæmonians mention those which they had conferred on the Athenians, but rather those which they had received from them. It is likewise the quality of a magnanimous man to ask nothing of anyone, or to do so only rarely, but to readily provide for the needs of others. And toward those indeed who are in a dignified position and in prosperous circumstances, he is great <note>in his behavior</note>, but moderate toward those who are in a middle condition. For to surpass the former is difficult and dignified, but it is easy to excel the latter. And

### CHAP. III. ETHICS.

to conduct himself with dignity among the former is not dishonorable, but among the lower class of men it is arrogant, in the same manner as it would be for a man to display his strength among the weak. It is also a characteristic of the magnanimous man not to involve himself in things that are held in high esteem, or where others hold the primary place. Likewise, he is inclined to be at leisure and prone to delay, except where great honor is to be obtained or some great work is to be accomplished; and to perform only a few things, but let those be great and celebrated. It is also necessary that he should openly hate and openly love; for to conceal love or hatred is the trait of one who is afraid. It is likewise the characteristic of the magnanimous man to regard truth more than opinion. He also speaks and acts openly; for this is the trait of the man who despises others. Hence he uses the greatest freedom of speech; for this belongs to him who speaks freely. Because of this, he is also a despiser of others and a lover of truth, except when he speaks ironically; but his language is ironic to the common people. The magnanimous man, likewise, is unable to live with any other person than a friend; for to do otherwise is servile. This is why all flatterers are mercenary, and all humble men are flatterers. Nor is he given to excessive admiration; for to him nothing is great <note>in human affairs</note>. Nor is he mindful of injuries; for it is not the characteristic of a magnanimous man to be mindful of things, especially of evils, but rather to overlook them. Nor does he speak about men; for he speaks neither about himself nor about another person. For he is not concerned that he himself may be praised, or that others may be blamed. Nor, again, is he given to praise. Hence, he does not defame anyone, not even his enemies, unless in order to

But a high-pitched voice and rapidity of movement are produced from intensity and from considering human affairs to be important. Such, therefore, is the magnanimous man.

He, however, who is deficient in magnanimity is pusillanimous; but he who exceeds it is proud and arrogant. However, neither of these characters appears to be bad; for they are not malevolent, but they stray from the mean. For the pusillanimous man, indeed, though deserving of good things, deprives himself of what he deserves; and he appears to have some defect because he does not think himself worthy of what is good. He is also ignorant of himself; for if he were not, he would aspire after the things of which he is worthy, such things being good. Such men, however, do not appear to be stupid, but rather to be sluggish. But an opinion of this kind seems to make them worse; for everyone desires what is suited to his merit. Pusillanimity, however, is more opposed to magnanimity than pride and arrogance; for it occurs more frequently and is a worse evil. Magnanimity, therefore, is, as we have said, concerned with great honor.

## CHAPTER IV.

It seems, however, that a certain virtue is concerned with honor, as we have observed before, which appears to have a similar relation to magnanimity as liberality has to magnificence; for both of these virtues are removed from great scale, but they dispose us in a proper way regarding moderate and small things. But just as in the receiving and giving of money there are a mean, an excess, and a deficiency; so also, in the desire for honor, there is the more and the less.

than is appropriate, and from where it is appropriate, and in the manner that is appropriate. For we blame the ambitious man for aspiring after honor more than is appropriate, and from where it is not appropriate <note>to obtain it</note>; and we blame the unambitious man for not deliberately choosing to be honored even for actions that are noble. Sometimes, however, we praise the ambitious man as manly and a lover of noble conduct; but we praise the unambitious man as modest and temperate, as we have observed before. But it is evident that since the lover of a certain thing is spoken of in many ways, we do not always refer the "lover of honor" to the same thing. When we praise him, it is because he desires honor more than the average person desires it; and when we blame him, it is because he desires it more than is appropriate. Since, however, the middle state is unnamed, the extremes appear to compete for it as if for an empty place. But in those things in which there are excess and deficiency, there is also a middle state. Men also aspire after honor more or less than is appropriate; and therefore, they also aspire after it in such a way as is appropriate. Hence, this habit is praised, which is an unnamed middle state concerning honor. It appears, however, in reference to ambition, to be a lack of ambition, and to be ambition in reference to a lack of ambition; and to be in a certain respect both in reference to both. This also appears to be the case in the other virtues. Here, however, the extremes are seen to be opposed to each other because the middle state is without a name.

## CHAPTER V.

But mildness is, indeed, a middle state concerned with anger. Since, however, the virtue which conducts itself moderately with respect to anger is unnamed—and this is also almost the case with the extremes—we refer mildness to the middle state, though it appears to incline rather toward the lack of anger, which deficiency is also unnamed. But the excess may be called a certain irascibility. For the passion is anger; but the causes of it are many and various. He, therefore, who is angry for the right reasons, and with the persons with whom it is proper to be angry, and furthermore, in such a manner as is proper, and when, and for as long as is proper, is praised. Hence, he will be a mild man, since mildness is praised. For the mild man wishes to be without disturbance, and not to be led by passion; but to be angry as reason may dictate in these things, and for as long a time as it prescribes. He appears, however, to err rather on the side of deficiency with respect to anger; for the mild man is not given to revenge, but is instead inclined to pardon. But the deficiency, whether it be a certain leniency, or whatever it may be, is blamed. For those who are not angry for causes for which it is proper to be angry appear to be stupid; and this is also the case with those who are not angry as

<summary>This page defines mildness as the virtuous middle state between the extremes of irascibility and a lack of anger. It explains that a mild person is guided by reason, becoming angry only for the right reasons and at the right time, while leaning toward pardon rather than revenge.</summary>

<language>English</language>

<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK IV.</header>

it is appropriate, nor when it is appropriate, nor with those people with whom it is appropriate; since they appear to be without feeling and to be free of pain. And also, since they do not get angry, they are not inclined toward revenge. For it is servile for a man to endure the insolent behavior of others toward himself and his own relatives.

Excess in anger, however, exists in many forms. For it is possible to be angry with people and for reasons that are not appropriate, and also to a greater or lesser degree, and for a longer time than is appropriate. All these excesses, however, are not found in the same person, for it is not possible that they should be. For evil destroys itself; and if it is complete and total, it becomes intolerable.

Those, therefore, who are irascible quickly become angry—both with things and for causes which should not make them angry, and also more intensely than is appropriate—but they quickly stop being angry, which is a very good thing. This happens to them because they do not restrain their anger but retaliate as soon as they have been injured. Thus, their anger is obvious because of its swiftness; but afterward, they cease being angry. The extremely irascible, however, are excessively quick to anger and are angry at everything and on every occasion, which is how they get their name. 

But those who are bitterly angry are freed from their anger only with difficulty and remain angry for a long time, for they hold back their anger <insert>from bursting forth</insert>. They stop being angry, however, when they have taken vengeance on those who angered them; for vengeance appeases anger, producing pleasure instead of pain. But if vengeance does not occur, they are oppressed with a heavy burden; because the way they are affected is not obvious, neither does anyone

It explains that while irascible people vent quickly, the bitterly angry harbor their feelings until they can achieve vengeance.</summary>

We also call those people severe in their anger who are angry for reasons they should not be, and to a greater degree and for a longer time than is proper, and who cannot be appeased without revenge or punishment.

To mildness, however, we oppose the excess rather than the deficiency; for it is more frequent, since it is more human to seek revenge for an injury. Severe people are also worse for the purpose of social interaction. But what we observed before is also evident from what we are saying now. For it is not easy to define how, with whom, for what reasons, and for how long a person should be angry, and also to what extent they may do so rightly or wrongly.

For he who deviates only slightly is not blamed, whether he leans toward the excess or the deficiency; since we sometimes praise those who are deficient in anger and call them mild, and sometimes we call those who are severely angry "virile," as being people who are able to govern others. It is not, therefore, easy to explain in words the extent and manner of deviation which is blameworthy; for the judgment of this lies in specific circumstances and in perception. This much, however, is evident: that the middle state is indeed praiseworthy, according to which we are angry with those people and for those reasons that it is appropriate to be so, and in such a manner as is appropriate, and everything else of that kind. But the excesses and deficiencies are blameworthy. And these, indeed, if they deviate only a little from the mean, are blameworthy to a small degree; if more, in a greater de-

### CHAPTER VI.

IN the associations of men with each other, however,

### CHAP. VI. ETHICS.

according to this middle habit, is the kind of person we wish a worthy friend to be, provided he also assumes, in conjunction with it, an affection resembling filial love. But it differs from friendship because it lacks the passion and the specific love resembling filial love toward those with whom it is practiced. For such a person does not accept everything in a certain way because they love or hate the person, but from a habit of approving or disapproving of things properly. For he who possesses this habit will be equally affable to those whom he does not know and to those whom he does know, to his associates and to those with whom he does not associate, except that his affability will be appropriate to each of these. For it is not proper to show the same attention—or to cause the same pain—to close friends as to strangers. 

We have, therefore, universally shown that he will conduct himself in his social interactions in a proper manner. By referring his actions to what is honorable in conduct and to what is useful, his aim will be neither to cause pain to others nor to delight them through obsequiousness. For this virtue appears to be concerned with the pleasures and pains that occur in social associations. But when the possessor of this virtue cannot delight his associates worthily, or without harming them, he shows his disapproval and deliberately chooses to give them pain <note>rather than to harm them through obsequiousness</note>. 

He will also not permit another person to be obsequious toward him in matters that involve significant disgrace or injury, especially when opposing such behavior produces only a little pain; instead, he will show his disapproval. Likewise, he will interact differently with those who are in a dignified position than with ordinary people, and with those who are more or less known to him. In a similar manner, regarding other differences, he will attribute to every person what is fitting for each to—

And with respect to consequences, if they are significant, he will be guided by them; I mean, he will follow what is noble in conduct and what is advantageous; and for the sake of great pleasure afterwards, he will cause pain in a small degree. Such, therefore, is the middle character, but he is unnamed. With respect, however, to those who delight others, he who aims at pleasing and nothing else may be called accommodating; but he who does this in order that he may derive some financial advantage, or such things as are obtained through money, is a flatterer. And he who is indignant with everything, as we have already said, is morose and litigious. The extremes, however, appear to be opposed to each other because the middle state is anonymous.

### CHAPTER VII. ETHICS.

In each particular instance, we shall know more of what pertains to conduct, and we shall be persuaded that the virtues are means, when we understand what occurs in all of them. Therefore, regarding the interactions of men with each other, we have already spoken about those who associate with a view to pleasure and pain. But let us now consider those who are truthful or false, both in words and deeds, as well as in dissimulation.

The arrogant man, therefore, appears to be someone who pretends to have qualities of a splendid nature which he does not actually possess, or to possess them to a greater degree than he does. The dissembler, on the contrary, denies what he possesses or makes it seem less than it really is. But the middle character, forming a just opinion of himself, is a man of truthfulness in his life and in his words, acknowledging that he possesses exactly what he does, and neither more nor less. Each of these actions, however, may be done for a specific purpose or for no purpose at all. But as a man is in character, so will be his words and actions, and such also will be his life, unless he is acting for a particular reason.

In itself, however, falsehood is bad and blameworthy; but truth is beautiful and praiseworthy. Hence, the truthful man, being a middle character, is praiseworthy; but of the two characters who lack truthfulness, both are blameworthy, though the arrogant man is more so than the other. We shall, however, speak about each of these, beginning with the truthful man. For we are not speaking of the man who is truthful in legal contracts or in matters pertaining to injustice or justice, for that belongs to another virtue; rather, we speak of him who, even when nothing of that kind is at stake, is truthful both in his words and in his life, because he is...

<summary>This page defines truthfulness as the middle state between arrogance (boasting) and dissimulation (understatement), noting that this personal integrity is distinct from legal justice.</summary>
<keywords>ethics, veracity, truthfulness, arrogance, dissembler, middle state, virtue, conduct, Aristotelian ethics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of truthfulness as a virtue, transitioning from the general definition of a truthful person to the specific motivations and characteristics of those who deviate from the truth, such as the arrogant and the vain.</meta>

# THE NICOMACHEAN . BOOK IV.

...he is such by habit. But such a person will appear to be a worthy man. For he who is a lover of truth, and who speaks the truth in matters where it is of no consequence whether he does so or not, will to an even greater degree speak the truth in matters where it is of consequence. For he will avoid what is false as something shameful, just as he would avoid it for its own sake; and such a man is worthy of praise. He will, however, <note>if it should be necessary to deviate from the mean</note> rather incline toward saying less than the truth; for this appears to be more tasteful, because excesses are troublesome and offensive. 

But he who pretends that he possesses qualities of greater importance than he really does, and does this for no other reason, resembles a depraved man; for otherwise he would not take delight in falsehood. Yet, he seems to be a vain person rather than a bad one. If, however, he does this for the sake of something else, such as glory or honor, he is not very blameworthy, as the arrogant man is; but if he does it for the sake of money, or of things pertaining to money, he is more contemptible. But the arrogant man is not defined by his capacity or power, but by deliberate choice; for he is arrogant by habit, and because he is that kind of character. Thus, also, regarding the man who lacks truthfulness: one delights in falsehood itself, while another delights in it because he aspires to glory or gain. 

Those, therefore, who are arrogant for the sake of glory pretend to possess those qualities for which men are praised or declared happy. But those who are arrogant for the sake of gain pretend to be the kind of characters who delight others and whose lack of those qualities can remain hidden, such as pretending to be a physician, a prophet, or a wise man. For this reason,

### CHAP. VII. ETHICS.

Most men pretend and claim things of this kind for themselves; for they possess the qualities mentioned above. Dissemblers, or the ironical, however, who speak less than the truth, indeed appear to be more refined in their manners; for they do not seem to speak for the sake of gain, but as a way of avoiding fastidiousness. But these persons especially deny that they possess qualities of a distinguished nature, just as Socrates also did. Those, however, who pretend that they do not possess small and obvious things are called crafty or subtle deceivers, and are very contemptible men. Sometimes, also, this species of dissimulation appears to be arrogance; such as, for instance, the clothing of the Lacedæmonians. For both excess and extreme deficiency alike relate to arrogance. But those who use irony moderately, and are ironical in things which are not very well-known or obvious, appear to be refined men. The arrogant man, however, seems to be the opposite of the man of truthfulness; for he is a worse character.

## CHAPTER VIII.

Since, however, there is a certain relaxation in life, and rest from labor, and since this respite is accompanied by jesting, it appears that here also there is a certain elegant way of conversing, in which appropriate things are said and are delivered in an appropriate manner; and similarly with respect to hearing what is appropriate to hear, and hearing it in a way that is fit. But there is a difference in speaking to some people rather than to others, and in hearing some things rather than others. It is evident, however, that in these things also there is an excess and deficiency relative to the mean. Therefore, those who exceed in the pursuit of the ridiculous appear to be scurrilous and troublesome; for they are entirely focused on being ridiculous, and aim more at exciting laughter than at speaking in a becoming manner and avoiding giving pain to the object of their ridicule. But those who do not say anything ridiculous themselves, and are indignant with those who do, appear to be boorish and rigid. However, those who jest elegantly are called witty and versatile, as having a flexible character; for such movements appear to be characteristic of one's disposition. But just as a judgment is formed of bodies from their movements, so like—

wise in manners. Since, however, there is an abundance of the ridiculous, and most men delight in jests and mockery more than is proper, the scurrilous are also called witty, as if they were polite and pleasant men. But that they differ, and to a great degree, is evident from what has been said.

To the middle state, also, tact is appropriate. But it is the role of a tactful man to say and hear such things as are suitable for a worthy and liberal man; for there are certain things which it is fitting for such a <unclear>man</unclear> to say and hear in jest. And the jesting of a liberal man <unclear>differs</unclear> from that of a servile man, and again, the jesting of an educated man differs from that of an uneducated man. But the truth of this may be seen from both ancient and modern comedies; for in the former, the ridiculous consisted in obscenity, but in the latter, the hint of obscenity rather than bluntness excited laughter. These things, however, differ significantly regarding what is proper and elegant. Should he who jests well, therefore, be defined by this: that he says what is becoming for a liberal man to say? Or by this: that he does not cause pain, or even that he delights the hearer? Or shall we say that such a thing is indefinite? For different things are hateful or pleasing to different people. He will also hear things of this kind <note>namely, things which are adapted to a worthy and liberal man</note>; for such things as a man is willing to hear, those also he appears likely to do. He will not, therefore, do <note>or say</note> everything; for mockery is a kind of verbal abuse. Legislators, however, forbid certain types of verbal abuse; and perhaps it would be proper if they also forbade mockery. The elegant and liberal man, therefore, will conduct himself as if he were a law unto himself.

But the scurrilous man is overcome by his desire for the ridiculous, and spares neither himself nor others if he can provoke laughter. But of the middle states which pertain to pleasure, one indeed is concerned with jokes, while the other is concerned with the social interactions which belong to the rest of life.

## CHAPTER IX.

With respect to shame, it is not appropriate to speak of it as a specific virtue; for it resembles a passion more than a habit. It is defined, therefore, as a certain dread of infamy; and, similar to fear, it is concerned with dreadful things. For those who are under the influence of shame turn red, or blush; but those who have the fear of death upon them are pale. Hence, both of these appear to be in a certain respect physical; which seems rather to belong to passion than to habit. This passion, however, is not suited to every age, but specifically to youth. For we think it necessary that young persons should be bashful, because they commit many errors as a result of living by passion, but are restrained from committing them by shame. And we indeed praise bashful young men; but no one praises a bashful old man. For we think that he ought not to do anything for which he should be ashamed; for shame does not pertain to a worthy man, since it is produced by bad conduct; for the things which cause shame are not to be done. But it makes no difference whether some things are in reality base, while others are only base according to opinion; for neither of these are to be done; so that shame is not to be accepted. A thing of this kind also, namely, to do some-

### CHAPTER I.

Now, therefore, let us turn our attention to justice and injustice; and consider what kind of actions they are concerned with; what kind of mean justice is, and of what things the just is the mean. Let our inquiry be conducted according to the same method as the preceding discussions. We see, therefore, that all men are willing to call that kind of habit "justice" through which we practice just things, <note>or are inclined toward the works of justice</note> and through which we act justly and desire what is just. In the same way, injustice is that habit through which men act unjustly and desire what is un-

just. Hence, these things must first be outlined by us; since sciences, powers, and habits do not exist in the same way. For there is indeed the same power and the same science for opposites; but there is not the same habit for opposites. Thus, for instance, opposite actions are not performed by health, but only those which are healthy; for we say that a person walks in a healthy manner when they walk in the way a healthy person walks. Often, therefore, one opposite habit is known from its opposite; but often habits are known from the subjects that possess them. For if a good physical habit is apparent, a bad physical habit will also be apparent. And from the things which produce a good physical habit, this good habit will be known, and from this good habit its producing causes will be known. For if a good physical habit consists of density of the flesh, a bad physical habit will necessarily consist of thinness of the flesh; and whatever produces a good physical habit will be productive of density in the flesh. Generally, however, it follows that if one opposite is spoken of in many senses, the other will also be spoken of in many senses; <gloss>1</gloss> as, if "the just" is used in many ways, so also is "the unjust." But justice and injustice are spoken of in many senses, though this is hidden because these meanings are so closely related; it is not as obvious as it is when the meanings are very different. <gloss>2</gloss> For the difference in the specific idea or form is great. For instance, *κλεις* (*clavis*) is spoken of ho-

homonymously; for it signifies both that part which is under the neck of animals <note>the collar-bone</note>, and that by which gates are shut, <note>namely, a key.</note> We must consider, therefore, in how many ways a person is called unjust. It appears that the unjust man is one who acts illegally, one who takes for himself more external goods than he should <note>or who is avaricious</note>, and also one who is unequal <note>that is, one who takes for himself less of what is evil than is equitable</note>; so that it is evident that the just man will be one who acts legally, and one who is equal or equitable. The just, therefore, will be both the legal and the equal; but the unjust will be the illegal and the unequal. Since, however, the unjust man is avaricious, he will be concerned with good—yet not with every kind of good, but with that in which there is prosperous and adverse fortune; which is indeed always good in itself, but not always good for a specific person. But men pray for and pursue this kind of good, though they should not. For they should pray, indeed, that things which are good in themselves <note>such as riches, etc.</note> may also be good for them; but they should choose those things which are good for their possessor <note>such as virtue and wisdom.</note> The unjust man, however, does not always choose that which is more, but in things which are simply evil, he chooses the less. But because a lesser evil appears in a certain respect to be good, and there is a desire to possess more of what is good than is equitable, for this reason the unjust man appears to be avaricious. He is also unequal and acts illegally; for this very thing—acting illegally, or inequality—comprehends and is common to all injustice. Since, however, he who acts illegally is unjust, and he who acts legally is just, it is evident that everything which is legal is, in a certain respect, just. For the things

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of justice and injustice, transitioning from the definition of the unjust man as lawless or unfair to how laws, established by legislative science, determine what is just for the community.</meta>

# THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK V.

...which are defined by the legislative science are legal; and we say that each of these is just. But the laws speak about everything, aiming for what is advantageous in common to all men, or to the best of men, or to those in authority; and this is done either according to virtue or some other standard. Hence, in one sense, we call those things just which are capable of producing and preserving happiness, and its components, through political community. 

The law, however, ordains that the actions of the brave man should be performed—such as that a soldier shall not leave his rank, nor flee from the enemy, nor throw away his weapons. Likewise, it ordains that the actions of the temperate man shall be performed—such as not to commit adultery, nor behave with insolent wantonness. Also, it ordains those of the gentle man—such as not to strike another person, nor slander anyone. The law ordains similarly with respect to the other virtues and vices, partly commanding and partly forbidding; the law indeed does this rightly when it is rightly framed, but erroneously when it is rashly framed. 

This justice, therefore <note>that is, legal justice</note>, is indeed a perfect virtue—yet not in an absolute sense, but in relation to another person. And on this account, justice frequently appears to be the best of the virtues; neither the evening nor the morning star is so admirable. We likewise say proverbially, *Every virtue is comprehended in justice.* 

And legal justice is especially a perfect virtue because it is the exercise of perfect virtue. It is perfect because he who possesses it is also able to employ virtue toward another person, and not only toward himself. For many people are indeed able to employ virtue in their own affairs, but not in the affairs of others. And on this account, it appears to have been well said by Bias, that *authority reveals the man*; for he who governs...

<summary>Aristotle defines legal justice as the practice of virtue toward others as mandated by law for the common good. He concludes that justice is the greatest virtue because it requires the exercise of moral excellence in social and political relationships, not just in private life.</summary>
<keywords>legislative science, legal justice, happiness, political community, bravery, temperance, Bias, social virtue, authority</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the definition of justice as a "perfect virtue," specifically how it functions in relation to others within a community. It completes the thought that while many can be virtuous in their private lives, justice requires the exercise of virtue toward one's neighbor.</meta>

<page-num>155</page-num>

### CHAP. I. ETHICS.

...has a relation to another person, and is now involved in the communal life. For this very same reason also, justice alone, of all the virtues, appears to be a foreign good, because it relates to another person; since it performs what is advantageous to another, namely either to a ruler, or to the community at large. He, therefore, is the worst of characters who acts wickedly both toward himself and toward his friends; but he is the best of men—not he who acts virtuously toward himself, but he who acts virtuously toward another person; for this is a difficult task. This justice, therefore, is not a part of virtue, but is universal virtue; nor is the injustice which is contrary to it a part of vice, but universal vice. The difference, however, between virtue and this justice is evident from what has already been said; for it is indeed the same as virtue, but not essentially. For insofar as it relates to another person, it is justice; but insofar as it is a habit of a certain description, it is simply virtue.

<summary>Aristotle explains that justice is "universal virtue" because it involves the exercise of virtue toward others, making it a "foreign good" that benefits the community. He distinguishes this social justice from private virtue by noting that while they involve the same character traits, justice is specifically defined by its relation to other people.</summary>

### CHAPTER II.

We investigate, however, that justice which is a part of virtue; for there is, as we say, such a justice. In a similar manner, we investigate the injustice which is a part of vice. That there is such a justice is indicated by this: he who acts according to other vices behaves unjustly, indeed, but does not take for himself more of an external good than he should. Such, for instance, is the man who throws away his shield through cowardice; or he who speaks ill of another from bitterness; or he who does not give financial assistance to another, through stinginess. But when a man takes for himself more than he should, he frequently does not act according to any one of such vices, nor yet according to all the vices, but according to a certain depravity; for we blame him, specifically for injustice.

There is, therefore, a certain other injustice, which is a specific part of universal injustice; and there is a certain "unjust" thing, which is a part of the whole category of the unjust that is contrary to law. Furthermore, if one person should commit adultery for the sake of gain and should receive money for doing so, but another should pay money and suffer a loss in his property by doing it, because he is under the influence of...

<summary>Aristotle distinguishes between "universal injustice" (breaking the law) and "particular injustice" (taking more than one's fair share of goods). He argues that specific acts of injustice can occur even when other vices like cowardice or stinginess are absent, identifying a distinct form of moral depravity focused on unfair gain.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Nicomachean Ethics, particular justice, particular injustice, vice, virtue, greed, moral depravity, external goods</keywords>

# CHAP. II. ETHICS:

...influence of <insert>strong</insert> desire, the latter will indeed appear to be intemperate rather than one who takes more than he should; but the former will be unjust, but not intemperate. It is evident that he is not <note>intemperate</note>, because he acts with a view to gain. Again, in all other unjust deeds, there is always a reference to a certain depravity. For example, if a man commits adultery, the reference is to intemperance; if he abandons his post in battle, the reference is to cowardice; but if he strikes another person, it is to anger. If, however, he gains money by the act, the reference is to no other depravity than to injustice. Hence, it is evident that there is a certain other injustice which is classified as a part, besides universal injustice, and which is synonymous with it; because the definition of each belongs to the same genus. For both exercise their power in relation to another person. 

But the injustice which is classified as a part is concerned with honor, or riches, or safety—or if all these could be encompassed by one name—and it is concerned with them because of the pleasure which results from gain. Universal injustice, however, is concerned with all such things as a worthy man is concerned with <note>in the exercise of justice</note>. That there are several kinds of justice, therefore, and that there is a certain justice which is different from universal virtue, is evident. What it is, however, and what kind of thing it is, must be explained.

The unjust, therefore, has been distinguished by us into the illegal and the unequal; and the just into the legal and the equal. But the first kind of injustice of which we have spoken exists according to the illegal. Since, however, the illegal and the unequal are not the same but different, standing in the relation of a part to a whole; for

<summary>Aristotle distinguishes "particular injustice" (motivated by gain and unfairness) from "universal injustice" (illegal behavior in general). He argues that acts done for profit are specifically unjust, whereas acts done for passion or fear are attributed to other vices like intemperance or cowardice.</summary>

Everything unequal is illegal, but not everything which is illegal is unequal; hence, the unjust and injustice are not the same as these, but different from them, in the same way as parts relate to wholes. For this injustice is a part of the whole of injustice; and, in a similar way, this justice is a part of the whole of justice. We must, therefore, speak about the justice and injustice which function as parts, and in the same way about the partial just and unjust. 

We shall omit the justice and injustice which are organized according to universal virtue—where the former is the exercise of the whole of virtue and the latter the exercise of the whole of vice toward another person. It is, likewise, evident how the just and the unjust which are organized according to these are to be distinguished. For nearly all of those things which are legal are ordered by universal virtue. For the law orders men to live according to every virtue and forbids them from acting according to any one of the vices. 

But the <term>efficient causes</term> of the whole of virtue are those legal actions which are established by the laws for the purposes of public education <note>discipline</note>. Regarding the education <note>discipline</note> of an individual, however, through which he is simply a good man—whether it belongs to political science or another science—this will be determined later.<note>That is, in the third book of the *Politics*, chapter 4.</note> For perhaps it is not the same thing to be a good man and a good citizen.

For among contracts, some are voluntary, while others are involuntary. The voluntary contracts, indeed, are buying, selling, lending money at interest, suretyship, hiring anything out, pledging, and hiring <note>a slave or an artisan</note>. But these contracts are said to be voluntary because their origin is voluntary. And of involuntary contracts, some are clandestine, such as theft, adultery, witchcraft, prostitution, deceiving another person's slave, insidious murder, and bearing false witness. But the violent contracts are those such as blows,¹ imprisonment, death, robbery, mutilation, slander, and insult.

---

¹ The reader who is not an expert in the philosophy of Aristotle will undoubtedly be surprised that he ranks *blows* among *contracts*. But in order to resolve this apparent absurdity, it must be observed that *particular justice* is divided by Aristotle into the *distributive* and the *commutative*; and that the *commutative* is that which provides rectitude to contracts and exchanges, through which a thing is transferred from one person to another, and universally, to actions through which one person acts and another suffers. Under the word *συναλλάγματα*—that is, *contracts*—Aristotle, therefore, also encompasses exchanges of every description.

# THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK V.

## CHAPTER III.

Since, however, the unjust person is unequal, and the unjust is also part of the unequal, it is evident that there is a certain middle point of the unequal; and this is the equal. For in any action where there is a "more" and a "less," there is also an "equal." If, therefore, the unjust is unequal, the just must be equal; which is, indeed, obvious to all people without the need for complex reasoning. 

But since the equal is a middle point, the just will also be a certain middle point. The equal, however, involves at least two things. It is necessary, therefore, that the just—which is both a middle point and equal—should be related to a specific thing and to specific people. Insofar as it is a middle point, it relates to certain things (namely, the "more" and the "less"). Insofar as it is equal, it relates to two things. And insofar as it is the just, it relates to certain people. 

Hence, it is necessary that the just should involve at least four elements: for the persons to whom the just pertains are two, and the things in which it consists are two. And there will be the same equality between the persons to whom justice pertains and the things in which it consists; for as the relation of the former is to each other, such also is the relation of the latter. For if the

If people are not equal, they will not have equal things. Conflicts, however, and accusations originate from this: when either equal people do not obtain equal things, or those who are not equal receive an equal distribution of things. This is also evident from the principle of distribution according to merit; for everyone acknowledges that justice in distributions should be based on a certain merit. However, not everyone agrees on what that merit is; democratic people say that merit is liberty; among the oligarchs, some say it is wealth, while others say it is noble birth; but the aristocrats say it is virtue.

The just, therefore, is something proportional; for the proportional is not only a characteristic of monadic number <note>meaning number consisting of units</note>, but of number universally. <note>That is, of number applied to things, such as ten men, eight horses, etc.</note> For analogy or proportion is equality of ratio and consists of at least four terms. That discrete proportion consists of four terms is evident; and this is also the case with continuous proportion. For this uses one term as two; as, for instance, as *A* is to *B*, so is *B* to *C*. Hence, *B* is used twice; so that if *B* is placed twice, the proportional terms will be four.

But the just, also, consists of at least four things, and the reason is the same; for the people to whom justice is distributed and the things which are distributed are divided in the same way. As the term *A*, therefore, is to *B*, so will *C* be to *D*. And, therefore, alternately, as *A* is to *C*, so will *B* be to *D*. Hence, the whole will be compared with the whole, which the distribution joins together; and if they are thus combined, they will be justly joined. The conjunction, there—

<summary>Aristotle explains that distributive justice must be proportional to merit, though different political systems define merit differently (liberty, wealth, birth, or virtue). He uses mathematical ratios to demonstrate how justice balances the relationship between people and the goods they receive.</summary>

For the proportional is a mean, and the just is proportional.<sup>1</sup> Mathematicians, however, call such a proportion as this "geometrical"; for in geometrical proportion it happens that the whole is to the whole as each part is to its corresponding part. But this proportion is not continuous; for

---

<note>
1 Because distributive justice is a certain proportionality, it has certain properties of proportion. The first property is that things which are proportional to each other are also alternately proportional. Thus, because as 10 is to 5, so is 8 to 4, it will alternately be as 10 is to 8, so is 5 to 4. And this property also aligns with distributive justice. For suppose there are two persons, one of whom has labored for one month, and the other for two months; distributive justice in this case requires that if one pound in money is given to him who has labored for one month, two pounds should be given to him who has labored for two months. And then the ratio of him who has labored for two months to him who has labored for one month will be the same as two pounds to one pound. Hence, alternately, as he who has labored for two months is to two pounds, so is he who has labored for one month to one pound.

The second property is: when there is the same ratio of the first term to the second as of the third term to the fourth, there is also the same ratio of the first and third terms taken together as of the second and fourth taken together. Thus, if there is the same ratio of 10 to 5 as of 8 to 4, there is also the same ratio of 10 and 8 taken together to 5 and 4 taken together—namely, there is the same double ratio. For as 10 is to 5, or as 8 to 4, so is 18 to 9. This property also aligns with distributive justice. For the same ratio which he who has labored for two months has to two pounds in money, he who has labored for one month has to one pound; and the same ratio also exists between two persons who have labored for three months combined and three pounds. Distributive justice, therefore, distributes common goods so that as the persons are related to each other, so are the goods distributed to such persons; and as all the persons are taken together, so are all the things distributed collectively taken.
</note>

<summary>Aristotle explains that distributive justice functions through geometrical proportion, ensuring that the ratio of goods granted to individuals matches the ratio of their respective merit or labor. This justice, therefore, consists in proportion; but what is unjust is contrary to proportion. Consequently, one person has more and another less <note>than he should</note>; which also happens to be the case in actions. For the person who commits an injury has more good than he should, while the one who is injured has less. The opposite, however, occurs regarding evil; for a lesser evil stands in the place of a good when compared to a greater evil. For a lesser evil is more desirable than a greater one. But what is desirable is good, and that which is more desirable is a greater good. This, therefore, is one species of what is just.

# CHAPTER IV.

The other remaining species of justice is corrective, which deals with both voluntary and involuntary contracts. But the form of this justice is different from the previous type. For the justice that distributes common property <note>or things of a public nature</note> is always based on the proportion mentioned above. For if the distribution is made from collective property, it will follow the same ratio that the contributed items have to each other; and the injustice which is opposed to

<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK V.</header>

this justice is different from that proportion. What is just in contracts, however, is a certain equality, and the unjust is inequality; yet this is not according to geometrical, but arithmetical proportion. For it makes no difference whether a good man deprives a bad man of his property, or a bad man deprives a good man; nor whether a good or a bad man commits adultery. Instead, the law only looks at the nature of the injury and treats the parties as if they were equals, regardless of whether one has committed the injury and the other has suffered it, or whether one has done harm and the other has received it.

Therefore, because this injustice is unequal, the judge tries to make it equal. For when one man inflicts a blow and another is struck, or one man kills and another is killed, the suffering and the action are divided into unequal parts; but the judge, through the punishment he imposes, tries to produce equality by taking away from the gain. For in such matters—though the name may not be perfectly appropriate for everything—the injury is called "gain" and the suffering of the injury is called "loss." But when the harm is measured, one is called loss and the other gain.

Therefore, the equal is the middle point <note>the mean</note> between the "more" and the "less." Regarding loss and gain, however, one is "more" and the other is "less" in opposite ways; for having more of what is good and less of what is evil is gain, and the opposite is loss. The middle point between them is the equal, which we call the just. Thus, corrective justice will be the middle point between loss and gain. Consequently, when people have disputes with one another <note>regarding legal affairs</note>, they turn to the judge; and to go to a judge is to go to justice. For a judge is intended to be, as it were, justice personified. They also seek a judge who is a middle point <note>a mediator</note>; and some persons

# CHAP. IV. ETHICS.

...call judges mediators, as if they would obtain justice if they reached the middle point <note>the mean</note>. Therefore, what is just is a certain middle point, since the judge is also a mediator. But the judge creates equality; as if a line were cut into unequal parts, he takes away from the larger section that amount by which it exceeds the half, and adds it to the smaller section. When, however, the whole is divided into two equal parts, then people say they have what is their own, because they have obtained the equal share. But the equal is the middle point between the greater and the less according to arithmetical proportion. <note>1. Any line, or any number, may be divided into unequal parts. *But between unequal lines and numbers, both an arithmetical and a geometrical mean may be obtained. An arithmetical mean, therefore, is that which exceeds the smaller quantity, not in the same ratio by which it is exceeded by the greater, but by the same quantity. However, a geometrical mean is that which exceeds the smaller quantity in the same ratio, but not by the same quantity, by which it is exceeded by the greater.* Thus, for instance, the arithmetical mean between the numbers 9 and 3 is 6, because 6 exceeds 3 as much as it is exceeded by 9; yet not in the same ratio. For 6 exceeds 3 in a double ratio, but is exceeded by 9 in a sesquialter ratio; since 9 contains 6 once, plus half of 6 besides. On the contrary, 6 is a geometrical mean between 3 and 12, because it is exceeded by 12 not by the same quantity, but by the same ratio by which 6 exceeds 3. It is evident that it exceeds and is exceeded in the same ratio. For as 6 exceeds 3 in a double ratio, so, likewise, it is exceeded by 12 in a double ratio.

Hence, if a line of 12 inches is divided unequally into two parts, one consisting of 9 and the other of 3 inches, an arithmetical mean must be found, which is a line of 6 inches. By this middle line, therefore, the unequal parts may be reduced to an equality. For if from the larger part (meaning the line of 9 inches) the amount by which it exceeds the middle line of 6 inches is taken away—that is, if 3 inches are taken away and added to the smaller part, or the line of 3 inches—those two lines will become equal, and the whole line of 12 inches will be divided into two equal</note>

This restoration is described through the mathematical concept of arithmetical proportion, illustrated by dividing lines and numbers into equal sections.</summary>

->THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK V.<-

Hence, also, the just (δικαιον) is named so because it is divided into two equal parts (οτι διχα εστιν), just as if it were said to be διχαιον; and a judge is called δικαστης, as if he were διχαστης, or one who divides a thing into two equal parts. For if, when two things are equal, that which is taken from one is added to the other, the latter will exceed what the former then becomes by two such parts. For if what is taken away from one of the equal things were not added to the other, the one would exceed the other by only one such part. The thing, therefore, to which something is added exceeds the mean by one part; and the mean, also, exceeds by one part that from which something is taken away. By this, therefore, we may know what ought to be taken away from him who has more, and what ought to be added to him who has less. For it is necessary to add to him who has less the amount by which the mean exceeds his share, but to take away from the greatest share that amount by which it exceeds the mean. Let there be three lines AA, BB, and CC, equal to each other. From AA let AE be taken and added to CC, and let that part be CD. Hence, the whole line DCC will exceed the line AE

<column-break/>

by two parts. In a similar manner, an unequal division takes place in contracts. For one person gains and receives more—for instance, 900*l.*; but another loses and receives less—for instance, 300*l.* The judge, however, so that he may reduce the contract to an equality, ought to find the arithmetical mean between the more and the less—i.e., between 900*l.* and 300*l.*—which mean is 600*l.* But having found this mean, he ought to take from him who has more that amount by which he exceeds it, and give it to him who has less; for thus the loss and gain will be equalized and divided in half, so that each will have 600*l.* And this is commutative justice.

By taking the excess from the gainer and giving it to the loser, the judge establishes the arithmetical mean, which constitutes commutative justice.</summary>

This also happens in other arts; for they would be undermined unless the party that receives an action suffers the same in quantity and quality as the party that acts performs. But these terms, loss and gain, are derived from voluntary contracts. For when a person obtains more than their own, they are said to gain; but when they have less than what they had at first, they are said to have lost—as in buying and selling, and other such things that the law permits. When, however, people have neither more nor less, but give exactly as much as they receive, they are said to have what is their own, and neither to lose nor gain. Therefore, justice is the mean of a certain gain and loss in things which are not voluntary, so that each of those who form a contract may have as much afterward as they had before.

¹ <note>Suppose each of the three equal lines AA, BB, and CC is 10 inches. Then, if one inch is taken from AA and added to BB, all the lines will become unequal, so that AA will be 9 inches, BB will be 11 inches, and CC will be 10 inches. Hence, CC will be an arithmetical mean between AA (which was reduced) and BB (which was increased)—namely, between 9 and 11. If, therefore, one inch is taken away from BB (the 11 inches by which it exceeds the mean of 10 inches), and that one inch is added back to AA (the 9 inches), all three will have 10 inches. Therefore, all will be equal to each other and to the mean.</note>

THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK V.

### CHAPTER V.

To some people, however, retaliation seems to be simply just, and this was also the opinion of the Pythagoreans; for they defined justice simply as retaliation. But retaliation is suited neither to distributive nor to corrective justice, even though Rhadamanthus <note>in Aeschylus</note> seems to assert that justice is this: "if a man suffers the same thing that he has done, the judgment will be equitable." For retaliation is frequently discordant. For instance, if a magistrate strikes a man, it is not appropriate for the man to strike him in return; and if someone strikes a magistrate, he ought not only to be struck back but also to be punished more severely. Furthermore, there is a great difference between voluntary and involuntary actions. But commercial trade is preserved by this kind of justice, provided the retaliation is based on proportion and not on equality. For the unity of a city is made permanent by proportional retaliation. For people either endeavor to return evil for evil—as it seems like slavery if they cannot retaliate—or they wish to be benefited in return when they benefit others. If this does not happen, there is no exchange, by which the permanent bond of society is formed. Hence, the tem-

It is necessary, therefore, that the builder of the house should receive from the shoemaker his work, and give his own work to him in return. It is necessary, therefore, that these should be equalized. This also takes place in the other arts; for they would be destroyed unless that which is passive received the same in quantity and quality as the agent produces. For the community of society is not produced from two physicians, but from a physician and a farmer, and in short, from different and not from equal characters; but it is necessary that these should be equalized. Hence, it is required that all things for which there is an exchange should be capable of being compared with each other; and for this purpose money

<page-num>170</page-num>

...was adopted and becomes, in a certain respect, a measure. For it measures all things, so that it likewise measures excess and defect; and it therefore determines how many shoes are equal to a house, or to food. It is necessary, therefore, that just as the ratio of a house-builder is to a shoemaker, so should be the ratio of the number of shoes to a house, or to food. For if this does not take place, there will be neither exchange nor community. And it will not take place unless the things being compared are, in a certain respect, equal. Hence, it is necessary, as has been observed before, that all things should be measured by one certain thing; and this is, in reality, need <note>Translated as "indigence" in the original text to represent the Greek *chreia*.</note>, which connects all things. For if mankind were not in want of anything, or if they were not in want in a similar way, either there would be no exchange, or it would not be the same. But money was adopted by agreement as a substitute for the exchange of needs; and for this reason money was called *nomisma* (νόμισμα), because it is not established by nature, but by law (νόμῳ); and it is in our power to change it and render it useless. Reciprocity, therefore, will take place when an equalization occurs. Hence, as the farmer is to the shoemaker, so is the work of the shoemaker to the work of the farmer. But it is necessary to bring them to the form of proportion when an exchange takes place; for without this, one of the extremes will have both excesses. When, however, each person has his own, they will thus be equal and communicate with each other, because this equality can be produced among them. Let the farmer be **A**, the food **C**, and the work of the shoemaker, being equal to the food, be **D**.

<summary>Aristotle explains that money serves as a universal measure that allows different goods and services to be compared and exchanged fairly according to a proportional ratio of need and labor. But without this reciprocity <note>The original uses "retaliation" to mean reciprocal exchange.</note>, there would be no social community. It is evident that need <unclear/>, however, connects everything as a single factor; for when men do not need each other—either both of them or just one—no exchange takes place, as it does when one is in need of what the other possesses; for instance, wine, for which an export of grain is granted. It is necessary, therefore, that this should be made equal. However, for the sake of future exchange—if nothing should be needed at present, so that it may be obtained when it is wanted—money serves as a kind of guarantee for us. For it is necessary that he who brings money should be able to receive what he needs in exchange for it. Money, therefore, is also subject to this same fluctuation; for it does not always maintain an equal value, though at the same time it is more stable. Hence, it is necessary that all things should be valued; for in this way, there will always be exchange, and where there is exchange, there will be community. Money, therefore, acting as a measure, makes things commensurate and thus equalizes them. For there would be no community without exchange, no exchange without equality, and no equality without common measurement. In reality, therefore, it is impossible for things that differ so much to become truly commensurate; but for the purpose of satisfying needs, this is sufficiently possible. Hence, it is necessary that there should be one specific thing used as a measure, and this is established by agreement. Hence, it is called <term>nomisma</term> (νομισμα) or money. For this causes all things to be commensurate, since all things are measured by money.

<meta>This page continues the demonstration of how money functions as a universal medium for equating the value of dissimilar goods, such as houses and beds, to facilitate fair exchange. It also begins a formal definition of justice as a proportional medium between excess and deficiency.</meta>

<page-num>172</page-num>
<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK V.</header>

Let a house be A, ten minae B, and a bed C. A, therefore, will be the half of B, if the house is worth five minae, or is equal to the value of five minae. But let the bed C be the tenth part of B. It is evident, therefore, how many beds are equal in value to the house, namely five. That such, however, was the exchange before there was money, is manifest; for it makes no difference whether five beds, or the equivalent value of five beds, are given for the house. Thus, therefore, we have shown what both the unjust and the just are.

But these things being determined, it is evident that a just action is a medium between doing and receiving an injury; for the former is to have more, but the latter less <note>than is just.</note> Justice, however, is a medium, not in the same manner as the previous virtues, but because it pertains to a medium¹ <note>between the more and the less;</note> whereas injustice pertains to extremes. And justice, indeed, is that by which a just man is said to act justly from deliberate choice, and to distribute justice both to himself, in making an agreement with another person, and to another who makes an agreement with another; yet not so as to attribute more of what is desirable to himself and less to his neighbor, and the opposite of that which is hurtful, but so as to distribute what is equal <note>to himself and others</note> according to analogy. And

---

<note>¹ For the other virtues are certain means, because they are mediums between two vices, one of which errs through excess and the other through deficiency. On the contrary, justice is not a medium between two vices in this manner; rather, it is opposed to injustice alone, which errs by giving to one person more and to another less, but gives to neither what is equal.</note>

<page-num>173</page-num>

he behaves in the same way toward another person who enters into a contract with someone else. Injustice, on the other hand, is that by which an unjust man is said to act unjustly from deliberate choice, and to distribute injustice both to himself and others; but this is the excess and deficiency of that which is beneficial or harmful, contrary to proportion. Hence, injustice is excess and deficiency, because it pertains to excess and deficiency. To the unjust man himself, it is an excess of that which is simply beneficial, but a deficiency of that which is harmful; but to others, he distributes in an entirely similar manner; and in whatever way the distribution happens to be made, it is contrary to proportion. In an unjust action, however, the lesser extreme is to be injured, and the greater is to injure. In this manner, therefore, we have discussed justice and injustice, and have shown the nature of each; and similarly, we have discussed the just and the unjust in a general sense.

The manner in which retaliation relates to justice has been shown by us before. It is necessary, however, not to be ignorant of the fact that what we are currently investigating is both "simply just" and "politically just." This type of justice exists among people living together in society—free and equal men, either proportionally or numerically—with a view toward providing the necessities of life. Hence, those among whom these conditions are not found do not have political justice toward each other, but only a certain kind of justice that exists according to a resemblance to political justice. 

For justice exists among those who also have law; and law exists among those with whom there is potential for injustice. For justice is the judgment of what is just and what is unjust. But where there is injustice, there is also the act of behaving unjustly; yet among all those who act unjustly, there is not necessarily injustice <note>as a settled character trait</note>. Injustice consists in a man allotting to himself more of what is inherently good, and less of what is inherently evil <note>than he should</note>. 

Therefore, we do not allow a man to govern, but rather reason; because a man does this for his own benefit <note>that is, he distributes to himself more of what is good and less of what is evil</note> and becomes a tyrant. ### CHAPTER VII.

Regarding political justice, however, one kind is natural, while the other is legal. Natural justice is that which has the same power everywhere, and this is not because it seems or does not seem to be justice to some. But legal justice is that which, originally, is a matter of indifference—whether it is established in this or that way—but once it is established, it becomes a matter of importance. Examples include the rule that captives shall be ransomed for one mina;¹ or that a goat shall be sacrificed instead of two sheep.² Furthermore, this includes such laws as are proclaimed concerning specific details, such as the decree that sacrifices shall be offered to Brasidas,³ and whatever is established by public decrees. To some people, however, all political justice seems to be of this kind, because that which has a natural existence is

---

¹ The Lacedaemonians and Athenians, during the Peloponnesian War, agreed that the captives on both sides should be ransomed for one mina.
² The Thebans in Egypt established a law that a goat should be sacrificed to Jupiter, and not two sheep. See the second book of Herodotus.
³ The Amphipolitans ordered sacrifices to be offered to Brasidas, the Lacedaemonian king, who fell fighting bravely in the Peloponnesian War.

<summary>Aristotle distinguishes between natural justice, which is universal, and legal justice, which consists of specific conventions and decrees that vary by state. He provides historical examples of legal justice, such as ransom amounts and specific religious sacrifices.</summary>

This, however, is not entirely, but only partially the case; though perhaps with the gods*, it is by no means to be admitted <note>that justice is mutable</note>; but with us there is something which is naturally mutable, though not everything. The same distinction, likewise, will be applied to other things. For the measures of wine and corn are not everywhere equal; but with those who buy wine and corn they are greater, and with those who sell them they are less. ### CHAPTER VIII.

Since, therefore, the things that are just and unjust are those which we have enumerated, a man then actually commits an injustice, or acts justly, when he acts voluntarily; but when he acts involuntarily, he neither commits an injustice nor acts justly, except by accident. For it happens that the things which he does are either just or unjust; but a deed unjustly done and a just action are defined by the voluntary and the involuntary; for when an action is voluntary, it is blamed; and at the same time, it is only then a deed unjustly done. Hence, there will be something unjust which is not yet a deed unjustly done, unless the voluntary element is added to it. But I define the voluntary, indeed,

--- Page 188 ---
<meta>This page continues the definition of voluntary action in relation to justice and injustice, specifically detailing the criteria of knowledge and power that distinguish voluntary deeds from those done by accident or compulsion.</meta>

<page-num>179</page-num>
# CHAP. VIII. ETHICS.

<warning>Ink blots obscure words in the lower half of the page.</warning>

As has been observed before, a voluntary action is that which a man does among the things that are in his power to do knowingly, and not ignorantly—namely, not being ignorant of the circumstances of the action. For instance, he must know who it is he strikes, what he strikes with, and for what reason; and he must do this neither by accident nor by compulsion. This would be the case, for example, if someone took his hand and struck another person with it. In that case, he would not strike willingly, because it was not in his power to avoid giving the blow. 

It may happen, however, that the person who is struck is a father; but the one who strikes him may merely know that he is a man, or someone who is present, while being ignorant that it is his father. A similar distinction must also be made regarding the purpose for which a thing is done, and concerning the action as a whole. Hence, that which is not known, or which is known indeed but is not in the power of the one who acts, or which he is compelled to do, is done involuntarily. For we both do and undergo many things that occur naturally while knowing they are happening, yet none of these is either voluntary or involuntary—such as growing old or dying. 

That which is accidental, however, <unclear>similarly</unclear> takes place in things unjust and just. For <unclear>if</unclear> a man returns a deposit unwillingly and out of fear, he cannot be said to either perform a just deed or to act justly, except by accident. In a similar manner, he <unclear>who</unclear>, out of compulsion and unwillingly, does not return a deposit, must be said to be unjust and to do an unjust <unclear>deed</unclear> by accident. But of voluntary actions, some indeed we perform with prior choice, and others without prior choice; those with prior choice are such as have been the subjects of previous deliberation, while those without it are such as have not been previously deliberated upon.

<vocab>Ethics, voluntary, involuntary, compulsion, accident, deliberation, choice, justice</vocab>

<summary>Aristotle defines voluntary actions as those done with knowledge and within one's power, distinguishing them from involuntary acts caused by ignorance, compulsion, or natural necessity. He argues that the moral quality of an act (as just or unjust) depends on whether it was intentional or merely accidental.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the examination of justice in action, moving from the definition of voluntary and involuntary acts to a specific classification of the types of harm that occur in social interactions.</meta>

<page-num>180</page-num>
# THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK V.

Since, therefore, there are three kinds of harm in social interaction—those accompanied by ignorance are errors, occurring when a person does not understand who the injured person is, nor the method, the instrument, nor the purpose for which the harm is done. For in this case, he will think either that *he* has not struck the person, or not with this instrument, or not this person, or not for this reason, but that something else happened differently from what he expected. For example, one person may strike another not for the purpose of wounding him, but of prodding him, and in so doing may accidentally wound him; or he may not strike the person whom he intended to strike, or not in the way he intended. When, therefore, harm is done unintentionally, it is a misfortune; but when it is done not unintentionally, yet without malice, it is an error. For a person errs when the source of the cause is within himself; but he is unfortunate when the source is external to him. When, however, harm is done knowingly, but without previous deliberation, it is an unjust act; as for instance, whatever happens to men through anger, or other passions which are necessary or natural. For those who injure others and err through the influence of these passions act indeed unjustly, and their deeds are unjust; nevertheless, they are not yet "unjust" people on account of these actions, nor are they depraved, for the harm which they did was not through depravity. But when a person injures another from deliberate choice, he is unjust and depraved. Hence, those deeds which are the effect of anger are rightly judged not to be the result of previous design. For the source of action is not in the person who is angry, but in the person who provoked his anger. Again, [when one person hurts another out of anger] there is no controversy about the

<summary>Aristotle distinguishes between three levels of harm: misfortune (accidental), error (intentional but without malice), and unjust acts (done through passion or deliberation). He concludes that while acts of passion are unjust, only harm committed through deliberate choice defines a person as depraved or truly unjust.</summary>
<keywords>social interaction, misfortune, error, unjust acts, deliberation, anger, depravity, voluntary action, Aristotle, Ethics</keywords>

### CHAP. VIII. ETHICS.

deed, as to whether it was done, but about the justice of it; for anger is excited on account of apparent injustice. For here there is no controversy about the existence of the thing, as there is in contracts, in which it is necessary that one of the contractors should be a depraved character, unless his conduct is the effect of forgetfulness; but acknowledging the fact, they dispute the justice of it. He, however, who hurts another person deliberately, is not ignorant of the deed. Hence, the one of these thinks he is injured, but the other thinks he is not. But he who does harm to another person from deliberate choice, acts unjustly; and he who injures another, according to those deeds which are performed unjustly, is unjust when he acts contrary to proportion, or to what is equal. In a similar manner also, he is just when he acts justly from previous choice; but he acts justly if he only acts voluntarily. Of involuntary actions, however, some deserve to be forgiven, but others do not. For such involuntary errors as are not only committed in ignorance, but also through ignorance, deserve to be forgiven; but such as are not committed through ignorance, but in a state of ignorance, yet from a passion that is neither natural nor human, do not deserve to be forgiven.

<vocab>justice, injustice, anger, contracts, character, deliberate choice, proportion, involuntary actions, ignorance, passion</vocab>
