### CHAPTER IX.

It may, however, be questioned whether we have sufficiently distinguished between being injured and committing an injury. In the first place, consider if the matter is as Euripides asserts when he absurdly says, “To speak briefly, I may kill my mother, both of us being willing; or I being unwilling, and she willing.” For is it true or not that a person can be willingly injured? Or is everyone injured involuntarily, in the same way that everyone who commits an injury does so voluntarily? Or do some persons suffer an injury voluntarily, and others involuntarily? And a similar inquiry may also be made with respect to obtaining justice; for to act justly is entirely a voluntary thing. Hence, being injured and obtaining justice are appropriately contrasted with each other, so that they are either voluntary or involuntary. It may, however, seem absurd if obtaining justice were always voluntary; for some persons obtain justice against their will. And this also may be questioned: whether everyone who suffers something unjust is "injured," or whether it is the same in suffering as it is in acting? For it is possible in both cases to obtain what is just by accident. And it is evident that the same may also take place in unjust things. For it is not the same thing to do unjust things and to commit an

<summary>This chapter questions whether a person can be injured voluntarily and investigates the relationship between voluntary action and suffering an injury or receiving justice. It suggests that just as one can act justly or unjustly by accident, one can also receive justice or suffer injustice by accident.</summary>

<keywords>Euripides, justice, injury, voluntary, involuntary, Nicomachean Ethics, agency, suffering injustice</keywords>

--- Page 192 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into whether an individual can be voluntarily injured, specifically addressing the distinction between suffering an injustice and being the victim of an injury.</meta>

### CHAP. IX. ETHICS.

injury; nor is it the same thing to suffer unjust things and to be injured. The same also takes place in acting justly and obtaining justice. For it is impossible to be injured unless there is someone who does the injury; or to obtain justice, unless there is someone who acts justly. 

But if to do an injury is simply to hurt someone willingly, and to hurt willingly is to do so knowing the person who is hurt, the instrument, and the manner in which he is hurt; and if the intemperate man willingly hurts himself; if this be the case, he will be voluntarily injured, and it will be possible for a man to injure himself. This, however, is also one of the things that are dubious: whether it is possible for a man to injure himself. Furthermore, a man may voluntarily, through intemperance, be injured by another person; so that it will be possible for a man to be injured voluntarily. 

Or shall we say that the definition <note>which we have given of doing an injury, namely, that it is to hurt someone voluntarily</note> is not right, but we must add the words, *to hurt, knowing the person who is hurt, the instrument, and the manner in which he is hurt, contrary to his will?* A man, therefore, may be hurt and suffer unjust things willingly; but no one is willingly injured. For no one wishes to be injured, not even the intemperate man; instead, he acts contrary to his will. For no one wishes for that which he does not believe to be good; yet the intemperate man does that which he does not think ought to be done. But he who gives what is his own—as Homer says Glaucus gave to Diomed:

> For Diomed’s brass arms of mean device,
> For which nine oxen paid, a vulgar price,
> He gave his own of gold divinely wrought,
> A hundred oxen the shining purchase bought.

<summary>This page explores the logical requirements for being "injured," concluding that while a person may suffer harm or lose property voluntarily (like Glaucus), one cannot be "injured" in a legal or moral sense against their own will. It distinguishes between willingly suffering an unjust act and being the victim of a formal injury, which requires the act to be against the victim's will.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on whether a person can be voluntarily injured, specifically looking at the role of the distributor in an unjust distribution.</meta>

# THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK V.

...is not injured; for it is in his power to give <note>or not to give.</note> But to be injured is not in his power; it is necessary that the person should exist by whom the injury is done. Concerning being injured, therefore, it is evident that it is not voluntary.

Of those things, however, which we proposed to discuss, two particulars remain to be explained: whether he who distributes to another person more than they deserve does an injury, or the person who receives the distribution. For if what we before observed is possible, and he who distributes—but not he who possesses more—does the injury, then if anyone distributes to another more than to himself, knowingly and willingly, he will be injuring himself; which humble men seem to do. For a virtuous man distributes less to himself than to others. Or shall we say that this matter is not so simple? For he who distributes less to himself than to others <note>of certain good things</note> will claim for himself more of some other good, if it should so happen; as for instance, renown, or that which is simply noble in conduct. Again, the doubt is also resolved by the definition of doing an injury; for he who does it suffers nothing contrary to his will. Hence he is not, on this account, injured; but even admitting that he is, he is only hurt. It is also evident that he who distributes <note>more than the receiver deserves</note> does an injury, but not the receiver. For it is not the person in whom injustice resides who does the injury, but the one for whom doing this is voluntary; this is the man from whom the principle of the action proceeds, which is in the distributor, but not in the receiver.

Furthermore, since to act is expressed in many ways, and

### CHAP. IX. ETHICS.

inanimate things kill in a certain respect—the hand as well as the servant by the command of his master; these indeed do not act injuriously, but they perform unjust things. Again, if a man judges while being truly ignorant, he does not commit an injury according to what is legally just, nor is his judgment unjust in a legal sense, yet in a certain respect it is unjust. For what is legally just differs from primary justice <note>or that which has a natural existence</note>. But if he should judge unjustly knowingly, he will claim for himself more of either favor or vengeance. Therefore, just as if someone should share in a deed unjustly done, so also he who judges unjustly for these reasons will gain more; for in those cases, he who awards a field to another person receives in return not a field, but money.

People, however, are of the opinion that it is in their power to commit an injury, and that for this reason it is easy to be just. But it is not so; for to have relations with the wife of a neighbor, to strike another person, and to give money with the hand are easy things and within the power of those who do them; but to do these things with a certain disposition of mind <note>To be unjust, it is not enough merely to commit an injury; it is also necessary that it be done with readiness and delight. For this disposition of mind, habit is likewise required, which is not acquired without difficulty and a long period of time.</note> is neither easy nor within the power of those who do them. In a similar manner, the common people imagine that there is no wisdom required in knowing what is just and what is unjust, because it is not difficult to understand those things of which the laws speak. These things, however, are not just, except by accident; they are only just when they are performed in a certain manner and distributed in a certain manner. But this is a greater task than

<summary>Aristotle argues that while individual unjust acts are easy to perform, being truly unjust or just requires a specific disposition of mind and long-term habit. For there, indeed, it is easy to know honey and wine, and hellebore, and burning and cutting; but how it is necessary to distribute these in order to produce health, and to whom and when they are to be distributed, is as great a task as being a physician. For this very reason, the multitude imagines that it is no less the role of a just <note>than of an unjust</note> man to commit an injury, because the just man is no less, but is even more, able to do each of these things <note>than the unjust man</note>. For according to them, a just man may have relations with the wife of another man and may strike another person; and a brave man may throw away his shield and, taking to flight, may run wherever he pleases. 

To act cowardly, however, and to commit an injury, is not merely to do these things (except by accident), but it consists in doing them with a certain disposition of mind <note>that is, with promptness and delight</note>; just as performing the duties of a physician and restoring health does not merely consist in cutting or not cutting, or in giving or not giving medicine, but in doing these things in a certain manner. But just things exist among those who share in things that are simply good, and in these things there is also excess and deficiency. For to some beings, perhaps to the gods, justice is not a good because in them there is no excess <note>or deficiency</note>; but to others, such as men who are incurable and vicious, no part of things simply good is beneficial, but all of them are harmful; and to others, they are useful to a certain extent. For this reason, justice is a human good.

He concludes that justice is a uniquely human good because it regulates the distribution of things that can be excessive or deficient, a condition that does not apply to the gods.</summary>

## CHAPTER X.

It now follows that we should speak concerning equity and the equitable, and show how equity indeed relates to justice, and the equitable to the just; for to those who consider rightly, the equitable appears to be neither simply the same as, nor yet different in genus from, the just. At times, we praise the equitable and the man of equity, so much so that when we apply this name to other things, we praise a person by calling him *more equitable* instead of simply *a good* man, showing by this that it is a better term. But at other times, to those who follow reason, it appears absurd that the equitable should be praiseworthy if it is something different from the just. For either the just is not a worthy thing, or the equitable is not just if it is different from the just; or, if they are both worthy things, they are both the same. The doubt concerning the equitable arises almost entirely from these details. All of these points, however, are in a certain way correct, and there is nothing in them that is contrary or adverse to itself. For the equita-

<summary>This page begins a discussion on the relationship between justice and equity, exploring the logical difficulty of how equity can be both distinct from justice and yet highly praiseworthy. The author suggests that while they seem different, they are not mutually exclusive or contradictory concepts.</summary>
<keywords>equity, justice, the equitable, ethics, Aristotle, moral philosophy, virtue</keywords>

--- Page 197 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous chapter regarding the relationship between justice and equity, specifically addressing why the law requires correction in particular cases where a universal rule does not perfectly apply.</meta>

<page-num>188</page-num>
<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK V.</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

ble being something that is just, is a better form of justice; and it is not better than the just as if it were some other category. The just, therefore, and the equitable are the same thing; and both of them being worthy things, the equitable is the more excellent of the two. A doubt, however, still remains: although the equitable is indeed just, it is not legally just, but is a correction of legal justice. The cause of this is that every law is universal, yet it cannot speak universally with accuracy about certain particular cases. In those things, therefore, in which it is necessary to speak universally, but in which this cannot be done correctly, the law assumes that which happens for the most part, not being ignorant of the error which has been committed. And in doing so, it acts no less rightly; for the fault is not in the law, nor in the legislator, but in the nature of the subject; for such is the inherent nature of matters pertaining to human action. When the law, therefore, speaks universally, and something happens outside of that universal rule, it is right to correct what the legislator has omitted and the error which he has committed by speaking in general terms—since the legislator himself would adopt such a correction if he were present, and would have legally established this if he had known of it. Hence, the equitable is just, and is better than a certain kind of justice. It is not, however, better than what is absolutely just, but it is better than the justice which errs by speaking in simple <note>and general</note> terms. And this is the nature of the equitable: it is a correction of law where law is deficient because it speaks universally. For this is the reason why all things are not according to law: because concerning certain things it is impossible to establish a law. Hence, a decree is necessary; for the rule for what is indefinite is itself indefinite, just as the rule used in Lesbian building <note>This refers to a flexible leaden rule used by builders in Lesbos to measure curved surfaces.</note>

**FROM** what has been said, it is also evident whether it is possible for a man to injure himself or not. For there are some just things established by law which pertain to the whole of virtue. Thus, for instance, the law does not command a man to destroy himself; and it forbids what

Again, when one man hurts another contrary to law, who has not hurt him, he commits an injury voluntarily; and a person commits an injury voluntarily when they know the person whom they injure, the instrument they use, and the manner in which they do it. But he who destroys himself through anger does this voluntarily, contrary to right reason, which the law does not permit. Hence, he commits an injury; but to whom? Is it not to the city, rather than to himself? For he suffers voluntarily, but no one is voluntarily the victim of an injustice. Therefore, the city also punishes him, and a certain disgrace is attached to the one <unclear>who</unclear> destroys himself, as one who injures the city. Furthermore, it is not possible for a man to injure himself in the specific way that makes a person "unjust," when they only act unjustly without being entirely depraved; for that character is distinct from total wickedness. For the unjust man is depraved in a specific respect, just as the timid man is, but he does not possess the whole of depravity. Therefore, he does not injure himself according to this type of wickedness; for if he did, the same thing might be taken away and added at the same time to the same person, but this is impossible. It is, however, necessary that the just and the unjust should always exist in more than one person. Again, he who commits an injury does it voluntarily, from deliberate choice, and with priority in time. For he who injures another because he has been injured by them does not appear to act unjustly; but he who injures himself suffers and performs the same acts at the same time. Furthermore, it would imply that a man could be injured voluntarily. To which may be added that no one commits an injury without some particular species of injustice; but no one commits adultery with his own wife, nor does anyone break through his own wall, nor commit a theft on his own property. In short, the

<summary>Aristotle argues that it is impossible to commit an injustice against oneself because injustice requires a voluntary act against an unwilling victim, and involves more than one person. He notes that suicide is considered an injury to the city (the state) rather than the individual, which is why the law punishes it through disgrace.</summary>

The impossibility that a man should injure himself is evident from the conclusions we have made regarding being voluntarily injured. It is likewise evident that both being injured and committing an injury are bad; for the one is to have less than the mean, and the other more—in the same manner as what is healthy in medicine and what contributes to a good physical condition in the art of gymnastics. At the same time, however, it is worse to commit an injury <note>than to be injured.</note> For committing an injury is accompanied by vice and is blameworthy—specifically, a vice that is either complete and absolute, or nearly so. For not everything that is voluntary is accompanied by injustice, but being injured occurs without vice or injustice. Essentially, therefore, it is less bad to be injured than to commit an injury; yet nothing prevents it from being a greater evil by accident. Art, however, pays no attention to this; it says that pleurisy is a more serious disease than a lame foot, though it may happen that the latter is a greater evil than the former—for example, if a man, because he is lame, should fall and thus be captured by enemies and put to death. Metaphorically speaking, however, and by analogy, a man as a whole is not just toward himself as a whole, but one part of him is just toward another part. This is not according to every kind of justice, but according to despotic or economic justice; for in these discussions, it must be admitted that the rational part of the soul differs from the irrational part. And if we look at these, it appears that there is a certain injustice of a man toward himself, because it is possible in these parts for a man to suffer something contrary to his own desires. As, therefore, between a governor and the governed,

## CHAPTER II.

THERE are, however, three things in the soul which have dominion over action and truth: namely, sense, intellect, and appetite. Of these, sense is the principle of no action; which is evident from this, that brutes have, indeed, sensation, but have no share in action <note>so as to have dominion over it.</note>. What, however, affirmation and negation are in the discursive energy of the rational part, pursuit and avoidance are in appetite. <note>For this pertains to beings who are capable of deliberate choice.</note>

<summary>This page defines the scientific and ratiocinative parts of the rational soul, explaining that deliberation only applies to things that can be otherwise. It also identifies sensation, intellect, and appetite as the three drivers of truth and action, noting that animals lack the capacity for deliberate action.</summary>
<keywords>scientific part, ratiocinative part, deliberation, soul, sensation, intellect, appetite, virtue, deliberate choice, ethics</keywords>

--- Page 205 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the rational soul, focusing on the relationship between practical reason, moral virtue, and deliberate choice.</meta>

<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK VI.</header>

Therefore, since moral virtue is a habit accompanied by deliberate choice, and deliberate choice is a desire aligned with deliberation, it is necessary that the reasoning be true and the desire be right if the deliberate choice is to be good, and that the one should assert and the other should pursue the same things. This reasoning activity of the soul, therefore, <note>by which we judge that something is to be desired</note> is practical reason and practical truth. However, regarding the reasoning activity of the mind which is contemplative—and neither practical nor productive—the good and the evil are simply truth and falsehood; for this is the work of the entire reasoning power. But the work of the practical and reasoning activity of the mind is truth existing in harmony with right desire.

The origin of action, then—the source from which motion is derived—is deliberate choice, though this origin is not the final cause of action. But desire and that reasoning which is for the sake of something <note>or which is directed to the final cause</note> are the origins of deliberate choice. Therefore, deliberate choice cannot exist without intellect and the reasoning activity of the mind, nor can it exist without moral habit; for good conduct, and its opposite in action, cannot exist without the reasoning process and character. 

The reasoning activity itself, however, does not move anything, except that which is for the sake of something and is practical; since this has authority over that which is productive. For everyone who produces anything does so for the sake of something; and the thing produced is not an absolute end, but is relative to and pertains to something else—though this is not the case with that which is doable through action. For good conduct itself is the end of action, and desire is directed toward that end. Therefore, deliberate

<summary>This page defines the requirements for good deliberate choice, stating that it requires both true reasoning and right desire. It distinguishes between contemplative truth and practical truth, identifying deliberate choice as the source of human action.</summary>
<keywords>moral virtue, deliberate choice, practical reason, contemplative reason, desire, action, final cause, intellect, character, productive reasoning</keywords>

--- Page 206 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the rational soul, focusing on the relationship between practical reason, moral virtue, and deliberate choice.</meta>

<header>CHAP. III, ETHICS.</header>

Choice is either <term>orectic intellect</term> <note>intellect influenced by desire</note>, or desire possessing a <term>discursive energy</term> <note>the soul's reasoning power</note>; and man is a source of action of this kind. Nothing, however, that has already happened is an object of deliberate choice. Thus, for instance, no one deliberately chooses to have destroyed Troy; for no one deliberates about what is past, but about that which is future and contingent. It is not possible for what has been done to be undone. Hence, Agatho says rightly,

> All things to God are possible but one,
> To undo that which is already done.

Therefore, the function of both intellectual parts is truth. Thus, those habits by which each of these parts states the truth are the virtues of both.

## CHAPTER III.

Taking, therefore, a more elevated starting point, let us speak again concerning these virtues. Let the habits by which the soul states truth in affirming or denying be five in number. These are art, science, prudence, wisdom, and intellect; for it is possible that both *<term>hypolepsis</term>*¹ and opinion may state what is false.

<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK VI.</header>

What science is, therefore, will be evident from this, if it is necessary to investigate accurately and not merely follow similarities. For we are all of the opinion <note>*Hypolepsis* is the soul's assent to each term of a syllogistic process, and *opinion* is the soul's assent solely to the conclusion of a syllogism.</note> that it is not possible for that which we know scientifically to exist in any way other than it does. But with respect to things which may exist otherwise, when they are out of our view, we are ignorant of whether they exist or not. The object of scientific knowledge, therefore, exists by necessity. Hence, it is eternal. For all beings which exist simply by necessity are eternal; and eternal things are ungenerated and incorruptible. Again, all science appears to be capable of being taught, and the object of scientific knowledge may be acquired through instruction. But all teaching is produced from things previously known, as we have said in the *Analytics*; for it partly consists of induction, and partly of syllogism. Induction, therefore, is indeed a principle, and the principle of the universal; <note>That is, so far as it stimulates the perception of the universal which is latent in the soul.</note> but syllogism proceeds from the universal. The principles, therefore, of which syllogism consists, are things for which there is no syllogism. <note>That is, the knowledge of them is not obtained by a syllogistic process.</note> Hence, they are obtained by induction. Science, therefore, is indeed a demonstrative habit, along with such other characteristics as we have added to its definition in the *Analytics*. For when a man believes that a thing exists in a certain manner, <note>That is, when he believes that the thing cannot exist otherwise than it does.</note> and

---

<summary>Aristotle defines scientific knowledge as the grasp of eternal and necessary truths, distinguishing it from contingent things that can change. He explains that science is a "demonstrative habit" acquired through instruction, utilizing induction to identify universal principles and syllogism to reach conclusions.</summary>

## CHAPTER IV.

However, concerning that which can exist differently than it does <note>or which has a varied existence and is contingent</note>, there is that which is productive and that which is practical. But production and action differ. Credibility, however, may be obtained concerning these things from exoteric discussions; so that the practical habit in conjunction with reason is different from the productive habit in conjunction with reason. And neither is contained by the other; for action is not production, nor is production action. But since the art of building is a productive habit in conjunction with reason, and since there is no art which is not a productive habit in conjunction with reason, nor any such habit which is not an art, art and a productive habit in conjunction with true reason will be the same thing. All art, however, is concerned with generation, and it devises and con-

Art, therefore, as we have said, is a certain habit of production in conjunction with true reason. But the lack of art, on the contrary <note>or the unskillful habit</note>, is a habit of production in conjunction with false reason, concerning that which may have a various existence.

## CHAPTER V.

Regarding prudence, we will understand what it is if we examine those whom we call prudent people. It appears, therefore, to be the characteristic of a prudent person to be able to deliberate well about things that are good and advantageous for them—not just in a partial sense, such as what contributes to health or strength, but about what contributes to a happy life as a whole. This is indicated by our calling people prudent about a specific thing when they reason well toward some worthy goal in matters where there is no specific art. So, in short, a person who is capable of deliberation will be a prudent person. However, no one deliberates about things that cannot be otherwise than they are, nor about things that it is impossible for them to perform. Therefore, if science indeed exists in conjunction with demonstration; but for those things whose principles can exist in various ways, there is no demonstration (since all these things are contingent); and if it is not possible to deliberate about things that exist by necessity, prudence will be neither science nor art. It will not be science because that which is practical can exist in various ways; and it will not be art because the category of action is different from the category of production. It remains, therefore, that it is a true habit

<summary>This page defines prudence as the ability to deliberate well about what is universally good for a happy life, distinguishing it from science and art because it deals with contingent actions rather than necessary truths or productive activities.</summary>
<keywords>prudence, phronesis, deliberation, science, art, action, production, happy life, Aristotelian ethics</keywords>

--- Page 211 ---
<meta>This page continues the definition of prudence (phronesis), distinguishing it from production and art by its relationship to action, virtue, and the rational soul.</meta>

<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK VI.</header>

in conjunction with reason, practical about human **good** and evil. For the end of production is different from the production itself, but the end of action is not always different from the action. Hence we are of the opinion that Pericles and people like him are prudent, because they are able to discern what is good for themselves and for mankind; we also think that household managers and politicians are people of this description. For this reason, we also call temperance by its name, <term>sophrosyne</term>, as it saves or preserves prudence (<term>phronesis</term>). But it preserves an opinion of this specific kind. For what is pleasurable and what is painful do not corrupt or distort every opinion—such as the opinion that a triangle has or does not have angles equal to two right angles—but only those opinions which pertain to what is practicable. For the principles of practicable things are the goals for which they are performed. But for someone who is corrupted by pleasure or pain, the principle is not immediately apparent, nor do they perceive that for the sake of this goal, and because of this principle, it is necessary to choose and perform all things. For vice destroys the principle. Therefore, it is necessary that prudence should be a habit in conjunction with true reason, focused on human good. 

Moreover, while there is a virtue belonging to art, there is no such virtue of prudence. And in art, someone who errs voluntarily is to be preferred to someone who errs involuntarily; but in prudence, someone who errs voluntarily is an inferior character, just as in the moral virtues. It is evident, therefore, that prudence is a specific virtue and not an art. But since there are two parts of the rational soul, prudence will be the virtue of the <term>doxastic part</term> <note>or that part which forms opinions of things</note>. For both opinion and prudence are concerned with that which may exist in various ways,

<summary>Aristotle defines prudence as a rational, practical habit focused on human good, distinguishing it from art because prudence is a virtue in itself and is corrupted by vice. He identifies it as the excellence of the soul's opinion-forming (doxastic) part, dealing with things that are contingent rather than necessary.</summary>

<header>CHAP. VI. ETHICS.</header>

Nor is prudence merely a habit joined with reason; an indication of this is that such a habit can be forgotten <note>i. e. it may be lost through forgetting</note>, but prudence cannot be.

---

## CHAPTER VI.

Since, however, science is an acceptance of universals and things which have a necessary existence, and there are principles of demonstrable things and of every science (for science is accompanied by reason), it follows that there will be neither science, nor art, nor prudence regarding the principle of the object of science. For the object of science is demonstrable, but art and prudence are concerned with things which may exist in various ways. Therefore, wisdom will not be the means through which this principle is known, for it is the role of a wise man to have demonstration regarding certain things. Hence, if the habits by which we state the truth and are never deceived—whether about things which are necessary or things which can exist in various ways—are science, prudence, wisdom, and intellect, and yet none of the first three can be the habit by which we know principles (and by "the three," I mean prudence, wisdom, and science), it remains that intellect is the habit by which principles are known.

<vocab>prudence, science, art, wisdom, intellect, principles, demonstration, existence, universals</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes that prudence cannot be forgotten like other habits and argues that since science, art, prudence, and wisdom cannot grasp the first principles of knowledge, it must be the intellect that knows them.</summary>
<keywords>prudence, intellect, first principles, science, wisdom, Aristotle, demonstration, universals</keywords>

THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK VI.

### CHAPTER VII.

We attribute wisdom in the arts, however, to those who are most accurately skilled in those arts. Thus we say that Phidias was a wise sculptor and Polycletus a wise statuary. Here, therefore, we signify nothing else by wisdom than that it is the virtue of art. But, in short, we think that certain people are wise in a general sense, not just in part, and that they are not anything else but wise men, as Homer says in his *Margites*:

> The gods made him neither a digger nor a plowman;
> Nor wise in anything else;

So it is evident that wisdom will be the most accurate of the sciences. Hence, it is necessary that the wise man not only know those things which are inferred from principles <note>the conclusions of scientific reasoning</note>, but that he should also perceive and state the truth about the principles themselves. Wisdom, therefore, will be intellect and science, and will possess as a head <note>or summit</note> the science of the most honorable things <note>that is, of divine natures</note>. For it would be absurd for anyone to imagine that political science or prudence is the most excellent thing of all, unless

# CHAP. VII. ETHICS.

For they will say that the being is prudent who examines what is excellent in particulars according to the nature of each, and to him they will entrust these particulars. Hence, they also say that some animals are prudent, namely, those that appear to have a power of foresight regarding what pertains to their life. From what has been said, therefore, it is evident that wisdom is the science and intellect of things that are naturally most honorable. Consequently, many people say that Anaxagoras, Thales, and similar individuals were indeed wise, but not prudent men, because they saw that these men were ignorant of what was beneficial to them <note>regarding a physical life</note>; and they say that they knew things that were superfluous, admirable, difficult, and divine, but which are useless because they did not investigate the human good.

Prudence, however, deals with human affairs and with those things that are the subjects of consultation; for we say that the specific work of a prudent man is to consult well. But no one consults about things that cannot be otherwise than they are, nor about things that do not lead to a certain goal, specifically a practical good. He who consults well, in a simple sense, is the one who determines through reasoning what is best for a human being among things that can be done. Prudence is not only directed toward universals; it is also necessary for it to know particulars, for it is practical, and action deals with particulars. Therefore, some people who have only experimental knowledge without formal science are better suited for practical affairs than those who possess scientific knowledge <note>without experience</note>. ## CHAPTER VIII.

Political science and prudence are indeed the same habit, though they do not have the same essence. But regarding the science pertaining to a city, the part which is legislative is, as it were, architectonic prudence; but the other part, which deals with particulars in the same way, is called by the common name "political science." This, however, is practical and concerned with consultation; for a decree is something practical as the extreme point.<sup>1</sup> Hence, only those who possess political science are said to act in a political capacity, for they alone act in the same manner as manual workers. ### CHAPTER IX.

However, to investigate and to consult differ; for to consult is to investigate something. But it is necessary to discuss good consultation and show what it is—whether it is a certain science, or opinion, or good conjecture, or some other category. It is not, therefore, science. For people do not investigate things they already know; but good consultation is a specific kind of consultation, and he who consults investigates and reasons. Neither is it good conjecture; for good conjecture is without reasoning and is something accomplished with speed; but people consult for a long time, and say that the objects of consultation ought to be performed rapidly, but that consultation itself should be done slowly. Again, sagacity and good consultation also differ from each other; but sagacity is a certain kind of good conjecture. Neither, there-

---

<note>He who descends from universals stops at particulars as the downward extremes.</note>
<note>¹ By *this sense*, Aristotle means the *common sense*, which is indivisible and is able to distinguish how contraries and things of a heterogeneous nature differ from each other; but by *that sense*, he means any one of the specific senses, such as sight, hearing, etc.</note>

<summary>Aristotle distinguishes "good consultation" from science, opinion, and conjecture, noting that consultation involves slow reasoning while conjecture and sagacity are characterized by speed. He also clarifies that prudence relates to a type of common sense that handles particular instances.</summary>

<page-num>227</page-num>
<header>CHAP. IX. ETHICS.</header>

fore, is good consultation any kind of opinion. But since he who consults badly errs, while he who consults well consults rightly, it is evident that good consultation is a certain correctness. Nor is good consultation either science or opinion; for in science, indeed, there is no "correctness" [in this sense], because there is no error; but truth is the correctness of opinion; and at the same time, everything about which there is an opinion is already definite and determined. Nevertheless, good consultation is not without reasoning. It falls short, therefore, of <term>dianoia</term> <note>or the discursive energy of reason</note>; for this is not yet a formal assertion; since opinion is not investigation, but is already a specific assertion. He, however, who consults—whether he consults well or ill—investigates something and reasons. But good consultation is a certain correctness of consultation; on which account, it must first be investigated what consultation is, and with what it is concerned.

Since, however, correctness is spoken of in many ways, it is evident that not every kind of correctness is good consultation. For the incontinent and the bad man obtain through reasoning that which they intend to achieve; so they will have consulted "correctly" in a technical sense, but have procured for themselves a great evil. But to have consulted well appears to be a certain good; for such a correctness of consultation as becomes the means of obtaining what is good is good consultation. Good, however, may be obtained by false reasoning; and a man may indeed achieve that which ought to be done, yet not through a proper medium, because the middle term may be false.* Hence, neither will that be

---

<note>* As when a man steals in order to relieve a worthy person in distress. But just as he who proves a true conclusion through false premises—</note>

<summary>Aristotle defines good consultation as a form of correctness in deliberation that requires reasoning, distinguishing it from science and opinion. He argues that true good consultation must reach a good end through a correct logical process, rather than arriving at a good result through faulty or immoral means.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, ethics, good consultation, deliberation, rectitude, dianoia, reasoning, incontinent man, middle term</keywords>

--- Page 221 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of "good consultation" (deliberation), refining its definition by distinguishing it from quick thinking and clarifying its relationship to specific or general ends.</meta>

<page-num>228</page-num>
->THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK VI.<-

good consultation according to which that is obtained which ought to be obtained, yet not through the proper means. Furthermore, it is possible that one man may obtain the object of his wishes by deliberating for a long time, while another does so by deliberating quickly. Therefore, neither of those is yet good consultation; rather, it is the correctness that exists according to utility, concerning what is proper, and how, and when it is proper. Again, it is possible to consult well *simply*, and also with a view to a certain end. Good consultation, therefore, *simply* is that which proceeds with correctness to an end *simply*; but a *certain* good consultation is that which proceeds with correctness to a *certain* end. Thus, if to consult well is the domain of prudent men, good consultation will be a correctness according to utility with a view to a certain end, of which prudence is the true principle.

# CHAP. X. ETHICS.

Nor is intelligence one of the particular sciences, such as medicine—for then it would be concerned with health—or geometry, for then it would be concerned with magnitudes. For intelligence is not concerned with things that always exist and are unchangeable, nor with things that are coming into existence, but with those things that may be the subject of doubt and deliberation. Hence, it is concerned with the same things as prudence; yet intelligence and prudence are not the same. For prudence is authoritative in nature, because its end is to determine what ought or what ought not to be done. But intelligence is solely concerned with making judgments. For intelligence is the same as right intelligence, since intelligent men are also rightly intelligent. Intelligence, however, is neither the possession nor the acquisition of prudence. But just as he who learns is said to understand what he learns when he uses science, something similar takes place in the use of opinion when forming a judgment about those things with which prudence is concerned, and judging them well when another person is speaking. For what is "well" is the same as what is done "beautifully" <note>In this context, "beautifully" refers to the Greek concept of *kalos*, meaning nobly or excellently.</note>. And from this the name "intelligence" was derived, according to which men are said to be rightly intelligent—namely, from the intelligence used in learning; for we frequently use the verb *to learn* as equivalent to the verb *to understand*.

<vocab>intelligence, synesis, prudence, phronesis, ethics, science, judgment</vocab>

<summary>This page distinguishes intelligence from both science and prudence, defining it as a judicial capacity for evaluating matters of deliberation rather than an authoritative capacity for action. It explains that intelligence involves judging the communications of others well, similar to how a learner understands a subject.</summary>
<keywords>intelligence, synesis, prudence, phronesis, judgment, deliberation, ethics, learning, Aristotle</keywords>

## CHAPTER XI.

But what is called upright decision, according to which we say that people judge rightly, is the correct judgment of the equitable person. As an indication of this, however, we say that the equitable person is especially inclined to pardon others, and that it is equitable to show forgiveness in certain cases. But pardon is an upright judicial decision made by the equitable person; and a decision is upright when it is made by one who observes the truth.

All these habits, however, reasonably lead toward the same conclusion. For we speak of upright decision, intelligence, prudence, and intellect in reference to the same people, when we say that they are individuals of upright decision, are endowed with intellect, and are prudent and intelligent. For all these powers pertain to the [downward] extremes and to particulars. And an intelligent person—one who decides rightly or is inclined to pardon—is one who possesses the power of judgment regarding matters with which the prudent person is concerned; for matters of an equitable nature are common to all good people in their interactions with others. Everything of a practical nature, however, pertains to particulars and the [downward] extremes. For it is necessary that a prudent person have a knowledge of these; and intelligence and upright decision are concerned with things of a practical

<header>CHAP. XI. ETHICS.</header>

nature; but these are extremes. And intellect pertains both to the upward and downward extremes. For intellect, and not the discursive energy of reason, is concerned with both first and last terms <note>that is, with universal principles</note>; the one indeed—that is, the intellect which is the principle of the demonstrative sciences—is concerned with immutable and first terms; but the intellect which is occupied in practical affairs <note>or which is the principle of prudence</note> is concerned with the extreme, with that which is contingent, and with the other proposition.¹ For these are the principles of that for the sake of which a thing is done <note>or the final cause</note>; for the universal is derived from particulars. Of these, therefore, it is necessary to have a sensible perception; but this is the <note>practical</note> intellect. Hence, these <note>habits</note> appear to be natural. And no one indeed is wise by nature; but every person possesses naturally the power of deciding rightly, together with intelligence and intellect. As an indication of this, we are of the opinion that these habits accompany the various ages of the life of man; and we say that this age <note>that is, old age</note> possesses intellect and upright decision, as if nature were the cause of this. Hence, also, intellect is both the principle and the end;² for from these, demonstrations are framed, and with these, they are concerned.

Therefore, [in practical affairs] it is no less necessary to pay attention to the unproven assertions and opinions of elderly or prudent people than to formal demonstrations; for because they possess insight gained from experience, they perceive the principles [of things that can be acted upon]. Thus, we have shown what wisdom and prudence are, what subjects each deals with, and that each is the virtue of a different part of the soul.

### CHAPTER XII.

Someone, however, might doubt the usefulness of these qualities. For wisdom does not contemplate any of the things that lead to human happiness, since it is not concerned with anything that is in a state of generation [or which is coming into being or passing into existence]. Prudence, however, does consider the things from which human happiness is derived; but for what reason is it necessary, since prudence deals with things that are just and beautiful and which are good for humans? We do not, however, become any more capable of virtuous action simply by knowing these things, since the virtues are habits. In the same way, things are not called healthy or conducive to a good physical condition simply because they have the power to produce health, but

because they proceed from habit. For we are not at all more practical by possessing the medical or gymnastic art. But if a man is not to be considered prudent for the sake of these things, but for the sake of becoming worthy, they will not be at all useful to those who are already worthy. Again, neither will prudence be useful to those who are not worthy; for it is of no consequence whether they are prudent themselves, or are persuaded by others who are; since this will be sufficient, in the same manner as in what pertains to health. For when we wish to be well, we do not at the same time learn the medical art. To which we may add, that it will appear to be absurd that prudence, which is subordinate to wisdom, should possess a greater authority; for that which is effective governs and presides in everything. These things, therefore, must be discussed, for the doubt now concerns them alone. In the first place, therefore, we say that these virtues <note>wisdom and prudence</note> are necessarily desirable *per se*, since they are the virtues of each part of the soul,¹ though neither of them should produce anything. In the next place, they do indeed produce something, yet not in the same manner as medicine produces health; but as health produces the activities of a good physical condition, thus also wisdom produces happiness. For being a part of the whole of virtue, by the possession and the activity of it, it renders a person happy. Furthermore, the work is accomplished by prudence and ethical virtue. For virtue renders the goal proposed by the agent right; but prudence provides correctness to the things which lead to the goal. <note>¹ In the physical virtues, which are the forerunners of the other virtues, the possession of the senses in perfection—and especially of the most noble of the senses, sight and hearing—may be called corporeal prudence. In the second place, corporeal strength may be called corporeal fortitude. In the third place, corporeal beauty may be called corporeal temperance. For justice is that habit which keeps the parts of the soul free from conflict; and health is that which produces concord and order among the disorderly elements of the body.

<header>CHAP. XIII. ETHICS.</header>

tues are prudences. And Socrates,¹ indeed, investigated partly with correctness, and partly with error. For because he thought that all the virtues are prudences, he erred; but it is well said by him that the virtues do not exist without prudence. But as an indication of this, all men now, when they define virtue, add to the definition "habit," and state that they energize according to right reason. And right reason is that which exists according to prudence. All men, therefore, appear in a certain respect to intuit that a habit of this kind, which exists according to prudence, is virtue. It is necessary, however, to change the definition slightly; for not only a habit according to right reason, but also a habit in conjunction with right reason,² is virtue. But prudence is right reason energizing about things of this kind. So-

---

<note>¹ Socrates, in the *Republic* of Plato, calls the virtues prudences or sciences, because the energies of all the virtues are according to right reason. Hence, he gives them this name from the better and superior part of the soul, just as we call man simply a rational animal, though he contains both rational and irrational powers. But we call him this because his irrational powers are in a certain respect rendered rational by being obedient to reason, and because it is more appropriate to name him from the more excellent and ruling part. Hence, there is no real disagreement between Socrates and Aristotle in what is said here of the virtues; the former naming them according to what is characteristic of their essence, but the latter considering the virtues and that which characterizes them as different things.</note>

<note>² "A man performs something *according to reason* both when he is moved by another and when he regards the end, in the same manner as nature produces according to reason; but he acts *in conjunction with reason* when he acts from knowledge; and regarding the end, operates according to reason." Paraphrase on the Nicomachean Ethics. The translation by Mr. Bridgman.</note>

<summary>This page discusses the definition of virtue in relation to prudence, refining Socrates' view by stating that virtue is not just "according to" right reason but "in conjunction with" it. It argues that while Socrates correctly identified that virtue requires prudence, he erred in claiming all virtues are identical to prudence.</summary>

--- Page 231 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between virtue and prudence, specifically critiquing and refining the Socratic view that all virtues are forms of knowledge or prudence.</meta>

<page-num>238</page-num>
<header>THE NICOMACHEAN ETHICS. · BOOK VI.</header>

Socrates therefore thought that the virtues were indeed types of reason, because they are all forms of knowledge; however, we believe that they exist in conjunction with reason. Hence, it is clear from what has been said that it is not possible to be a truly good man without prudence, nor a prudent man without moral virtue. In this way, we can also refute the argument that some use to claim that the virtues are separate from one another—the claim that the same person is not naturally suited to all the virtues. Therefore, one might have acquired *this* virtue, but not yet *that* one. This can indeed happen with natural virtues, but it is not possible with those virtues by which a man is called a simply good man. For all the virtues are present at the same time that prudence, which is a single virtue, is present. But it is evident that even if prudence were not a practical matter, it would still be necessary because it is the virtue of a specific part of the soul, and because a deliberate choice will not be right without both prudence and virtue. For virtue provides the goal, while prudence causes us to perform the actions that contribute to that goal. Prudence, however, does not rule over wisdom, nor over the superior part of the soul, just as medical science does not rule over health. Medicine does not use health, but rather considers how it may be attained. It issues prescriptions, therefore, for the sake of health, but it has no authority over it. Furthermore, to say that prudence rules over wisdom is like saying that political science rules over the gods because it organizes everything done in the city.

<summary>Aristotle concludes that true virtue requires both moral character and prudence, refuting the idea that virtues can be fully separated. He clarifies that while prudence guides the soul toward virtuous ends, it does not hold authority over higher wisdom, much like medicine serves health without commanding it.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Socrates, prudence, moral virtue, ethical virtue, wisdom, reason, soul, natural virtues, deliberate choice</keywords>

### CHAPTER I.

For the sake of the liberal and philosophical reader, the following account of the heroic character is subjoined from the manuscript Scholia of Proclus on the *Cratylus*. If any apology were necessary for the insertion of this extract, it will be sufficient merely to add that, independent of its excellence, the manuscript from which it is taken is among the number of the rarest currently existing.

> "Everywhere, the extremities of a prior order are joined with the summits of a secondary order. Thus, for instance, our master Hermes (*ο δεσποτης ημων ερμης*), being an archangelic monad, is celebrated as a god. But Plato calls the whole extent between gods and men demons; and they, indeed, are demons by nature. Those demons, however, that are now mentioned, together with the demi-gods or heroes, are not demons and heroes by nature, for they do not always follow the gods; but they are only so from habit, being souls who naturally give themselves over to generation, such as was the great Hercules and others of the like kind. But the peculiarity of heroic souls is magnitude of operation, the elevated and the magnificent; and such heroes it is necessary to honor and to perform funeral rites to their memory, in accordance with the exhortation of the Athenian Guest. This heroic genus of souls, therefore, does not always follow the gods, but is undefiled and more intellectual than other souls. And it descends, indeed, for the benefit of the life of men, as partaking of a destiny inclining downwards; but it has much of an elevated nature, which is properly liberated from matter. Hence, souls of this kind are easily led back to the intelligible world, in which they live for many periods; while, on the contrary, the more irrational kind of souls are either never led back, or this is accomplished with great difficulty, or continues for a very insignificant period of time.
> "Each of the gods, also, is perfectly exempt from secondary natures; and the first and more universal demons are likewise established above a habit of this kind. They employ, however, terrestrial and partial spirits in the generations of some of the

human race; not physically mingling with mortals, but moving nature, perfecting its power, expanding the path of generation, and removing all impediments. Fables, therefore, through the similarity of names, conceal the things themselves. For spirits of this kind are named similarly to the gods, who are the leading causes of their series. Hence, they say either that gods have connection with women, or men with goddesses. But if they were willing to speak plainly and clearly, they would say that Venus, Mars, Thetis, and the other divinities produce their respective series, beginning from on high and extending to the last of things; each of these series contains within itself many essences differing from each other, such as the angelic, demoniacal, heroic, nymphal, and the like. The lowest powers, therefore, of these orders have much communion with the human race: for the extremities of primary natures are born together with the summits of secondary natures. And they contribute to our other natural operations and to the production of our species. On this account, it frequently appears that from the mixture of these powers with men, heroes are generated who appear to possess a certain prerogative above human nature. But not only does a demoniacal genus of this kind physically sympathize with men, but a different genus sympathizes with other animals, as Nymphs with trees, others with fountains, and others with stags or serpents.

“But how is it that at one time the gods are said to have connection with mortal females, and at another time mortal females with the gods? We reply that the communion of gods with goddesses gives existence to gods, or demons, eternally; but heroic souls, having a twofold form of life—namely, *doxastic* [opinion-based] and *dianoetic* [reasoning-based]—the former of which is called by Plato in the *Timaeus* *the circle of difference*, and the latter *the circle of sameness*, and which are characterized by the properties of *male* and *female*;—hence, these souls at one time exhibit a godlike power by acting according to the masculine prerogative of their nature, or *the circle of sameness*, and at another time according to their feminine prerogative, or *the circle of difference*; yet in such a way that, according to both these energies, they act with rectitude and without merging themselves

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the nature of the heroic soul, specifically addressing how these souls relate to the Platonic "circles of sameness and difference" and the origins of their divine parentage.</meta>

->Mestor the brave, renowned in ranks of war,<-

in the darkness of the body. They also know the natures prior to their own and exercise providential care over inferior concerns, without, at the same time, having that inclination toward such concerns found in the majority of mankind. But the souls which act erroneously, according to the energies of both these circles—or which, in other words, neither show accurate examples of practical or intellectual virtue—these differ in no respect from *gregarious* souls, or the herd of mankind, with whom the circle of sameness is fettered, and the circle of difference undergoes all kinds of fractures and distortions.

“As it is impossible, therefore, for these heroic souls to act with equal vigor and perfection according to both these circles at once, since this is the domain of natures more divine than the human, it is necessary that they should sometimes descend and act, principally according to their doxastic part, and sometimes according to their more intellectual part. Hence, one of these circles must act naturally, and the other be hindered from its proper energy. On this account, heroes are called *demi-gods*, <gloss>demigods</gloss>, as having only one of their circles illuminated by the gods. Those among these, therefore, who have the circle of sameness unfettered, who are inspired to an intellectual life, and are moved by it according to a divine energy—these are said to have a god for their father and a mortal for their mother, due to a defect in the doxastic form of life. But those, on the contrary, who act without impediment according to the circle of difference, who act with proper rectitude in practical affairs, and at the same time *enthusiastically*—or in other words, under the inspiring influence of divinity—these are said to have a mortal for their father and a goddess for their mother. In short, the rectitude of energy in each of these circles is to be attributed to a divine cause*. Hence, when the circle of sameness has dominion, the divine cause of illumination is said to be masculine and paternal;

---

<note>Let it, however, be carefully observed that this divine cause illuminates, invigorates, and excites these circles in the most unrestrained and impassive manner, without destroying the freedom of action in the circles themselves, or causing any partial affection, sympathy, or inclination in the illuminating deity.</note>

It distinguishes these superior souls from the "gregarious" mass of mankind whose souls are distorted and fettered.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of heroic souls, focusing on the distinction between those who prioritize practical virtue (like Achilles) and those who ascend to intellectual being (like Minos and Rhadamanthus), while introducing the concept of divine virtue in contrast to "brutal" nature.</meta>

<header>CHAP. I. ETHICS. 243</header>

> ->And last, great Hector, more than man divine;<-
> ->For sure he seemed not of terrestrial line!¹<-

Therefore, if, as it is said, men become gods² from being men through an excess of virtue, the habit which is opposed to a brutal habit will indeed be like this. For just as there is neither vice nor virtue in a brute, so

but when the circle of difference predominates, it is said to be maternal. Hence too Achilles, in Homer, acts with rectitude in practical affairs and at the same time exhibits specimens of magnificent, vehement, and divinely-inspired energy, being the son of a goddess. And such is his attachment to practical virtue that even when in Hades, he desires a union with a body so that he may assist his father. While, on the contrary, Minos and Rhadamanthus, who were heroes illuminated by Jupiter, raised themselves from generation to true being and involved themselves with mortal concerns no further than absolute necessity required.”

<note>¹ Priam, in Homer, complains that the bravest of his sons—Mestor, Troilus, and Hector—had fallen in battle, and that only cowardly sons survived. I have given the whole passage, for Aristotle only cites what relates to Hector. The passage is to be found in *Iliad*, 24. v. 255, etc. The translation is by Pope.</note>

<note>² The wisest of the ancients never supposed that men, however exalted their virtue might be while living, actually become gods at length; for this was an opinion that prevailed only during the corruption of the heathen religion, and especially during the decline and fall of the Roman Empire. This opinion, in short, is diametrically opposite to the most fundamental principles of heathenism, as is evident from the golden verses attributed to Pythagoras and from the writings of Plato—not to mention other respectable authorities which might be cited to confirm this assertion. It is necessary to observe, therefore, that very good men were said by the ancients to become gods through the likeness they bear to divinity. Hence, Plato, in the *Sophist*, calls the Elean guest, or stranger, a *god*. In short, as Proclus well observes, Plato, in many places, honors those who participate in the gods by the same names as the gods, and calls them gods. Thus, not only</note>

---

<note>The Athenian guest in the *Laws*, and also Socrates in the *Phaedrus*, calls a divine soul a god. Nor is it surprising that beings who are always united to the gods, and who complete one golden chain together with them, should be called gods. Plato, likewise, in many places, calls daemons gods, though they are essentially subordinate to and exist around the gods. For in the *Phaedrus*, *Timaeus*, and other dialogues, you will find that he extends the title of gods as far as to daemons.

Hence, according to Plato and the wisest of the ancients, that nature is simply a god which is characterized by a superessential unity. Intellectual natures are gods according to union. Divine souls are gods according to participation. Daemons are gods according to contact with the gods. And the souls of men are gods through similarity.</note>

<summary>Aristotle distinguishes divine and brutal natures from human virtue and vice, noting that while the divine is rare and honorable, the brutal is typically found in extreme vice or physical disease. He introduces a new study of incontinence, effeminacy, and luxury alongside their opposites, continence and endurance.</summary>

apparent <gloss>that is, what is commonly admitted as true</gloss>, and the proposed doubts. In the next place, we should show everything that is especially probable regarding these passions; but if not everything, then at least the greater part and the most important points. For if difficult doubts are resolved, and the probable things remain, we shall have sufficiently accomplished our purpose.

Continence, therefore, and endurance appear to be among the worthy and commendable things; while incontinence and effeminacy are among the bad and blameworthy things. The continent man and the one who sticks to the decision of reason are the same person; and the incontinent man is the same as the one who departs from the decision of reason. Indeed, the incontinent man, knowing that certain things are bad, does them because of passion; but the continent man, knowing that his desires are bad, does not follow them because he is obedient to reason. All people admit that the temperate man is continent and possesses the virtue of endurance; but regarding such a person, some say that he is in every respect temperate, while others say he is not. Some say confusingly that the intemperate man is incontinent, and the incontinent man is intemperate; but others say that they differ from each other. With respect to the prudent man, they sometimes say he cannot be incontinent; and at other times, that certain people who are prudent and skillful are incontinent. Furthermore, people are said to be incontinent regarding anger, honor, and gain. These, therefore, are the assertions concerning continence and incontinence.

<meta>This page continues the examination of incontinence, specifically addressing the Socratic paradox that no one acts against their better judgment knowingly, but only through ignorance.</meta>

<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK VII.</header>

## CHAPTER II.

It may, however, be questioned how someone who thinks correctly can act incontinently. Some say, therefore, that it is impossible for a man to act incontinently who knows that he ought not to; for it would be a terrible thing if, when knowledge is present as Socrates thought, anything else should have mastery and pull the man about like a slave. For Socrates, in short, opposed reason with this opinion, as if there were no such thing as incontinence; since he said that no one acted contrary to what they understood it was best to do, except through ignorance <note>of what was best</note>. This assertion, therefore, contradicts those things which are clearly apparent; and it is necessary to inquire about this passion: if anyone acts incontinently through ignorance, what is the nature of this ignorance? For it is evident that he who acts incontinently does not think he ought to act that way until he is under the influence of the passion. There are, however, certain people who admit some of these points but not others. For they grant, indeed, that nothing is better than knowledge; but they do not admit that no one acts contrary to what appears to them to be better. And for this reason they say that the incontinent man, possessing not knowledge but opinion, is overcome by pleasures.

<summary>This chapter examines the Socratic view that incontinence is impossible because no one knowingly does wrong, suggesting instead that such actions arise from ignorance. It counters this by noting that many people believe knowledge is supreme yet still act against their better judgment, often because they hold mere opinions rather than certain knowledge.</summary>

<keywords>Socrates, incontinence, knowledge, ignorance, passion, Nicomachean Ethics, opinion, pleasure</keywords>

--- Page 240 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical difficulties (aporiai) regarding incontinence, specifically addressing the contradictions that arise when comparing incontinence to prudence, temperance, and the holding of opinions.</meta>

<header>CHAP. II. ETHICS.</header>

If, however, it is opinion—and neither knowledge nor a strong but a weak <term>*hypolepsis*</term>—which resists (as is the case with those who are hesitant), pardon should be granted to the one who yields to strong desires; but wickedness is not to be pardoned, nor anything else which is blameworthy. The incontinent man, therefore, is overcome by desire while prudence resists at the same time; for prudence is most strong. But this is absurd; for the same person would be, at the same time, both prudent and incontinent. No one, however, would say that it is the nature of a prudent man to voluntarily perform the most shameful actions. To this may be added what we have shown before: that the prudent man is a man of action; for he is familiar with particulars and possesses the other virtues.

Again, if being a continent man consists in having strong and base desires, the temperate man will not be a continent man, nor the continent a temperate man; for it is not the nature of the temperate man to have excessive <note>desire</note> or to have base desires. But it would be necessary for him to have them if this were admitted; for if the desires are indeed good, the habit which prevents a man from following them is bad; so that not all continence would be good. If, however, the desires are weak but not bad, there is nothing admirable <note>in continence</note>; and if they are bad and weak, there is nothing great in it.

Furthermore, if continence gives steadfastness to every opinion, even to a false opinion, it is a bad thing; and if incontinence causes an abandonment of every opinion, there will be a certain kind of incontinence which is good—such as that of Neoptolemus in the *Philoctetes* of Sophocles. For he is to be praised for not persisting in what he was

It also questions whether continence is always virtuous, noting that sticking to a false opinion is not a virtue and that abandoning a bad resolution can be praiseworthy.</summary>

Because these men wish to force an agreement with paradoxes—so that they may appear clever when they succeed—the syllogism formed by their reasoning becomes very questionable. For the active power of reasoning is trapped when the person whose agreement they seek is unwilling to continue because the conclusion is unpleasant, yet is unable to move forward because he cannot refute the argument. 

From a certain perspective, however, it may happen that foolishness combined with incontinence results in a virtue. For an incontinent man might do the opposite of what he believes he should do; but if he believes that good things are evil and should not be done, he will end up doing what is good rather than what is evil. 

Furthermore, he who pursues and performs what is pleasant because he has been persuaded and deliberately chooses to do so appears to be a better character than the man who does so not out of reasoning, but out of incontinence. This is because the first man can be more easily cured, as he can be convinced to change his mind. But the incontinent man is subject to the proverb that says, “*When water chokes you, what is there to drink?*” For if he had been persuaded to do what he did, he would have stopped once he was convinced to change his mind; but as it is, he acts in this manner even though he is not persuaded that he should.

## CHAPTER III.

In the first place, therefore, it must be considered whether the incontinent act knowingly or not, and in what manner they act knowingly. In the next place, we must consider what kind of things the incontinent and the continent man are concerned with. I mean, whether they are concerned with all pleasure and pain, or with certain definite pleasures and pains; and whether the continent and the enduring man are the same or different characters. And in a similar manner, we must consider such other things as are related to this theory. The starting point, however, of this inquiry is whether the continent and incontinent man differ in the things they are concerned with, or in the manner in which they are concerned with them. My meaning is this: is the person considered incontinent simply because he deals with these particular things? Or is it because he is affected in a certain way? Or is it from both of these? In the next place, whether

<meta>This page continues the inquiry into whether incontinence is defined by the object of the desire or the manner of the action, transitioning into a discussion on the difference between acting against opinion versus acting against knowledge.</meta>

<page-num>250</page-num>
<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK VII.</header>

Are incontinence and continence concerned with all things, or not? For he who is simply incontinent is not concerned with all things, but with those things with which the intemperate man is concerned; nor is he called incontinent simply because he is affected by these things; for if he were, incontinence would be the same as intemperance; but rather because he is affected by them in this particular manner. For the intemperate man is led [by his desires] through deliberate choice, thinking that it is always necessary to pursue the pleasure at hand; but the incontinent man does not think this is necessary, yet he pursues it anyway. Therefore, the assertion that it is true opinion and not <term>science</term> <note>knowledge</note> through which men act incontinently is of no consequence to the present discussion; for some of those who form opinions of things have no doubt of their truth, but think that they know accurately. If, therefore, those who form an opinion act to a greater degree contrary to their opinion than those who have scientific knowledge because they believe uncertainly, <term>science</term> will in no respect differ from opinion. For some persons believe no less firmly in things about which they form an opinion than others do in things which they know scientifically. This is evident from Heraclitus.<sup>1</sup> Since, however, we say that a man "knows" in two ways—for both he who possesses knowledge but does not use it, and he who uses it, are said to have scientific knowledge—it makes a difference whether a man possessing knowledge,

---

<summary>Aristotle distinguishes between incontinence and intemperance, noting that while the intemperate act by choice, the incontinent act against their own judgment. For this appears to be absurd, but it is not if he does not contemplate. Again, since there are two modes of propositions—that is, universal and particular propositions—nothing prevents someone who possesses both of these from acting contrary to <term>science</term>; using the universal proposition, but not the particular one; for particulars are what can be put into practice. There is a difference, also, regarding the universal; for one difference is in the man himself, while the other is in the thing. Thus, for instance, a man may know that dry food is beneficial to every man, and that he himself is a man, or that food of a certain kind is dry food; but whether this specific food is of that kind, he either does not know, or he does not <term>energize</term> <note>act or function</note> as if he did know. There is an immense difference, therefore, between these modes; so that no absurdity follows for a man to "know" in this way; but for him to know in any other way, it would be extraordinary. Furthermore, <term>science</term> may be inherent in men in a way different from that which we have just now mentioned. For we see that <term>habit</term> <note>disposition or state of being</note> differs when possessing <term>science</term> but not using it; so that a man possesses knowledge in a certain respect and yet does not possess it, as is the case with someone who is asleep, insane, or intoxicated. Men are disposed in this way when they are under the influence of the <term>passions</term>; for anger, the desire for sexual pleasures, and certain other things of this kind evidently produce a change in the body, and in some persons they also produce insanity. It is manifest, therefore, that the incontinent man must be said to be affected similarly to these persons. Nor is it any indication of the contrary if such persons utter sentences which

And those who first learn a science indeed connect the words, but do not yet know their meaning; for it is necessary that knowledge should increase with advancing age, but this requires time. Hence, it must be admitted that the incontinent speak in the manner of actors <note>i.e., without attending to the meaning of what they say</note>.

Again, the cause of this may also be examined naturally as follows: opinion, indeed, is either universal or particular, and of the latter, perception is now the mistress. But when one conclusion is produced from both of these, it is necessary that, so far as it pertains to theory, the soul should state the conclusion, but so far as it pertains to practice, it should immediately act. Thus, for instance, if everything sweet ought to be tasted, and this thing is sweet (as being one of many specific instances), it necessarily follows that he who is able and is not hindered must act at the same time that he says this <note>i.e., taste the sweet thing</note>. When, therefore, the universal proposition is indeed present which prohibits a thing from being tasted, but another proposition says that everything which is sweet is delightful, and another says that this particular thing is sweet—and this causes the man to <term>energize</term> <note>act or function</note>—and when desire also happens to be present, then the universal proposition says this thing is to be avoided, but desire leads toward the fulfillment of it. For each of the parts of the soul is able to move or excite. Hence it happens that a man acts incontinently through both reason, in a certain respect, and opinion; though opinion is not essentially, but only accidentally, con-
