---

<note>¹ Here Aristotle clearly shows that while he seems to oppose Socrates (or Plato, which is the same thing), he actually explains his opinion. For when, as Aristotle informs us in the 2nd chapter, Socrates said, “That no one acted contrary to what he understood it was best to do, except out of ignorance of what was best,” his meaning was that no one acted against what he thought was best when he possessed science properly so called—that is, when he had scientific knowledge not only of the universal but also of the particular proposition. ## CHAPTER IV.

**But** next we must show whether anyone is simply incontinent, or if everyone who is incontinent is so only in part; and if there is anyone who is simply incontinent, what the things are with which he is concerned. That the continent, therefore, and those of endurance, and that the incontinent and effeminate, are concerned

<note>
of error. And the falsehood occurs regarding the major proposition. Thus Orestes says, “My mother has killed my father; every woman who kills her own husband ought to be killed; my mother, therefore, ought to be killed.” Here it is evident that Orestes erred through the major proposition. For though every woman who kills her husband ought to be killed, she should not be killed by her own son. Since, therefore, the major proposition is false, for this reason Orestes is said to have erred involuntarily, because we fall into falsehood involuntarily. For no one willingly admits what is false, since all men naturally love the truth.
</note>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the nature of incontinence, transitioning from the discussion of knowledge to the specific objects of lack of self-control. <page-num>255</page-num>
<header>CHAP. IV. ETHICS. 255</header>

with pleasures and pains, is evident. Of those things, however, which produce pleasure, some are necessary; but others, though they are desirable in themselves, can be pursued to excess. Necessary things are those which refer to the body; I mean such things as pertain to food, and the use of sexual pleasures, and similar physical concerns, regarding which we define temperance and intemperance. Other things, however, are not indeed necessary, yet are desirable in themselves; I mean such as victory, honor, wealth, and similar good and delightful things. Those, therefore, who exceed in these contrary to the right reason which is in them, we do not call simply incontinent, but with an addition we call them incontinent of riches, of gain, of honor, and of anger; we do not call them simply incontinent, as they are different from those who are truly so, and are named only by similarity. This is just as the name of a certain person who was victorious in the Olympic games was *Anthropos*, that is, *man*; for he had as a proper name the common name of man, and yet at the same time he was distinct <note>from man universal, or the species of man, as being an individual.</note> As an indication of this, incontinence is blamed not only as an error, but also as a certain vice, either simply or partially; but no one blames those who are incontinent of honor or gain, etc., as simply bad. With respect, however, to those who are concerned with physical enjoyments, with which we say the temperate and intemperate man are concerned, he who without deliberate choice pursues the excesses of delightful things, and avoids the excesses of painful things—namely hunger and thirst, heat and cold, and everything pertaining to touch and taste—yet contrary to deliberate choice and reasoning

<summary>Aristotle distinguishes between simple incontinence, which involves necessary physical pleasures like food and sex, and qualified incontinence regarding naturally desirable things like honor or wealth. He notes that only the former is blamed as a vice in the absolute sense.</summary>

power, is called incontinent—not with the addition of a specific thing, such as anger, but simply called incontinent. As an indication of this, those who are concerned with the former are called effeminate, but not those who are concerned with the latter. Hence, we arrange the incontinent and intemperate, and also the continent and temperate man, in the same class, but not those who are incontinent of honor, or gain, etc., because they are, in a certain respect, concerned with the same pleasures and pains. Although they are concerned with the same things, they do not act in the same manner; for the intemperate, indeed, pursue depraved pleasures from deliberate choice, but the incontinent do not. Hence, we would rather call someone intemperate if they pursue excessive pleasures and avoid moderate pains while feeling little or no desire, rather than someone who does so because they are influenced by a vehement desire. For what would the former character do if he were impelled by a powerful desire, and suffered violent pain from the want of necessary things? Since, however, among desires and pleasures, some rank in the category of things beautiful and worthy; for among delightful things, some are naturally desirable, others are contrary to these, and others have an intermediate status, according to the division we have before made; and these last are such as riches, gain, victory, and honor: in all these, therefore, and things of a similar kind, and in those that have an intermediate status, men are not blamed for being merely influenced by the passions, and for desiring and loving, but for the manner in which they are influenced by them, and for indulging them to excess. Hence, with respect to those who are overcome by the passions, or

<summary>This page distinguishes simple incontinence from qualified forms (like honor or wealth) and explains that intemperance involves deliberate choice, whereas incontinence does not. It also categorizes pleasures into those that are naturally worthy, those that are opposite, and those that are intermediate, like riches or victory.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of incontinence, specifically distinguishing between things that are naturally good but pursued to excess and things that are objects of simple incontinence.</meta>

<page-num>257</page-num>
<header>CHAP. IV. ETHICS.</header>

...pursue anything naturally beautiful and good in a way that is contrary to reason—such as those who pursue honor more than is proper, or are irrationally attached to their parents and children. For these also rank among the number of things that are good, and those who attend to them are praised; yet there is, at the same time, a certain excess even in these things—as when someone, like Niobe, contends about them even with the gods, or like Satyrus, who was called *Philopator* because of his attachment to his father; for through this attachment, he appeared to be very infatuated. Therefore, there is no depravity, indeed, in these things for the reason already assigned: because each of these things is naturally desirable in itself; but excesses in them are bad and are to be avoided. This, however, is not the case with incontinence; for incontinence is not only to be avoided but is also among the number of blameworthy things. But because of the similarity of the passion, when speaking about each of these, it is common to add the term "incontinence," just as we say that a man is a "bad physician" or a "bad actor," though we would not call him simply a "bad man." Therefore, just as we do not call these men simply "bad" because their actions are not a vice but only similar to vice by analogy, so too in that case <note>namely, in the immoderate pursuit of honor, gain, etc.</note>, only those things should be considered true incontinence and continence which deal with the same subjects as temperance and intemperance. But regarding incontinence in anger, we speak of it by way of similarity. Hence, also, by adding a qualification, we say that a man is "incontinent of anger," just as we say he is "incontinent of honor" or "gain."

True incontinence only applies to the same physical pleasures and pains associated with temperance and intemperance.</summary>

## CHAPTER V.

Since, however, some things are naturally pleasant, and of these, some are universally so, while others are pleasant only to specific kinds of animals and men; and since other things are not naturally pleasant, but some become so because of physical deformities, and others because of custom or depraved natures—this being the case, similar dispositions may be observed in each of these. By savage habits, I mean a disposition like that of the woman who is said to have cut open the bellies of pregnant women and devoured the fetus; or the habits that certain savage nations around Pontus are said to possess. For among these people, some take delight in feeding on raw meat, others on human flesh, and others feast on their own children at banquets. Or such a habit as Phalaris is said to have possessed [for he is reported to have eaten his own son]. These habits, therefore, are savage. Some of these habits, however, are produced in certain individuals by disease or insanity, as was the case with the man who sacrificed and ate his mother,¹ and also with the man who ate the liver of his fellow-

<note>¹ Ὁ Ξέρξης ὁ τῶν Περσῶν βασιλευς μανεις, εφαγη την εαυτου μητερα κρεουργησας. ASPASIUS.—that is, “Xerxes, king of the Persians, being insane, ate his mother, having for this purpose torn her into pieces.”</note>

<summary>This chapter distinguishes between things that are naturally pleasant and those that become pleasant through deformity, habit, or depravity, focusing on "savage habits" like cannibalism and insanity-driven violence.</summary>
<keywords>savage habits, natural pleasure, Phalaris, Pontus, Xerxes, depravity, cannibalism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter V regarding savage habits and whether they can be classified as simple incontinence, clarifying that such behaviors often stem from nature or disease rather than moral choice.</meta>

<page-num>259</page-num>
<header>CHAP. V. ETHICS.</header>

servant. But other savage habits either originate from disease or from custom, such as pulling out one's hair, biting one's nails, and besides these, eating coals and earth; to which may be added sexual relations between males. For these habits are produced in some people by nature, but in others from custom, as a result of being accustomed to them from childhood. No one, therefore, would call those in whom nature is the cause of these habits "incontinent," just as women are not called incontinent because in sexual relations they are not active, but passive. In a similar manner, those are not called incontinent who are in a diseased condition because of custom. The possession, therefore, of each of these habits is something beyond the boundaries of vice, in the same way as a savage nature is. But when someone possesses these propensities, to subdue them or be subdued by them is not simple continence or incontinence, but is only called so by resemblance; just as he who behaves in this manner with respect to anger is "incontinent" of this passion, though passion itself is not to be called incontinent. For every vice—folly, timidity, intemperance, and ferocity—when excessive, is either savage or the effect of disease. For he who is naturally disposed to be afraid of everything, even if a mouse should make a noise, is timid according to a savage timidity; but he who is afraid of a cat is timid because of disease. And among those who are stupid, those who are most irrational by nature and live only by their senses resemble savage animals, as is the case with some nations of remote barbarians; but those who are so because of disease, such as epilepsy or insanity, are in a morbid state. It is, however, possible that someone may sometimes possess these habits and yet not be

It is evident, therefore, that incontinence and continence are only concerned with those things that temperance and intemperance are concerned with, and that another species of incontinence exists regarding other things, which is named metaphorically and not simply.

## CHAPTER VI.

Let us, however, now consider whether the lack of self-control regarding anger is not less shameful than the lack of self-control regarding desires. For anger, indeed, seems to hear something of reason, but to hear it carelessly; just like hasty servants, who run away before they have heard the whole of what is said to them, and thus make mistakes in performing what they are ordered to do; or like dogs who bark at a noise alone, before they perceive whether the person who makes it is a friend or not. In this way also anger, through the heat and swiftness of its nature, does hear reason, but does not hear its commands, because it rushes impetuously toward vengeance. For reason, indeed, or the imagination, makes it evident that something has been done with insolence or contempt; but anger, as if concluding logically that it is necessary to be hostile to one who has acted in this manner, is immediately enraged. Desire, however, if the senses, or reason <gloss>corrupted by the senses</gloss>, merely says that a thing is delightful, rushes to the enjoyment of it. Hence, anger in a certain respect follows reason; but desire does not. Desire, therefore, is more shameful than anger. For he who lacks self-control regarding anger is, in a sense, defeated by reason; but he who lacks self-control regarding desire is subdued by desire, and not by reason. Again, it is more pardon-

<summary>Aristotle argues that the lack of self-control regarding anger is less shameful than that regarding desire, as anger attempts to follow reason’s logic concerning perceived slights, whereas desire ignores reason entirely to pursue pleasure.</summary>
<keywords>anger, desire, reason, self-control, incontinence, ethics, vengeance, pleasure, logic</keywords>

Thus, a man who was accused of striking his father said, as an apology for it, that his own father, and even his grandfather, had committed the same fault. Pointing to his child, he likewise said, "He will strike me when he becomes a man; for this is a family failing." Another person, also, being dragged around by his son, ordered him to stop at the gates of his house; for he himself had dragged his father as far as that place. Furthermore, those who act more deceitfully are more unjust. The quick-tempered man, therefore, is not deceitful, nor is anger; rather, he is open in what he does. The differences, however, of these must be understood. For, as we said at the beginning, some desires are human and natural, both in their kind and magnitude; but others are savage; and others arise from defects and diseases. But temperance and intemperance are concerned only with the first of these. Hence, we neither call brutes temperate nor intemperate, except metaphorically, though one species of animal differs briefly from another in insolence, in lustfulness, and in greediness. For they have neither any deliberate choice nor reasoning process; but they depart from nature in the same manner as insane men. 

Ferocity, however, is a lesser evil than vice, but it is more terrible; for that which is most excellent is not corrupted through this, as it is in men; but those who suffer from this malady are simply deprived of it <gloss>[i.e., of reason]</gloss>. ### CHAPTER VII.

However, with respect to the pleasures and pains that exist through touch and taste, and also regarding the desires and aversions pertaining to these (about which intemperance and temperance have been defined previously), it is possible indeed for a man to be so inclined as to be overcome by those pleasures and pains to which the majority of people are superior; and it is also possible that he may overcome those by which the majority are overcome. But among these characters, he who is overcome by pleasures is incontinent, and he who overcomes them is continent. Also, he who is overcome by pains is effeminate, but he who overcomes them is a man of endurance. However, the habits of most men lie between these, though they tend toward the worse habits. This being the case, the man who pursues excessive pleasures—or who pursues pleasures excessively, or by deliberate choice and for their own sake, and not for any other reason that may occur—is an intemperate man. For such a man will necessarily not repent of his conduct, and thus he is incurable. But he who is deficient in the pursuit of pleasures is the opposite of this character; and he who remains in a middle ground between both is the temperate man. In a similar manner, he is also intemperate who avoids bodily pains, not because he is overcome by them, but out of deliberate choice. But among those who pursue pleasures without deliberate choice, one is indeed led to the pursuit by pleasure, while another is led by the avoidance of pain; thus they differ from each other. It will, however, be obvious to everyone that a person who does something shameful without desiring it, or while desiring it only slightly, is a worse character than one who does so with intense desire; and also that he who strikes a man while not being angry is a worse character than the man who strikes another through the impulse of anger. For what would such a person do if he were influenced by passion? Hence, the intemperate man is worse than the incontinent man. Therefore, of the characters we have mentioned, one takes the form of effeminacy, while the other is incontinent. But the continent man is the opposite of the incontinent, and the man of endurance is the opposite of the effeminate man. For endurance consists in resisting, while continence consists in subduing. <meta>This page continues the comparison between the intemperate person and the incontinent person. is incurable, while the incontinent man is curable. For depravity seems to resemble diseases like dropsy and consumption, but incontinence seems to resemble epilepsy; since the former, indeed, is continuous without interruption, but the latter is not a continuous wickedness. And, in short, the category of incontinence is different from that of vice; for vice, indeed, is hidden, but incontinence is not hidden. But among these characters, those who are impulsively incontinent are better than those who possess reason, indeed, but do not abide by its decisions; for the latter are overcome by a weaker passion and not without previous deliberation, unlike the former. For the man who is incontinent through weakness resembles those who become intoxicated quickly from a little wine—even from less than what it takes to intoxicate most men.

That incontinence, therefore, is not a vice is evident; but perhaps it is partially so. For incontinence is contrary to deliberate choice, but vice conforms to it. The intemperate and the incontinent man, however, are similar in their actions, just as Demodocus said of the Milesians: namely, “The Milesians are not indeed foolish, yet they act as if they were so.” Similarly, incontinent men are not indeed unjust, yet they act unjustly. Since, therefore, the incontinent man is one who does not pursue bodily pleasures to excess and contrary to right reason because he is convinced to do so, while the intemperate man is convinced to pursue such pleasures because he is intemperate—this being the case, the former is easily induced to change his opinion, but the latter is not. For virtue preserves the principle <note>of right conduct</note>, but depravity corrupts it; and in actions *the goal* <note>or the end</note> is the principle, in the same manner as hypotheses are principles in mathematics. Hence, neither in the

Neither in mathematics, nor here, are principles taught; instead, virtue—whether physical or ethical—is the cause of correct opinion concerning the principle <note>of conduct</note>. A person of this description, therefore, is temperate, while the opposite character is an intemperate man. There is also a certain person who, through passion, departs from right reason; passion indeed subdues him so much that he does not act in accordance with right reason, and yet passion does not so far subdue him as to cause him to be convinced that pleasures of this type ought to be pursued extensively. This person is the incontinent man, who is better than the intemperate man and is not absolutely a bad man. For in him, that which is best—the principle <note>of right conduct</note>—is preserved. But there is another person opposite to this <note>namely, the continent man</note>, who persists <note>in the decision of reason</note> and is not diverted from it by passion. From these things, therefore, it is evident that one of these habits is virtuous, while the other is bad.

### CHAPTER IX.

Therefore, is the continent man someone who persists in any kind of reason and any kind of deliberate choice, or is he someone who persists specifically in right reason? And is the incontinent man someone who does not persist in any kind of deliberate choice or reason, or is he someone who simply fails to persist in right reason and an accurate deliberate choice, as we questioned before? Or should we say that the continent man is someone who persists by accident in any kind of reason and deliberate choice, but essentially in true reason and right deliberate choice—and that the incontinent man, conversely, does not persist in these? For if someone chooses or pursues one thing for the sake of another, he pursues and chooses the latter *per se* <note>for its own sake</note>, but the former only by accident. However, we are speaking simply of that which is *per se*. Hence, it is possible that one person may persist in any kind of opinion while the other may abandon any kind of opinion; however, the former simply persists in true opinion, while the latter does not. There are, however, some people who are inclined to persist in an opinion; these are called pertinacious—such as the obstinate and those who are not easily persuaded to change their minds. These people do, indeed, share something in common with the continent man, in the

<summary>This chapter questions whether continence involves adhering to any deliberate choice or specifically to right reason, concluding that while a continent man may accidentally adhere to any opinion, he essentially adheres to the truth. It also introduces the "pertinacious" or obstinate person as someone who resembles the continent man in their persistence but differs in their underlying character.</summary>

For the confident man is not changed through passion and desire; since when it so happens, he is easily persuaded. But the pertinacious <note>stubborn or obstinate</note> man is not persuaded by reason; since most people of this description yield to desire and are led by pleasures. Those persons, however, are pertinacious who hold certain opinions of their own, as well as unlearned and rustic men. And those who hold certain opinions of their own are pertinacious through pleasure and pain. For they rejoice when they are victorious if they are not induced to change their opinion, and they are grieved when their opinions—as if they were decrees—are without efficacy <note>effect</note>. Hence, they resemble the incontinent more than the continent man. 

But there are certain persons who do not persist in their opinions, yet not through incontinence, such as Neoptolemus in the *Philoctetes* of Sophocles; since he did not persist in his opinion, yet not on account of pleasure, but on account of the beautiful <note>noble or honorable</note> in conduct. For to him it was beautiful to speak the truth, though he was persuaded by Ulysses to lie. For not every one who does anything on account of pleasure is intemperate, or a bad, or an incontinent man; but he is intemperate who does it on account of base pleasure. Since, however, there is a character of such a kind as to be delighted less than is proper with corporeal <note>physical</note> pleasures, and who does not abide by the decisions of reason, the continent man is a medium <note>middle ground</note> between this character and the incontinent man. It is also necessary, if continence is a good thing, that both the contrary habits should be bad, as it appears they are.

Because, however, one of these characters <note>namely, the man who is delighted less than is proper with corporeal <note>physical</note> pleasures</note> exists among only a few men and is rarely seen; hence, as temperance appears to be contrary to intemperance alone, so also is continence contrary to incontinence.

But since many things are named based on similarity, the continence of the temperate man also follows according to similarity.

For both the continent and the temperate man do nothing contrary to reason through the influence of corporeal pleasures; but the one, indeed, <note>namely, the continent man</note> possesses, but the other does not possess, depraved desires.

And the one, indeed, is a man of such a description as not to be delighted contrary to reason, but the other is delighted, indeed, yet is not led by this delight.

The incontinent and the intemperate man also resemble each other, though they are indeed different characters.

Both, however, pursue corporeal delights; but the latter, indeed, thinks that it is proper, and the other does not think that it is proper, to pursue them.

<header>CHAP. X. ETHICS.</header>

## CHAPTER X.

NOR is it possible for the same person to be both prudent and incontinent at the same time; for we have shown that a prudent man is also a man of good character. Furthermore, a man is not prudent merely because he knows what ought to be done, but also because he acts correctly; however, the incontinent man does not act correctly. Nothing, however, prevents the incontinent man from being skillful. Hence, also, certain people sometimes appear to be prudent but incontinent, because skillfulness differs from prudence in the way we have explained before; as far as reason is concerned, these qualities are indeed close to each other, but they differ in terms of deliberate choice. Therefore, the incontinent man does not <note>possess reason</note> as one who knows and contemplates, but as one who is asleep or intoxicated. And he indeed acts voluntarily; for in a certain way, he acts knowing both what he does and the purpose for which he acts as he does. But he is not depraved, for his deliberate choice is good; so he is only half-depraved and not unjust, because he is not deceitful. For one type of incontinent man <note>that is, the man who is incontinent due to weakness</note> does not persist in what he has decided; while the man who is of a melancholy temperament,

<meta>Arist.</meta>

<summary>Aristotle explains that prudence and incontinence are incompatible because prudence requires acting correctly on one's knowledge. He distinguishes the incontinent man as one who acts voluntarily but without the persistence or proper deliberate choice of a prudent person.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Ethics, prudence, incontinence, skillfulness, deliberate choice, character, weakness, melancholy temperament</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of incontinence, specifically contrasting the character of the incontinent man with that of the bad man and comparing human nature to habit.</meta>

<page-num>274</page-num>
<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK VII.</header>

<note>i.e., the man who is precipitately <term>incontinent</term></note> is not, in short, inclined to deliberate. The <term>incontinent</term> man, therefore, resembles a city that indeed decrees everything that is proper and has good laws, but uses none of them, as Anaxandrides reproachfully said,

> ->The state consults, but neglects its own laws.<-

But the bad man resembles a city that indeed uses laws, but uses bad laws. <term>Incontinence</term>, however, and <term>continence</term> are concerned with that which exceeds the habit of the majority; for the continent man persists more, but the incontinent man less, than the majority are able to do <note>in the decisions of reason</note>. The incontinence, however, of the melancholy is more easily cured than the incontinence of those who indeed deliberate but do not persist in doing what they have deliberated to do. Those, also, who are incontinent from habit may be more easily cured than those who are naturally so; for it is easier to change habit than nature. For on this very account it is difficult to change habit—namely, because it resembles nature, as Evenus also says,

> ->By long attention habit is produced,<-
> ->And is at length as nature to mankind.<-

We have shown, therefore, what <term>continence</term> and <term>incontinence</term>, and also what <term>endurance</term> and <term>effeminacy</term> are, and how these habits exist with reference to each other.

<header>CHAP. XI. ETHICS.</header>

## CHAPTER XI.

The discussion, however, of pleasure and pain belongs to the person who philosophizes about political science; for he is, as it were, the architect of the end, looking toward which we say that one thing is evil, but another is simply good. Furthermore, it is also necessary that we should make these the objects of consideration. For we admit that ethical virtue and vice are concerned with pleasures and pains; and most people assert that happiness is accompanied by pleasure. For this reason, they also call the *blessed man* (μακάριον) one who especially rejoices (ἀπὸ τοῦ μάλιστα χαίρειν). To some, therefore, no pleasure appears to be good, neither essentially nor by accident; for they argue that good and pleasure are not the same thing. To others, some pleasures appear to be good, but many of them appear to be bad. Others again, in the third place, assert that even if all pleasures were good, the highest good nonetheless cannot be pleasure. 

In short, they say pleasure is not good because all pleasure is a <term>generation</term> <note>a process of coming into being</note> tending toward a sensible nature <note>as its end</note>; but no generation is of the same nature as its end; just as the act of building a house <note>the energy of building, οἰκοδόμησις</note> is not the same as the house itself. Again, the temperate man avoids pleasures. Furthermore, the prudent man pursues a free-

<summary>The author explains that the study of pleasure and pain is essential to political science and ethics, while noting three common arguments used to claim that pleasure is not the highest good.</summary>

To which may be added, that pleasures are an impediment to the activity of prudence, and this in proportion to the delight which they afford; as is the case with sexual pleasure; for no one, when engaged in this pleasure, can intellectually perceive anything. Again, pleasure is not the product of art; though everything which is good is the work of art. Furthermore, children and animals pursue pleasures. But that all pleasures are not worthy is indicated by this: that some are base, disgraceful, and harmful; for some pleasures produce disease. It is also evident that pleasure is not the best of things, because it is not an end, but a <term>generation</term> <note>a process of coming into being</note>. Such, therefore, are nearly all the assertions regarding pleasure.

## CHAPTER XIII.

IT is also admitted that pain is an evil and is to be avoided; for one kind of pain, indeed, is simply evil, but another is evil in a certain respect because it is an impediment. That, however, which is contrary to what is to be avoided—insofar as it is something to be avoided and is evil—is good. Pleasure, therefore, is necessarily something good. For the solution of Speusippus is not appropriate: namely, that pleasure is contrary to pain, just as the greater is contrary to the less and the equal; since no one will say that pleasure is a specific evil.<sup>1</sup> But nothing prevents a certain pleasure from being the best of things, even if certain pleasures are bad, just as a certain science <note>namely, wisdom</note> is the best of sciences, though some sciences are bad. Perhaps, too, it must necessarily be the case that if of every <term>habit</term> <note>disposition or state</note>

<sup>1</sup> If pleasure is opposed to pain, just as the greater is to the less and the equal, then it will be opposed as excess is to deficiency; and pleasure will be, of itself, an evil.

<summary>This page argues that because pain is an evil and an impediment, its contrary—pleasure—must be a good. It refutes Speusippus's argument that pleasure is an evil, suggesting instead that a specific pleasure can be the highest good even if other pleasures are harmful.</summary>

there are unimpeded energies, whether felicity is the energy of all the habits, or of some one of them, this energy, if it is unimpeded, is most desirable. But this is pleasure; so that a certain pleasure will be the best of things, even if many pleasures, should it so happen, are simply bad. And on this account, all men are of the opinion that a happy life is a pleasant life, and they reasonably connect pleasure with felicity. For no energy is perfect when it is impeded; but felicity is among the number of perfect energies. Hence, the happy man requires the goods pertaining to the body, and external possessions, and also good fortune, lest [the lack of] these should be an impediment [to his felicity]. Those, however, who say that a man, if he is a good man, may be happy even though he should be tormented on a wheel and fall into the greatest calamities, say nothing to the point, whether they assert this willingly or unwillingly. Because, however, the happy man requires good fortune, prosperity appears to some persons to be the same as felicity, though it is not the same; since prosperity, when it is excessive, is an impediment to felicity. Perhaps, likewise, it is no longer just to call prosperity [when it is excessive] good fortune; for the definition of prosperity has a reference to felicity. That all animals, too, and all men, pursue pleasure, is an indication that pleasure is in a certain respect the best of things.

<meta>This page continues the argument from Chapter XIII, specifically exploring why all beings pursue pleasure and how this pursuit is linked to a divine element within their nature, while clarifying the distinction between physical pleasures and the ultimate good.</meta>

<header>CHAP. XIII. ETHICS.</header>

Because, however, neither the same nature nor the same habit is, or appears to be, the best, not everyone pursues the same pleasure, even though pleasure is pursued by everyone. Perhaps, also, they do not pursue the pleasure they imagine, nor that which they say they pursue, but rather the same pleasure; *for all things naturally possess something divine.*¹ Physical pleasures, however, have inherited the name because people are mostly occupied with them, and all animals partake in them. Because, therefore, these pleasures alone are <note>generally</note> known, they are the only pleasures imagined to exist. It is, however, evident that unless pleasure is good, and also <term>energy</term> <note>activity or actualization</note>, it will not be possible for the happy man to live delightfully. For why would delight be necessary for a happy life,

---

¹ This is said by Aristotle in a very Platonic way: that all men pursue the same pleasure, by which he means the pleasure that is good, *because all things naturally possess something divine.* For because everything possesses this symbol or impression of divinity in its essence, there is in everything an innate desire for the first principle as the end of all things. Thus, even before appetite, there is a certain hidden perception of that which is first. Hence, also, as a result of this natural sense or perception—which is entirely hidden from the other senses—heavy and light objects move in a straight line toward a place suited to their natures and reject the opposite. Thus, the roots of trees seek out moisture and avoid dryness; and leaves wisely turn away from the shade and joyfully seek the light of the sun, along with his invigorating warmth. Through this wonderful sense and appetite, therefore, all things are turned toward the first without knowing the first; and as the great Theodorus the Platonist says, “*All things pray except the first.*” *In a similar way, also, the soul—prior to clear knowledge and the choice of the will—desires the One itself, through a natural sense and inclination impressed in her essence by the One.*

<summary>Aristotle explains that all beings are naturally driven toward a singular divine good, though many mistake common physical pleasures for this ultimate aim. He argues that pleasure and purposeful activity are essential for a truly happy and delightful life.</summary>

Why, then, should pain be avoided? Therefore, the life of a worthy person will not be more delightful unless his <term>energies</term> <note>activities or actualizations</note> are more delightful.

### CHAPTER XIV.

Regarding bodily pleasures, however, we must consider the claims of those who say that some pleasures are highly desirable—namely, those that are noble—but that bodily pleasures, with which the intemperate person is occupied, are not. Why, then, are the pains contrary to these pleasures bad? For good is contrary to evil. Or should we say that necessary pleasures are good in the sense that whatever is not evil is good, or that they are good up to a certain point? For in those habits and movements where there is no excess of what is better, there is no excess of pleasure; but in those where there is an excess of what is better, there is also an excess of pleasure. However, there is an excess of bodily goods; and a bad person becomes bad by pursuing the excess of pleasure, and not necessary pleasures.

<summary>Aristotle argues that if pleasure is not fundamentally good, then pain cannot be fundamentally evil, and the virtuous life would not be inherently more enjoyable. He then begins an analysis of bodily pleasures, suggesting they are necessary and good only to a point, whereas vice consists in pursuing them to excess.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Nicomachean Ethics, bodily pleasures, pain, excess, intemperance, virtue, happiness</keywords>

--- Page 276 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of bodily pleasures from Chapter XIV, specifically exploring why people pursue them to excess and why they are often mistaken for the highest good.</meta>

<page-num>283</page-num>
<header>CHAP. XIV. ETHICS.</header>

pleasures. For all depraved men take delight in food, wine, and sexual pleasure, but not in the proper manner. And they are affected in the opposite way regarding pain; for they do not avoid excessive pain, but pain altogether. For pain is not the opposite of excess, except to the person who pursues excess. Since, however, it is necessary not only to speak the truth but also to explain the cause of a false assertion—for this contributes to credibility, since when the reason why a thing seems to be true (even though it is not) appears reasonable, it then gives greater credibility to the truth—this being the case, it must be shown why bodily pleasures appear to be more desirable. In the first place, they appear to be so because they drive out pain; because of extreme pain, people pursue excessive bodily pleasure as a remedy. But these remedies are intense, and for this reason bodily pleasures are pursued because they become more apparent when placed beside their opposite. Pleasure, therefore, does not appear to be good for these two reasons, as we have observed before: because some pleasures are the actions of a depraved nature—either from birth, like those of a brute animal, or originating from habit, such as those of bad men—while other pleasures are remedies because they satisfy a lack. It is better to already possess the completed state than to be in the process of achieving it. Other pleasures, however, happen to be the pleasures of that which is in a perfect condition.¹ From accident, therefore, they

¹ That is, when desire is satisfied, as Aspasius explains the word <term>τελειουμένων</term>; or according to the interpretation of Thomas Aquinas, "when those who desire are *perfected* and restored to an entire condition of nature."

<summary>Aristotle explains that bodily pleasures are often pursued as intense remedies to drive out pain, which makes them seem more desirable than they truly are. He distinguishes between pleasures arising from a depraved nature and those that serve as restorative remedies for a deficiency.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of bodily pleasures from Chapter XIV, specifically explaining how physical states and temperaments—such as youth or a melancholic nature—drive individuals to seek intense pleasures as a relief from natural or accidental pain.</meta>

<page-num>284</page-num>

are worthy pleasures. Again, intense pleasures are pursued by those who are incapable of finding delight in other pleasures. These people, therefore, create certain cravings for themselves. Hence, when pleasures are harmless, they are not to be condemned; but when they are harmful, they are bad. For those who pursue these pleasures have no others to enjoy; and if neither these nor any others are present, the common people suffer because of <note>the lack</note> of nature. For a living creature is always under strain, as physical arguments testify; for as <term>physiologists</term> say, seeing and hearing are painful, but we are now accustomed to these <note>activities, and therefore do not perceive the pain</note>. In a similar manner in youth, because of the growth which then takes place, we are affected like those who are intoxicated, and youth is a delightful <note>period of human life</note>. But the <term>melancholic</term> naturally always require a remedy; for their bodies experience constant distress from their temperament <note>through the bitterness of the bile</note>, and they are always agitated by intense appetite. Pleasure, however, always drives out pain—both the pain that is the opposite of the pleasure and any pain that is accidental—if the pleasure is strong; for this reason, the melancholic become intemperate and corrupt. But the pleasures that are without pain do not involve excess; these are derived from things that are naturally delightful, and not just accidentally so. By things that are delightful by accident, I mean remedies; because it happens that we are cured of a malady while the healthy part of the body performs its function, the remedies appear to be delightful for this reason. But by things naturally delightful, I mean those which produce an action of such a nature <note>that is, which produce an...</note>

<summary>This page discusses why people pursue intense bodily pleasures, explaining that they often serve as a remedy for physical strain or the "bitterness" of certain temperaments. It distinguishes between accidental pleasures, which function as cures for pain, and natural pleasures that do not lead to excess.</summary>

<page-num>285</page-num>
<header>CHAP. XIV. ETHICS.</header>

activity essentially adapted to such a nature]. Nothing, however, which remains the same is always delightful, because our nature is not simple, but there is also something in it *different*, according to which it is perishable.¹

<note>¹ Aristotle, by asserting that our nature contains something which is *different*, and through which it is perishable, evidently alludes to and agrees with the doctrine of his divine master Plato in the *Timaeus* regarding the composition of the soul. For he there composes it from *essence, sameness,* and *difference*. To understand this, it is necessary to observe that there are five genera of being from which all things—after the first being—are composed: namely, *essence, permanency, motion, sameness,* and *difference*. For everything must possess *essence*; it must remain in its cause, from which it must also proceed and to which it must be *converted* <note>returned</note>; it must be the *same* as itself and certain other natures, and at the same time *different* from others and distinct within itself. Plato, however, for the sake of brevity, assumes only three of these in the composition of the soul: namely, *essence, sameness,* and *difference*; for the other two must necessarily exist in conjunction with these. As *sameness*, therefore, predominates in intelligible and indivisible essences, but *difference* in things sensory and divisible, the soul, by its alliance to a sensory nature, contains in itself something which is *different*. Because of this, in its irrational part, it is subject to the fate of death. For again, Plato in the same dialogue asserts that the soul is a medium between an essence that is indivisible and always existing according to *sameness* of being, and a nature that is divisible among bodies; that is, it is a medium between *intellect* and the whole of *corporeal life*.

When Aristotle also says *that nothing which remains the same is always delightful to us because our nature is not simple*, we may infer that it is impossible for the human soul to always remain in one condition of being, even if that condition were attended with the highest happiness of which it is capable. Therefore, as the rational soul is immortal, it must necessarily circulate. For when it is in a state of the most exalted happiness, eventually becoming weary of its blessedness, it gradually falls into a subordinate condition of being, and from this fallen condition, it again gradually rises to the peak of its happiness.</note>

<vocab>Aristotle, Plato, Timaeus, Ethics, soul, essence, sameness, difference, five genera of being, happiness, immortality, rational soul</vocab>

<summary>Aristotle argues that humans cannot find permanent delight in any single thing because our nature is composite rather than simple. A commentary explains this through the Platonic doctrine of the soul's composition from essence, sameness, and difference, concluding that the soul must naturally cycle through different states of existence.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Plato, Timaeus, nature of the soul, essence, sameness, difference, five genera of being, immortality, rational soul, Ethics, Thomas Taylor</keywords>

<page-num>286</page-num>

# THE NICOMACHEAN ETHICS. BOOK VII.

Hence, if one part does anything, this is unnatural to the other nature. But when both parts are equalized, that which is performed appears to be neither painful nor pleasing. For if there is any being whose nature is simple, the same action will always be most delightful to this being. Hence, God always rejoices according to one simple pleasure; for there is not only an activity of [corporeal] motion, but also of immobility; and pleasure exists more abundantly in rest than in motion. But change is the sweetest of all things, according to the poet, because of a certain depravity. For as a depraved man is changeable, so likewise is the nature which requires change; for it is neither simple, nor good. And thus we have spoken concerning continence and incontinence, pleasure and pain, and have shown what each of them is, and how some of them are good, while others are bad. It now remains for us to speak concerning friendship.

<summary>Aristotle explains that while humans require change due to their complex and imperfect natures, a simple nature like God finds constant pleasure in rest. This concludes his analysis of continence, incontinence, pleasure, and pain, leading into the discussion on friendship.</summary>

### CHAPTER I.

AFTER these things, it follows that we should discuss friendship; for it is a certain virtue, or exists alongside virtue. Besides this, it is most necessary for life; for no one would choose to live without friends, even if they possessed every other good. For the rich, and those in power or high office, seem to be especially in need of friends. For what advantage is there in such prosperity if the opportunity for beneficence <note>The act of doing good or being charitable.</note> is taken away? Beneficence is primarily directed toward friends, and it is most praised when it is performed in this way. Or how can prosperity be preserved and kept safe without friends? For the more...

<summary>Aristotle introduces the topic of friendship, arguing that it is a necessary virtue for all people, regardless of their wealth or status. He suggests that friends are essential for both the exercise of good deeds and the protection of one's prosperity.</summary>

the greater it is, the more insecure it is. But in poverty and other misfortunes, friends are considered to be the only refuge. Friendship is also useful to the young, by preventing them from error, and to elderly men, through the attention it pays to their needs and the assistance it provides for their limitations in action, which arise from the weakness <note>Originally "imbecility," meaning physical or mental frailty.</note> of age. It is likewise useful to those in the prime of life, because it aids them in performing noble <note>Originally "beautiful," referring to actions that are morally fine or virtuous.</note> actions.

->When two in concord meet.¹<-

For because of it, they are more able both to think and to act. Friendship also appears to be naturally inherent in the parent toward the offspring; and this is true not only in the human race, but also in birds and most animals, as well as between those of the same nation, and especially among humans; hence, we praise those who are philanthropic. One may also see when traveling how accommodating and friendly every person is to another. It seems, too, that friendship holds cities together, and legislators pay more attention to it than to justice. For concord appears to be something similar to friendship; legislators are especially desirous of bringing this about, and they primarily work to expel sedition, which is hostile to concord. And indeed, when citizens are friends, there is no need for justice; but even when they are just, they still require friendship. Among things that are just, that which is most just also appears to be of a friendly nature. Friendship is not only necessary, but it is also a noble thing. For we praise those who are

lovers of friends; and an abundance of friends appears to be one among the number of noble <note>Originally "beautiful," translating the Greek *kalon*, which implies moral excellence.</note> things. Again, some are of the opinion that the same people are good men and friends. There is, however, no small controversy concerning friendship. For some consider it to be a certain similarity, and that similar people are friends; from which, also, it is said "like tends toward like, a jackdaw to a jackdaw," etc. Others, on the contrary, say that all such people are "potters" to each other.¹ And they investigate these things from a more profound and physical origin; as Euripides says:

> Earth, when she is dry, rejoices in the rain;
> And venerable heaven, when filled with rain,
> Delights to fall on earth. ———

And Heraclitus asserts "that what is in opposition is beneficial; that the most beautiful harmony results from differing things; and that all things arise from strife." Others, however, are of a contrary opinion regarding friendship, and among these is Empedocles. For he says "that similar things aspire after similar things." Therefore, we shall omit those questions that are of a physical nature, for they are not suited to the present inquiry <note>Originally "speculation," meaning philosophical investigation.</note>. But we shall direct our attention to those that pertain to human affairs, and to the character <note>Originally "manners."</note> and passions of men; such as whether there

For things that are specifically different can also admit of the more and the less; but we have spoken of these things before.

## CHAPTER II.

THESE things, however, will perhaps become evident when the object of love is known; for it seems that not everything is loved, but only that which is lovable; and this is either what is good, or what is pleasant, or what is useful. That, however, may appear to be useful through which some good or pleasure is obtained. Hence, the good and the pleasant will be objects of love as ends. Therefore, do men love that which is [simply] good, or that which is good for them? For these are sometimes in conflict. A similar inquiry may also be made concerning the pleasant. It appears, however, that everyone loves that which is good for himself; and that while the good is that which is simply the object of love, that which is good for each person is the object of love for that person.

<vocab>friendship, wicked, species, love, pleasant, useful, Aristotle, Nicomachean Ethics</vocab>

<summary>Aristotle questions whether friendship can exist among the wicked and whether there are multiple types of friendship, ultimately identifying the three objects of love: the good, the pleasant, and the useful.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Nicomachean Ethics, types of friendship, objects of love, the good, the pleasant, the useful, moral philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the objects of love—the good, the pleasant, and the useful—and applies these categories to the definition of friendship.</meta>

<page-num>291</page-num>
<header>CHAP. II. ETHICS.</header>

what is good for each person is the object of love for that person. Each person, however, does not love what is truly good for them, but what appears to be so. But this makes no difference, for that which appears to be good will be the object of love. However, since there are three things through which love is produced—namely, the good, the pleasant, and the useful—friendship is not said to exist in the love of inanimate things; for there is no reciprocal love, nor a wish that any good might befall them. For it would perhaps be ridiculous to wish for some good to happen to wine; if a person does so, they only wish that it may be preserved so that they may have it. But it is said to be necessary to wish a friend well for their own sake; and those who have this wish for their friends are called benevolent, even if the same wish is not returned by the other person. For it is said that benevolence is friendship when it is mutual, though perhaps it should be added that this benevolence must not be hidden. For many people are benevolent toward those they have never seen, because they believe them to be worthy or useful; and those they have never seen may also be benevolent toward them. They indeed appear to be benevolent toward each other; but how can they be called friends when they are unaware of how each feels toward the other? Therefore, to be friends, it is necessary that they be benevolent and wish each other well for one of the reasons we have mentioned: namely, for the sake of the good, the pleasant, or the useful.

### CHAPTER III:

THESE, however, are specifically different from each other; and therefore, the types of love and the friendships also differ. For there are three species of friendship, equal in number to the objects of friendly love; since in each there is a reciprocal love that is not hidden. But those who love each other wish well to each other, to the extent that they love. Those, therefore, who love each other on account of utility, do not mutually love for each other's own sake, but only insofar as they obtain some benefit from each other. This is also the case with those who love on account of pleasure. For they do not love those who are versatile because they possess certain character qualities, but because they afford them pleasure. And those who love on account of utility possess this friendly love on account of the benefit which they derive from it. Those, likewise, who love on account of pleasure, love on account of that which is delightful to them. And the attachment of these people is not based on the person, but is produced only insofar as the object of their attachment is useful or delightful. These friendships, therefore, are accidental; for the object of their attachment is not loved for being the kind of person he is, but only insofar as he provides them with some good or some pleasure. Such friendships,

<summary>Aristotle explains that there are three types of friendship based on utility, pleasure, and virtue, noting that utility and pleasure-based friendships are accidental because they center on what a person provides rather than who they are.</summary>
<keywords>three species of friendship, utility, pleasure, reciprocal love, accidental friendship, Nicomachean Ethics</keywords>

--- Page 286 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on why friendships based on utility and pleasure are easily dissolved once the underlying benefit or enjoyment disappears. It specifically examines how age influences these types of associations.</meta>

<page-num>293</page-num>
### CHAPTER III: ETHICS.

therefore, are easily dissolved, as the objects of them do not remain in a similar condition; for if they are no longer delightful or useful, they cease to be loved. And what is useful is not permanent, but becomes different at different times. Hence, when that through which they were friends is dissolved, their friendship is also dissolved, because it existed only for the sake of that utility. 

A friendship of this kind, however, appears to exist especially among elderly men; for those who have arrived at this period of life do not pursue what is delightful, but what is useful. Nor is the delightful pursued by those young men in the prime of life who make utility the object of their pursuit. But such persons do not live together very much, for sometimes they are not pleasing to each other. They do not, therefore, require an association of this kind unless they are useful to one another, for they are delightful to each other only so far as they hope for some good. Among these friendships based on utility, hospitable friendship <note>The relationship between a host and a guest from a different city or land, known in Greek as xenia.</note> is also ranked. 

But the friendship of young men appears to exist for the sake of pleasure; for they live according to passion and especially pursue that which is delightful to them and that which is present in the moment. In consequence of the changing of age, however, other things become delightful; for this reason, they rapidly become and cease to be friends. Their friendship changes along with that which they find delightful, and the change of such pleasure is rapid. Young men are also romantic; for much of the romantic inclination exists according to passion and for the sake of pleasure. Hence they love and rapidly cease to love, frequently changing in the same day. They wish, however, to spend the day with each other and to live together; for in this way they obtain what their friendship requires.

<summary>This page explains that friendships based on utility and pleasure are fleeting because they depend on changing needs and passions. It notes that utility-based friendships are common among the elderly, while pleasure-based friendships are typical of the young.</summary>

The friendship, however, of good men, and of those who are similar in virtue, is perfect; for they similarly wish well to each other, so far as they are good; and they are good in themselves. But those who wish well to their friends for their friends' sake are especially friends; for they are thus disposed toward them on their own account <note>i.e., personally</note>, and not by accident. The friendship of these, therefore, remains as long as they are good men; and virtue is stable. And each of these is simply good, and good to his friend; for good men are simply good and are useful to each other. In a similar manner, they are also delightful to each other; for good men are simply and mutually delightful. For to each, their own actions and similar actions <note>namely, those that are similarly virtuous</note> are attended with pleasure. But the actions of good men are of this kind or resemble them. It reasonably follows, also, that such a friendship is stable; for all those things exist in it together which ought to be present among friends. For all friendship is on account of what is good or on account of pleasure—either simply or relative to the one who loves—and this according to a certain similarity. But in this friendship <note>that is, in the friendship founded on virtue</note>, all the above-mentioned qualities are essentially inherent; since in this all the rest are similar, and that which is simply good is also simply delightful. These things, however, are especially lovely; and in these, the most excellent love and friendship principally exist. But it is likely that such friendships are rare, for persons of this description are few. Furthermore, virtuous friendship requires time and familiarity; for according to the proverb, it is not possible for men to know each other until they have eaten a peck of salt together. Nor is it appropriate for one person to become inti-

They quickly, therefore, develop the wish to be friends, but they do not develop friendship itself. ## CHAPTER IV.

The friendship, however, which exists for the sake of the pleasant <note>the delectable</note> has a similarity to virtuous friendship; for good men are also pleasant to each other. This is also the case with friendship that exists for the sake of utility; for good men are also useful <note>that is, useful</note> to each other. But among these <note>namely, those who are friends through pleasure</note>, friendships are especially permanent when an equality, such as an equality of pleasure, exists between them. When the flower of age, however, is no more, sometimes the friendship also ends. For to the one the sight of his friend is no longer pleasing, and to the other bland attention is no longer paid. Many of these, however, continue permanent in their friendship, if each loves the character of each from custom, in consequence of possessing similar manners. But the friendship of those who are friends on account of utility, is dissolved together with the advantage; for they were not friends of each other, but of the profit. On account of pleasure, therefore, and on account of utility, it is possible for bad men to be friends to each other, and also for worthy with bad men, and for those who are neither good nor bad with each other, and with the good or the bad; but it is evident that the good alone can be friends through or on account of themselves. For bad men are not delighted with each other, unless each derives some advantage from the other. And the friendship of good men alone is unattended with slander; for it is not easy to believe anything <note>bad</note> of him who has been tried by us for a long time. Among these also there is mutual trust, and a confidence that the one will not injure the other, and such other particulars as are thought worthy to be ranked in true friendship. ### CHAP. IV. ETHICS.

...connected together on account of utility, in the same manner as cities (for warlike alliances between cities appear to take place for the sake of advantage); and since those are likewise called friends who, like children, love each other on account of pleasure, perhaps, indeed, it is necessary that we also should call such persons friends, and should admit that there are many types of friendship. And we must indeed name the friendship of good men, in as much as they are good, as that which is primarily and properly so called; but we must admit that the rest are called friendships because of their similarity to it. For they are friends as long as there is something good and similar between them; since that which is pleasurable is something good to the lovers of pleasure. These friendships, however, are not very closely joined, nor do the same persons become friends for the sake of both the useful and the pleasurable; for things which arise by accident are not often united. But friendship being divided into these types, bad men, indeed, will be friends on account of pleasure or advantage, through which they are similar; but good men will be friends on their own account; for they are friends in as much as they are good. These, therefore, are friends in the absolute sense; but the others are friends only by accident, and by being compared to these.

## CHAPTER V.

Just as with the virtues, where some people are said to be good according to their character, while others are good according to their activity, it is the same with friendship. For those friends who live together delight in and benefit each other; but those who are asleep or are separated by distance are not actively engaged in friendship, yet they are still disposed in such a way that they are able to act as friendship requires. For distance does not destroy friendship itself, but only the active exercise of it. If, however, the absence is long, it seems to lead to a forgetting of the friendship; which is why it is said that *silence dissolves many friendships*. Neither the elderly nor sour people seem well-suited for friendship, for there is little pleasure to be found in them. No one can constantly spend time with someone who is mournful or unpleasant, for nature seems especially to avoid what is painful and to seek out what is pleasing. Those who welcome each other's company and yet do not live together are more like well-wishers than friends, since nothing is so characteristic of friends as living together. For those in need seek out some benefit, and even those who are happy and blessed constantly seek each other's company; for it is not in the least bit fitting

<summary>This chapter distinguishes between the internal state of friendship and its active practice, noting that while distance hinders the activity of friendship, only long-term absence or a lack of communication destroys the bond itself. It also emphasizes that living together and sharing pleasure are essential components of a functional friendship.</summary>

<keywords>habit vs. activity, distance in friendship, silence, elderly and austere, living together, benevolence, well-wishers</keywords>

--- Page 292 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the necessity of social interaction for friendship, emphasizing that true friendship is a stable state of character rather than a fleeting emotion.</meta>

<page-num>299</page-num>
<header>CHAP. V. : ETHICS.</header>

...that these people should lead a solitary life. But it is not possible for people to live together if their company is not delightful, and if they are not pleased with the same things, which is what fellowship appears to involve. The friendship of good men, therefore, is friendship in the truest sense, as we have frequently observed. For that which is simply good or delightful appears to be lovable and desirable; but to each person, that which is lovable and desirable is what seems so to him. A good man, however, is lovable and desirable to another good man for both of these reasons.

<term>Affection</term> <note>Originally "dilection," meaning the feeling of liking.</note>, however, is similar to a passion, but friendship is like a <term>habit</term> <note>A stable state of character or disposition.</note>; for affection can be felt even toward inanimate things. But reciprocal love exists in conjunction with deliberate choice, and deliberate choice comes from habit. We also wish well to those whom we love for their own sake, not because of a passing passion, but because of a habit. And those who love a friend love what is good for themselves; for when a good man becomes a friend, he becomes a good thing to the person to whom he is a friend. Each, therefore, loves what is good for himself, and they mutually return to each other what is equal, both in wishing well and in providing delight; for it is said that equality is friendship. These qualities are especially present in the friendship of good men.

<summary>This passage argues that true friendship is a stable state of character (habit) rather than a mere feeling (passion), and that it requires mutual delight, reciprocal well-wishing, and equality.</summary>
<keywords>friendship, affection, habit, deliberate choice, equality, virtue, Aristotle, Ethics</keywords>

## CHAPTER VI.

Friendship, however, exists to a lesser degree among austere and elderly men, in proportion as they are more morose and take less delight in social associations; for these associations appear to be especially friendly and effective at fostering friendship. Hence, young men rapidly become friends, but not elderly men; for they do not become friends with those in whom they take no delight. In a similar manner, neither do the austere quickly become friends. But people of this description are indeed benevolent toward each other; for they wish well and provide assistance for each other’s needs. They are not, however, very close friends, because they do not constantly associate with one another, nor are they delighted with each other—both of which appear to be the defining features of friendship. 

But it is not possible to be a friend to many in terms of perfect friendship, just as it is not possible to love many at one and the same time; for this resembles an excess, and a thing of this kind is naturally adapted to take place toward one person. Moreover, it is not easy for many people to please the same person very much at one and the same time, nor perhaps would it be a good thing if it were easy. Experience and familiarity, likewise, are necessary

<summary>This passage explains why elderly and austere people form fewer friendships due to their lack of social interaction and delight, and it asserts that perfect friendship can only exist toward one person at a time.</summary>
<keywords>friendship, benevolence, associations, perfect friendship, Nicomachean Ethics, Aristotle, elderly, austerity</keywords>

<header>CHAP. VI. ETHICS.</header>

But it is possible to please many people for the sake of utility and delight; for there are many people of this sort <note>namely, those who are to be pleased in this way</note>, and a small amount of experience is enough for this purpose. Of these two, however, the friendship that exists through what is delightful is more similar <note>to true friendship</note> when the same things are done by both people, and they take delight in each other or in the same things. This is the case in the friendships of young men, for there is more of a generous spirit in these friendships. But the friendship that exists for the sake of utility is the friendship of merchants <note>and of those who are occupied in coarse and narrow-minded pursuits</note>. 

And those who are truly blessed <note>namely, who are as happy as the condition of human nature allows</note> do not lack anything useful or delightful <note>because they already possess everything of this kind</note>. For they wish to live with certain people, and they only endure what is painful for a short time; since no one could endure it continually—not even the good itself—if it were accompanied by distress. Therefore, they look for friends who can provide them with delight. It is, however, perhaps necessary to look for good men who are of this kind <note>that is, who are delightful</note> and who are also delightful to their friends; for in this way, they will have the qualities that ought to be present in friends. 

But people in authority and power seem to utilize their friends by making a distinction between them; for some are useful and others are delightful to them. However, the same results are not often achieved by both types. For they neither look for those who are delightful in conjunction with virtue, nor for those who are useful for noble purposes; but, seeking pleasure, they look for

<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK VIII.</header>

We have, however, already observed that the virtuous man is at the same time pleasing and useful. But such a person will not be the friend of someone who surpasses others in power and authority, unless that person also surpasses others in virtue; for if he does not, the one who is superior in power will not be able to achieve equality through proportion <note>literally, "according to the analogous"</note>. Men of this description, however, are rare.

The above-mentioned friendships, therefore, consist in equality. For either the same things are achieved by both, and they mutually wish for the same things, or they exchange one thing for another—as, for instance, pleasure for utility. But we have already observed that these are friendships to a lesser degree, and that they are less permanent. They appear, however, because of their similarity and dissimilarity to the same thing, to be and not to be friendships. For from their similarity to the friendship that is based on virtue, they appear to be friendships; since one of these contains what is delightful, while the other contains what is useful. Both of these are inherent in virtuous friendship. They differ, however, in this: virtuous friendship is free from calumny and is stable, while these others change rapidly and differ in many other ways. And because of this dissimilarity to the friendship that is based on virtue, they do not appear to be friendships.

## CHAPTER VII.

THERE is, however, another species of friendship, which exists according to superiority <note>This refers to friendship between unequals, where one person has a higher status or different role than the other.</note>; such as that between a father and his son, and in short between an older and a younger man, between a husband and his wife, and between every ruler and the person who is ruled. But these friendships, also, differ from each other. For there is not the same friendship between parents and children as there is between rulers and the ruled; nor is it the same between a father and son as between a son and his father; nor between a husband and wife as between a wife and husband. For the virtue and also the role of each of these are different; and the reasons for which they love are different. Their loves, therefore, and their friendships are different. Hence, neither are the same things done by each toward the other, nor is it appropriate that they should be required. But when children, indeed, give to their parents those things which offspring ought to give to those who brought them into the world, and parents give to their children those things which it is proper to give to their offspring, then the friendship between such people will be stable and excellent. It is, however, necessary in all the friendships which exist according to superiority—

<summary>This chapter introduces friendships based on inequality or hierarchy, explaining that the roles, virtues, and obligations differ depending on the specific relationship, such as between parents and children or rulers and subjects.</summary>
<keywords>friendship, hierarchy, social roles, parents and children, marriage, virtue, ethics, Aristotle</keywords>

--- Page 297 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on friendships involving superiority, specifically addressing how love and equality function when there is an imbalance between the parties.</meta>

<page-num>204</page-num>
<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK VIII.</header>

proportionality, so that the love should be analogous; as, for instance, that the better person should be loved to a greater degree than they love, and that this should also be the case with the more useful person, and in a similar manner with each of the rest. For when love exists according to merit, then in a certain respect equality is produced, which appears to be the defining characteristic of friendship. Equality, however, does not seem to exist in the same way in matters of justice as it does in friendship. For in matters of justice, indeed, the equality which is according to merit ranks first, while that which is according to quantity ranks second. But in friendship, the equality which is according to quantity ranks first, and that which is according to merit ranks second. 

This, however, becomes evident if there is a great gap in virtue or vice, or wealth, or some other things; for then they are no longer friends, nor do they consider themselves qualified to be so. But this is most apparent in the gods, for they most abundantly surpass everyone in everything that is good.<note>A good person, because they are similar to the divine, may be said to be the friend of divinity. But since the divine nature is so transcendent, there is no reciprocal exchange of similarity. When it is said that God is the friend of good people, it only means that they share in divinity through their closeness, alliance, and readiness, as much as is possible for human nature. In this way, the following beautiful passage from Diogenes must be understood: “All things belong to the gods; good people are the friends of the gods; and friends share all things in common. It is impossible, therefore, for a person beloved by the gods not to be happy, or for a wise and just person not to be beloved by the gods.”</note> It is also evident in kings; for those who are much inferior to them do not think themselves worthy of their friendship.

<summary>Aristotle explains that in friendships between unequals, love must be proportional to merit to maintain a sense of equality. However, if the gap between two people in status, wealth, or virtue becomes too vast—as between gods and men or kings and subjects—friendship becomes impossible.</summary>

### CHAP. VII. ETHICS.

...worthy to be their friends. Nor do those who have no worth aspire to be friends with the best or the wisest of men. In cases such as these, therefore, there is no precise definition of the limit, as long as they remain friends. For although many things may be taken away, the friendship may still remain; but if they are separated by a great distance from each other, as is the case between man and divinity, friendship no longer exists. For this reason, it is also doubted whether friends would wish the greatest of goods for their friends—such as, for instance, for them to become gods; for in that case, they would no longer be friends to them. Nor, therefore, would they be a benefit to them; for friends are a benefit to each other. Hence, if it is rightly said that a friend wishes well to his friend for his friend's own sake, it is necessary that the friend should remain as he is. One may wish that the greatest good might befall his friend, provided he remains a man. And perhaps he does not wish that every possible good may befall him; for everyone wishes to obtain good for himself above all.
