### CHAPTER VIII.

The majority of people, however, seem because of ambition to be more desirous of being loved than of loving. Consequently, most people love flatterers. For a flatterer is a friend who is inferior [to the person he flatters], or pretends to be so, and also claims to love to a greater degree than he is loved. But being loved seems to be close to being honored, which most people strive for. It seems, however, that they do not choose honor for its own sake, but incidentally. For most people are delighted when they are honored by those in power, because of the hope [of the benefits they may receive from them]; for they imagine they will obtain from them what they need. They are delighted, therefore, with honor as a sign that they will be helped. But those who strive for honor from virtuous and intelligent people desire to confirm their own opinion of themselves. They rejoice, therefore, that they are virtuous individuals, trusting the judgment of those who say that they are virtuous; but they are delighted to be loved *for its own sake*. Hence, it would seem that this is a better thing than being honored, and that friendship is something desirable in itself. Friendship, however, seems to consist more in loving than in

<summary>Aristotle explains that most people prefer being loved over loving because they associate it with being honored, though true satisfaction comes from being loved for one's own sake rather than seeking honor for gain. He concludes that friendship is inherently desirable and is defined more by the act of loving than by being loved.</summary>

For some mothers give their children to be privately educated by others and love them, knowing them to be their own offspring, but they are not anxious to be loved in return if both cannot be achieved; it appears to them to be sufficient if they see their children doing well. And they love their offspring, even though the children are unable to pay that attention to their mother which is appropriate because they do not know her. Since, therefore, friendship consists more in loving than in being loved, and we praise those who are lovers of their friends, to love appears to be the virtue of friends. Hence, those in whom this quality exists according to merit are stable friends, and the friendship of such people is stable. 

But in this way, also, those who are unequal may especially become friends; for thus they will be equalized. Equality, however, and similarity are friendship, and especially the similarity of those who resemble each other in virtue; for being stable in themselves, they are also stable toward each other, and neither require anything wicked nor are they subservient to anything of this kind, but, as I may say, they prohibit what is base. For it is the responsibility of good men neither to err themselves nor to permit their friends to be subservient to erroneous conduct. But wicked men have no stability, for they do not remain consistent even with themselves; they are only friends for a short time, being delighted with the wickedness of each other. Useful and pleasing men, however, remain friends for a longer time, for they continue as friends as long as they provide each other with pleasure and advantage. But the friendship which exists on account of utility appears to be composed of opposites, such as the friendship of

Perhaps, also, one contrary does not desire another in essence, but only by accident. But the appetite is directed toward the <term>medium</term>; for this is good. Thus, for instance, it is good for a dry thing not to become moist, but to reach the <term>medium</term> <note>between dryness and moisture</note>; and in a similar manner for a hot thing and other substances. These things, however, must be set aside; for they are more irrelevant than is appropriate.

<header>CHAP. IX. ETHICS.</header>

# CHAPTER IX.

It seems, however, as we said in the beginning, that both friendship and justice are concerned with and exist in the same things; for in every community there appears to be a certain justice and friendship. People, therefore, call their fellow sailors and fellow soldiers friends, and likewise those who associate with them in other activities. But the extent of their friendship corresponds to the extent of their associations; for the same is true of the extent of justice. The proverb also rightly says, “that all things are common among friends”; for friendship consists in sharing. Among brothers, however, and close companions, all things are common; but among others they are limited to certain boundaries; and to some indeed more so, but to others less; for with respect to friendship, also, some are friendships in a greater and others in a lesser degree. 

What is just also differs; for there is not the same justice between parents and children as there is between brothers, nor is it the same between associates as it is between fellow citizens. The same holds true in other types of friendships. Wrongs, therefore, are different toward each of these groups, and they become more serious the closer the friendship is with the person harmed. Thus, for instance, it is more terrible

<summary>Aristotle explains that friendship and justice are closely linked and exist within every form of community or association. He notes that the depth of friendship and the requirements of justice vary depending on the relationship, with harms being more significant when they occur between close friends.</summary>

<keywords>friendship, justice, community, associations, sharing, moral obligation, social relationships, Nicomachean Ethics</keywords>

--- Page 303 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter IX of Book VIII, further examining how the severity of an injustice is tied to the closeness of a friendship and how all social associations are branches of the larger political community.</meta>

<page-num>310</page-num>
<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK VIII.</header>

thing to defraud an associate of money than a fellow citizen; and not to assist a brother than to refuse assistance to a stranger; and to strike a father than to strike any other person. But justice is naturally adapted to increase at one and the same time with friendship, as they exist in the same things and are equally extended. All associations or societies, however, resemble the parts of the political or civil community. For men journey together with a view to a certain advantage, and in order to procure something which pertains to human life. Political community, also, appears to exist for the sake of advantage, to have been established with a view to this from the beginning, and to continue so. For the attention of legislators is directed to this, and they say that what is advantageous in common is just. Other associations, therefore, partially aspire after utility. Thus, sailors aspire after the utility pertaining to navigation, or to the acquisition of wealth, or something of the like kind; but soldiers aspire after the utility pertaining to war, whether riches are the object of their desire, or victory, or the capture of cities. The like, also, takes place among tribes and the populace. Some associations, however, appear to have been formed on account of pleasure, such as the association from the celebration of festivals, or from societies instituted to promote good fellowship; for these exist for the sake of sacrificing and social interaction. But all these appear to be subject to political community; for political community does not aspire after present advantage, but to that which pertains to the whole of life; performing sacrifices, and for this purpose forming assemblies, bestowing honors on the gods, and affording a cessation from labor, in conjunction with pleasure. He argues that all specific social groups—whether for trade, war, or pleasure—are subordinate to the political community, which seeks the long-term common advantage of all its members.</summary>

# CHAPTER X.

THERE are, however, three types of a <term>polity</term> (political system), and an equal number of deviations from them, which are, so to speak, the corruptions of these systems. The political systems are: a kingdom, an aristocracy, and a third based on the distribution of honors according to wealth, which it seems may be appropriately called a <term>timocracy</term>. Most people, however, are accustomed to call it <note>simply</note> a <term>polity</term>. Among these, a kingdom is the best, and a <term>timocracy</term> is the worst. The deviation from a kingdom is <term>tyranny</term>; for both are <term>monarchies</term>. However, they differ greatly from each other. For the tyrant looks to his own advantage, but the king looks to the advantage of those whom he governs. *For he is not truly a king who is not self-sufficient, and who does not surpass his subjects in every kind of good.* A man of this description lacks nothing. Therefore, his attention will not be directed

<summary>Aristotle categorizes political systems into three forms—monarchy, aristocracy, and timocracy—and identifies their corruptions, specifically distinguishing a king's altruism from a tyrant's selfishness. He asserts that a true monarch must be self-sufficient and superior in virtue to ensure he rules for the benefit of his subjects.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, political systems, monarchy, aristocracy, timocracy, tyranny, kingship, political community, virtue, government corruption</keywords>

--- Page 305 ---
<meta>This page continues the classification of political systems and their deviations, specifically detailing how each system degrades into its corrupt form and introducing domestic parallels for these structures.</meta>

<page-num>312</page-num>
<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK VIII.</header>

to what is advantageous to himself, but to the benefit of those whom he governs; for he who is not a person of this description would be merely a king by election. A tyrant, however, is the opposite of a king <note>properly so called</note>, for he pursues his own good. And from this it is more evident that he is the worst of rulers; for that which is the opposite of the best is the worst.

The transition from a kingdom is into a tyranny; for a tyranny is the corruption of a monarchy. And a corrupt king becomes a tyrant. The transition from an aristocracy is into an oligarchy through the vice of the rulers, who distribute civil offices in a manner contrary to merit; they bestow upon themselves all, or the greater part, of everything that is good, and always appoint the same persons as magistrates, paying more attention to wealth than to anything else. Therefore, those who govern are few, and are corrupt instead of being the most worthy men.

But the transition from a timocracy is into a democracy, since these political systems border on each other. For in a timocracy, the multitude also has power, and all those who are of a certain wealth are equal. A democracy, however, is corrupt in the smallest degree; for it deviates but little from the form of a <term>polity</term> <note>that is, from a timocracy</note>.

In this manner, therefore, political systems are especially changed; for thus they are changed the least and most easily. The resemblances, however, and, as it were, the models of them may be derived from families. For the community, or society, between a father and his children has the form of a kingdom; for a father pays attention to his children <note>for their own sakes</note>. Hence, also, Homer calls Jupiter father; for the purpose of a kingdom is to be a paternal government. But among the Persians, the government of a father is tyrannical;

for they use their children as slaves. Likewise, the rule of a master over his servants is tyrannical; for in this form of rule, only that which is advantageous to the master is carried out. This, therefore, appears to be right; but the Persian system of rule is erroneous. For where things differ, the forms of rule also differ. But the relationship between husband and wife appears to be aristocratic. For the man rules according to merit, and in those matters where it is appropriate for the man to rule; but he permits his wife to rule over those things suited to be managed by a woman. If the man, however, exercises dominion over everything, the rule is changed into an oligarchy; for he does this contrary to merit, and not because he is the more virtuous character. But it sometimes happens that women, because they are heiresses, rule [even in matters pertaining to men]. Therefore, the rule in this case is not based on virtue, but exists through wealth and power, just as it is in oligarchies. And the rule of brothers resembles a timocracy; for they are equals, except for the differences in their ages. Hence, if there is a great difference in their ages, the friendship is no longer fraternal. But a democratic government is especially visible in those families which are without a master; for here, everyone rules equally. In those families, also, where the ruler is a man of weak understanding, everyone has the power to act as they please.

<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK VIII.</header>

## CHAPTER XI.

In each of the polities, however, friendship appears to have the same extent as justice. And the friendship, indeed, between a king and his subjects consists in a superiority of beneficence; for he benefits his subjects because, being a good man, he is attentive to their interests like a shepherd, in order that they may prosper. This is why Homer calls Agamemnon the shepherd of the people. Paternal friendship is similar, but it differs in the magnitude of the benefits it confers. For the father is the cause of his child's existence—which appears to be a matter of the greatest consequence—and he also provides the child with nourishment and education. The same things are likewise attributed to ancestors; for a father is naturally suited to rule over his children, ancestors over their descendants, and kings over their subjects. But these friendships consist in superiority; for this reason, parents are also honored. Therefore, justice in these relationships is not the same for both parties, but exists according to merit; for the friendship exists in the same way. There is, likewise, the same friendship between a husband and wife as in an aristocracy; for it exists according to virtue, and a more substantial good is attributed to the better character, and that which is

<summary>Aristotle explores the parallel between friendship and justice within various social and political structures, comparing a king’s care for his subjects and a father’s care for his children to a shepherd’s role. He concludes that in these unequal relationships, friendship and justice are distributed based on merit and the magnitude of benefits provided.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Nicomachean Ethics, friendship, justice, polity, beneficence, Homer, Agamemnon, paternal friendship, ancestors, aristocracy, virtue, merit</keywords>

--- Page 308 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of how friendship and justice are manifested in various political systems and social relationships, specifically focusing on timocracies, corrupt systems like tyrannies, and the distinction between a person’s role and their humanity.</meta>

<page-num>315</page-num>
<header>CHAP. XI. ETHICS.</header>

what is appropriate is assigned to each. For in this way, justice is also achieved. But the friendship between brothers resembles that of companions; for they are equals and of the same age; and people of this description mostly devote themselves to the same disciplines and have similar characters. Therefore, the friendship that exists in a timocracy resembles this; for in this form of government it is necessary for the citizens to be equal and virtuous people. Consequently, they govern by turns and on equal terms. Such, then, is the friendship of brothers. In corrupt political systems, however, just as there is little justice, there is also little friendship, and it exists to the smallest degree in the worst system. For in a tyranny, there is either no friendship or very little; since among those where there is nothing in common between the ruler and the ruled, there is no friendship, for there is not any justice either. But the friendship between them resembles that between a craftsman and his tool, between the soul and the body, and between a master and his servant; for these are indeed benefited by those who use them. There is not, however, any friendship with, nor justice toward, inanimate things, just as there is none toward a horse or an ox, or toward a slave in his capacity as a slave, since they have nothing in common. For a slave is *a living instrument; but an instrument is a lifeless slave.* Therefore, so far as he is a slave, there is no friendship between him and his master; but there may be in so far as he is a man. For it appears that there is a certain justice due from every man toward every man who is able to participate in law and agreements; and therefore, there may also be friendship between any two people, so far as each is a man. In tyrannical

<summary>Aristotle compares the fraternal friendship found in a timocracy with the lack of friendship and justice in a tyranny, where the ruler and ruled share nothing in common. He argues that while a slave is viewed as a "living instrument" without rights, friendship is still possible between a master and slave on the basis of their shared humanity.</summary>

governments, however, there is but little friendship and justice; but there is very much of each in democracies; for among those that are equal, many things are common.

### CHAPTER XII.

ALL friendship, therefore, as we have before observed, consists in communion; but it may be divided into that which exists between kindred, and that which exists between associates. But political friendships—the friendships of those of the same tribe, of those who sail together, and such like—are more similar to the friendships of associates; for they appear to exist, as it were, from a compact. Among these, also, hospitable friendship may be ranked. The friendship, likewise, of kindred appears to be multiform, and the whole of it depends on paternal friendship. For parents love their children as being something of themselves; but children love their parents as being something proceeding from them. Parents, however, have a greater knowledge of their offspring <note>so as to know more accurately that they are their offspring</note> than the offspring know that they proceeded from their parents; and that from which a thing is generated has a greater familiarity and alliance with the thing produced,

<summary>Aristotle explains that friendship and justice are most prevalent in democracies due to equality and shared interests, and he begins a classification of friendships, rooted primarily in the paternal bond. He distinguishes between friendships of kinship and those of association, noting that parental love stems from seeing children as an extension of oneself.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, friendship, justice, democracy, communion, kindred, associates, political friendship, paternal friendship, parental love</keywords>

than the thing produced has with its maker. For that which originates from something is the property of that from which it originates; just as a tooth, or a hair, or anything else, is the property of its owner. However, the source from which a thing originates is not the property of any of the things that originate from it, or at least it is so to a lesser degree. <note>1 When cause and effect are of such a nature that the latter derives its existence from the former, then the effect is the property of the cause; since before proceeding from it, it was contained within it. But the cause is not, properly speaking, the property of the effect; for the effect only participates in the cause, but does not contain the whole of it within itself.</note>

The love of parents for their children is also superior to that of children for their parents in terms of length of time; for parents love their children as soon as they are born, but children love their parents only after a period of time, when they begin to understand or perceive who their parents are.

From these points, it is also evident why mothers love their children more <note>than fathers love them</note>. Parents, then, love their children as themselves; for those who proceed from them are, as it were, their other selves by being separated from them. Children, however, love their parents as the source from which they came.

Brothers, on the other hand, love each other because they were born from the same parents; for their shared identity with their parents causes them to be the same with each other. Hence it is said that they share the same blood, the same root, and other such expressions. They are, therefore, in a certain respect, one and the same thing in separate bodies.

Being educated together and having an equality of age also greatly contribute to friendship; for, according to the proverb,

->“Equals delight in those of the same age.”<-

And those who are accustomed to the same things are associates. Hence, also, fraternal friendship is similar to the friendship of associates. Cousins, likewise, and the remaining kindred become joined through the friendship of brothers, because they <note>indirectly</note> originate from the same persons. Some, however, become more united in friendship, and others less, because the source of their lineage is nearer or more remote. But the friendship of children toward their parents, and of men toward the gods, is as toward that which is good and transcendent. For parents and the gods confer the greatest benefits; for they are the causes of existence and of being nourished, and, when children are of a proper age, of being educated. A friendship, also, of this kind, possesses the pleasant and the useful in a greater degree than the friendship of strangers, because their life is, to a greater degree, shared in common. 

Those things, however, are to be found in fraternal friendship which exist in the friendship of associates; and in a greater degree among those who are virtuous, and in short, among those who are similar, in proportion as they are more familiar and love each other from their birth; and in proportion as those who are born from the same parents, who are nourished together, and similarly educated, are more similar in their manners. In this friendship, likewise, the proof which is obtained from time is most abundant and most firm. And things pertaining to friendship exist analogously in the remaining gradations of kinship. But the friendship between husband and wife appears to be according to nature; for man is more a <term>connubial</term> than a <term>political animal</term>; and this is so because a family is more primary and more necessary than a city, and the procreation of offspring is more com—

# CHAP. XII. ETHICS.
<page-num>319</page-num>

common to all animals. In other animals, therefore, the communion proceeds this far <note>that is, as far as to the procreation of offspring</note>; but men and women not only live together for the sake of begetting children, but also with a view toward the necessities and conveniences of life. For their tasks are immediately divided, and those of the husband are different from those of the wife. Hence, they assist each other, contributing their own private possessions to the common <note>good of the family</note>. Because of these things, therefore, both the useful and the pleasant appear to be contained in this friendship. It will also exist on account of virtue, if the husband and wife are worthy characters. For there is a virtue pertaining to each, and they will rejoice in such a thing. Children, however, appear to be a bond; and hence those marriages that are without children are more quickly dissolved. For children are a common good to both the husband and wife; and that which is common connects. To inquire also how a husband ought to live with his wife, and in short, one friend with another, appears to be nothing else than to inquire how justice exists between them. For it does not appear that there is the same justice between one friend and another, nor between a stranger, an associate, or a disciple with one another.

### CHAPTER XIII.

Since, therefore, there are three kinds of friendship, as was observed in the beginning, and according to each some are friends in equality, but others according to superiority; for similarly good men are friends, and <note>between worthy men who are not equally worthy</note> the more worthy may be the friend of the less worthy, and in a similar manner with respect to friendships which exist on account of pleasure, and on account of utility, they may be equal or unequal, and different in the advantages with which they are accompanied;—this being the case, it is necessary that those friends who are equal should be equalized in loving and other things <note>pertaining to friendship</note>, but that those who are unequal should render to each other that which is proportionate to their superiority.¹ Accusations, however, and complaints reasonably take place only, or principally, in that friendship which is founded on utility. For those who are friends on account of virtue are readily disposed to benefit each other; for this is the characteristic of virtue and friendship. But with those who compete with each other in kindness, there are no accusations nor contests;

---

¹ <note>Namely: By however much one friend is better than another, by that much more he ought to be loved.</note>

<summary>This chapter explains that while equal friends should love equally, unequal friends must love in proportion to each other's superiority. It also notes that conflicts and complaints primarily occur in friendships based on utility rather than those based on virtue.</summary>
<keywords>friendship, equality, superiority, virtue, utility, pleasure, Nicomachean Ethics, Aristotle</keywords>

--- Page 314 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Chapter XIII, moving from friendships of virtue to those of pleasure and utility, focusing on why utility-based friendships are more prone to conflict and the distinction between legal and ethical utility.</meta>

<page-num>321</page-num>
<header>CHAP. XIII. ETHICS.</header>

For no one is angry with a person who loves and benefits him; instead, if he is grateful, he will repay him by benefiting him in return. However, the person who gives more [in the benefits which he confers], obtaining what he desires, will not accuse his friend, for each person aspires toward what is good. Nor do accusations and complaints often occur in friendships that are founded on pleasure; for both people obtain the object of their desire at the same time, if they enjoy living together. Indeed, a person would appear ridiculous if he accused someone for not providing him delight, when he is not forced to spend his time with him. 

But friendship that is founded on utility is full of accusations and complaints. Since these friends use each other for the sake of advantage, they are always in need of more, and imagine they have less than is appropriate. They blame their friends because they do not receive as much as they need, even though they believe they deserve to obtain it. Yet those who provide the benefits are not able to supply as much as those being benefited require. 

It appears, however, that just as justice is twofold—for one kind is unwritten, but the other is legal—so also with respect to the friendship founded on utility, one kind is ethical, while the other is legal. Accusations, therefore, occur especially when agreements are formed and dissolved without considering the same kind of friendship [as the one by which the parties are united]. 

The legal friendship founded on utility is that which exists by contract. One kind is entirely commercial, involving immediate exchange [namely, such as takes place in buying and selling]; but another kind is more liberal, in which one thing is to be given for another at a stated time, but by agreement. In this friendship, however, what is owed is manifest and is not ambiguous, but a friendly...

## THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK VIII.

...delay is permitted to take place. Hence, in some of these cases, there are no legal proceedings; instead, it is thought necessary to love those who make agreements out of a sense of loyalty. But ethical friendship does not consist of formal agreements. Rather, what it gives, it gives as if to a friend, and this applies to whatever is shared between them. The giver, however, thinks it appropriate that he should receive an equivalent in return, or even more, as if he had not given the gift but lent it. If he does not receive the repayment he expected from the arrangement, he blames his friend. 

This happens because all or most men desire what is truly noble, but deliberately choose what is advantageous. It is noble to perform a benefit without intending to be benefited in return, but it is advantageous to receive a benefit. Therefore, anyone who is able should make a repayment equivalent to the benefit they received, and do so willingly; for one must not return a friend's kindness reluctantly. If, therefore, he made a mistake from the start and accepted a benefit from the wrong person—for he was not benefited by a true friend, nor by one who acted for his sake—then repayment must be made as if the benefit were based on a formal agreement. 

Consequently, a person who has been benefited by such an individual should promise to repay if he can; if he cannot, the one who conferred the benefit should not expect to be repaid. Thus, if possible, repayment should be made. In the beginning, however, when a benefit is offered, it is necessary to consider who is offering it and with what intention, so that one may either accept or refuse it. But a question may be raised as to whether repayment should be measured by the advantage to the person who receives it, or by the generosity of the person who gave it. For those

Since, therefore, this friendship exists on account of utility, its measure is the advantage of the one who is benefited. For he is the person who is in need, and his friend assists him in order that he may receive an equal benefit in return. The assistance, therefore, provided by the person who is benefited will be as great as that which he received. And just as much or even more must be given by him in return; for it is more noble and fitting. But in those friendships which are founded in virtue, there are no accusations; and the deliberate choice of the benefactor resembles a measure. For the authority of virtue and character consists in deliberate choice.

### CHAPTER XIV.

**DISSENSIONS**, however, take place in those friendships which exist based on superiority; for each person thinks it right that he should have more than the other. But when this occurs, the friendship is dissolved. For the person of better character thinks it is proper that he should have more than the other; for more ought to be distributed to a good man. This is also the case with the person who is more useful; for they say it is not right that he who is useless should have an equal portion with him who is useful; since acts of service would be performed, and not friendship, unless what is done out of friendship is according to the merit of the deeds. For they are of the opinion that, as in financial negotiations, those who invest a larger sum of money receive more profit, so it should also be in friendship. The contrary, however, is the opinion of the person who is needy and of lesser character; for these think that it is the duty of a good friend to assist those who are in want. For what advantage, they ask, is there in being the friend of a worthy or powerful man if no benefit is to be gained from him? It seems, however, that each side thinks correctly, and that it is necessary to distribute more to each person from the friendship—yet not more of the same thing, but more of

<summary>This chapter discusses how conflicts arise in unequal friendships because both the superior party and the needy party believe they deserve a larger share of the friendship's benefits based on merit or need, respectively.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, ethics, friendship of superiority, merit, justice in friendship, equality, reciprocity</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how unequal friendships are maintained through a distribution of benefits that is proportional to each person's merit or need.</meta>

<page-num>325</page-num>
<header>CHAP. XIV. ETHICS.</header>

Honor indeed belongs to him who excels, but more gain belongs to him who is needy; for honor is the reward of virtue and generosity, while gain is the support for poverty. This also appears to be the case in politics. For he is not honored who brings no good to the community; since that which is common is given to him who benefits the community, and honor is something common. For it is not possible for a man to be both enriched and honored by the community at the same time, because no one is willing to accept less in every respect. Thus, honor is given to the one who is poorer in wealth, but money is given to the one who is to be compensated with gifts. For distribution according to merit creates equality and preserves friendship, as we have observed before.

In this manner, therefore, it is necessary to act toward those who are unequal; and he who is benefited either in wealth or in virtue should repay the one who benefited him with honor, thus rewarding him as far as he is able. For friendship requires what is possible, rather than what is exactly according to merit. For a repayment according to merit is not possible in all things, such as in the honors conferred on the gods and parents; since no one can ever bestow these according to what they deserve. However, he who pays homage to them to the utmost of his power appears to be a worthy man. Hence, though it would seem not to be lawful for a son to abandon his father, yet it is lawful for a father to abandon his son. For a return ought to be made by him who is a debtor. But a son can do nothing that matches the benefits he has received from his father, so he will always be his debtor. Those, however, to whom others are indebted have the power of releasing their debtors; and, therefore, a father has this power. At the same time, however, no

so happens, he has nothing which can excite love. Frequently, however, the beloved complains that her lover, having formerly promised everything, now performs nothing <note>that he had promised</note>. But things of this kind happen when one indeed loves the object of his love on account of pleasure, while the other loves her lover on account of utility; and these things are not present in both. For since the friendship exists on account of these things, it dissolves when those things are not accomplished which are the final causes of their love. For they do not love each other, but rather what each possesses, which is not stable. Hence, such also are their friendships <note>namely, they are not stable</note>. The friendship, however, which is founded in character <note>that is, virtuous friendship</note>, since it exists in itself <note>or independent of external circumstances</note>, is permanent, as we have before observed. But friends also disagree when other things happen to them, and not those which were the objects of their desire; for when a man does not obtain that which he desired, it is just as if he obtained nothing. Thus, a certain person promised a harper that he would reward him in proportion to the excellence of his singing. But in the morning, when the harper demanded the fulfillment of his promise, he said that he had "returned pleasure for pleasure." If, therefore, this <note>that is, pleasure</note> had been the wish of each, the harper would have been sufficiently recompensed; but if the object of one was delight and of the other gain, and if the object of one was accomplished but not the other, the compact between them was not well fulfilled.

<header>CHAP. I. ETHICS.</header>

For a person will attend to those things which they lack, and for their sake will give what is required. Regarding the recompense that should be made, however, should it be estimated by the one who gives or by the one who receives? For the one who gives first seems to leave it to the receiver to determine what the recompense should be; which they say Protagoras¹ also did. For when he had taught anything, he ordered the student to estimate what appeared to be the worth of the knowledge he had gained, and he received payment according to that valuation. In matters of this kind, however, it is sufficient for some people to say:

->Let the price a friend appoints be sufficient.²<-

Those, however, who first receive money and afterwards perform nothing they had promised to do—because of the excessive greatness of their promise—are deservedly accused; for they do not perform what they had agreed to accomplish. But the sophists are perhaps compelled to do this because no one would give money for the things they actually know. These people, therefore, because they do not perform that for which they received a reward, are justly blamed. With those people, however, among whom there is no contract for services performed, we have already observed that those who first give to others for their own sake are not to be blamed; for this is the kind of friendship founded on virtue. This conduct, likewise, as it seems, should be adopted by those who are associates in philosophy; for the value of philosophy is not to be measured by money, nor can any honor be conferred that is equivalent to its dignity. But perhaps it is sufficient if a recompense as great as possible is made, in the same manner as toward the gods and one's parents. However, where the gift is not of this kind, but is conferred with a specific purpose in mind <note>that is, with a view to some recompense</note>, a remuneration perhaps should especially be made which will appear to both friends to be according to merit. But if this should not happen to take place, it may seem not only necessary but also just that the person who first received the gift should determine what constitutes equal compensation. For if as much benefit or pleasure is returned as was received, the remuneration will be according to merit. For this also seems to occur in commerce; and in some places, there are laws that forbid legal processes regarding voluntary contracts—as if it were proper that in associations of this kind there should be no other judge, nor any other law, but that all differences should be decided by the person in whom trust is placed and by whom such contracts are made. For they believe that the person who was entrusted to estimate the repayment will judge more justly than the one who placed that trust; since, for the most part, those who possess something and those who wish to receive something do not value it equitably. For everyone thinks that their own property and what they give are of great value. At the same time, however, the repayment should be as great as it is determined to be by those who receive the gift. Perhaps, however, a thing is not to be estimated to be worth as much as it appeared to its possessor, but to be

<summary>This page explores how to determine the fair value of a gift or service, arguing that in matters of philosophy or virtue, the recipient should judge the recompense, much like in voluntary contracts where trust is placed in the receiver's judgment.</summary>

## CHAPTER II.

However, details such as the following are doubtful: namely, whether all things should be assigned to a father and if he should be obeyed in everything. Or whether a sick person should indeed obey the physician; and whether someone voting for the general of an army should prefer a person skilled in military affairs. Similarly, whether it is proper to serve a friend rather than an honorable man. And whether repayment should be made to a benefactor rather than a companion, if it is impossible to do so for both. It is not, therefore, easy to define all such details accurately; for they contain many various differences in magnitude and smallness, and in what is noble and what is necessary. But it is obvious that not everything should be given to the same person; and that, for the most part, benefits should be returned to those from whom they were received, rather than gifts being bestowed on companions—just as it is more proper to repay a loan to the person from whom it was borrowed than to give a present to a companion. Perhaps,

He concludes that while these cases are complex, it is generally more important to repay debts and return benefits than to give new gifts to companions.</summary>

<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK IX.</header>

However, this must not always be done. For if anyone should be taken prisoner by robbers, one might ask whether he who ransoms him should be ransomed in return—whoever he may be—or whether the ransom money should be given back to someone who demands it as his due, even if he has not been taken prisoner; or whether, in preference to all these, a father ought to be ransomed. For it would seem that a man should ransom his father even before himself. Generally, therefore, as we have said, a debt ought to be paid; but if a gift surpasses the debt in terms of what is noble or necessary, we should lean toward it <note>rather than toward the discharge of a debt</note>. For sometimes it is not fair to return a favor which another person has first conferred, when that person indeed conferred the benefit knowing that it was bestowed on a worthy man, but the repayment would be made to someone whom the payer believes to be a wicked man. For sometimes a loan should not even be granted to someone who has previously lent. For the one <note>the wicked man</note>, thinking that he shall receive back what he has lent, grants a loan to the worthy man, but the other <note>the worthy man</note> does not expect that what he has lent will be returned by the wicked man. Therefore, whether the situation actually exists this way—that the merit of the parties is unequal—or whether it does not actually exist but is merely perceived to be so, they will not appear to be acting irrationally. Therefore, as has been frequently observed, statements concerning emotions and actions are only as definite and certain as the subjects with which they deal. It is not, therefore, unclear that the same things should not be bestowed on everyone, nor should everything be given to a father, just as not all things are to be sacrificed to Jupiter. Since, however, different things are

He also explains that one is not always obligated to return favors or loans to those perceived as unworthy or wicked.</summary>

<page-num>333</page-num>
<header>CHAP. II. ETHICS.</header>

...to be returned to parents, brothers, associates, and benefactors, a repayment should be made to each of those things that are proper and appropriate. And indeed, this is how men seem to act. For they invite their relatives to weddings because their lineage is common, and therefore, the actions that are concerned with this are also shared. For the same reason, they also think it especially necessary that relatives should be present at funerals. But it would seem that it is particularly necessary to supply our parents with sustenance because we are their debtors; and it is more honorable to provide these things to the causes of our existence than to ourselves. Honor also is to be paid to parents as it is to the gods; yet not every kind of honor is to be paid to them. For the same honor is not to be paid to a father and a mother; nor, again, is the same honor due to a wise man or the general of an army; but paternal honor should be paid to a father, and maternal honor to a mother. To every elderly man, likewise, honor is to be paid according to his age, by rising from our seat and giving it up to him, and by other similar actions. To associates and brothers, again, freedom of speech must be granted, as well as a shared participation in all things. To relatives, also, to those of the same tribe, to fellow citizens, and to all the rest of mankind, we should endeavor to distribute what is appropriate, and judiciously determine what pertains to each according to closeness, virtue, or usefulness. A judgment, therefore, can more easily be made regarding those who are of the same <term>genus</term>; but for those of a different <term>genus</term>, the decision is more difficult. We must not, however, give up on this account, but must decide as far as circumstances will permit.

### CHAPTER III.

The dissolution of friendships also involves a question, namely: whether friendship should be dissolved with those who do not remain our friends. Or should we say that for those who are friends on account of utility and pleasure, when they no longer possess these qualities, it is not at all unreasonable that the friendship should be dissolved? For they were the friends of these things <note>namely, of things useful and pleasurable</note>, and when these fail, it is reasonable to suppose that they will no longer be attached to each other. He, however, may be <note>justly</note> accused who, loving another person on account of utility or pleasure, pretends that he loves on account of character <note>that is, virtuously</note>. For as we said in the beginning, many disagreements take place among friends when they are not in reality the kind of friends they think they are. When, therefore, anyone is deceived and believes that he is loved on account of his character, though at the same time he does nothing that is virtuous, he should blame himself. But when he is deceived by the pretensions of the other, it is right to accuse the deceiver, and even more so than those who counterfeit money, because the wickedness relates to a more honorable thing. If, however, he admitted him into his friendship

<summary>Aristotle discusses the conditions for ending friendships, arguing that those based on utility or pleasure naturally dissolve when those benefits cease. He highlights that deceiving someone into believing a friendship is based on virtue when it is actually based on self-interest is a serious moral failing.</summary>

Since not everything deserves to be loved, but only that which is good. Neither, therefore, is a bad man to be loved, nor is it necessary that he should be. For it is not proper to be a lover of what is depraved, nor to be made like a bad man. And we have already observed that like is a friend to like. Should the friendship, therefore, be immediately dissolved? Or shall we say, not with all persons, but only with those who are incurable because of their depravity? For assistance ought rather to be given to the character of those who are capable of being corrected than to their worldly possessions, because this is better and more suited to friendship. He, however, who dissolves such a friendship will appear not to act absurdly at all; for he was not a friend to this man as he is now, or to a man of this sort. Hence, as he cannot restore him to virtue after he has changed in this way, he abandons him. 

But if one indeed continues <note>such as he was at first</note>, and the other should become more worthy, so as to greatly surpass him in virtue, is the latter still to treat the former as a friend? Or is this not possible? This, however, becomes especially evident when there is a great gap between them, as in friendships formed from childhood. For if one of these should still remain a child in understanding, while the other should become a most excellent man, how can they be friends when they are neither devoted to the same pursuits, nor delighted and pained by the same things? For these shared feelings will not exist between them toward each other. But without these, they cannot be friends, for they cannot live together. Concerning these details, however, we have already spoken. Shall we say, then—

He also notes that a friendship may naturally dissolve if one person significantly outgrows the other in virtue or maturity, as they can no longer share the same interests or life.</summary>

¹ What Aristotle says here concerning the worthy man living with himself accords with what Plato, in the *<term>Timaeus</term>*, asserts regarding the soul of the world. For among other prerogatives possessed by this soul, he says, "That the <term>demiurge</term>, or its creative cause, established the world as a single solitary nature, able through virtue to associate with itself, needing nothing external, and sufficiently known and friendly to itself. And for all these reasons, he made the universe a happy god." On this passage, Proclus beautifully observes the following: "In what is said here, Plato clearly shows the nature of the solitude that he ascribes to the world, and that he calls that being solitary which looks to itself, and its own inner workings, and to the true measure of its nature. For those who live in solitude are their own saviors, as far as human causes are concerned. The universe, therefore, is solitary in this manner, as being self-sufficient and possessing the power to preserve itself, not through any lack, but through a transcendence of power. He also adds that it is self-sufficient through virtue. For among partial beings <note>such as human beings</note>, only he who possesses virtue is able to associate with and love himself. But every bad man, looking at his own inward baseness, is indignant with himself and his own essence; instead, he is foolishly amazed by external things and seeks the company of others because he is unable to look into himself. The worthy man, however, perceiving himself to be beautiful, rejoices and is delighted; bringing forth beautiful conceptions within himself, he embraces the conversation with himself. For we naturally become familiar with the beautiful, but turn away from deformity."

depravity, flee from life, and destroy themselves. Depraved men, likewise, look for those with whom they may spend their time but flee from themselves; for when they are alone, they remember the many crimes they have committed and expect that the evils which attend such wickedness will befall them; however, they forget these things when they are with others. Also, because they possess nothing lovable, they do not feel friendly toward themselves. Therefore, people of this description neither rejoice nor sympathize with themselves, for their soul is in a state of internal conflict; one part of it is pained because of depravity when it abstains from certain things, while the other part is delighted. One part pulls *this* way and the other *that* way, as if the soul were being torn apart <note>by sense and reason</note>. Even if it is not possible for him to be pained and pleased at the same time, after a short while he is pained that he was pleased and wishes that these delightful things had not happened to him; for bad men are full of regret. Therefore, the bad man does not appear to be friendly even toward himself, because he possesses nothing lovable. But if it is very miserable to be in this condition, everyone should strenuously flee from depravity and endeavor to be virtuous; for in this way, a man will be friendly toward himself and will become a friend to others.

<summary>Aristotle describes how depraved individuals suffer from internal conflict and self-loathing, seeking the company of others only to escape their own painful memories. He concludes that one must flee from vice and strive for virtue to achieve genuine self-friendship and the ability to be a friend to others.</summary>

# CHAPTER V.

**BENEVOLENCE**, however, indeed resembles friendship, yet it is not friendship; for benevolence may be shown toward unknown persons and secretly, but friendship cannot. These things, therefore, have been stated before. But neither is it dilection; for it possesses neither impulse nor appetite, and these follow from dilection. And dilection indeed exists in conjunction with custom, but benevolence may be suddenly produced. Thus, spectators sometimes suddenly become benevolent toward those who compete for prizes at public festivals and join them in their wishes for success, but they do not cooperate with them at all. For, as we have said, they suddenly become benevolent toward them and love them superficially. Benevolence, therefore, appears to be the beginning of friendship, just as the pleasure received through sight is the beginning of love; for no one loves who has not been previously delighted by the form [of the beloved object].¹

> "Do you not know that there is a greater pleasure in beholding than in embracing the beloved object? For the eyes, when looking at each other, reflect the images of bodies as in a mirror. But the <term>efflux</term> of beauty, flowing through the eyes into the soul, has a certain mingling in an <term>ecstasy</term> apart from the body <note>that is, the beauty proceeding from one body through the eyes is mingled with the beauty proceeding from the other, separate from the body</note>. And the pleasure arising from the physical union of bodies is small; since the connection of bodies is innate. For the eye is the <term>conciliator</term> of love."

The pleasure, therefore, arising from this mingling of forms through the eyes is greater than that which is produced by physical union, because the mingling in the former case is in a certain respect incorporeal, being the mixture of forms in the imagination. For the imagination neither consists of parts alone, nor is it without parts. For if it were <term>partible</term> alone, it could not preserve many impressions of forms within itself, since the subsequent would obscure the pre-existing figures; for no body can contain a multitude of figures at once and according to the same position, but the former will be obliterated by the succession of the latter. And if it were alone <term>impartible</term>, it would not be inferior to the rational power of the soul, which surveys all things <term>impartibly</term>.

<summary>This page argues that visual beauty and the "mingling of forms" in the imagination provide a higher pleasure than physical union. It also clarifies that while benevolence is the starting point of friendship, it lacks the active cooperation required for a true friendship.</summary>

<header>CHAP. V. ETHICS.</header>

Hence it may be metaphorically said that benevolence is sluggish friendship; but through length of time and custom, it may become friendship—yet not that friendship which is founded on utility, nor that which is founded on delight; for benevolence does not exist because of these things. For, he indeed who is benefited returns benevolence for the favors he has received, acting justly in doing so. But he who wishes prosperity to the actions of anyone, hoping that through him he shall be enriched, does not appear to be benevolent to him, but rather to himself; just as he is not a friend if he pays attention to him with a view to a certain advantage. In short, benevolence is produced through virtue and a certain integrity, when someone appears noble, or brave, or the like to another person; in the same manner as we said it was produced toward those who compete for prizes at public festivals.

## CHAPTER VI.

**Concord** also seems to belong to friendship; for this reason, it is not merely an agreement in opinion, because that can exist even between people who do not know each other. Nor are people said to be in concord if they are unanimous about just anything, such as the celestial bodies; for it does not belong to friendship to be in concord about such things. Rather, cities are said to be in concord when the citizens are unanimous about matters that contribute to the general good, when they deliberately choose the same course of action, and when they carry out what has been collectively decided. People, therefore, are in concord regarding practical affairs, and specifically those of great importance that might affect two people or the entire community. For instance, cities are in concord when all citizens believe that magistrates should be elected, or that a military alliance should be formed with the <term>Lacedæmonians</term> <note>the Spartans</note>, or that <term>Pittacus</term> should be the <term>archon</term> <note>a high-ranking magistrate in ancient Greece</note>, especially since he is also willing to accept the position. But when every citizen wants to be the archon himself, as was the case among the <term>Phoenicians</term>, then they are in a state of sedition. For concord does not consist of everyone having the same idea about a subject, whatever it may be, but rather when they agree in wishing

<summary>This chapter defines concord as a civic form of friendship where citizens agree on the common good and shared practical actions. It distinguishes this from mere shared opinion or scientific agreement, noting that true concord requires a shared will regarding public leadership and policy.</summary>
<keywords>concord, friendship, political science, common good, Pittacus, archon, Lacedæmonians, sedition, Aristotle</keywords>

--- Page 336 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of concord, specifically how it functions as a form of political friendship when virtuous citizens agree on leadership and the common good.</meta>

<page-num>345</page-num>
<header>CHAP. VI. ETHICS.</header>

the same thing for the same person; as when the people and worthy men agree in wishing that the most excellent men may govern; for thus all the citizens obtain what they desire. Concord, however, appears to be political friendship, as it is also said to be; for it is concerned with what is advantageous, and with those things which relate to the purposes of life. But a concord of this kind exists among worthy men; for these are in concord with themselves and with each other, since they are, as I may say, focused on the same things. For the wishes of men of this description are permanent, and do not undergo a flux and reflux, like the <term>Euripus</term> <note>a strait in Greece famous for its strong and frequent reversals of current</note>. For their will is directed to things just and advantageous; and these they desire in common. But bad men cannot be concordant, except in a small degree; just as they cannot likewise be friends; since they desire to have the greater share of advantages for themselves, but in labors and public services they are deficient. Each man, however, wishing that these things may fall to himself, looks for ways to prevent others from obtaining that which he desires. For concord perishes when justice, which is a common good, is not preserved. It happens, therefore, that they are in a state of sedition, pressuring each other indeed, but being themselves unwilling to do what is just.

<summary>This page describes concord as a "political friendship" that exists among virtuous men who share stable goals and a commitment to justice. Conversely, it notes that wicked men fall into civil strife because their selfish desires for personal advantage and avoidance of public duty destroy the common good.</summary>

<keywords>concord, political friendship, virtuous men, justice, sedition, common good, Euripus, civil strife</keywords>

<page-num>346</page-num>
<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK IX.</header>

# CHAPTER VII.

Benefactors, however, appear to love those they benefit more than those who are benefited love their benefactors; and the cause of this is investigated as something that seems contrary to reason. To most men, therefore, the cause appears to be this: that the recipients are debtors and the givers are creditors. Hence, as in the case of loans, where debtors wish their creditors did not exist while creditors are concerned for the safety of their debtors; so too do benefactors wish those they have benefited to exist so that they may obtain a recompense. Those who are benefited, however, are not concerned about making a recompense. <term>Epicharmus</term> <note>a Greek philosopher and dramatist</note>, therefore, would perhaps say that most men assert these things because they focus on the depravity of mankind; yet to act in this manner seems to be consistent with human nature. For the multitude are unmindful of the benefits they have received, and are more desirous to receive benefits than to give them. It would seem, however, that the cause of this is more natural, and does not resemble the situation regarding a loan of money; for creditors do not love their debtors, but merely wish them to be preserved for the sake of receiving back what they have lent. But benefactors

<summary>This chapter explores the paradox of why benefactors tend to feel more affection for those they help than the recipients feel for them in return. love, and are fond of those they have benefited, though at present they derive no advantage from them, nor are likely to derive any in the future. And this, also, happens to be the case with craftsmen; for every artist loves his own work more than he would be loved by it, if it should come to life. Perhaps, however, this particularly happens to be the case with poets; for they love their own poems beyond measure, and have an affection for them as if they were children. Similar therefore to this is that which pertains to benefactors; for he who is benefited is their work. Hence, this person is more dear to them than a work is to him by whom it is produced. The cause however of this is, that existence is to all beings desirable and lovely; but we exist in <term>energy</term> <note>referring to 'energeia,' or actualized activity</note>; for we exist by living and acting. He, therefore, who produces a work, is in a certain respect in <term>energy</term> <note>present in the work</note>. Hence, he loves the work with a parental affection, because existence also is dear to him. But this is natural; for what the agent is in <term>capacity</term> <note>potentiality</note>, is indicated by the work in <term>energy</term>. At the same time, also, to the benefactor, that which results from the action is beautiful, so that he is delighted with him in whom it is inherent; but to him who is benefited nothing is beautiful in the benefactor, but if anything is, it is <term>utility</term>; and this is in a less degree delightful and lovely. The <term>energy</term>, however, of present good is delectable, as is likewise the hope of future, and the memory of past good; but that good is most delectable which subsists in <term>energy</term>, and in a similar manner that which is lovely. To him who benefits, therefore, the work remains; for a beautiful deed is lasting; but to him who is benefited, the advantage passes away. The memory, likewise, of beautiful deeds is delectable; but the memory of useful

<summary>Aristotle argues that benefactors love those they help because the recipient represents the benefactor's own "energy" or life-activity, much like a poem represents the poet. He distinguishes between the lasting beauty of a noble deed for the giver and the fleeting utility of the benefit for the receiver.</summary>

And <term>dilection</term>, indeed, resembles production; but to be loved is similar to being passive. To love, therefore, and those things that pertain to friendship, belong to those who excel in action. Again, all people love to a greater degree those things which are laboriously obtained. Thus, for instance, people love the wealth which they have themselves procured more than that which they have received from others. It appears, therefore, that being benefited is a thing unattended by labor; but to benefit is laborious. For this reason, also, mothers love their children to a greater degree than fathers; for the part which they play in the procreation of them is more laborious than the part which fathers sustain, and they know with greater certainty that the children are their own offspring. It would seem that this reasoning also applies to benefactors.

<header>CHAP. VIII. ETHICS.</header>

## CHAPTER VIII.

It may, however, be doubted whether a man ought to love himself more than some other person. For those who love themselves excessively are criticized, and they are called "lovers of themselves" as a term of disgrace. And a bad man, indeed, appears to do everything for his own sake, and even more so the more depraved he is. Hence, he is accused of doing nothing without a view toward his own advantage. But the worthy man does everything for the sake of what is noble in conduct; and the more worthy he is, the more he acts in this manner and for the sake of his friend, while neglecting to act for his own sake. 

The actions of men, however, are not without reason inconsistent with these assertions. For they say that he who is a friend in the highest sense ought to love his friend in the highest degree; but he is a friend in the highest sense who wishes well to the person who is the object of this wish for that person's own sake, even if no one should know it. These qualities, however, are especially inherent in a man toward himself, as is everything else by which a friend is defined. For we have observed before that all friendly acts proceed from oneself and extend to others. And all proverbs

It would seem, however, that such a man as this is a lover of himself to a greater degree; for he assigns to himself the things that are most eminently beautiful and good, finds gratification in his most principal part <note>intellect</note>, and is obedient to it in all things. But just as that which is the most principal part of a city appears to be preeminently the city itself—and this is the case in every other system of things—so also, that which is most principal in a man is especially the man himself. He, therefore, who loves this principal part of himself is especially a lover of himself, and is gratified by this. Hence, also, one man is called continent and another incontinent because in the former, the intellect has dominion, but it does not in the latter; this is because every man is essentially this <note>that is, intellect</note>. Likewise, men appear to have performed actions—and to have performed them willingly—which they have especially performed in conjunction with reason. It is not hidden, therefore, that every man is principally intellect, and that the worthy man principally loves this. Hence, he will be especially a lover of himself according to a different type of self-love from the disgraceful kind. It differs from the disgraceful kind as much as living according to reason differs from living according to passion, and as much as the desire for what is beautiful in conduct differs from the desire for what appears to be advantageous. All men, therefore, approve of and applaud those who, in a manner superior to others, endeavor to perform beautiful actions. But

<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK IX.</header>

If what is truly noble in conduct were what all men competed for, and if they endeavored to perform the most noble deeds, then whatever is appropriate would be possessed by all men in common, and the greatest of goods would be possessed by every individual; since virtue is a thing of this kind. Hence, it is necessary for a good man to be a lover of himself; for he himself is benefited by acting well, and he also benefits others. But it is not right for a wicked man to be a lover of himself; for he will hurt both himself and his neighbors because he is subservient to low passions.

With the wicked man, therefore, there is a conflict between what he ought to do and what he actually does; but *the worthy man does those things which he ought to do.* For every intellect chooses what is best for itself, and the worthy man is obedient to his intellect. However, what is said of the worthy man is true: that for the sake of his friends and his country, he will do many things, even if it is necessary to die for them. For he will give up riches, honors, and in short, those goods for which mankind competes, so that he may secure for himself what is noble in conduct.

For he would rather choose to be greatly delighted for a short time than to experience a small delight for a long time, and to live worthily for one year than to live aimlessly for many years. He will also prefer one noble and great action to many small actions. And this is perhaps the case with those who have died for their country or their friends. Worthy men, therefore, choose a great good for themselves and will give up their riches so that they may obtain a greater number of friends. For in this way, indeed, riches fall

In contrast, a wicked man's self-love is harmful because it is driven by base passions rather than intellect.</summary>

He behaves in the same way regarding honors and power; for he will give up all these to his friend, because this is noble and praiseworthy for him. Therefore, he reasonably appears to be a worthy man, who chooses what is noble in conduct instead of these things. It may likewise happen that he may give up certain actions to his friend, and that it may be better for him to be the cause of his friend performing them than to do them himself. Hence, in all praiseworthy things, the worthy man appears to assign to himself more of what is truly noble. In this manner, therefore, as we have said, it is necessary for a man to be a lover of himself, but he should not be so in the way that the masses love themselves.

<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK IX.</header>

### CHAPTER IX.

Regarding the happy man, it is also questioned whether he will need friends or not. For it is said that those who are blessed and self-sufficient have no need of friends, because they already possess all truly good things. Since they are self-sufficient (so they say), they do not lack anything; yet, since a friend is a man’s other self, he provides those things which the man cannot obtain by himself. Therefore, it is also said, “When the divine is favorable, what need is there for friends?” However, it seems absurd that those who attribute every good to the happy man would deny him friends, who appear to be the greatest of external goods. But if it is the role of a friend to benefit rather than to be benefited, and if it is also the role of a good man and of virtue to benefit others—and if it is better to do good to friends than to strangers—then the virtuous man will need those whom he may benefit. For this reason, it is also asked whether there is a greater need for friends in adversity or in prosperity; because the unfortunate person needs those who can benefit him, while the fortunate person needs those whom he can benefit. Perhaps, however, it is also absurd to make the blessed man a solitary being; for no one would choose-

<summary>Aristotle examines the paradox of whether a happy, self-sufficient person needs friends, noting that while some argue the blessed lack nothing, friends are actually the greatest of external goods and necessary for exercising virtue.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Nicomachean Ethics, happiness, friendship, self-sufficiency, virtue, prosperity, adversity</keywords>

--- Page 346 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the beginning of Chapter IX regarding whether the happy man requires friends, specifically addressing the nature of happiness as an activity rather than a possession.</meta>

<page-num>355</page-num>
<header>CHAP. IX. ETHICS.</header>

to possess every good by himself; since man is a social animal and is naturally adapted to live with others. This, therefore, will also be the case with the happy man; for he possesses those things which are naturally good. But it is evident that it is better to spend time with friends and virtuous men than with strangers and random people. Hence, the happy man has need of friends. In what respect, therefore, is the first assertion true—that the happy man does not need friends? Is it because the majority of people think those persons are friends who are useful to them? The blessed man, therefore, will not be in any need of such persons, since true good is already present with him. Neither, therefore, will he be in want of those who are friends for the sake of the delightful, or he will want them only for a short time; for since his life is pleasant, he will have no need for external pleasure. But by not needing friends of this description, he does not appear to need friends at all.

This, however, is perhaps not true. For we observed at the beginning that happiness is a certain activity; but regarding activity, it is evident that it is in the process of becoming, or is passing into existence,¹ and is not present with him who acts like a static possession. But if being happy consists in living and acting, and the activity of the good man is inherently worthy and delightful, as we observed at the beginning; if also that which is appropriate is among the things that are delightful, but we are better able to observe our

But power refers to activity; and that which has the principal authority in a thing consists in its activity. But that which is desirable to him ought to be present with him, or in this respect he will be lacking. The man, therefore, who is to be happy will require worthy friends.

### CHAPTER X.

**ARE** numerous friends, therefore, to be acquired? Or, as it seems to have been elegantly said of hospitality,

> ->Neither lack guests, nor be a guest of many;<-

may it also in friendship be appropriately said, that a man should neither be without a friend, nor again should have

<summary>Aristotle argues that since existence is naturally good and delightful to a virtuous person, they must also perceive and share in the existence of a friend through shared life and thought. He begins a new inquiry into whether there is an ideal limit to the number of friends one should have, comparing it to the rules of hospitality.</summary>
