--- Page 350 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the ideal number of friends for a virtuous person, following the conclusion that a happy man requires worthy friends to share in the consciousness of a good existence.</meta>

<page-num>359</page-num>
<header>CAP. X. ETHICS.</header>

...excessive number of friends? This assertion, therefore, will indeed appear to be very well suited to those who regard utility in friendship; for to be alternately serving many people is laborious, and life is not long enough for them to perform this. Hence, more friends than are sufficient for the proper purposes of life are superfluous, and are obstacles to a virtuous life. Therefore, there is no need of them. And regarding the friends who are acquired for the sake of pleasure, a very few are sufficient, in the same way as sauce is to food. But should a good man have many worthy friends? Or shall we say that there is a certain limit to the number of friends, in the same way that there is for a city? *For a city will not consist of only ten men, nor is it still a city if it is composed of a hundred thousand men.*¹ Perhaps, however, one specific number of citizens cannot be assigned, but any number may be admitted which falls between certain definite limits. There is also, therefore, a certain definite number of friends; and perhaps those people are *not* numerous² with whom it is possible for a man to live; for this appears to be the most essential part of friendship.

---

<note>¹ For in a city consisting of a hundred thousand people, the citizens cannot be known to each other, and therefore it will not be so much a city as a region. Besides, in a city properly so called, there must be orderly and good government; but it is impossible in a community of a hundred thousand men for the governors to know how every man lives, though this is essentially necessary for good government. Hence, in London, which is said to consist of a million inhabitants, it is well known that twenty or thirty thousand people rise every morning without knowing how they shall support themselves through the day.</note>
<note>² In the original, οἱ πλεῖστοι; but it should clearly be οὐ πλεῖστοι.</note>

<summary>Aristotle argues that there is a practical limit to the number of friends one should have, as serving too many is an obstacle to a virtuous life. He compares the ideal number of friends to the size of a city, which must be large enough to be self-sufficient but small enough for citizens to know one another and for the government to function effectively.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Ethics, friendship, utility, city size, population, governance, London, social limits, virtue</keywords>

--- Page 351 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the appropriate number of friends, moving from the practical limitations of utility and pleasure to the inherent difficulty of sharing a virtuous life with many people simultaneously.</meta>

<page-num>360</page-num>
<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK IX.</header>

But it is evident that it is not possible for a man to live with many people and distribute himself among them. Furthermore, it is necessary <note>if the friends are numerous</note> that they should also be friends to each other, if they all intend to spend their time together; but achieving this among many friends is laborious. It is likewise difficult to share joy and grief appropriately with many persons; for it is likely that it may happen at one and the same time that a man should rejoice with one person and grieve with another. Perhaps, therefore, it is well not to endeavor to have a great number of friends, but only as many as are sufficient for the purposes of social life; for it would seem that it is not possible to be a very close friend to many persons.

Hence, it is also not possible to love many; for love is a certain excess of friendship. But this is confined to one person; and, therefore, intense love must be confined to a few. That this, indeed, is the case, seems to be verified by experience. For there are not many friends according to that friendship which exists among close associates; rather, the friendships which are celebrated are said to have existed between only two persons. Those, however, who are the friends of many people and are intimately acquainted with all of them, appear to be the friends of no one, except in a political sense, and these persons are also called obsequious. It is possible, therefore, to be a political friend to many persons, even if the one who is so is not obsequious, but a truly worthy man; but it is not possible to be a friend to many on account of virtue and for their own sake. We must be satisfied if we can find even a few such people <note>that is, who are true friends</note>.

<summary>Aristotle explains that intimate friendship is necessarily limited to a few people because of the emotional and practical difficulty of sharing one's life and virtues with a large group. He distinguishes between "political" friendship with many and the rare, virtuous friendship that usually occurs only between two people.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Nicomachean Ethics, friendship, virtue, social life, obsequious, political friendship, intimacy</keywords>

--- Page 352 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general limitations of friendship to Chapter 11, which investigates whether friends are more essential during times of good fortune or times of hardship.</meta>

<page-num>361</page-num>
<header>CHAP. XI. ETHICS.</header>

# CHAPTER XI.

BUT is there a greater need for friends in prosperity or in adversity? For they are sought after in both. Those who are in adversity require assistance, and those who are in prosperity are in need of associates and those whom they may benefit, since they wish to confer favors on others. In adversity, therefore, friends are more necessary; for this reason, when this is the case, there is a need for useful friends. In prosperity, however, friends are a more worthy and beautiful possession; because of this, men whose circumstances are prosperous search for worthy friends, for it is more desirable to benefit these people and to pass through life with them. For the very presence of friends is delightful in both prosperity and adversity, since the grief of those who are in affliction is lightened when their friends share in their sorrow. Hence, it may also be questioned whether friends share a part of the affliction of their friends, as if it were a part of a burden. Or is this not the case, but rather that the presence of friends being delightful, the realization that they share the sorrow produces a reduction of the grief? Whether, therefore, those who are

But the presence of friends appears to be something mixed; for the sight itself of friends is delightful, especially to those in adverse circumstances, and it becomes a certain aid against affliction. For a friend possesses a consoling power, both in his presence and his words, if he is skillful; since he knows the character of his friend, and what pleases and pains him.

It is painful, however, to perceive that our friend is distressed by our misfortunes; for everyone avoids being the cause of pain to his friends. Hence, those who have a manly nature are careful to prevent their friends from being distressed along with them; unless they perceive that by causing their friends a little pain, they shall themselves experience a relief from great affliction. And, in short, they do not permit others to lament with them, because they are not themselves prone to lamentation. But weak women (<term>$\gamma\acute{\upsilon}\nu\alpha\iota\alpha$</term>), and men who resemble them, are delighted with those who groan with them, and love them as their friends and the companions of their sorrow. In all things, however, it is necessary to imitate the better character.

But the presence of friends in prosperity is accompanied by a pleasant association, and with the realization that they are delighted with our good fortune. Hence, it would seem to be necessary that in prosperity we should readily and cheerfully invite our friends to partake of our good fortune; for it is noble to be beneficent, but we should be hesitant to invite them to partake of our ill

->That I am wretched, is sufficient. <note>Euripides, in *Orestes*.</note><-

But friends should be called upon especially when, having suffered small troubles, we can be greatly helped by their presence. On the contrary, it is perhaps proper to go to those who are in adversity without being asked and with a cheerful spirit. For it is the duty of a friend to help, and especially to help those who are in need and who do not think it right to ask for relief; for this is better and more pleasing to both. With friends who are fortunate, however, we should cheerfully cooperate; for in prosperity, too, there is a need for friends. But we should be slow to go to a friend in order to be helped by him; for it is not noble to be too eager and ready to receive benefits. It is perhaps necessary, however, to be cautious that we do not behave unpleasantly by rejecting the kindness of our friends; for this sometimes happens to be the case.

## CHAPTER XII.

**JUST AS**, therefore, the sight of the beloved is most delightful to lovers, and they prefer this sense over the others because love primarily exists and is produced from it, is it also most desirable for friends to live together? For friendship is a partnership. And in the same way that a person feels toward himself, he also feels toward his friend. Since it is desirable for everyone to perceive, with respect to himself, [that he exists and lives], this is therefore also desirable with respect to a friend. This activity, however, is achieved among friends by living together; so it is reasonable that they desire this. And whatever a person's very being consists of, or whatever it is for which he chooses to live, he wishes to spend his life in that activity with his friends. Hence, some friends drink together, others play dice together, others engage in gymnastics and hunt together, or study philosophy together. They each spend their time together in that activity to which they are most attached in life. For because they wish to live with their friends, they do these things and share in them, as these are the things through which they believe they associate with one another. The friendship,

<summary>Aristotle compares the desire of friends to live together to the desire of lovers to see one another, noting that friendship is a shared life or partnership. He explains that friends spend their time together participating in the specific activities—such as drinking, hunting, or philosophizing—that they value most.</summary>
<keywords>friendship, companionship, shared life, activity, social habits, Aristotle, Nicomachean Ethics</keywords>

--- Page 356 ---
<meta>This page concludes Chapter 12 and the discussion on friendship, transitioning into the study of pleasure.</meta>

<page-num>365</page-num>
<header>CHAP. XII. ETHICS.</header>

The friendship, therefore, of bad men is depraved; for being unstable, they share with each other in bad things; and they become depraved by becoming like each other. But the friendship of worthy men is worthy, and it is mutually increased by their mutual conversation. They also appear to become better by acting together and correcting each other. For they mutually express those things with which they are delighted; which is why it is said,

> ->From good men, what is good is learned. <note>Theognis, v. 35.</note><-

And so much for friendship. It remains to discuss pleasure next.

der. But it appears to be a matter of the greatest importance with respect to the virtue of character to take joy in those things in which it is proper to take joy, and to hate those things which it is proper to hate. For these things extend throughout the whole of life, and have an influence and power toward virtue and a happy life; since things which are pleasant are indeed the objects of deliberate choice, while those that are painful are avoided. It seems, however, that matters of this kind are by no means to be passed over in silence, especially since they involve great ambiguity. 

For some say that pleasure itself is the good, but others on the contrary assert that pleasure is a very bad thing; the former, perhaps, being truly persuaded that it is so; but the latter thinking that it will be more beneficial to our lives to show that pleasure is ranked among bad things, even if it is not. For the majority of people are inclined toward it and are slaves to their pleasures. Therefore, they say, it is necessary to lead them toward the pursuit of the opposite of pleasure; for in this way they may arrive at the mean. 

Perhaps, however, this is not well said. For arguments regarding things that pertain to passions and actions are less convincing than deeds. When, therefore, these arguments conflict with our sensory perceptions, they are despised and also undermine the truth. For he who criticizes pleasure, if he is at any time seen to desire it, seems to be drawn toward it as if every pleasure were of this attractive nature; for distinguishing one pleasure from another is not a skill possessed by the majority. True assertions, therefore, appear to be not only most useful with respect to knowledge, but also with respect to life; for they are believed when they agree with deeds. Hence they encourage those who understand them to live in accordance with them. Of things of this

## CHAPTER II.

**EUDOXUS**, therefore, thought that pleasure was the good itself, because all animals are seen to desire it, both those that are rational and those that are irrational. But in all things, that which is worthy of choice is good, and that which is especially worthy of choice is the best of things. And because all things tend toward the same thing, it is an indication that the object toward which they tend is, for all things, that which is most excellent. For every thing discovers that which is good for it, in the same manner as it discovers nourishment. Hence, that which is good for all things, and which all things desire, is the good itself. These arguments, however, are made credible more through the virtuous character of their author than through themselves; for it appears that he was a remarkably temperate man. It does not seem, therefore, that he made these assertions as a friend of pleasure, but because he believed them to be true. It likewise appeared to him to be no less evident that pleasure is the good itself from its contrary. For pain is of itself avoided by all animals; and in a similar manner, the contrary to pain is worthy of choice to all

# THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK X.

animals. But that which is especially desirable is what we choose not for the sake of something else, nor for the sake of any other thing. It is, however, acknowledged that pleasure is a thing of this kind; for no one asks another person what the final cause is for their delight—as if pleasure were a thing desirable in itself, and something that makes the good to which it is added even more desirable; such as, for instance, acting justly and temperately. Therefore, the good is increased by its own nature. This argument, then, demonstrates that pleasure ranks among the number of goods, and that it is no more of a good than any other good. For every good, when combined with another good, is more desirable than when it stands alone. Plato also refutes an argument of this kind, showing that pleasure is not the good itself; for he says that a pleasant life, in conjunction with wisdom, is more desirable than a life without it. But if the mixed life is better <note>than a life of pleasure alone</note>, pleasure will not be the good itself; for the absolute good will not become more desirable by anything being added to it. It is evident, however, that neither will anything else be the absolute good if it becomes more desirable in conjunction with something that is of itself good. ¹

These three orders are: that good which is imparticipable and superessential; that which is imparticipable and essential; and that which is essential and participable. Of these, the last is the kind that our nature contains; the good which ranks among forms or ideas is essential; and that which is beyond forms is superessential. ### CHAP. II. ETHICS.

...Therefore, is the thing of this kind in which we also participate? For a thing of this kind is the object of investigation. Those, however, who deny that what all beings desire is good, say nothing to the purpose; for we maintain that what appears to all beings to exist, does indeed exist. But he who undermines this belief does not assert anything more credible. For if they denied that what is desired by beings is good,

subject; the next after this ranks as essence, and a part of essence—I mean *the good* which exists among forms—and the last ranks as that which is neither a habit nor a part. When, therefore, Socrates, in the *Republic*, says, "That to the multitude pleasure seems to be *the good*, and to the more refined it seems to be prudence," he signifies that good which resides in our nature and which, because it is an impression of the ineffable principle of things, may be called the summit or flower of our essence. And when he also says that the *idea of the good* is the greatest study, which makes both just things and other things that employ it useful and profitable, and that we do not sufficiently know it—these assertions agree with *the good* that is within us, with that which is in forms, and with that which is understood to precede all things. For *the idea of the good* signifies a participated form, a separate intelligible, and that which exists separately before all intelligibles; since the term *idea*, according to Plato, indicates that object of desire which is established before *all things*—that is, before all things belonging to a certain series. Thus, for instance, *the good* in our nature is prior to everything else pertaining to the soul; *the good* which ranks among forms is prior to everything which is the source of essential perfection; and *the good* which reigns in the intelligible world is prior to every series, and to all things.

In consequence of this triple meaning of the term *the good*, the objections of Aristotle are made as if Plato, in what he says of good in the *Philebus*, focused on the supreme good, the principle of all things, and not on that good which resides in our nature and is derived from the supreme good.

For if both pleasure and pain were bad, it would be necessary that both should be avoided; but if neithe<unclear>r</unclear> is bad, neither is to be avoided, or each is similarly to be avoided. But now, indeed, it appears that pain is avoided as an evil, and that pleasure is chosen as a good. In this manner, therefore, they are opposed to each other.**

## CHAPTER III.

NEITHER does it follow that if pleasure is not among the number of qualities, it is not for this reason good; for neither are the activities of virtue qualities, nor is happiness a quality. They say, therefore, that good is definite, but that pleasure is indefinite, because it admits of more and less. Hence, if they form this judgment from the state of being delighted, the same thing will also take place in justice and the other virtues, in which men evidently assert that there is more and less of things of this kind; for some are more just and brave than others. It is likewise possible to act justly and to live temperately to a greater or lesser degree. But if they admit this to take place in pleasures, they do not seem to have assigned the cause of it, whether some pleasures are indeed unmixed, while others are mixed. What, however, prevents health, which is a definite thing, from admitting more and less, just as pleasure does? For there is not the same symmetry in all things, nor is there always one certain symmetry in the same thing; but even when experiencing a decrease, it remains for a certain time, and differs in being more or less. A thing of this kind, therefore, may also happen to pleasure. As they likewise admit that good itself is perfect, but that motions and genera—

tions are imperfect, they endeavor to show that pleasure is motion and generation. They do not, however, appear to argue this well, since pleasure is not motion. For swiftness and slowness seem to be appropriate to all motion, and though not *per se*, such as the motion of the world, <note>Namely, of the fixed sphere. For this sphere, because of the steadiness of its motion, experiences neither swiftness nor slowness, as is demonstrated in the treatise *On the Heavens*; but if it is compared with the motions of the planets, it will be found that its motion is much swifter than their motions.—**EUSTRATIUS**.</note> yet they are appropriate with reference to another motion. But neither of these is inherent in pleasure. For it is possible, indeed, to be rapidly delighted, in the same manner as it is possible to be swiftly enraged; but it is not possible for the delight itself to be swift or slow, not even with reference to something else. It is possible, however, for walking and growth to be swift or slow, and everything else of a similar kind. Hence, it is possible to be changed quickly and slowly into pleasure; but it is not possible to act swiftly according to it; namely, to be delighted swiftly according to it.

And in what manner will pleasure be generation? For it appears that no random thing is generated from any random thing, but that a thing is dissolved into that from which it was generated. And that of which pleasure is the generation, pain is the destruction. They also say that pain is the deficiency of that which exists according to nature, but that pleasure is the complete fullness of it. But these are bodily passions. If, therefore, pleasure is the complete fullness of that which exists according to nature, that in which this fullness takes place will also

But when complete fullness, indeed, occurs, someone may be delighted, just as when he is cut he may be pained. This opinion, however, seems to have originated from the pleasures and pains associated with food; for when we lack nourishment, and have been previously pained as a result of this want, we are delighted by being completely filled. 

This, however, is not the case for all pleasures. For mathematical pleasures are not accompanied by pain, nor are those sensory pleasures which exist through smell, hearing, and sight. Many memories and hopes are also accompanied by no pain. Of what, therefore, will these be the "generations"? For in these cases there has been no previous lack of anything for which these could be the complete fullness. 

But to those who bring forward disgraceful pleasures <insert>in confirmation of this opinion, it may be said that these are not inherently</insert> delightful. For it must not be admitted that if these are delightful to those who are of a bad disposition, they are also inherently delightful; rather, they are so only to those specific people. Similarly, things are not inherently healthy, sweet, or bitter just because they seem so to those who are diseased; nor, again, are those things white which appear so to those whose eyes are clouded with rheum. It may likewise be said in reply that pleasures are indeed desirable, yet not from these sources; just as being rich is desirable, but not through treachery; and being healthy is desirable, but not by eating just any kind of food. Or it may be said that pleasures are specifically different. For those pleasures which are produced by worthy pursuits are different from those produced by base ones, and it is not

<meta>This page continues the argument that pleasures are specific to the activities they accompany and concludes the evaluation of various philosophical opinions regarding whether pleasure is the ultimate good.</meta>

<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK X.</header>

...possible for anyone to be delighted with the pleasure of a just man if they are not just, or with the pleasure of a musician if they are not a lover of music; and in a similar manner in other things. A friend, also, who is a different person from a flatterer, appears to demonstrate that pleasure is not "the good," or that pleasures are specifically different; for it seems that the association with a friend is for the sake of what is good, but with a flatterer, it is for the sake of pleasure. The one is condemned, but the other is praised, because their associations are directed toward different ends. 

Besides, no one would choose to live while possessing a childish understanding throughout their entire life, even if they were delighted as much as possible with those things which are the objects of childish delight; nor would anyone choose to rejoice in doing something most shameful, even if they were never to feel grief for having done it. We likewise earnestly apply ourselves to many things, even if they should bring us no pleasure, such as seeing, remembering, knowing, and possessing the virtues. It makes no difference if pleasure follows these things by necessity; for we would choose these even if no pleasure were produced from them. 

Therefore, it appears to be evident that pleasure is not "the good" itself, and that not all pleasure is desirable. It is likewise evident that some pleasures are desirable in themselves, but differ in species, or in the things from which they are produced. And this much may suffice regarding what others have asserted concerning pleasure and pain.

<summary>Aristotle argues that pleasure is not the absolute good, noting that genuine happiness depends on virtuous character and that certain values, like knowledge and virtue, are chosen for their own sake regardless of the pleasure they provide.</summary>

<header>CHAP. IV. ETHICS.</header>

## CHAPTER IV.

What pleasure is, however, or what kind of thing it is, will become more evident by resuming the consideration of it from the beginning. For sight, indeed, seems to be perfect at any time, since it does not lack anything which, occurring later, will bring perfection to its form. Pleasure also seems to resemble a thing of this kind, for it is a certain whole. Nor can a pleasure be taken at any time where its form would be perfected by the addition of a longer time. Hence, it is also not a motion; for all motion exists in time and is directed toward a certain end. Thus, for instance, the motion involved in building a temple is perfect when it achieves what it intends to accomplish. It is perfect, therefore, either in the total time or at the specific moment of completion. But in the various parts of that time, all the motions are imperfect, and are specifically different from the whole motion and from each other. For the arrangement of the stones is different from the upright erection of a pillar, and these motions are different from the construction of the temple as a whole. And the motion employed in building the temple is indeed perfect, for it lacks nothing required for its intended goal; but the motion employed in laying the foundation and the roof is

<vocab>Ethics, pleasure, motion, perfection, temple, foundation</vocab>

<summary>Aristotle begins a new inquiry into the nature of pleasure, arguing that it is a complete whole at any moment, unlike motion, which is an incomplete process that only reaches perfection when its goal is achieved.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, ethics, pleasure, motion, perfection, teleology, building a temple, completeness</keywords>

We have, therefore, accurately discussed motion else-

motion and pleasure are different from each other, and
that pleasure is something whole and perfect.

This would also seem to be evident from the impos-
sibility of being moved except in time, but the possibility
of being delighted without time, <note>That is, in an instant;</note>
for that which is achieved in *the now*, or an instant, is a
certain whole. From these things, however, it is mani-

generation; for motion and generation are not predi-
cated of all things, but of those only which may be dis-

<summary>Aristotle argues that while motions like walking are incomplete until their destination is reached, pleasure is a complete whole at any single moment. He concludes that pleasure cannot be defined as a motion or a process of generation because it does not require time to be perfected.</summary>

distributed into parts, and are not wholes. For there is no generation of sight, nor of a point, nor of the <term>monad</term>; nor is there either motion or generation of these. Neither, therefore, is there any of pleasure; for it is a certain whole. Hence, from what has been said, it is evident that a certain pleasure is conjoined with every sense in energy, which acts without being impeded. But the energy of the sense is perfect which is well-disposed toward the most beautiful of the objects that fall under that sense; for perfect energy appears to be especially a thing of this kind. It is, however, of no consequence whether it is said that the sense itself acts, or that in which it exists. But in everything, the energy is the most excellent of that which is disposed in the best manner toward the most excellent of the things which are subject to it. But this energy will be most perfect and most delightful. For there is pleasure according to every sense; and in a similar manner, according to every discursive energy of the soul, and every contemplation. But the most perfect energy is the most delightful; and that is the most perfect which is the energy of that which is well-disposed toward the best of the things subject to it. Pleasure, however, perfects energy. But pleasure does not perfect energy in the same manner as the object of sense perfects sense, when both are in a good condition; just as neither health nor a physician are similarly the cause of being made well. It is evident, however, that pleasure is produced according to each of the senses; for we say that things which are seen, and things which are heard, are delightful. And it is also evident that they are especially delightful, when

Pleasure, however, completes the energy, not as an inherent habit, but as a certain supervening end, such as the bloom of youth in those who are in their prime. However, as long as the sensible or intelligible object is as it ought to be, as is the faculty that judges or contemplates, pleasure will exist in the energy; for when the passive and the active elements are similar and exist in the same way in relation to each other, the same result is naturally adapted to be produced. How, then, does it happen that no one is continually delighted? Is it because he <insert>eventually</insert> becomes weary? For all human concerns are incapable of continuous energy. Therefore, pleasure cannot be generated in an uninterrupted continuity either, for it follows from energy. Some things, however, delight when they are new, but afterward—for this reason <insert>because pleasure cannot be produced incessantly</insert>—do not delight in the same way. For at first, indeed, the discursive power of the soul inclines toward and intently acts upon these things, just like those who look intently at anything. Afterward, however, an energy of this kind is no longer produced, but it becomes relaxed. Hence, the pleasure also is dimmed. It may be thought, however, that all men aspire to pleasure because all of them desire to live; but life is a certain energy, and everyone acts upon and in those things which he especially loves. Thus, for instance, the musician acts with his hearing upon melodies, but the lover of learning acts with the discursive power of his soul

Reasonably, therefore, all men aspire to pleasure; for it gives perfection to the life of each person, which is a desirable thing. We shall, however, omit for the present the consideration of whether we choose to live for the sake of pleasure, or choose pleasure for the sake of living; for these things appear to be joined together and do not allow for separation. For pleasure is not produced without energy, and pleasure gives perfection to every energy.

### CHAPTER V.

HENCE, also, pleasures appear to be specifically different; for we are of the opinion that things that are specifically different are perfected through <note>perfections</note> that are specifically different. For this appears to be the case with both natural and artificial productions; as, for instance, with animals and trees, pictures and statues, houses and furniture. In a similar manner, therefore, we are of the opinion that energies which are specifically different are perfected by things that differ in species. But the energies of the discursive powers of reason differ from the energies of the senses,

<meta>This page continues Chapter V of Book X, discussing how specific pleasures correspond to and enhance specific activities (energies), while contrasting pleasures can interfere with one another.</meta>

and these are specifically different from each other. Hence, also, the pleasures which give perfection to these are specifically different. This, however, will also become apparent from this fact: that each of the pleasures is intimately associated with the energy which it perfects. For appropriate pleasures increase energy; since those who act in conjunction with pleasure perform everything more accurately and with more exquisite judgment. Thus, for instance, those become <note>more excellent</note> geometricians who are delighted to practice geometry, and they understand everything geometrical to a greater degree. The like also takes place with the lovers of music, the lovers of architecture, and the lovers of the other arts; for each of these makes progress in his proper employment if he delights in it. Pleasures, therefore, increase energies; but things which increase them are appropriate to them; and for things which are specifically different, the things which are appropriate are also specifically different. Again, this will to a greater degree become apparent from considering that pleasures which are produced from different things are an impediment to energies. For the lovers of the flute cannot attend to a discourse if they hear anyone playing the flute, because they are more delighted with the melody of the flute than with the present energy <note>that is, than with what is being said</note>. The pleasure, therefore, which is produced by the melody of the flute, spoils the energy of discourse. And in a similar manner, this also happens in other things when a man acts at one and the same time regarding two things; for the more delightful energy expels the other; and this occurs to a still greater degree if it very much surpasses the other in pleasure, so as to render it impossible to act according to the

<summary>Aristotle argues that specific pleasures are uniquely tied to the activities they perfect, enhancing the skill and focus of the practitioner. Conversely, he notes that pleasures from different sources can conflict, with the more intense pleasure often distracting from or displacing another activity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how specific pleasures enhance their corresponding activities (energies) while unrelated (foreign) pleasures hinder them. It also begins to explore how the moral quality of an activity determines the moral quality of its associated pleasure.</meta>

<page-num>383</page-num>
<header>CHAP. V. ETHICS.</header>

...another energy. Therefore, when we are very much delighted with anything, we do not perform anything else to any great degree; but when we are only moderately pleased with certain things, we can do others. For example, those who eat sweets in the theaters are especially accustomed to doing so when the performers act poorly. However, since appropriate pleasure gives accuracy to energies and makes them more lasting and better, while unrelated pleasures corrupt them, it is evident that these pleasures differ greatly from each other. For almost all unrelated pleasures produce the same effect as appropriate pains <note>that is, as the pains which result from certain energies</note>. Thus, if it is unpleasant and painful for someone to write, or for someone to reason, the former will not write and the latter will not reason, because the activity is painful. Therefore, regarding energies, the opposite happens with appropriate pleasures and pains. But those pleasures and pains are appropriate which essentially result from energy. And regarding unrelated pleasures, we have already observed that they produce an effect similar to pain, for they corrupt the activity, though not in the same manner.

Since, however, energies differ in probity and depravity—some being desirable, others to be avoided, and others being neither—pleasures also have a similar mode of existence; for there is an appropriate pleasure in every energy. The pleasure, therefore, which is appropriate to a worthy energy is worthy, but that which is appropriate to a bad energy is corrupt; for the desires of things truly beautiful are praiseworthy, but those of base things are blameworthy.

<summary>This page explains that pleasures specific to an activity improve its performance, whereas unrelated pleasures act as distractions similar to pain. It also establishes that the moral quality of a pleasure is directly tied to the integrity or depravity of the activity from which it arises.</summary>

Pleasures, however, which are in <term>energies</term>, are more appropriate to those <term>energies</term> than desires are. For desires, indeed, are separated <note>from activities</note> by time and by nature; but pleasures are close to <term>energies</term> and are so indistinct from them as to make it doubtful whether the <term>energy</term> is the same as the pleasure. It does not appear, however, that pleasure is either the <term>discursive energy</term> of reason or sensation; for it would be absurd <note>to suppose that it is</note>; though on account of the inseparability of pleasure from <term>energy</term>, it seems to certain people to be the same with it. Therefore, just as <term>energies</term> are different, so likewise are pleasures. But sight differs from touch in purity, and hearing and smell differ from taste. Hence, the pleasures of these senses also differ similarly; and those which pertain to the <term>discursive energy</term> of reason likewise differ, and both these differ from each other. 

It seems, however, that there is an appropriate pleasure for every animal, just as there is also an appropriate work; for this pleasure is that which exists according to its <term>energy</term>. And this will be apparent from a survey of each particular case. For there is one pleasure of a horse, another of a dog, and another of a man; and, as Heraclitus says, an ass would prefer straw to gold, because food is more delightful to asses than gold. The pleasures, therefore, of animals that are specifically different are likewise specifically different, but it is reasonable to suppose that the pleasures of the same animals are without a specific difference. There is considerable variety, however, in pleasures among men; for the same things are painful to some and pleasing to others; and to some, indeed, they are painful and hateful, but to others delightful and lovely. This, likewise, happens to be the case in sweet things; for the same things do not appear sweet to

But if this is well said, as it appears that it is, and virtue—and the good man, so far as he is good—is the measure of everything, those things will be pleasures which appear to be so to the good man, and those things will be delectable in which he rejoices. It is, however, by no means surprising if things which appear to him to be of a troublesome nature should to another person <note>of a different character</note> appear to be delectable. For many corruptions and harmful circumstances happen to men; but these are not <note>simply</note> delectable, except to these specific persons and to those who are thus disposed. With respect, therefore, to those pleasures which are acknowledged to be base, it is evident that they are not to be called pleasures, except by corrupt men. But with respect to those pleasures which appear to be worthy, what is the quality of the pleasure, or what shall we say the pleasure is, which is proper to men? Or shall we say that this is evident from <term>energies</term>? For pleasures follow as a consequence of these. Whether, therefore, there is one <term>energy</term>, or whether there are many <term>energies</term> of the perfect and blessed man, the pleasures which give perfection to these may be properly called the pleasures of man; but the remaining pleasures, in the same manner as the <term>energies</term>, may be named the pleasures of man in a secondary and varied way.

### CHAPTER VI.

Having, therefore, spoken about the virtues, friendships, and pleasures, it remains for us to define happiness, since we accept that it is the goal of human concerns. Therefore, by summarizing what we have said before, the discussion will be more concise.

We have said, then, that happiness is not a state of being; for if it were, it might be present in someone who passes their whole life in sleep, living the life of a plant, and also in someone who is involved in the greatest calamities. If, therefore, these things cannot be accepted, but happiness must rather be referred to a certain <term>energy</term>, as we have observed before—and since some <term>energies</term> are necessary and choice-worthy for the sake of other things, while others are choice-worthy for themselves—if this is the case, it is evident that happiness must be considered one of the <term>energies</term> which are choice-worthy in themselves, and not one of those which are choice-worthy for the sake of something else. For happiness is not in need of anything, but is self-sufficient. 

Moreover, those <term>energies</term> are choice-worthy in themselves from which nothing except the <term>energy</term> itself is the object of pursuit. The actions which are in accordance with virtue appear to be things of this kind; for to perform noble and

# CHAPTER VII.

If, however, happiness (<term>felicity</term>) is an activity (<term>energy</term>) according to virtue, it is reasonable to suppose that it is an activity (<term>energy</term>) according to the most excellent virtue; and this will be the virtue of the most excellent [part or power]. Whether,¹

---

<note>
¹ Aristotle, having spoken of the physical virtues in the 6th book (p. 463), and having largely discussed the ethical and political virtues up to the present book, now directs his attention to the virtues called theoretic or contemplative. In order, however, to show that the division of the virtues made by Plato and the best of his disciples is also adopted by Aristotle, the following summary account of them from the Platonic Olympiodorus is added:

The first of the virtues are the *physical*, which (as has been previously observed) are common to brute animals, being mingled with the temperaments and for the most part contrary to each other; or rather pertaining to the animal nature. Of these virtues Plato speaks in the *Politicus* and the *Laws*. The *ethical virtues*, which are above these, are produced by custom and a certain right opinion, and are the virtues of children when well educated. These virtues, also, are to be found in some brute animals. They likewise transcend the temperaments, and on this account are not contrary to each other. Plato presents these virtues in the *Laws*. They pertain, however, at the same time both to reason and the irrational nature. In the third rank above these are the *political virtues*, which pertain to reason alone; for they are scientific. But they are the virtues of reason adorning the irrational part as its instrument: through prudence adorning the gnostic power, through fortitude the irascible power, and
</note>

<summary>This page begins Aristotle's exploration of the highest form of happiness, which is defined as activity according to the most excellent virtue. A lengthy note clarifies the hierarchy of virtues—physical, ethical, and political—as defined by the Platonic and Aristotelian traditions.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, Plato, Olympiodorus, happiness (felicity), activity (energy), virtue, physical virtues, ethical virtues, political virtues, theoretic virtues, contemplative virtues, Politicus, Laws, gnostic, irascible</keywords>

--- Page 381 ---
<meta>This page continues the footnote from page 389, which provides a summary of the various levels of virtue as described by Olympiodorus and Plato, contrasting them with Aristotle's system.</meta>

<page-num>390</page-num>
<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK X.</header>

...therefore, if this is the intellect, or something else which appears to rule and lead by nature, and to have a conception of things beautiful and divine; or whether through temperance, the power of the soul which is characterized by desire; but ordering all the parts of the irrational nature through justice. And of these virtues Plato speaks much in the *Republic*. These virtues, too, follow each other. Above these are the <term>cathartic virtues</term>, which pertain to reason alone, withdrawing from other things to itself, setting aside the instruments of sense as useless, repressing also the activities (<term>energies</term>) through these instruments, and liberating the soul from the bonds of generation. Plato particularly discusses these virtues in the *Phaedo*. Prior to these, however, are the <term>theoretic virtues</term>, which pertain to the soul introducing itself to natures superior to itself, not only <term>gnostically</term> (through knowledge), as someone might be led to think from the name, but also <term>orectically</term> (through desire); for it strives to become, as it were, intellect instead of soul; and intellect possesses both desire and knowledge. These virtues are the converse of the political; for as the latter act (<term>energize</term>) regarding subordinate things according to reason, so the former act regarding more excellent things according to intellect. These virtues Plato presents in the *Theaetetus*.

I omit to mention another gradation of the virtues besides these, namely the <term>paradigmatic</term>, because they are not mentioned by Aristotle. But for an account of them, I refer the reader to my translation of Plato, vol. iv. p. 273.

I shall only add, that to the superficial reader it will doubtless appear that Aristotle has omitted to mention the <term>cathartic virtues</term>, and that these virtues therefore form no part of the Peripatetic system of ethics. To this I reply, that Aristotle does not indeed expressly mention these virtues, but that he evidently alludes to their existence in the eighth chapter of this book, when he says that *the ethical virtues are connected with the passions, and exist in the composite of soul and body.* Hence, as the <term>theoretic virtues</term> are not connected with the passions, and therefore, as he says, the life according to intellect is divine, with respect to human life these virtues evidently require for their existence a previous purification from the passions; and this purification from the passions constitutes the <term>cathartic virtues</term>.

<summary>This page outlines the Platonic hierarchy of virtues—political, cathartic, theoretic, and paradigmatic—and argues that Aristotle’s focus on the "divine life" of the intellect implicitly requires the cathartic virtues as a prerequisite.</summary>

it is itself divine, or the most divine of all our parts;—the energy of this, according to its proper virtue, will be perfect happiness (felicity). But we have said that this energy is contemplative. And this appears to accord with what we before asserted, and also with truth. For this energy is the most excellent; since intellect is the best of all our parts, and of objects of knowledge those are the most excellent about which intellect is conversant. This energy also is most continuous: for we are able to contemplate more incessantly than to perform any action whatever. We likewise think that pleasure ought to be mingled with happiness; but the energy according to *wisdom* is acknowledged to be the most delectable of all the energies according to virtue. Wisdom, therefore, appears to possess pleasures admirable both for their purity and stability. It is reasonable also to think that those who *possess knowledge*, live more pleasantly than those who *investigate*. That, too, which is called *self-sufficiency*, will especially subsist about the contemplative energy. For of the necessaries of life, the wise and the just man, and the rest of those who possess the moral virtues, are in want; but even when they are sufficiently supplied with these, the just man is in want of those towards whom, and together with whom, he may act justly; and in like manner the temperate and the brave man, and each of the rest. But the wise man when alone is able to contemplate; and by how much the wiser he is, by so much the more does he possess this ability. Perhaps, indeed, he will contemplate better when he has others to cooperate with him; but at the same time he is most sufficient to himself. military transactions: for no one chooses to wage war,
or prepare for it, for the sake of waging war; since he
would appear to be a complete murderer who would
make enemies of his friends for the sake of fighting and
slaughter. The activity also of the politician is of a busy
nature, and, besides the management of public affairs, is
employed in procuring power and honor, or a happiness
for himself and the citizens different from the political
activity—which also, as something different, we clearly
investigate. If, therefore, political and military actions
surpass in beauty and magnitude all other virtuous ac-
tions, but these are of a busy nature, aim for a certain
end, and are not desirable <note>eligible</note> for their own sakes; but the
activity of the intellect, which is contemplative, appears to
excel other activities in intensity <note>ardor</note>, and to desire no other end
besides itself; if also it possesses its own proper pleasure, which
increases its activity, and has, in addition to this, self-
sufficiency, leisure, and unwearied power, so far as the
condition of human nature will permit, with whatever
else is attributed to the blessed, and appears to exist <note>subsist</note> ac-
cording to this activity;—if such is the case, this will be
the perfect happiness of man, when it attains a full
length of life: for nothing belonging to happiness is in-
complete. Such a life, however, will be more excellent than
that which is merely human; for man will not live this way so

<summary>Aristotle distinguishes between the "busy" virtues of politics and war and the "leisurely" virtue of contemplation, arguing that the latter is the highest and most self-sufficient form of human happiness. He concludes that a life dedicated to the intellect is superior to a merely human life, as it aligns with the divine and complete nature of true happiness.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument that the contemplative life, or the life of the intellect, is the highest form of human happiness because it aligns with the divine element within humanity.</meta>

### CHAP. VII. ETHICS.
393

...far as he is man, but as far as he contains in himself something divine. And as much as this part excels the composite nature, so much does its activity <note>energy</note> surpass the activity belonging to every other virtue. If, therefore, the intellect is divine with respect to man, the life lived according to the intellect will also be divine compared to human life. We should not follow the exhortations of those who tell us that, because we are men, we should be wise only in human affairs, or that because we are mortal, we should regard only mortal concerns. Instead, as much as possible, we should strive for immortality and do everything in order to live according to our most excellent part. For this part, though it is small in size, far excels all things in power and dignity. It would also seem that each of us is this part,¹ since that which holds dominion is also more excellent. It would therefore be absurd for a man not to choose his own life, but the life of something else. That which was previously asserted also accords with what is now said: for that which is intimately allied to any nature is most excellent and pleasant to that nature; and hence, a life according to the intellect will be most excellent and pleasant to man, since this part is most truly man. This life, therefore, is also the most happy.

---

¹ The *true man*, according to both Aristotle and Plato, is the intellect: for the essence of everything is the *summit* of its nature.

<summary>Aristotle argues that the life of the intellect is the highest and happiest human life because the intellect is the most divine and essential part of a person. He encourages readers to strive for immortality by living according to this superior part rather than focusing solely on mortal, human concerns.</summary>

### CHAPTER VIII.

BUT *that happiness* ranks in the second place which exists according to another virtue;¹ for the activities according to this virtue are human. For we act justly and bravely, and perform other things conforming to the virtues, toward each other—in contracts, in necessities, in all kinds of actions, and in the passions—preserving for everyone that which is appropriate and proper. All these things, however, appear to be human. Some things, likewise, appear to arise from the body, and moral virtue <note>virtue of manners</note> is in many instances joined to and made familiar with the passions. Prudence, also, is joined with moral virtue, and moral virtue with prudence; since the principles of prudence exist according to the ethical virtues, and the rectitude of the ethical virtues exists according to prudence. These, however, are connected with the passions, and will exist regarding the composite <note>that which consists of soul and body</note>. But the virtues of the composite nature are human. The life, therefore, and the happiness conforming to them, will also be human. The happiness of the intellect, however, is separate;

---

¹ Namely: the happiness which consists in an active life, or in the exercise of prudence and the moral virtues.

<summary>Aristotle explains that the second type of happiness is found in human moral virtues and practical wisdom, which govern our social interactions and passions. Unlike the purely intellectual life, this happiness is tied to our physical nature and the composite of soul and body.</summary>
<keywords>happiness, moral virtue, prudence, human nature, passions, composite nature, ethics, Aristotle</keywords>

<header>CHAPTER VIII. ETHICS.</header>

...for this much may be asserted about it;' since to discuss it accurately is a larger undertaking than is suited to the present treatise. It would also seem that this happiness requires few external supplies, or at least fewer than ethical happiness does. For let it be admitted that both require necessities, and this equally (though the political character works to a greater degree regarding the body and such things), since this is of little consequence; yet it makes a great difference with respect to their activities. For the generous man will be in need of wealth in order to perform generous deeds, and the just man will also need it in order to make repayments. For the wills of men are hidden, and those who are not just pretend they wish to act justly. But the brave man will be in need of power in order to achieve something consistent with the virtue of courage; and the temperate man will be in need of the means of acting temperately. For how will this man, or anyone who possesses any of the other virtues, make his character known? It is an object of inquiry, however, whether deliberate choice has greater authority in virtue, or whether it belongs to actions, since virtue exists in both of these. It is evident, therefore, that what is perfect will be in both. But many things are required for the perfection of actions; and in proportion as they are greater and more beautiful, a greater number of things are necessary. However, for one who acts according to contemplative *virtue*, there is no need for such things as far as it concerns this activity; but, as I may say, they are impediments to his contemplation. Yet insofar as he is a human being and lives among many...

But that perfect happiness is a certain contemplative activity may become apparent from this: we consider the gods to be especially blessed and happy. What kind of actions, however, is it fitting to ascribe to them? Shall we say just actions? Or will they not appear ridiculous if they form contracts and return deposits, and do other things of this kind? Shall we say then that they are brave, enduring things of a terrible nature and encountering dangers because it is noble to do so? Or that they are liberal? But to whom will they give? It would, however, be absurd to suppose that there is money among them, or anything of this kind. And if they are temperate, what will this temperance be? Or is the praise not inappropriate which celebrates them as not having depraved desires? But if we should enumerate everything pertaining to actions, it would appear to be small and unworthy of the gods.¹ All men, however, acknowledge that they live, and therefore that they are active; for it must not be supposed that they pass their life in sleep like Endymion. ¹ <note>All the virtues exist in divinity, but according to a *divine* and not a *human* characteristic; for the virtues are good, and all goodness originates from divinity. Aristotle, therefore, when he excludes from the gods all the virtues except the theoretical, intends only to signify that they do not exist in them as they do in us, but are essentially different in them, as being characterized by a divine intellect.</note>

<note>¹ This contemplative energy of divinity is beautifully illustrated by Proclus as follows, in his admirable manuscript commentary on the *Parmenides* of Plato:—"It is by no means appropriate to doubt the indivisible knowledge of divinity, which knows sensible things without possessing physical senses, and knows divisible natures without possessing a divisible energy. Without being present to things in a specific place, divinity knows them prior to all local presence and imparts to everything that which everything is capable of receiving. The unstable essence, therefore, of apparent things is not known by Him in an unstable way, but in a definite manner; nor does He know that which is subject to all kinds of changes with uncertainty, but in a manner that is perpetually the same. For by knowing Himself, He knows everything of which He is the cause, possessing a knowledge transcendently more accurate than that which is coordinate to the objects of knowledge; since a causal knowledge of everything is superior to every other kind of knowledge. Divinity, therefore, knows without busily attending to the objects of His thought, because He remains within Himself, and, by only knowing Himself, knows all things. Nor does He lack sense, opinion, or science in order to know sensible natures; for it is He Himself who produces all these, and who, in the unfathomable depths of His own thoughts, comprehends a united knowledge of them according to their cause, and in one simplicity of perception. It is just as if someone, having built a ship, should place in it men he himself created, and, in consequence of possessing a diverse art, should add a sea to the ship, produce certain winds, and afterwards launch the ship into the newly created ocean. That which is most closely allied to this [activity of God] will be most suited to achieve happiness. As an evidence of the truth of this, other animals, which are completely deprived of such an activity, do not share in happiness. For the entire life of the gods is indeed blessed, but the life of men is blessed only insofar as they possess a certain likeness to such an activity. Among other animals, however, none are happy, because they do not participate in contemplation in any respect. Therefore, as far as contemplation extends, so far also does happiness extend; and the happiness of those beings is greater in whom there is more contemplative activity; and this is not by chance, but is due to contemplation itself, for it is inherently honorable. Hence, happiness will be a form of contemplation.

Thus, and to a far greater degree, that divine intellect—the creator of the universe—possessing the causes of all things, both gives existence to and contemplates whatever the universe contains, without departing from the contemplation of himself. But if, with respect to intellect, one kind is more partial and another more universal, it is evident that there is not the same intellectual perfection in all things; rather, where intelligible realities have a more universal and undivided existence, there the knowledge is more universal and indivisible; and where the number of forms proceeds into multiplicity and extension, there the knowledge is both one and multiform. Hence, this being admitted, we cannot wonder when hearing the Orphic verses, in which the theologian says:

> Αυτη δε Ζηνος και εν ομμασι πατρος ανακτος,

<header>CHAP. VIII.   ETHICS.</header>

External prosperity, however, will be necessary <insert>to the person who acts according to theoretical virtue</insert>, since he is a human being; for human nature is not self-sufficient for the purposes of contemplation; but it is also necessary that the body should be healthy, and that it should be supplied with food and other necessities. It must not, however, be supposed that because it is not possible for a person to be blessed without external goods, the happy person will therefore require many of these, or very great ones; for neither a condition of self-sufficiency, nor judgment, nor action, consists in an excess <insert>of external goods</insert>. But it is possible for those who have no power over the earth and sea to perform noble deeds; since a person, from moderate possessions, may be able to act according to virtue. The truth of this, however, may be clearly seen; for private individuals appear to act no less fairly, but even more so, than powerful rulers. But moderate possessions are sufficient for this purpose; for the life of the person who acts according to virtue will be happy. And Solon perhaps well defined those who are happy by saying that they are such as are moderately provided with external possessions, and who perform the most noble actions, and live temperately; since it is possible for those whose possessions are but moderate to do those things which ought to be done. Anaxagoras likewise appears to have thought that the happy man was neither the rich man nor the powerful ruler, when he says, "that it would not be at all surprising if I should be considered by the multitude as an absurd and miserable man." For the multitude judge from external circumstances, having a sensory perception of these alone. The opinions, also, of the wise seem to agree with these assertions. Things of this kind, there-

<meta>This page concludes the argument from the previous page that ethical claims must be tested against real-world actions, and that the person who lives a life of the mind is both most favored by the divine and the most truly happy.</meta>

<page-num>400</page-num>
<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK X.</header>

...fore, carry a certain credibility. However, a judgment regarding the truth in practical affairs must be formed from deeds and the way of life; for it is in these that the primary authority resides. Therefore, it is necessary to consider what has been said before by referring it to the deeds and the life of a person. And when the assertions agree with deeds, they are to be accepted; but when they are inconsistent, they are to be considered as mere words. But the person who acts according to the intellect, who cultivates it, and is mentally disposed in the best way, is also, it would seem, most dear to the divine. For if the gods pay any attention to human affairs, as it appears they do, it is also reasonable to suppose that they will be delighted with that which is most excellent and most like themselves; and this is the intellect. It is also reasonable that they will reward those who especially love and honor this, as they take care of what is dear to themselves and act rightly and well. It is, however, quite evident that all these things are especially present in the wise man. Therefore, he is most dear to the divine. It is also probable that the same man is the happiest. So in this way, the wise man will also be especially happy.

## CHAPTER IX.

ARE we therefore to think that if these things and the virtues—and likewise friendship and pleasure—have been sufficiently described, our purpose is completely fulfilled? Or shall we say, as has been observed before, that the goal in practical affairs is not to survey and know each particular, but rather to perform it? Therefore, it is not sufficient in the case of virtue to know it, but there should also be an effort to possess and use it; or must we strive in some other way to become good men? If, therefore, words were sufficient by themselves to make men worthy, they ought justly, as Theognis says, to be valued at a great price, and it would be necessary to obtain them. Now, however, they appear to be powerful enough to encourage and excite noble-minded young men, and to make those whose character is noble and who are truly lovers of honorable conduct obedient to virtue; but they are incapable of urging the multitude toward integrity. For the multitude are not naturally inclined to be obedient out of a sense of shame, but out of fear; nor do they abstain from bad conduct because of the disgrace with which it is at-

controlled, but through punishment. For, living by passion, they pursue their pleasures and the things through which they may be obtained; but they avoid the pains opposed to these. They have no conception, however, of what is beautiful in conduct and truly delightful, because they have never tasted it. What argument, therefore, can lead such men to orderly conduct? For it is not possible, or at least it is not easy, to erase with words those things which have for a long time been impressed upon their characters. But perhaps we must be satisfied if, having everything present by which we are accustomed to become worthy, we are able to partake of virtue.

Some, however, are of the opinion that men become good by nature, others by habit, and others by teaching. Regarding goodness from nature, it is evident that this is not in our power, but is inherent in those who are truly fortunate through some divine cause. But it must be considered whether it is true that words and precepts do not have power over all men; it is necessary that the soul of the listener should have been previously prepared by habits to rejoice and hate properly, like land intended to nourish seed. For he who lives under the influence of passion will not listen to the reasoning that dissuades him from such a life. How, then, is it possible to persuade someone so disposed to alter his conduct? In short, passion does not appear to yield to reason, but to force. Therefore, it is necessary that character should already exist in a way that is appropriate to virtue, so that its possessor may love what is beautiful and be disgusted by what is

<meta>This page continues Aristotle's argument that since most people are not moved by reason alone, the state must use laws and the threat of punishment to cultivate virtue and maintain order.</meta>

<header>CHAP. IX. ETHICS.</header>

disgraceful in conduct. To obtain, however, a proper education for virtue from our youth is difficult without being nurtured by laws which enforce the duties of virtue. For to live temperately and to be accustomed to endurance is not pleasing to the majority, and especially to youth. Hence, it is necessary that education, studies, and pursuits should be ordained by laws; for through custom they will cease to be painful. But perhaps it is not sufficient for youth to obtain a proper education and to have proper attention paid to them, but it is also necessary, when they have arrived at the maturity of manhood, that they should study and be accustomed to these things; and in these we shall likewise require the assistance of the laws; and in short, throughout the whole of life. For the majority are more obedient to necessity than to reason, and to punishment than to what is beautiful in conduct. Hence, some persons are of the opinion that legislators ought indeed to inspire men to virtue and exhort them to it for the sake of what is beautiful in conduct, because worthy men will readily, due to their character, obey their exhortations; but that discipline and punishments should be inflicted on those who disobey them and who are naturally less suited for it; and that the incurable should be entirely removed from the community. For, they say, the worthy man, and he who lives with a view to what is beautiful in conduct, will be obedient to reason; but the bad man who pursues pleasure is to be punished by pain, like a beast of burden. Hence, they add, it is necessary that such pains should be employed as are especially contrary to the pleasures which they embrace.

If, therefore, as we have said, it is required that he

But the law possesses a necessarily compelling power, being a mandate proceeding from a certain prudence and intellect. And those who oppose the impulses of depraved men, though they are right in doing so, are hated <note>by the multitude</note>. Law, however, when it ordains what is equitable, is not met with resentment. But in the city of the Lacedæmonians alone, the legislator appears to have paid attention to education and pursuits; while in most cities such things are neglected, and everyone lives as he pleases, in the manner of the Cyclops:

> By these no statutes and no rights are known,
> No council held, no monarch fills the throne;

But if these matters are neglected by the public, it would seem appropriate for every individual to contribute to the advancement of their children and friends in virtue, or at least intend to do so. From what has been said, however, it would seem that this can be achieved especially by someone who possesses the power of a legislator. For care for the general welfare is carried out through the laws, while fair and just care is accomplished through virtuous men. It does not, however, seem to make any difference whether the laws are written or unwritten, nor whether one person or many are disciplined by them—just as it makes no difference in music, gymnastics, or other subjects of study. For just as legal institutions and customs are effective in cities, so are a father's commands and customs within families; and these are even more effective because of the family bond and the benefits parents bestow upon their children. For children already loved their parents before receiving these commands and are naturally inclined to be obedient to them. Furthermore, private education differs from public education in the same way that in medicine <note>specific prescriptions differ from universal ones</note>. For generally, indeed, fasting and rest are beneficial to one who has a fever; but for this specific person, they perhaps are not. And a master of the <term>pugilistic art</term> will perhaps not assign the same style of fighting to all his pupils. It would seem, however, that individual cases will be handled more accurately when private attention is paid to them; for then each person will better receive what is specifically suited to them. But the physician, the trainer of gymnastic exercises, and every other professional will best care for an *individual* if they know *universally* what is beneficial to everyone, or to persons

He argues that a father's influence in the home functions similarly to a legislator's influence in a city, but with the added strength of natural affection.</summary>
