of a certain kind; for sciences are said to be, and in reality are, concerned with what is universal. Perhaps, however, nothing prevents a man from attending to a specific thing properly, even if he lacks scientific knowledge, provided he accurately observes from experience what happens in each particular case—just as some people seem to be excellent physicians for themselves, but are unable to provide medical help to anyone else.

Perhaps, however, it would seem <note>notwithstanding this</note> to be no less necessary for someone who wishes to become a skilled professional and a theorist to proceed toward what is universal and to know it as far as it can be known; for we have already observed that sciences are concerned with this. Perhaps it is also necessary that whoever wishes to make others better through the care he gives them—whether they are many or few—should strive to become skilled in legislation, if it is indeed through laws that we can be made into good men. 

Hence, it is not the task of just any random person to make someone—or someone committed to his care—properly disposed toward virtue. Rather, if it belongs to anyone to achieve this, it is the task of the man of science, just as in medicine and other arts where specific care and prudence are required.

Is it necessary, therefore, to consider next how or from where a person may acquire legislative skill? Or, as in other arts, is this to be learned from those who are familiar with the management of public affairs? For this appears to be a part of political science. Or shall we say that a similar thing does not seem to happen in political science as in other sciences and faculties? For in the others, the same people are seen to both teach the skills and practice them, as is evident with physicians and painters. But the

<summary>Aristotle explains that while experience allows for specific care, true expertise in cultivating virtue requires an understanding of universal principles and legislation. He then questions whether legislative skill is learned from politicians, noting that politics seems different from other arts where practitioners and teachers are the same people.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Nicomachean Ethics, universal science, experience, legislation, virtue, political science, medicine, education</keywords>

--- Page 398 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on how to acquire legislative skill and political science, contrasting the claims of teachers (sophists) with the actual practice of statesmen.</meta>

<page-num>407</page-num>
<header>CHAP. IX. ETHICS.</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

# CHAP. IX. ETHICS.

Sophists indeed claim to teach politics, yet none of them actually serve in a political capacity. It would also seem that those who are engaged in the administration of public affairs do so through a certain power and experience, rather than by the exercise of their reasoning faculty. For they do not appear to either write or speak about such matters, though perhaps doing so would be better than composing legal or public speeches. Nor, again, is it evident that they have made either their own children, or the children of their friends, into statesmen. It is reasonable, however, to suppose that they would have done so if they had been able; for they could not have left anything better to their cities, nor could they have deliberately chosen anything more excellent than this power, either for themselves or their dearest friends.

Nevertheless, experience appears to contribute in no small degree <note>to the management of public affairs</note>; for otherwise, men would not become more political simply by being accustomed to political life. Hence, experience seems to be necessary for those who desire to be skilled in political science. Those sophists, however, who claim to teach political science appear to be very far from possessing this ability; for, in short, they neither know what kind of thing it is, nor what the subjects are with which it is concerned. For if they did, they would not assume it to be the same as the art of rhetoric, or inferior to it; nor would they think it is easy for a person to act as a legislator simply by collecting the most celebrated and well-regarded laws. They say that the best laws are simply to be selected, as if that selection did not require intelligence, or as if judging correctly was not one of the greatest of tasks, in the same way as it is in matters pertaining to music. For skillful men judge correctly regard—

<summary>Aristotle critiques the sophists for attempting to teach politics without practical experience, arguing that political science and legislation require more than just a collection of laws; they require high-level judgment and intellect.</summary>
<keywords>sophists, politics, political science, legislation, experience, rhetoric, judgment, public affairs</keywords>

--- Page 399 ---
<meta>This page concludes the final chapter of Book X of the Nicomachean Ethics, transitioning from a critique of the Sophists' approach to political science toward Aristotle's own proposed study of legislation and constitutions, which serves as the introduction to his *Politics*.</meta>

<page-num>408</page-num>
<header>THE NICOMACHEAN BOOK X.</header>

...ing the works in which they are skilled, and understanding by what means or in what manner they may be accomplished, and what their appropriate adaptations are. But for the unskilled, it is enough not to be ignorant of whether a work is well or poorly done, in the same way as one judges the art of painting. Laws, however, resemble political works. How, therefore, can anyone be fit to become a legislator from these collections, or judge which of them are the best? For it does not appear that people become skilled in medicine by merely reading medical books; even though these books try to point out not only the cures but also the remedies to be applied and the methods of treatment, while also distinguishing the physical habits <note>or constitutions</note> of each person. It seems, however, that these things are beneficial to those who are already skilled, but useless to those who lack scientific knowledge. 

Perhaps, therefore, collections of laws and constitutions may be useful to those who are able to examine and judge what is well-established or the opposite, and what the appropriate adaptations are in these cases. But those who discuss such matters without the practical political habit will never be able to judge well, except by chance—though perhaps they will become more intelligent in these specific details. Since, therefore, the political thinkers before us have failed to investigate legislation, it will perhaps be better for us to consider it more attentively, and in short to discuss the nature of a constitution <note>universally</note>, so that the philosophy concerning human affairs may be brought to as much perfection as possible. 

In the first place, therefore, if anything has been well said by the ancients on this subject, we shall try to recount it. Next, from the collections that have been made of constitutions, we shall try to examine the nature of those things that preserve and corrupt cities, and the...

<summary>Aristotle argues that true legislative skill requires practical experience rather than just reading collections of laws, and he outlines his plan to study constitutions and the causes of political stability and decay.</summary>
<keywords>legislation, political science, medicine analogy, constitutions, political habit, Aristotle, ancients, philosophy of human affairs</keywords>

several polities, and from what causes some of them are well-governed, while others are poorly governed. For when these things have been surveyed, perhaps we shall be better able to perceive what kind of polity is the best; how each is to be arranged, and what laws and customs it should use. We shall begin, therefore, the discussion of politics.¹

¹ From this, it is evident that the treatise on *Politics* ought to follow the *Nicomachean Ethics* immediately; but as there are two other treatises on ethics, namely the *Great* and the *Eudemian Ethics*, I thought it better to bring together all that Aristotle has written on morals before presenting the reader with his *Politics*.
